Dedication

**To Gautama, the Supreme Arahant,**\
**whose liberation teaching stands as a profound path to the Deathless.**

To the ancient seekers of truth who, guided by their Teacher and firmly grounded in their conviction, pursued the path of liberation with sincerity, courage, and unwavering dedication.

To those who have sought the truth of **Attā, Citta, and Viññāṇa** with sincerity and discernment, and who have never been content to accept inherited conclusions without examination.

And to all who have the courage to seek truth beyond convention, to examine the nature of existence with unwavering conviction, and to pursue the path of liberation with confidence in Gautama's Dhamma.

May this work contribute to the recovery and faithful articulation of Gautama\'s original liberation teaching, and serve those who aspire to realise the Deathless.

**\**

**Acknowledgments**

This work is the product of many years of study, contemplation, renunciant practice, and sustained inquiry into the nature of existence and Gautama's path to liberation. Its development has been shaped by the teachings preserved in the ancient spiritual literature of India, particularly the Pāḷi discourses, Vedic knowledge, and early Jain renunciant literature.

I am deeply indebted to the generations of teachers, sages, and renunciants whose dedication preserved these bodies of spiritual knowledge. Their words and contemplative inquiries have provided an invaluable foundation for examining questions concerning *Attā*, *Citta*, *Viññāṇa*, consciousness, and liberation. Although separated from us by millenniums, their contributions continue to invite deep reflection and investigation.

My years of *bhikkhu* training and meditation practice have also been essential to this inquiry. The disciplines of renunciation, ethical conduct, concentration, and contemplative examination have continually reminded me that spiritual questions cannot be approached through intellectual discussion alone. The pursuit of understanding must remain connected to the actual purpose of the path: liberation.

I also wish to acknowledge those who have supported my *bhikkhu* life, teaching, research, and writing. Their generosity, encouragement, and practical assistance have made it possible to devote time and effort to these undertakings. I remain grateful to all who contribute, in ways both visible and unseen, to the preservation and transmission of spiritual knowledge.

Finally, I acknowledge the readers who approach this work with sincerity, intellectual independence, and a willingness to examine its arguments carefully. The questions addressed in these pages concern matters of profound significance. They deserve neither unquestioning acceptance nor premature dismissal, but careful attention to the original terminology, textual evidence, and meaning of the teachings.

**The Intellectual Foundation of My Writings**

The writings of Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera are guided by an Arahanist intellectual foundation, with a singular purpose: to seek, examine, and communicate the truth concerning spiritual existence and Gautama\'s original liberation teaching.

The following principles guide this body of work:

**1. Truth is the objective.**

Truth is the ultimate purpose of intellectual and spiritual inquiry. Our concern is to recognise what is true, examine it carefully, and communicate it faithfully.

**2. Gautama\'s original liberation teaching is central.**

The early **Pāḷi** suttas still constitute the primary textual foundation of this inquiry. The words, terminology, and teachings preserved in these discourses deserve direct and careful examination.

**3. The wider Indian spiritual heritage is essential.**

The inquiry draws upon the three major bodies of Indian spiritual literature: Vedic, Jain, and **Pāḷi** sources. Together, they provide an extensive textual foundation for examining spiritual existence, consciousness, renunciation, and liberation.

**4. Vedic knowledge deserves its proper standing.**

Vedic knowledge, including the teachings concerning Ātman and Brahman, the Munis, and the Upaniṣads, is approached with the seriousness and respect it deserves as an integral part of India\'s spiritual heritage.

**5. Arahanism provides the intellectual standpoint.**

Arahanism is the framework through which these questions are examined, with Gautama\'s liberation teaching at its centre. It is not simply a subdivision of Buddhism or the Śramaṇa tradition, but a distinct commitment to recovering, articulating, and preserving the liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

**6. The pursuit of truth takes precedence over the study of religious developments.**

These writings are concerned principally with identifying and communicating truth, rather than tracing the historical evolution of religious doctrines or cataloguing their subsequent additions and transformations.

**7. Textual precision is indispensable.**

Every examination must remain attentive to original terminology, linguistic context, textual evidence, and the precise meaning of the teachings. Conclusions must arise from careful investigation rather than inherited assumptions.

**A Guiding Principle**

**Seek the truth. Examine the evidence. Communicate what is true.**

These principles guide my books, essays, translations, commentaries, and ongoing research. They reflect a sustained commitment to the pursuit of truth and the recovery of Gautama\'s original liberation teaching.

Bhante Mudita

*Ācariya & Spiritual Founder*

Arahanist Order of Canada

## Sammā-sambuddha: Restoring Gautama\'s Original Attainment and the Meaning of Liberation

***An Arahanist Examination of Linguistic Distortion, Historical Reinterpretation, and the Secularization of Gautama\'s Teaching***

### 1. When Translation Begins to Distort Understanding

The conventional translation of *Sammā-sambuddha* as \"Fully Self-Awakened One\" presents a serious problem in the modern understanding of Gautama\'s title.

The Pāli expression does not contain the word *self* as an independent lexical component. Nevertheless, this addition has become firmly established in Buddhist textbooks, academic publications, and popular explanations.

Why introduce a word that the original expression does not contain?

This is not a trivial matter of translation preference. When an additional concept is repeatedly inserted into an ancient religious expression, readers eventually begin to accept that concept as part of the original meaning. An interpretation becomes a translation, and a translation becomes an established understanding.

The consequences extend beyond linguistics. They influence how people understand Gautama, the nature of his attainment, and ultimately the purpose of his teaching.

Arahanism considers this an example of a much wider problem in the transmission of Gautama\'s original liberation teaching: the gradual replacement of original meanings with subsequent interpretations.

### 2. Gautama Attained Liberation, Not Merely an Abstract Awakening

Before examining the translation of his title, we must first clarify the nature of Gautama\'s attainment.

From the Arahanist perspective, Gautama\'s attainment must be understood fundamentally as liberation (*vimutti*).

He purified his citta, eradicated the underlying defilements, and became completely unbound from worldly bondage.

His attainment was not simply the acquisition of knowledge, the discovery of a philosophical truth, or an intellectual awakening. It was the complete attainment of liberation from worldly bondage through the purification of his own being.

This distinction matters.

Modern presentations frequently employ the language of awakening without adequately communicating what that attainment actually involved. Consequently, readers may understand Gautama primarily as a philosopher who discovered a new way of understanding existence, rather than as a supreme renunciant who attained complete liberation from the worldly control and entrapment.

For Arahanism, the ultimate objective of Gautama\'s teaching is not intellectual enlightenment or an improved worldly existence.

It is the eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*; the purification of *citta*; and the attainment of liberation.

The entire path must therefore be understood in relation to its ultimate destination:

***Sīla → Samādhi → Paññā → Nibbidā → Virāga → Vimutti.***

Without preserving this objective, discussions of awakening risk becoming detached from the very liberation that gives Gautama\'s teaching its purpose.

### 3. The Linguistic Problem with \"Fully Self-Awakened One\"

The Pāli expression *Sammā-sambuddha* should be examined according to its actual linguistic components.

- *Sammā* --- rightly, correctly, properly.

- *Sam-buddha* --- fully awakened.

The term *sammā* appears throughout Gautama\'s teaching, particularly in the Noble Eightfold Path:

- *Sammā-diṭṭhi* --- Right View.

- *Sammā-saṅkappa* --- Right Intention.

- *Sammā-vācā* --- Right Speech.

- *Sammā-kammanta* --- Right Action.

- *Sammā-ājīva* --- Right Livelihood.

- *Sammā-vāyāma* --- Right Effort.

- *Sammā-sati* --- Right Mindfulness.

- *Sammā-samādhi* --- Right Concentration.

Its consistent semantic emphasis is rightness and correctness.

It does not mean self.

The assertion that *Sammā-sambuddha* means \"self-awakened\" has no legitimate basis in either the linguistic composition of the Pāli title or historical fact. It is an unfounded attribution of meaning, introducing a concept that is not expressed by the original terminology and does not represent the truth. Such a claim is deceptive and may serve a dark agenda. The word *sammā* means rightly, correctly, or properly---that is all; it does not mean *self*. There is no justification for substituting an invented lexical meaning for the actual meaning of the Pāli.

A translation must be faithful to the original language, not shaped by preconceived interpretations, theological preferences, or established conventions. An interpretation unsupported by the linguistic evidence cannot be elevated into a factual definition simply through repetition or institutional acceptance.

The title must be translated according to what the Pāli actually says: **the Rightly and Fully Awakened One**.

Independent discovery should be explained independently. Translation should preserve the original terminology.

**\"Fully Self-Awakened One\" is therefore not an acceptable substitute for a linguistically precise translation.**

Arahanism adopts: **Sammāsambuddha --- The Rightly and Fully Awakened One.**

### 4. The Neglected Cosmological Dimension of Gautama\'s Attainment

This is the part of Gautama\'s life and attainment that deserves considerably more attention than it receives in many modern presentations: his relationship with the wider spiritual of ancient India and cosmological Brahma deities.

The early Pāḷi textual tradition does not present Gautama as existing in a universe devoid of higher dimensional beings. References to devas, manussas, Brahmās, and numerous other classes of beings occur throughout the Pāḷi Canon. These beings are not merely incidental elements of its literary setting; they form part of the cosmological environment within which Gautama\'s teaching, spiritual knowledge, and interactions are presented.

One particularly familiar account concerns Brahmā Sahampati, who approaches Gautama following his attainment and requests that he teach the Dhamma for the benefit of beings. This episode is commonly understood as an encounter occurring after Gautama\'s attainment. However, the account itself does not establish that his relationship with Brahmā and the higher spiritual worlds originated only at that point. The broader question of his prior spiritual and cosmological relationships deserves more description.

From the Arahanist perspective, Gautama\'s attainment should be examined within the wider context of his spiritual cultivation, particularly his development of samādhi and direct knowledge extending beyond ordinary sensory experience. His relationship with higher beings, the cosmological worlds, and the spiritual traditions of ancient India should not be separated from an examination of his life and attainment.

This inquiry also invites a broader reconsideration of ancient Indian cosmological traditions concerning human origins and the relationship between humanity and higher beings. Such traditions appear in different forms across the Pāḷi Canon, Vedic literature, and other ancient Śramaṇa sources. Their similarities, differences, and underlying terminology deserve careful examination.

Consider the following Pāḷi verse: *Tasmā hi bhūtā nisāmetha sabbe,*\
*Mettaṁ karotha mānusiyā pajāya; Divā ca ratto ca haranti ye baliṁ,*\
*Tasmā hi ne rakkhatha appamattā.*

An Arahanist rendering is: \"Therefore, O all beings, listen closely, all of you! Extend loving-kindness towards the **progeny of Mānusa** (the human race); for day and night they bring devotional offerings. Therefore, protect them with diligent vigilance.\"

Arahanism understands *Mānusiyā pajāya* as the progeny of Mānusa, associating the human race with the ancient tradition of Mānusa as an ancestral cosmic mankind race. This places humanity within a wider cosmological genealogy, rather than treating human existence as an isolated earthly phenomenon. The verse thus reflects a relationship between human beings and higher orders of existence, in which human devotional offerings and the protection extended by other beings form part of a broader spiritual relationship.

Similarly, Gautama\'s traditional association with the solar lineage deserves closer attention. His designation *Ādiccabandhu* (Kinsman of the Sun god) should be considered alongside ancient Indian traditions concerning solar ancestry, divine genealogy, and the relationship between humanity and celestial beings.

Taken together, these subjects open a significant dimension of inquiry into Gautama\'s life that is frequently overlooked in modern presentations. His spiritual environment, cosmological relationships, and place within the wider religious heritage of ancient India deserve truthful historical and textual description.

**Gautama\'s attainment should be examined within the full cosmological and spiritual context of the ancient world in which he lived, rather than through an artificially narrowed conception of his life and spiritual experience.**

### 5. An Ideological Question Behind the Historical Reinterpretation

This brings us to a more difficult question.

Why has the presentation of Gautama\'s attainment increasingly been separated from its wider multidimensional cosmological and spiritual context?

Could the historical development of Buddhist thought have involved not merely linguistic changes, but also deeper ideological preferences concerning the relationship between Gautama, Brahmā, and the ancient spiritual traditions?

This question deserves serious discussion.

The growing preference for describing Gautama as entirely self-awakened, together with the tendency to present his attainment through increasingly isolated philosophical and psychological categories, raises questions about the assumptions underlying later interpretations.

It would be difficult to claim a deliberate historical agenda without documentary evidence. Nevertheless, the possibility that intellectual or religious opposition to Brahmanical cosmology influenced subsequent representations should not be excluded from scholarly investigation.

An important distinction must be maintained: questioning the historical motivations behind a particular interpretation is not the same as asserting that a coordinated conspiracy has been demonstrated.

What deserves examination is the cumulative direction of these developments.

When references to Brahmā and higher beings are marginalized, when Gautama\'s cosmological relationships with the *manussa* gods are treated as peripheral, and when his liberation is increasingly explained through the language of isolated self-awakening, instead of a historical and factual process of purification-unbinding-liberation, a particular picture of Gautama gradually emerges.

Whether intended or unintended, that picture may be substantially narrower than the one preserved in the early textual tradition.

Arahanism therefore calls for these matters to be reconsidered without predetermined ideological assumptions.

### 6. From Original Liberation Teaching to Modern Reductionism

The problem of terminology must also be understood within the broader historical transformation of Gautama\'s teaching.

From the Arahanist historical perspective, the original liberation tradition gradually lost its distinct identity through subsequent doctrinal developments, institutional transformations, and reinterpretations.

This is what Arahanism identifies as the historical displacement of Gautama\'s original liberation teaching and its transformation into the diverse religious traditions now collectively known as Buddhism.

This development cannot be attributed to one translation, one institution, or one historical event. It must be examined as a cumulative process.

Modern scholarship and secular interpretations have introduced another dimension to this transformation.

Gautama\'s teaching is frequently approached through modern philosophical, psychological, and cognitive frameworks. His practices become mindfulness techniques; his gradual worldly withdrawal brahmacariya training system, termed as ***sīla,*** is reduced to be called ethical discipline, and then becomes a system of personal well-being; and his liberation teaching becomes a philosophy for managing human experience.

These approaches may identify certain aspects of his teaching, but they do not adequately represent its ultimate purpose.

The complete renunciant path, directed towards the eradication of defilements and the attainment of Arahantship, is increasingly displaced by objectives that can be accommodated within ordinary worldly life.

This is a profound reduction.

Gautama did not establish his teaching merely to make people more comfortable with the world. His teaching provides a path for those who seek to become completely free from worldly bondage.

For Arahanism, this original purpose must be restored to the centre of historical, textual, and doctrinal examination.

### 7. The Responsibility of Restoring Original Terminology

The translation of ancient terminology carries a responsibility.

Translators must distinguish between the actual words of an original expression, their grammatical construction, their semantic meaning, and subsequent doctrinal interpretations.

These categories must not be confused.

An explanatory interpretation may be useful, but it must never be presented as though it were an explicit component of the original terminology.

This is particularly important when translating the titles and fundamental teachings of Gautama.

The authority of a textbook does not establish the accuracy of its translations. Academic repetition does not transform an inserted concept into an original lexical component.

Arahanism therefore insists on renewed philological examination of Gautama\'s terminology, using the early textual tradition as its primary foundation.

The objective is not to replace one inherited assumption with another, but to restore accuracy, distinguish original teaching from subsequent mis-interpretation, and recover the true meaning of liberation.

### 8. The Official Arahanist Position

Arahanism adopts the following terminology:

  --------------------------------------------------------------
  **Pāḷi**           **Arahanist English**
  ------------------ -------------------------------------------
  *Sammā*            Rightly; correctly

  *Sambuddha*        Fully Awakened

  *Sammāsambuddha*   **The Rightly and Fully Awakened One**

  *Sammāsambodhi*    Right and Full Awakening

  *Vimutti*          Liberation from worldly bondage

  *Vimutta*          Liberated or unbound from worldly bondage
  --------------------------------------------------------------

Accordingly:

**Gautama, the Sammāsambuddha --- The Rightly and Fully Awakened One.**

His attainment must be understood through liberation from worldly bondage, complete purification, and freedom from worldly bondage, rather than reduced to an abstract concept of self-awakening.

### Conclusion: Restore the Original, Preserve the Liberation

The translation of *Sammāsambuddha* may appear to be a small linguistic question. In reality, it opens a much larger discussion about how Gautama\'s original teaching has been interpreted, transmitted, and transformed.

An additional word becomes an accepted translation. An accepted translation influences doctrinal understanding. Doctrinal interpretations accumulate, and eventually the original purpose of a teaching can become obscured.

The same problem appears in the treatment of Gautama\'s attainment, his relationship with the wider multidimensional cosmological worlds and deities, and the ultimate objective of his renunciant teaching.

Arahanism seeks to examine these developments critically and restore the central importance of Gautama\'s original liberation teaching.

We must not allow later religious conventions, academic preferences, or secular philosophical frameworks to determine what Gautama\'s original words are permitted to mean.

His attainment was the realization of liberation from worldly bondage. His teaching provides the path towards liberation from the worldly bondage. His example demonstrates the possibility of complete purification and freedom from worldly bondage.

**Arahanism therefore rejects \"Fully Self-Awakened One\" and officially adopts \"The Rightly and Fully Awakened One\" as the English rendering of Sammā-sambuddha.**

The principle is uncompromising:

**Preserve the original terminology. Restore the meaning of liberation. Do not allow subsequent interpretations to replace Gautama\'s teaching.**

## I. The Historical Problem of Translating Attā as Self

### 1.1. A Translation That Became a Doctrine

Few Pāli terms have had consequences as far-reaching for the interpretation of Gautama\'s teaching as *Attā*. Conventionally translated into English as *self*, the word has become central to the modern presentation of Buddhist doctrine, particularly through the familiar expression *Anattā*, frequently rendered as \"no-self\" or \"non-self.\"

The difficulty is that a translation does more than communicate a word\'s approximate meaning. It establishes a conceptual framework through which readers understand the teaching itself. Once a particular English equivalent becomes standard, it can gradually determine how subsequent generations interpret the original term, sometimes narrowing a concept whose religious and philosophical significance is considerably broader.

The translation of *Attā* as *self* illustrates this problem.

In ordinary English, *self* is commonly understood through psychological and personal categories: one\'s identity, personality, individuality, subjective experience, or sense of being an individual person. In philosophical discourse, it may refer to the subject of experience, the identity that persists through time, or the supposed underlying substance of an individual. In contemporary psychological language, it can refer to self-image, self-concept, self-awareness, or the mental representation of one\'s own identity.

These meanings are not interchangeable. Nor do they necessarily convey the full religious significance of Sanskrit *Ātmā* and its Middle Indo-Aryan cognates.

The Arahanist concern is that the conventional translation has encouraged readers to approach *Attā* primarily as a psychological or philosophical question about personal identity. The deeper spiritual dimension of the term consequently becomes obscured. A concept historically embedded in the religious vocabulary of ancient India is increasingly interpreted through the categories of modern psychology and philosophy.

The problem is therefore not simply whether *self* is an acceptable dictionary equivalent. It is whether the habitual use of this English word has progressively restricted the conceptual horizon through which Gautama\'s teaching is understood.

Arahanism identifies this restriction as a significant source of **doctrinal dilution and reduction**.

### 1.2. The Difference Between Personal Identity and Soul-Spirit

The English word *self* can refer to several different things.

It may refer to the empirical individual: the person who has a name, a body, memories, relationships, preferences, and a recognizable personality. It may refer to the psychological subject who experiences sensations, emotions, and thoughts. It may also be used in a more substantial philosophical or religious sense to describe an underlying principle of individual existence.

These meanings must be distinguished.

An individual\'s personality is not necessarily equivalent to that individual\'s deepest spiritual identity. Memories change. Perceptions change. Emotional dispositions change. Physical characteristics change. The ordinary sense of personal identity is conditioned by numerous processes and circumstances.

If *Attā* is understood exclusively through this changing psychological identity, the religious dimension of the term becomes difficult even to recognize.

The Arahanist interpretation therefore distinguishes between the empirical personality and the deeper spiritual individual.

The empirical personality belongs to the conditioned manifestation of human existence. It is associated with the body, sensory experience, memory, perception, thought, and other processes through which an individual operates in the world.

The Soul-Spirit, as understood in Arahanism, refers to a deeper dimension of individual spiritual existence. It is not simply the personality, the ego, the collection of memories, or the temporary psychological image one constructs of oneself.

This distinction is essential to the wider Arahanist understanding of *Citta--Ātmā*. The individual must investigate and purify the total sphere of the Mind-Heart *Citta* rather than mistake its superficial activities for the entirety of spiritual existence.

Consequently, translating *Attā* as *self* without explaining which sense of self is intended creates a serious interpretive problem. The reader may assume that Gautama\'s teaching concerns the existence or non-existence of an ordinary psychological personality, while the ancient religious question involves a much wider inquiry into the nature of individual existence, spiritual identity, and liberation from the worldly entrapment.

Arahanism therefore adopts **Soul-Spirit** as its **official** English rendering of *Attā* and Sanskrit *Ātmā*.

This rendering is a deliberate doctrinal and terminological choice. It seeks to recover the religious depth of the concept and prevent its automatic reduction to modern psychological terminology.

It should be understood as an Arahanist interpretive translation, rather than a claim that every occurrence of *Attā* in Pāli can be translated mechanically as Soul-Spirit. The term also functions extensively as a reflexive pronoun and in expressions concerning oneself, one\'s own actions, or one\'s own welfare. Its meaning must always be determined by context.

The central argument concerns the spiritual and philosophical use of the term, particularly where the discussion involves the nature of existence and liberation from worldly control.

### 1.3. Psychological Reduction of a Spiritual Concept

The modern interpretation of *Attā* has developed within a broader intellectual environment in which religious concepts are frequently translated into psychological categories.

This development has affected the presentation of Buddhist teaching in several ways.

First, the question of *Attā* is frequently reduced to the question of personal identity. The individual is encouraged to examine whether the personality, thoughts, memories, emotions, or bodily constitution can constitute a permanent self. Since these phenomena are constantly changing, the conclusion is commonly presented as the absence of any self.

Second, *Anattā* is frequently explained as the denial of an enduring personal spiritual essence. The teaching becomes a philosophical statement about the absence of an independently existing individual identity.

Third, the path of liberation is increasingly described through psychological outcomes: reducing ego, overcoming self-centred thinking, diminishing attachment to identity, developing emotional balance, and cultivating awareness of changing mental processes.

These subjects may have practical relevance to spiritual training. However, they do not reflect the true meaning of liberation in Gautama\'s original teaching, which essentially is **soteriological**.

The central objective of the renunciant path is far more radical. It concerns the complete eradication of worldly bondage, subjectively defined as the defilements of the *citta* or *attā*, termed *rāga, dosa,* and *moha*. The purification of the *citta* or *attā*---that is, the cessation of these defilements that sustain worldly bondage---leads to the untying of the knots that yoked the human soul-spirit to this world, the unbinding of the fetters, and ultimately, liberation from the world.

When the entire modern teaching is interpreted primarily through psychological categories, its ultimate religious objective becomes increasingly difficult to distinguish from ordinary programmes of psychological development.

This is the dilution and reduction that Arahanism challenges.

The problem is particularly visible when *Viññāṇa*, *Citta*, and *Attā* are treated as though they were interchangeable expressions for consciousness, mind, or personal awareness.

They are not interchangeable within the Arahanist framework.

*Viññāṇa* refers to sense-generated consciousness arising in dependence upon the relevant sense faculty and external worldly object. *Citta* refers to the broader sphere of Mind-Heart, encompassing mental activity (the brain, this life) and deeper dimensions of individual consciousness (the heart, seat of the soul, the accumulative sum of past lives). *Ātmā* refers to the innermost Soul-Spirit within this broader spiritual understanding.

To understand these distinctions, consciousness itself must be considered beyond its ordinary association with human cognition. Consciousness is not simply a by-product of brain activity or an isolated function of individual organisms. Within the Arahanist understanding, consciousness is a fundamental principle of existence, extending beyond individual beings into the wider cosmos. The universe itself is conscious, and consciousness holds primacy in understanding the nature of existence.

There are different dimensions and manifestations of consciousness. These include sense-generated consciousness (*viññāṇa*), individual mental consciousness (*citta*), deeper spiritual consciousness, and universal consciousness. They are related, but they do not represent identical levels or functions. Individual consciousness exists within a much wider field of cosmic consciousness, while the soul-spirit represents a deeper dimension of individual existence.

Furthermore, Soul and Spirit are not identical existence. Within the Arahanist understanding, Spirit belongs to a dimension beyond the manifestive world constituted by the four great elements of earth, water, fire, and wind. As Spirit enters the manifestive world, it undergoes successive stages of transformation through the wind realm, fire realm, water realm, and finally the earth realm. Upon completing this progression and taking residence within a physical being, Spirit becomes Soul, the individual spiritual essence embodied within the manifested world. Thus, Soul and Spirit represent distinct stages and dimensions of spiritual existence, and their relationship must be understood through this broader cosmological framework rather than the limitations of ordinary psychological terminology.

Consequently, reducing all these dimensions to the single category of consciousness obscures essential distinctions. The human being cannot be adequately understood through the physical brain, sensory processes, or psychological personality alone. A proper understanding requires examining the relationship between individual consciousness, universal consciousness, *Citta*, and the deeper reality of *Ātmā*.

Modern day academia's collapsing these distinctions into a single psychological category of consciousness makes it difficult to understand the different dimensions of human existence addressed by the original liberation teaching from Gautama, the supreme Arahant.

It also creates a problem when interpreting the five aggregates. If the aggregates are assumed by modern Buddhism scholars to constitute the entirety of the individual, then the rejection of their identification as *Attā* can easily be transformed into the conclusion that no spiritual individual exists at all. This is utterly nihilism.

### 1.4. The Translation of *Anattā* and the Consequences of Interpretation

The problem becomes especially significant in the translation of *Anattā*.

The expression is conventionally rendered as *non-self* or *no-self*. These translations are often used interchangeably, despite the fact that they can communicate different philosophical propositions.

\"Not-self\" may indicate that a particular phenomenon is not properly identified as the Soul-Spirit. \"No-self\" may communicate a broader denial that any such spiritual individual exists.

The distinction is substantial.

In the *Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta* (SN 22.59), Gautama examines each of the five aggregates and explains why none should be regarded as *Attā*. The sutta identifies their instability, their susceptibility to affliction, and the impossibility of exercising complete mastery over them.

The Arahanist interpretation gives particular importance to the object of this examination: the five aggregates.

The body, feeling, perception, formations, and sense-derived-consciousness are investigated as conditioned phenomena. Their instability and lack of mastery make them impossible to be the ultimate spiritual identity of the individual.

Arahanism therefore interprets the sutta as a rejection of the identification of the conditioned aggregates as Soul-Spirit.

The rejection of the five aggregates as *attā* carries a significant implication. By **ordinary human logic**, any person of reasonable intelligence can recognize that when the five aggregates are specifically identified and rejected as *attā*, this distinction points towards **an *attā* beyond the five aggregates**. If *attā* did not exist at all, there would be no meaningful reason to distinguish each of the five aggregates as *not attā*. The teaching of *anattā* therefore points towards the existence of *attā*, while establishing that it is not constituted by form, feeling, perception, formations, or sense-derived consciousness. What remains is to discover the true nature and constituents of *attā*, an investigation that requires deeper meditative penetration rather than intellectual reasoning alone.

The wrong translation of *Anattā* can obscure this distinction when the English expression *no-self* is treated as a complete philosophical conclusion rather than an mis-interpretive rendering by mainstream Buddhism "scholars".

### 1.5. The Need to Re-examine the Original Vocabulary

A responsible examination of Gautama\'s teaching must begin with the original terminology.

The Pāli language preserves distinctions that can become blurred when several different terms are translated through the same English vocabulary.

The relationship between *Attā*, *Citta*, and *Viññāṇa* is one such example.

Arahanism therefore proposes a more careful approach.

The word *Attā* should not automatically be reduced to the psychological concept of self. Its use should be examined in its original linguistic, textual, and religious contexts. Where it refers to the deeper spiritual individual, Arahanism translates it as **Soul-Spirit**.

This terminology is intended to restore a distinction that the conventional English vocabulary frequently obscures: the distinction between the temporary psycho-physical manifestation of an individual and the deeper spiritual dimension addressed by the Arahanist path of purification.

The purpose of this restoration is not merely terminological. It affects how the five aggregates are understood, how consciousness is classified, how *Anattā* is interpreted, and ultimately how the goal of liberation is conceived.

Before examining these matters in detail, it is necessary to establish the linguistic relationship between *Attā*, *Ātmā*, and *Appā*.

## II. Attā, Ātmā, and Appā

### 2.1. A Shared Ancient Linguistic Heritage

The Pāḷi word *Attā* corresponds to the Sanskrit *Ātmā* (*Ātman*), as well as to corresponding forms found in Middle Indo-Aryan languages, including the Prakrit forms *Attā* and *Appā*. These are not unrelated expressions that happen to resemble one another. They represent historically connected linguistic forms of the same ancient Indic term.

This relationship places the Pāḷi term within a much wider linguistic and spiritual heritage of ancient India. The term was not confined to one religious group or philosophical school. Its corresponding forms occur in ancient literature associated with Vedic and Brāhmaṇical traditions, Śramaṇa movements, Jain traditions, and Buddhist teachings.

The linguistic relationship can be summarised as follows:

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Language or tradition**                   **Corresponding form**
  ------------------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Vedic and Classical Sanskrit                *Ātman* / *Ātmā*

  Pāḷi                                        *Attan* / *Attā*

  Ardhamāgadhī and other Prakrit traditions   *Attā* / *Appā*

  Other Middle Indo-Aryan traditions          *Attā*, *Appā*, and related forms
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------

The Pāli Text Society\'s dictionary identifies *attan* as corresponding to Vedic Sanskrit *ātman*. The *Critical Pāli Dictionary* likewise identifies the relationship between Pāli *attan* and Sanskrit *ātman*, documenting its grammatical forms and usage throughout Pāḷi literature.

The historical linguistic relationship is therefore clear: Pāḷi *Attā* and Sanskrit *Ātmā* belong to the same ancient Indic linguistic inheritance. Their corresponding forms are also found in the wider Middle Indo-Aryan textual heritage.

This continuity is significant for understanding the spiritual history of ancient India. The concept of *Attā/Ātmā* was present across different communities and traditions, including those of Brāhmaṇas, Śramaṇas, Jains, and Bhikkhus. It formed part of a shared vocabulary through which questions concerning individual existence, consciousness, spiritual identity, and liberation were expressed and discussed.

Different communities undoubtedly developed their own interpretations of the term. The use of a common word does not mean that every tradition understood its nature, significance, or relationship with liberation in precisely the same way. Nevertheless, their differing interpretations belong to a connected historical and spiritual heritage and should be studied in relation to one another.

For Arahanism, this wider linguistic heritage is particularly important. The meaning of *Attā* in Gautama\'s teaching cannot be investigated adequately by isolating Pāḷi literature from the broader Indic traditions in which the corresponding term was already established and continued to be used. Sanskrit, Pāḷi, Ardhamāgadhī, and other Middle Indo-Aryan sources therefore provide essential material for investigating the historical meaning and spiritual significance of *Attā/Ātmā*.

Arahanism approaches these sources as part of the wider ancient Indian renunciant and spiritual heritage that it seeks to recover and articulate. Their terminology, teachings, and interpretations are relevant to understanding the historical development of the spiritual concepts that underpin the path of liberation.

### 2.2. Sanskrit *Ātmā*: The Older Religious and Philosophical Vocabulary

The Sanskrit term *Ātman* occupies an important place in the religious and spiritual literature of ancient India. Its corresponding nominative form, *Ātmā*, expresses the same term encountered in Pāḷi as *Attā*.

Its usage developed across different historical periods and textual environments. In Vedic literature, the term appears in discussions concerning life, the individual, and the nature of existence. In the Upaniṣadic literature, it assumes particular importance in investigations into the innermost spiritual principle of the individual and its relationship with ultimate reality.

The Upaniṣads contain diverse discussions of *Ātman*. Some passages investigate it as the innermost principle of the individual, while others explore its relationship with *Brahma*, the source of cosmic *manussa* rendered as the ultimate ground of existence. These teachings developed across different texts and historical periods and cannot be reduced to a single uniform doctrine. Nevertheless, they demonstrate the profound spiritual and philosophical significance that *Ātman* had acquired in ancient Indian thought.

This significance was not confined to Brāhmaṇical literature. The wider spiritual landscape of ancient India included Śramaṇa, Jain renunciants, Munis, Yogis, and other contemplative traditions that investigated individual existence, spiritual identity, consciousness, and liberation. Their respective understandings differed, but questions concerning the nature of the individual and the possibility of liberation for freedom and immortality formed an important part of this broader renunciant heritage.

Gautama\'s teaching emerged within this established spiritual environment. Questions concerning the individual, consciousness, existence, death, and liberation were already being explored by different communities through their own religious vocabularies and spiritual disciplines. The investigation of what constitutes the individual beyond the immediately observable body was among the common concerns of ancient Indian spiritual thought.

For Arahanism, this historical background is essential. The Sanskrit *Ātmā* and Pāḷi *Attā* belong to the same ancient Indic linguistic inheritance, and their spiritual significance must be investigated across the wider textual heritage in which they occur. The Vedic and Upaniṣadic traditions, together with Jain and other Śramaṇa literature, are therefore integral to understanding the historical development of these concepts.

This does not mean that Gautama adopted every interpretation of *Ātman* found in earlier Brahmanical or other Indian traditions. Nor does the shared terminology establish that these communities held identical doctrines. Gautama\'s teaching must be established through his own suttas, examined in their original linguistic and doctrinal context.

The Arahanist investigation therefore proceeds in two interconnected directions: understanding the wider ancient Indian spiritual heritage of *Ātmā/Attā*, and determining the precise significance of *Attā* within Gautama\'s own teaching. Neither earlier Brahmanical interpretations nor later Buddhist doctrinal formulations should be allowed to predetermine that investigation.

Arahanism approaches this heritage as part of the wider religious and renunciant tradition that it seeks to recover and articulate, while maintaining Gautama\'s liberation teaching as its central doctrinal foundation.

### 2.3. Pāli *Attā*: A Middle Indo-Aryan Form of *Ātman*

The Sanskrit term *Ātman* refers to the Soul-Spirit, the innermost spiritual reality of the living being. Its significance is extensively attested throughout the Vedic and wider ancient Indian spiritual literature, where *Ātman* features prominently in discussions of spiritual existence, individual identity, consciousness, and the nature of life beyond the physical body. This understanding of *Ātman* represents one of the most valuable spiritual inheritances of the Aryan civilization when they migrated to northern India, hybridizing with the local yogic and related renunciant traditions and forming a profound civilizational foundation of ancient India\'s religious and philosophical heritage. Its significance extends far beyond the ordinary notion of an individual person, reaching into the deepest questions concerning the nature and destiny of the Soul-Spirit.

Pāli *Attā* is the Middle Indo-Aryan counterpart of Sanskrit *Ātmā*, with *attan* representing its corresponding stem form. This linguistic relationship is recognised by standard Pāli lexicographical scholarship. The Pāli Text Society\'s dictionary traces *attan* to Vedic Sanskrit *ātman*, while the *Critical Pāli Dictionary* documents its grammatical forms and extensive usage throughout Pāli literature. Thus, *Attā* and *Ātmā* belong to the same ancient Indic linguistic inheritance.

In Pāli literature, *Attā* is also used reflexively to mean oneself or one\'s own person. This ordinary usage, however, raises an important linguistic question. Sanskrit already possesses the word *sva*, meaning one\'s own or belonging to oneself, alongside other expressions serving similar reflexive functions. Pāli likewise retains related forms, including *sa* and *saka*. Why, then, did a term with such an established spiritual significance as *Attā* also become commonly used to express ordinary self-reference?

Arahanism considers this development a possible indication of a gradual degeneration in spiritual understanding. As *Attā* became increasingly familiar as an everyday expression for oneself, its deeper significance as Soul-Spirit could be obscured. A term rooted in the ancient spiritual vocabulary of *Ātman* was increasingly understood through the narrower concept of ordinary personal identity. In this way, its spiritual meaning became overshadowed by its colloquial usage.

From the Arahanist perspective, this ordinary usage should not be allowed to displace the spiritual foundation of the term. The fact that *Attā* can function grammatically as a reflexive expression does not make this its essential spiritual meaning. These usages must be distinguished, and the deeper significance of the word must be preserved.

This distinction is particularly important when interpreting Gautama\'s suttas. *Attā* appears in different grammatical, philosophical, and spiritual contexts, and its meaning must be understood in relation to the particular passage. Expressions such as *attadīpa* and *attasaraṇa*, for example, occur in important instructions concerning spiritual refuge and support. Their significance cannot be settled simply by applying the everyday meaning of *self*.

The significance of *Attā* extends far beyond mere discussions. Throughout the Pāli suttas, practitioners are repeatedly directed towards the inward dimension of their own existence. The terminology of *ajjhatta*, corresponding to Sanskrit *adhyātma*, is particularly important in this respect. Arahanism understands this inward orientation in relation to the deeper spiritual dimension of the individual, the Soul-Spirit, rather than merely the ordinary worldly identity or surface activities of the mind.

This inward orientation is directly connected with the attainment of Arahantship. In AN 4.170, the *Yuganaddha Sutta*, the expression *ajjhattameva cittaṃ santiṭṭhati* describes the *citta* becoming firmly established on higher soul-spirit *atta* (*ajjh-atta*). Arahanism understands this as a crucial dimension of spiritual development: the *citta* must become established in the inward, higher dimension of the soul-spirit, through which the path to liberation arises and develops.

Arahanism therefore distinguishes the ordinary grammatical use of *Attā* (a degenerational use) from its deeper spiritual significance. Where the term refers to the innermost spiritual individual, Arahanism adopts **Soul-Spirit** as its designated English rendering. This preserves the ancient spiritual meaning of *Ātman* and its Pāli counterpart *Attā*, rather than allowing their significance to be confined to the commonplace English word *self*.

### 2.4. Jain Prakrit *Appā*: The Language of the Śramaṇa Environment

The relationship between Sanskrit *Ātman*, Pāli *Attā*, and Jain Prakrit *Appā* becomes particularly significant when examining the religious literature of ancient Jain communities.

In Ardhamāgadhī and other Prakrit forms associated with Jain textual traditions, forms such as *Attā*, *Ātā*, *Āyā*, and *Appā* occur as linguistic counterparts of Sanskrit *Ātman*. Research on Ardhamāgadhī vocabulary, including the study *In Search of the Original Ardhamāgadhī*, published by the Prakrit Text Society, examines these forms in relation to the language preserved in Jain canonical literature, including the *Ācārāṅga*.

This linguistic evidence is important because Jainism preserves one of the most substantial surviving traditions of the ancient Śramaṇa heritage. Its canonical and religious literature provides an extensive record of the terminology, spiritual practices, and renunciant concerns of ancient Indian communities.

In Jain religious and philosophical literature, *Appā* and related forms are associated with the individual soul and sentient being. Jain terminology references, for example, record *Attā* in connection with the soul, sentient substance, and sentient being. This usage demonstrates that the linguistic heritage of Sanskrit *Ātman* extended well beyond Brahmanical literature and remained significant within the spiritual vocabulary of ancient renunciant traditions.

The Jain understanding of the soul must, of course, be distinguished from the Arahanist understanding of *Attā*. Jain teachings concerning *Jīva*, bondage, karma, and liberation developed within their own religious and doctrinal framework. Nevertheless, their terminology and spiritual investigations preserve important evidence of how the nature of the individual soul and the possibility of liberation were understood and explored in ancient India.

The significance of this comparison therefore extends beyond linguistic correspondence. The presence of *Appā* in Jain literature demonstrates that the vocabulary associated with *Ātman* was shared across different traditions and linguistic environments. It also provides evidence that questions concerning the soul, spiritual bondage, renunciation, purification, and liberation were central concerns among ancient Indian renunciants.

This wider heritage is particularly relevant to understanding the religious environment in which Gautama taught. His teaching emerged within an ancient Indian world already rich in renunciant traditions, spiritual disciplines, and investigations into the nature of existence and liberation from worldly bondage. The Jain textual heritage preserves an important part of this broader historical and spiritual landscape.

Arahanism therefore approaches Jain literature as part of the wider ancient Indian renunciant heritage that it seeks to recover and articulate. Its terminology, teachings, and records of spiritual discipline contribute to understanding the historical development of the concepts and practices associated with liberation.

The shared linguistic and spiritual inheritance also points towards a wider common ground among these ancient traditions. Their teachings reflect a sustained concern with the nature of the Soul-Spirit, the condition of worldly bondage, the possibility of spiritual purification, and the attainment of liberation. Gautama\'s teaching belongs within this broader heritage. Rather than viewing his teaching in isolation from the spiritual knowledge preserved by earlier traditions, Arahanism understands it within the wider context of ancient Indian renunciant inquiry. Gautama addressed the same fundamental concerns, while presenting his own distinctive approach to overcoming worldly bondage and attaining complete liberation. It is through this distinctive approach, preserved in his suttas, that Arahanism understands the particular significance of his teaching.

The task is to recognise the shared ancient linguistic and spiritual inheritance, understand the different doctrinal interpretations that developed within it, and establish Gautama\'s distinctive teaching through the early suttas.

### 2.5. The Ancient Indian Renunciant Heritage: A Common Field of Spiritual Inquiry

Ancient India was home to diverse communities of spiritual practitioners and renunciants, including Brāhmaṇas, Śramaṇas, Jains, Munis, Ṛṣis, Yogis, and other seekers of spiritual realization. Among these communities were individuals who renounced household life, devoted themselves to spiritual discipline, and pursued answers to the fundamental questions of human existence and liberation. The Brāhmaṇa tradition, in particular, should not be separated from this wider renunciant heritage, as the Pāli discourses preserve numerous accounts of Brāhmaṇas who undertook spiritual training and pursued the highest realization.

**Restoring the Brāhmaṇas to the Ancient Indian Renunciant Heritage**

One persistent problem for modern mainstream academia, on accounts of ancient Indian religious history is the artificial separation of Brāhmaṇas and Śramaṇas, often presenting the former primarily as ritual priests and householders, and the latter as renunciants and seekers of liberation. This division is un-historical, mis-leading and may bear dark agenda behind this. It **obscures** an important dimension of India\'s spiritual heritage: Brāhmaṇas were also among those who pursued renunciation, spiritual discipline, contemplation, and liberation.

The early Buddhist suttas themselves preserve abundant evidence of Gautama\'s encounters with Brāhmaṇas and their spiritual aspirations. Gautama himself was a Brāhmaṇa. The *Dhammapada* even dedicates an entire chapter, the *Brāhmaṇavagga*, to the qualities of the true Brāhmaṇa, presenting spiritual purity, restraint, wisdom, and liberation as the defining characteristics of this ideal.

Arahanism therefore **rejects** standard mainstream academia's assumption that Brāhmaṇa identity and renunciant vocation are mutually exclusive. The Brāhmaṇas must be recognised alongside Śramaṇas, Jains, Munis, Ṛṣis, Yogis, and other spiritual practitioners as participants in the wider ancient Indian religious and renunciant heritage. Gautama\'s teaching should be understood within this broader spiritual landscape, rather than through ignorant, delusional and rigid historical classifications that **obscure** its shared heritage and the spiritual concerns of his contemporaries.

**There Was No Such Thing as a Unified Śramaṇa Movement**

Another persistent problem in modern academic accounts of ancient Indian religious history is the repeated description of the ancient renunciant traditions as the **"Śramaṇa movement."** This expression is frequently used as though ancient India witnessed the emergence of a single, identifiable movement that brought together various ascetics, renunciants, and seekers of liberation, often portrayed as a collective opposition to the Brāhmaṇa tradition. This is purely delusional modern day mainstream academic fantasy.

Arahanism rejects this historical construction. The expression *Śramaṇa* designates a renunciant, ascetic, or spiritual practitioner. Its use does not establish the existence of a unified religious movement, a common institutional organization, a founding event, or a coordinated body of doctrine. Treating *Śramaṇa* as the name of one historical movement is an unwarranted expansion of the term.

Ancient India possessed a wide and diverse population of spiritual practitioners, centuries and even millenniums before Gautama' Buddha's time. These included Brāhmaṇas, Śramaṇas, Jains, Munis, Ṛṣis, Yogis, Pabbajitas, forest-dwellers, contemplatives, and other seekers of spiritual realization.

They represented different groups and traditions within a much wider field of spiritual pursuit, united by fundamental aspirations: the search for truth, the purification of the individual, the transcendence of ordinary human existence, and the attainment of immortality or the Deathless. They sought to understand the deeper nature of existence, overcome the limitations and suffering of conditioned life, and realize a higher spiritual state. Naturally, different practitioners explored different disciplines, adopted different methods, and developed different explanations along the way. Such variations are to be expected among people pursuing the same profound questions. Differences in method and terminology do not necessarily indicate fundamentally different spiritual purposes. Beneath these diverse approaches lay a common aspiration towards truth, purity, spiritual perfection, and liberation from mortality.

There is no historical justification for automatically gathering all these practitioners into a single *Śramaṇa movement*. A Brāhmaṇa renunciant did not thereby become a member of a Śramaṇa movement. A Jain Muni did not necessarily identify himself as a member of such a movement. A Vedic Ṛṣi, a forest-dwelling Yogi, and a Buddhist Pabbajita each belonged to particular spiritual contexts and traditions. Their participation in the wider heritage of renunciation does not make them members of one unified historical enterprise.

The problem becomes even more apparent when the supposed *Śramaṇa movement* is presented as a collective opposition to Brahmanism. This framework imposes a delusional and reductionistic division upon a much more complex historical landscape. It assumes that the principal distinction in ancient Indian spiritual life was between Brāhmaṇas, supposedly committed to ritual and household existence, and Śramaṇas, supposedly committed to renunciation and liberation. As already demonstrated, this delusional division excludes Brāhmaṇa renunciants and misrepresents the diversity of ancient spiritual life.

The repeated use of the expression "Śramaṇa movement" does not make the historical construction valid. What evidence establishes that these diverse practitioners constituted one movement? Where is the common institutional structure, the shared founding authority, the coordinated organization, or the unified doctrine that would justify such a classification? The mere existence of the word *Śramaṇa*, or the fact that different renunciants pursued spiritual discipline, cannot establish any of these things.

The expression is therefore misleading when used as though it referred to a single, historically unified movement. It converts a broad designation for renunciant practitioners into an fantasized collective institution and then uses that imagined institution to organize the history of ancient Indian religion. This is a serious methodological failure for modern day mainstream academia. It substitutes a delusional academic classification for the historical diversity that the evidence requires us to recognize.

**The Renunciant Heritage Long Predates Gautama**

The wider heritage of renunciation in ancient India also extends far beyond the lifetime of Gautama Buddha. Spiritual practitioners, contemplatives, ascetics, and seekers of higher realization existed centuries and even millenniums before him. The pursuit of spiritual knowledge, the discipline of renunciation, contemplation, austerity, and the search for liberation were already established concerns within the ancient Indian religious landscape.

The Vedic heritage preserves accounts of Ṛṣis, Munis, and other spiritual practitioners. The Upaniṣadic literature records sustained inquiries into the nature of existence, the individual spiritual principle, and ultimate reality. Other ancient traditions preserve their own accounts of ascetic discipline, spiritual purification, and liberation. These developments belong to a long and diverse history that cannot reasonably be reduced to the appearance of one supposed *Śramaṇa movement*.

Gautama therefore appeared within an already established world of spiritual inquiry and renunciant practice. He encountered existing traditions, spiritual teachers, ascetics, and seekers who were pursuing fundamental questions concerning existence and liberation. His teaching must be understood within this historical setting, while its distinctive path and final attainment must be established through his own discourses.

The historical sequence matters. The ancient Indian renunciant heritage did not originate with Gautama, nor can its diverse practitioners be treated as though they emerged together as one organized movement immediately before his teaching. Their traditions had different histories, and some extended back through millenniums of spiritual development.

Arahanism consequently rejects the idea that the history of ancient Indian renunciation can be adequately explained through a singular "Śramaṇa movement." The term *Śramaṇa* should be understood in relation to the renunciant practitioners it designates, while the broader historical subject should be described as the **ancient Indian renunciant heritage**.

This terminology better reflects the actual diversity of the spiritual landscape. It recognizes the Brāhmaṇas, Śramaṇas, Jains, Munis, Ṛṣis, Yogis, Pabbajitas, and other practitioners without falsely assigning them to one organization or movement. It also restores the long historical background against which Gautama\'s liberation teaching appeared.

The distinction is fundamental to Arahanism\'s historical approach: **there was a vast and diverse ancient Indian renunciant heritage; the existence of that heritage does not establish a single, unified Śramaṇa movement.** Gautama\'s teaching belongs within this wider history, and its particular significance must be understood through the path of liberation he taught and the attainment realized by his elder disciples.

Their inquiries concerned the relationship between bodily existence and the spiritual individual, the nature of consciousness, the causes of suffering and worldly bondage, the consequences of action, the importance of moral discipline, and the possibility of liberation from the cycle of worldly existence. Renunciation, spiritual purification, and direct realization occupied an important place in this wider religious heritage.

Although these communities developed different explanations and methods, they shared many fundamental spiritual concerns. Their teachings addressed the condition of the individual, the nature of existence, the consequences of karma, the discipline required for spiritual purification, and the possibility of attaining liberation from worldly entrapment. They therefore belong to a wider historical field of ancient Indian renunciant inquiry, within which different traditions explored the same fundamental problem: how to overcome worldly bondage and attain spiritual freedom.

The vocabulary of *Ātman*, *Attā*, *Appā*, and related expressions belongs to this wider linguistic and spiritual heritage. Its presence across Sanskrit and Prakrit religious literature reflects the historical continuity of ideas concerning the spiritual individual and the pursuit of liberation.

Gautama taught within this rich renunciant environment. His teaching should therefore be understood in connection with the wider spiritual knowledge and inquiries of ancient India, rather than separated from them by the boundaries of later religious institutions and sectarian classifications. He addressed fundamental questions that had long occupied renunciant practitioners, while presenting his own distinctive approach to overcoming worldly bondage.

For Arahanism, the defining feature of Gautama\'s teaching is its uncompromising orientation towards complete liberation through renunciation. The path requires the abandonment of household life, the cultivation of *sīla*, the development of *Samādhi* and *Paññā*, and the complete eradication of *rāga, dosa,* and *moha*. Its ultimate objective is Arahantship: the complete purification of the *citta* and *attā* from the defilements that sustain worldly bondage.

This spiritual objective also provides the foundation for Arahanism\'s understanding of *Attā*. The individual is not understood merely through bodily existence, worldly identity, or the changing activities of sense-generated consciousness. The inward spiritual dimension, expressed through the vocabulary of *Attā* and *Ajjhatta*, has a central place in the investigation of the path to liberation from worldly bondage.

The relationship between Sanskrit *Ātman*, Pāli *Attā*, and Jain Prakrit *Appā* therefore opens onto a much wider subject than linguistic correspondence. It connects the vocabulary of the ancient Indian spiritual individual with the renunciant traditions that investigated the nature of existence and the possibility of liberation.

The essential task is now to examine Gautama\'s own suttas: how they describe the individual, distinguish the five aggregates from *Attā*, explain the nature of *Citta* and *Viññāṇa*, and direct the practitioner towards inward realization and Arahantship. Through this examination, Arahanism seeks to articulate Gautama\'s distinctive liberation teaching within the wider spiritual heritage of ancient India.

### 2.6. Shared Vocabulary and a Common Spiritual Heritage.

The linguistic relationship between Sanskrit *Ātmā*, Pāli *Attā*, and Jain Prakrit *Appā* reflects a shared inheritance within the ancient Indian spiritual traditions. These terms belong to a wider vocabulary through which spiritual practitioners expressed their understanding of the living being, the Soul-Spirit, the condition of worldly existence, and the possibility of liberation.

Across the Vedic, Upaniṣadic, Jain, and early Buddhist traditions, these fundamental concerns occupied an important place in spiritual inquiry. Certain Upaniṣadic teachings explore *Ātman* in relation to *Brahman* and the ultimate ground of existence. Jain teachings articulate a distinctive understanding of *Jīva* as the individual living substance, its bondage, and its liberation. Gautama\'s discourses address the nature of *Attā*, the relationship between the living being and the five aggregates, and the path towards complete liberation from worldly bondage.

Each tradition developed its own explanations and methods of spiritual cultivation. Their differences should be understood within the broader context of their shared concerns. The existence of distinct doctrinal formulations does not diminish the historical and spiritual relationship reflected in their language. Rather, it reveals how different communities approached fundamental questions concerning existence, bondage, purification, and liberation.

Arahanism understands Gautama\'s teaching within this wider ancient Indian spiritual heritage. Gautama addressed the fundamental concerns that had long occupied India\'s spiritual practitioners, while presenting his own distinctive approach to overcoming worldly bondage and attaining complete liberation. His suttas preserve the foundation of this approach and remain the primary source through which its meaning must be understood today.

This is particularly important when investigating the meaning of *Attā*. Its significance cannot be settled simply by adopting a conventional English translation or allowing later doctrinal reduction and dilution to determine how the Pāli term should be understood. Its usage must be studied throughout Gautama\'s suttas, with attention to the language, context, and purpose of each teaching.

Arahanism therefore approaches *Attā* as a central subject in recovering the Soul-Spirit of Gautama\'s liberation teaching. Its relationship with *Citta*, *Viññāṇa*, *Ajjhatta*, and the five aggregates must be understood through the suttas themselves. This investigation is essential to establishing how Gautama\'s teaching explains the living being, the condition of worldly bondage, and the attainment of Arahantship.

### 2.7. The Arahanist Terminological Restoration

On the basis of the linguistic, historical, and spiritual examination presented in this chapter, Arahanism establishes the following official terminology. These renderings are intended to recover and preserve the deeper spiritual significance of the original terms, providing a consistent vocabulary for examining Gautama\'s liberation teaching and its wider ancient Indian heritage.

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Original term**   **Official Arahanist rendering**    **Explanation**
  ------------------- ----------------------------------- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  *Attā*              **Soul-Spirit**                     The deeper spiritual individual, when the term is used in its spiritual and doctrinal sense.

  *Ātmā*              **Soul-Spirit**                     The corresponding Sanskrit term, extensively used in Vedic and wider ancient Indian spiritual literature.

  *Appā*              **Soul-Spirit**                     A related Prakrit form preserved in Jain and other Middle Indo-Aryan textual traditions.

  *Citta*             **Mind-Heart**                      The broader sphere of individual mental and spiritual consciousness, encompassing mental activity and its deeper inward dimension.

  *Viññāṇa*           **Sense-generated consciousness**   Conditioned consciousness arising in dependence upon a relevant sense faculty and its corresponding external worldly object.
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

These terminological distinctions establish the foundation for the discussions that follow. Each term has its own significance, and their relationships must be understood carefully to recover the spiritual structure of Gautama\'s teaching.

The Arahanist rendering of *Attā* as **Soul-Spirit** does not mean that every occurrence of the word must be translated identically. Like many ancient terms, *Attā* can serve ordinary grammatical and reflexive functions, including references to oneself or one\'s own person (a colloquial degenerational usage in Arahanist point of view). The present terminology establishes its official rendering when it carries the deeper spiritual significance of the individual Soul-Spirit.

The same principle applies to Sanskrit *Ātmā* and Prakrit *Appā*. Their linguistic relationship reflects a shared vocabulary within the wider ancient Indian spiritual heritage. Arahanism recognises this inheritance and gives these corresponding forms a consistent English rendering when they refer to the spiritual individual.

The distinctions among *Citta*, *Mano*, *Cetanā*, and *Viññāṇa* are essential to understanding the inner functioning of the spiritual individual.

*Citta*, rendered as **Mind-Heart**, encompasses the broader sphere of individual mental and spiritual consciousness. It represents the overall field within which the individual\'s mental and spiritual functions operate.

*Mano* (Sanskrit *Manas*), rendered as **Mind-Will**, represents a particular and especially important directing faculty within the Mind-Heart. Through *Mano*, the individual exercises will, directs attention, makes decisions, and determines the orientation of mental activity. It is this faculty that provides the basis for understanding the individual\'s capacity to exercise free will.

*Cetanā*, rendered as **volition**, refers to the activity of willing and intending within the broader functioning of *Citta*. It encompasses the volitional operations of the Mind-Heart, including the directing activity exercised through *Mano*. In this sense, *Mano* represents a particular directing faculty, while *Cetanā* describes the volitional activity taking place within the Mind-Heart.

*Viññāṇa*, rendered as **sense-generated consciousness**, refers to conditioned consciousness arising through the relationship between a relevant sense faculty and its corresponding object.

These distinctions establish four related but different dimensions of individual functioning. *Citta* encompasses the broader Mind-Heart; *Mano* exercises its directing faculty of will; *Cetanā* expresses its volitional activity; and *Viññāṇa* arises through sensory conditions. None of these terms should be indiscriminately substituted for another. *Viññāṇa* cannot be taken as a complete representation of the Mind-Heart or the spiritual individual, nor should it be rendered simply as universal or general consciousness, as is common in standard modern academic translations. Such a reduction obscures its specific function as sense-generated consciousness and incorrectly gives the impression that it represents the entirety of consciousness and the full spiritual dimension of the living being.

For Arahanism, this terminological restoration is part of recovering the depth of Gautama\'s liberation teaching. The meaning of these terms must be understood through their relationships within the suttas and their place in the path towards complete liberation from worldly bondage.

The next task is to examine the relationship between *Citta* and *Ātmā*, and to establish why *Viññāṇa* must not be mistaken for the totality of consciousness or the entirety of the spiritual individual.

### 2.8 The Scope of Arahanism and Its Relationship with the Wider Renunciant Heritage

**The religious scope of Arahanism:** Arahanism embraces the wider renunciant spiritual heritage of ancient India, encompassing the traditions and practitioners associated with Brāhmaṇas, Śramaṇas, Jains, Munis, Ṛṣis, Yogis, Pabbajitas, Bhikkhus, and Bhikkhunīs. These traditions represent different historical expressions of India\'s ancient spiritual and renunciant culture. Arahanism seeks to recover and articulate their wider spiritual inheritance within a distinct religious framework grounded in Gautama\'s liberation teaching and directed towards the attainment of Arahantship.

Arahanism is not simply a reconstruction of the teachings transmitted by the immediate disciples of Gautama Buddha. It is an attempt to recover and articulate a much wider renunciant spiritual heritage of ancient India, with Gautama\'s liberation teaching at its centre. Its historical and intellectual scope extends beyond the institutional boundaries of Buddhism and encompasses the diverse traditions of renunciation, spiritual cultivation, and liberation that developed across the Indian subcontinent over many centuries.

The ancient Indian spiritual landscape included Brāhmaṇas, Śramaṇas, Jains, Munis, Ṛṣis or Isīs, Yogis, Pabbajitas, Bhikkhus, and Bhikkhunīs. These names represent different communities, vocations, and spiritual orientations, and their teachings should not be treated as doctrinally identical. Nevertheless, they provide evidence of a much wider culture of spiritual seeking in which renunciation, meditation, austerity, self-discipline, and liberation were recurring concerns.

This heritage did not begin with Gautama. Vedic and pre-Vedic spiritual traditions had already developed their own seers, contemplatives, and forms of spiritual discipline. The traditions of Munis, Ṛṣis, and early renunciants provide an important historical context for understanding the world into which Gautama was born. The Śramaṇa movements, including Jain traditions, further demonstrate the diversity of renunciant paths in ancient India. Their teachings concerning *Ātmā*, *Appā*, spiritual purification, austerity, and liberation are therefore relevant to Arahanism\'s investigation of the wider Indic understanding of spiritual existence.

Gautama\'s dispensation emerged within this broader environment while establishing its own distinctive teaching and discipline. The Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī Saṅgha became a particularly important institutional expression of the renunciant life. The Pāḷi Suttas and Vinaya preserve the central doctrinal and disciplinary foundations upon which Arahanism is established. However, understanding Gautama\'s teaching also requires attention to the wider spiritual vocabulary, practices, and intellectual traditions of his time. Terms such as *Attā*, *Ātmā*, *Appā*, *tapas*, *jhāna*, *samādhi*, *muni*, and *pabbajjā* cannot be fully investigated by isolating Pāḷi literature from the broader Indic textual heritage.

The historical scope of Arahanism also extends beyond Gautama\'s lifetime. Renunciant communities continued for centuries after him, transmitting and developing different forms of spiritual practice. Later Buddhist generations inherited Gautama\'s teachings, but they also produced new interpretations, institutions, devotional practices, and spiritual ideals. Arahanism can share historical inheritances, terminology, and certain practices with these later Buddhist communities without necessarily accepting their subsequent doctrinal developments.

This distinction is particularly important in understanding the relationship between Arahanism and later Buddhism. Many later Buddhist traditions came to embrace a broad religious culture in which householders and lay practitioners could pursue spiritual development without undertaking complete renunciation. The aspiration to Buddhahood, understood in various later traditions through the ideals of awakening and the Bodhisattva path, became an important spiritual orientation. Such paths can accommodate participation in ordinary worldly life and do not universally require every practitioner to abandon household existence.

Arahanism maintains a different and unambiguous spiritual objective. Its central aspiration is the attainment of Arahantship through complete renunciation of worldly life. The renunciant does not merely seek spiritual awakening while continuing to participate in the ordinary structures of worldly existence. The renunciant goes forth, abandons household life, undertakes the discipline of the renunciant, and pursues the complete eradication of *rāga, dosa,* and *moha*. Renunciation is fundamental to this objective, not an optional expression of religious devotion.

The distinction is therefore one of spiritual destination and the life required to reach it. Arahanism places the liberation of the renunciant at the centre of its teaching. Its concern is the complete release of the individual from worldly bondage and the attainment of Arahantship.
