It does not define its purpose around the cultivation of a lay religious life or the accommodation of spiritual practice within ordinary worldly commitments.

This position also explains why Arahanism cannot be reduced to a Buddhist sect defined by the historical succession of Buddhist institutions. Its intellectual horizon reaches further back than Gautama\'s immediate community and further forward than the earliest generations of his disciples. It recognises the wider Indian heritage of renunciation while maintaining Gautama\'s liberation teaching as its principal doctrinal foundation.

The subsequent transformation of religious culture is also relevant. Renunciant traditions have never entirely disappeared, and monastic communities continue to exist. Nevertheless, in many Buddhist societies, lay religious participation, devotional practice, merit-making, and household-centred religious life have acquired considerable prominence. The renunciant ideal has consequently lost some of the central cultural position it held in the earliest liberation-oriented communities. Arahanism seeks to restore the primacy of the renunciant objective rather than simply reproduce the religious culture of later Buddhist institutions.

Accordingly, Arahanism neither claims all ancient Indian renunciants as its members nor indiscriminately combines the doctrines of different religions. It studies their historical and spiritual contributions, recognises shared elements where the evidence supports them, distinguishes their differences, and grounds its own path in Gautama\'s teaching.

**Arahanism therefore has a historical horizon extending across the wider renunciant heritage of ancient India, but a clearly defined spiritual destination: complete renunciation of the world and the attainment of Arahantship.** Its relationship with earlier Indian traditions and later Buddhist communities is one of historical connection, textual investigation, and selective recognition---not doctrinal subordination or indiscriminate assimilation.

## III. Universal Consciousness and the Source of Existence

### 3.1. Consciousness as a Fundamental Question of Existence

Before examining the relationship between *Citta* and *Ātmā*, it is necessary to address a more fundamental question: What is the source of consciousness, and how is consciousness related to the existence and manifestation of the universe?

Consciousness is among the most fundamental questions concerning the nature of existence. It concerns the origin of the universe, the nature of the individual living being, the relationship between the spiritual individual and the manifestive world, and the conditions through which an individual experiences existence. These questions cannot be adequately understood when every occurrence of consciousness is treated as though it refers to the same phenomenon.

The English word *consciousness* is commonly used to describe several different dimensions of existence. It may refer to awareness arising through the senses, individual mental activity, accumulated personal experience, or a fundamental principle associated with the existence of the universe itself. These meanings are frequently brought together under a single term, creating confusion about their respective origins, functions, and relationships.

Arahanism maintains that consciousness must be understood through these distinctions. The consciousness involved in seeing, hearing, thinking, and experiencing the physical world cannot automatically be regarded as the entirety of individual consciousness. Likewise, the consciousness associated with an individual living being must be distinguished from the fundamental consciousness associated with the Source of the Universe.

Within the Arahanist cosmological framework, three fundamental dimensions must therefore be distinguished:

**\**

**1. Universal Consciousness**

Universal Consciousness is the fundamental consciousness associated with the Source of the Universe. It belongs to the cosmological dimension from which the universe and its manifestations arise. Its significance extends beyond the individual experience of living beings and concerns the origin and fundamental nature of existence itself.

**2. Citta--Ātmā: The Individual Spiritual Dimension**

*Citta* (Mind-Heart) and *Ātmā* (Soul-Spirit) belong to the individual spiritual dimension. *Ātmā* represents the innermost spiritual individual, while *Citta* constitutes the broader sphere of individual mental and spiritual functioning through which that individual experiences and engages with existence.

Their relationship is particularly important. Although *Citta* is associated with the individual Soul-Spirit, it can become defiled, dominated by worldly attachments, and directed towards experiences that perpetuate bondage. In such a condition, *Citta* ceases to serve the liberation of *Ātmā* and becomes the very instrument through which the Soul-Spirit remains trapped in worldly existence. The purification of *Citta* is therefore essential to the liberation of *Ātmā*.

**3. Viññāṇa: Sense-Generated and Experiential Consciousness**

*Viññāṇa*, rendered in Arahanist terminology as **sense-generated consciousness**, refers to conditioned consciousness arising through the relationship between relevant faculties and their corresponding objects. It is associated with the individual\'s experiences of the manifestive world.

Its significance, however, extends beyond the moment in which a particular sensory experience occurs. Within the Arahanist understanding, the consciousness generated through the experiences of successive lives becomes incorporated into the individual\'s *Citta*. Consequently, *Citta* encompasses accumulated experiential contents extending across successive existences, including impressions, attachments, tendencies, and defilements. These accumulated conditions contribute to the continuing bondage of the individual Soul-Spirit.

These three dimensions must be understood in their proper relationships. Universal Consciousness concerns the fundamental source of existence; *Citta--Ātmā* concerns the individual spiritual dimension; and *Viññāṇa* concerns conditioned and experiential consciousness associated with the individual\'s engagement with the manifestive world.

The distinction is essential because sensory consciousness cannot explain the entirety of the Mind-Heart, and the accumulated contents of the Mind-Heart cannot simply be equated with the innermost Soul-Spirit. Similarly, the individual spiritual dimension must not be confused with Universal Consciousness or reduced to the temporary cognitive processes through which a living being experiences the physical world.

This distinction also reveals the deeper significance of spiritual purification. The fundamental problem of the individual is not simply the occurrence of sensory experience, but the accumulated conditioning and defilement of *Citta*, through which worldly attachment and bondage continue. As long as *Citta* remains governed by these conditions, *Ātmā* cannot attain complete freedom. The path towards liberation from worldly entrapment therefore requires the purification and proper discipline of the Mind-Heart.

The investigation that follows will examine these dimensions individually and establish their relationships within the Arahanist understanding of existence. It will begin with Universal Consciousness and the Source of the Universe, before turning to the individual Soul-Spirit, the Mind-Heart, and the conditioned consciousness through which worldly experience becomes accumulated within the individual.

### 3.2. Universal Consciousness and the Manifestation of Existence

In the Arahanist understanding of existence, consciousness is fundamental to the universe itself. The universe is not understood as an originally unconscious material structure from which consciousness subsequently emerges. Rather, consciousness is primordial, and the manifestation of the universe takes place through the transformation of primordial consciousness into manifested existence.

**Primordial Consciousness is the Source of the Universe.** It precedes the manifested worlds and constitutes the fundamental origin from which their existence unfolds. What is commonly called consciousness in ordinary human experience, however, is generally the transformed and manifested consciousness: consciousness that has entered the manifestive world and become associated with its various forms of existence.

This distinction is essential. Primordial Consciousness belongs to the unmanifested source of existence, whereas manifested consciousness constitutes the substance of the manifested universe. The two are related, but they occupy different dimensions of existence.

**The Manifestation Through the Four Great Elements**

According to the Arahanist cosmological framework, the manifestation of the universe takes place through the four great elements/realms: *Paṭhavī* (Earth), *Āpo* (Water), *Tejo* (Fire), and *Vāyo* (Wind).

Through the process of manifestation, unmanifested consciousness enters the elemental dimensions of existence and undergoes successive transformations. The Wind dimension is particularly significant in this process. It is associated with movement, energy, and waves, through which manifested consciousness begins to assume increasingly differentiated forms.

As manifestation progresses, these forms undergo further aggregation and transformation, developing into particles and increasingly substantial constituents of the material world. Through the successive operation and combination of the four great elements, manifested existence develops towards the material structures associated with the Earth dimension.

The fundamental constituents of the manifested universe are therefore understood, within this cosmology, as originating from manifested consciousness. What appears to be empty space, dust, particles, energy, and matter belongs to different stages and forms of this wider process of manifestation.

The material universe and its constituent forms are consequently understood as expressions of a more fundamental conscious origin. Consciousness is present at the foundation of manifested existence, while the different forms of the universe represent successive conditions of its manifestation.

**From Spirit to Soul: The Individual Manifestation of Consciousness**

This cosmological process also provides the foundation for understanding the origin of the human spiritual individual.

Within the Arahanist framework, the human Spirit originates in primordial unmanifested consciousness. As this spiritual essence enters the manifestive world, it becomes enveloped by manifested consciousness, forming the individual Soul. The Soul therefore represents the Spirit within its manifested and embodied condition.

The relationship can be understood through the analogy of a Russian nesting doll. At the innermost dimension is the Spirit, while manifested consciousness forms an encompassing layer through which the individual becomes associated with the manifestive world. The resulting Soul is the individual spiritual existence within this manifested condition.

This distinction between Spirit and Soul is important. The innermost spiritual essence and the manifested individual are related, yet their conditions are different. The Soul carries the individual into the manifestive world, where it becomes associated with the experiences and conditions of embodied existence.

**The Accumulation of Viññāṇa and the Defilement of the Soul**

The formation of the individual Soul also provides the background for understanding the accumulation of *Viññāṇa* within *Citta*.

Through successive experiences in the manifestive world, *Viññāṇa* arises in dependence upon the relevant sense faculties and their external worldly objects. Within the Arahanist understanding, this experiential consciousness becomes incorporated into the individual\'s *Citta*, contributing to its accumulated contents across successive lives.

Consequently, the Mind-Heart carries accumulated experiences, impressions, attachments, and tendencies extending beyond the present lifetime. These accumulated conditions can progressively defile *Citta* and affect the condition of the individual Soul.

The original spiritual essence thus becomes associated with an increasingly conditioned and defiled Mind-Heart. Instead of serving the liberation of *Ātmā*, *Citta* becomes dominated by accumulated experiences, desires, and worldly attachments. The Soul remains bound within this condition until the Mind-Heart is purified.

This explains why Arahanism places such importance on the purification and discipline of *Citta*. The problem of worldly existence extends beyond the physical body and its temporary sensory experiences. It concerns the accumulated conditioning of the Mind-Heart and its relationship with the innermost Soul-Spirit.

The manifestation of the universe, the formation of the individual Soul, and the accumulation of experiential consciousness therefore belong to a connected cosmological and spiritual account. Understanding this relationship provides the foundation for examining how the individual becomes bound to manifested existence and, through the purification of *Citta*, ultimately attains liberation from worldly entrapment of the individual Spirit.

### 3.3. Ātmā: The Individual Soul-Spirit

Within the Arahanist understanding of existence, *Ātmā* represents the individual Soul-Spirit: the innermost spiritual individual which is originated from the Primordial Consciousness and whose existence becomes associated with the manifested universe.

The nature of *Ātmā* must be understood in relation to the distinction between Spirit and Soul. Spirit represents the innermost spiritual essence originating in Primordial Consciousness. As this essence enters the manifestive world, it becomes enveloped by manifested consciousness, forming the individual Soul. The Soul therefore carries the original Spirit within its manifested condition, providing the individual with continuity through successive existences.

This relationship can be understood through the analogy of a Russian nesting doll. At the innermost dimension is Spirit, surrounded by layers of manifested consciousness that constitute the individual Soul. These layers represent the individual\'s association with manifested existence and the accumulated conditions through which it experiences successive lives. The innermost Spirit remains the spiritual essence of the individual, while the encompassing Soul carries the conditions of its manifested existence.

**The Individual Ātmā and the Mind-Heart**

The relationship between *Ātmā* and *Citta* is central to understanding the individual spiritual being.

*Citta*, rendered as **Mind-Heart**, encompasses two closely related dimensions: the Mind and the Heart. Although they belong to the same individual *Citta*, they have distinct functions and significance.

The **Mind** is associated with the present physical existence, particularly the brain and nervous system. Through this dimension, the individual engages with the immediate world, processes sensory information, forms thoughts, exercises will, makes decisions, and directs actions. Its functioning is closely connected with the present physical body and the conditions of the current lifetime.

The **Heart**, however, represents the deeper spiritual dimension of *Citta*. It is the inward abode of *Ātmā*, through which the continuity of the individual extends beyond the present physical existence. Within the Arahanist understanding, the Heart carries the accumulated spiritual and experiential contents of successive lives. Its significance therefore extends far beyond the memories and mental activities associated with the present lifetime.

In this sense, the Mind principally concerns the individual\'s present embodied existence, while the Heart represents the deeper continuity of the individual across successive existences. Together, they constitute the Mind-Heart (*Citta*), within which the individual Soul-Spirit exists and experiences the conditions of manifested life.

**The Heart and the Embodied Individual**

The relationship between the spiritual Heart and the physical body is particularly significant in the Arahanist understanding of human existence.

During embryonic development, the heart becomes one of the earliest functioning organs of the developing human being. The brain and nervous system also develop from the earliest stages of embryonic formation and subsequently become increasingly integrated with the growing body.

Arahanism understands the spiritual significance of the Heart in relation to this embodied development. The individual Soul-Spirit is associated with the developing human being, while the physical body and its nervous system provide the bodily foundation through which the present-life Mind functions.

The distinction between the spiritual Heart and the physical heart must therefore be maintained. The physical organ belongs to the manifested body, whereas the spiritual Heart concerns the deeper individual dimension associated with *Ātmā* and the continuity of successive lives.

**Ātmā and Primordial Consciousness**

The individual Soul-Spirit ultimately belongs to the wider cosmological account of Primordial Consciousness and the manifestation of existence.

Primordial Consciousness is the Source of the Universe. Through the process of manifestation, consciousness enters the manifested dimensions of existence, providing the foundation for the emergence of individual spiritual beings and the material world.

Within this cosmology, the Spirit represents the innermost spiritual essence originating in Primordial Consciousness. Through its association with manifested consciousness, it becomes the individual Soul. Each individual therefore possesses a spiritual origin that reaches beyond the temporary conditions of the present physical body.

The individual *Ātmā* must consequently be understood within this relationship between Primordial Consciousness, manifested existence, and the formation of the Soul-Spirit. Its existence extends beyond the present lifetime, while its condition within manifested existence is influenced by the accumulated contents of *Citta*.

This brings us back to the central problem of spiritual bondage. Although *Ātmā* carries the innermost spiritual essence of the individual, its association with a defiled *Citta* prevents it from attaining freedom. The Mind may become occupied with the immediate concerns of the present life, while the deeper Heart carries accumulated conditions from successive existences. As *Viññāṇa* continues to contribute to the contents of *Citta*, attachments and defilements can become further established.

The liberation of *Ātmā* therefore requires the purification of the entire Mind-Heart. The present-life Mind must be disciplined, while the accumulated defilements within the deeper spiritual dimension must be overcome. Through the purification of *Citta*, the Soul-Spirit can ultimately be purified and unbound, untying from the knots of manifested existence, and released from worldly bondage.

### 3.4. Citta: The Mind-Heart and Its Relationship with Ātmā

**The Nature of Citta**

In Arahanism, *Citta* is understood as the **Mind-Heart**, the broader individual sphere of mental and spiritual consciousness. It encompasses two closely connected dimensions: the Mind, which functions through the present physical existence, and the Heart, which represents the deeper spiritual dimension and carries the continuity of the individual across successive lives. Together, these constitute the Mind-Heart through which the individual experiences existence, responds to the manifested world, and undergoes spiritual development or decline.

The Mind is principally associated with the present life. Through the brain, nervous system, and bodily faculties, it enables the individual to perceive, think, remember, reason, imagine, deliberate, and make decisions. It directs attention towards objects, interprets experiences, and influences bodily conduct. Its activities are closely connected with the conditions of the present physical existence and the sensory world.

The Heart belongs to a deeper dimension of individual existence. It is the spiritual abode of *Ātmā* and the repository of accumulated spiritual and experiential contents from successive lives. These include impressions, attachments, tendencies, and other conditions that continue to influence the individual beyond the duration of a single physical life. The Heart therefore carries the deeper continuity through which the individual brings the accumulated consequences of previous existence into the present one.

The Mind and Heart are distinct dimensions, yet they function as an integrated whole. The Mind engages with the immediate world, while the Heart carries the deeper continuity of the individual. Their combined functioning constitutes *Citta*. Consequently, the condition of *Citta* reflects both the individual\'s present mental activities and the accumulated spiritual conditioning carried from previous lives.

**The *Manussa* Heritage and the Inherited Condition of Human *Citta***

The condition of human *Citta* must also be understood in relation to the cosmic heritage of humanity. According to the Arahanist cosmological understanding, human beings possess a dual spiritual inheritance: the structural and lineage inheritance of *Manussa*, associated with Brahmā as its source, and the fundamental spiritual essence originating from Primordial Consciousness.

The present human population of Earth, however, is understood within this framework to have undergone a hybridization during its creation between the cosmic *Manussa* divine lineage and a terrestrial, monkey-like primate. Arahanism associates this event with the final human-creation account preserved in ancient Sumerian tablets. This hybridization is understood to have introduced additional characteristics into the human constitution, producing a spiritual condition more heavily burdened by certain instinctive and worldly tendencies (mainly the excessive sexual desires and fear) than that of the original *Manussa* ancestors.

The significance of this inheritance lies in its influence upon the human Mind-Heart. The present human condition exhibits particularly strong tendencies towards sensual desire, sexual craving, fear, anger, aggression, and other forms of negativities. In the language of Gautama\'s teaching, these tendencies are closely associated with *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. They influence how the Mind responds to the manifested world and how the deeper Heart retains and perpetuates attachments across successive lives.

This inherited condition may also provide an Arahanist interpretation of the idea of Original Sin preserved in the western biblical tradition. From this perspective, humanity carries a spiritual burden arising from its inherited constitution. The predicament is therefore not simply a consequence of individual choices made during the present life. Human beings inherit conditions that existed before their individual birth and must nevertheless confront their consequences through spiritual discipline.

This does not mean that the human Soul-Spirit has lost its fundamental *Manussa* nature. On the contrary, the human constitution retains a substantial *Manussa* inheritance. This is essential because it preserves the capacity for spiritual purification and restoration. Arahanism further understands the wider cosmic population to include many forms of sentient species, such as insectoid and animaloid beings, whose constitutions may possess little or no *Manussa* inheritance through their respective histories of cosmic development and hybridization. Human beings retain a particularly significant connection with the *Manussa* lineage and therefore the possibility of restoring their spiritual condition and ultimately rejoining the *Manussa* or Brahmā family.

This understanding also explains why the human liberation path requires such sustained effort. The inherited spiritual burden makes the eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* especially demanding. Human beings must overcome both the conditions accumulated through their own successive experiences and the deeper tendencies associated with their inherited constitution.

**How Citta Becomes Associated with Ātmā**

Within the Arahanist understanding, *Ātmā* and *Citta* are intimately associated throughout the individual\'s existence in the manifested world. *Ātmā* is the individual Soul-Spirit, while *Citta* constitutes the Mind-Heart through which that spiritual individual experiences and engages with manifested existence.

The relationship may be understood through the image of a Russian nesting doll. At the innermost dimension is Spirit, originating from Primordial Consciousness. As it enters manifested existence, it becomes enveloped by successive layers of manifested consciousness, forming the individual Soul-Spirit. This Soul-Spirit, understood as *Ātmā*, dwells within the deeper spiritual dimension of the Heart. The Mind-Heart accompanies and surrounds the individual through its experiences in the manifested world.

Through this association, *Citta* becomes the field in which the experiences of manifested existence are received, retained, and accumulated. The arising of *Viññāṇa* through sensory experience contributes to this process. Each experience can leave an impression, strengthen a tendency, or establish an attachment. As these conditions accumulate, they influence the disposition and orientation of the Mind-Heart.

The inherited constitution of humanity and the accumulated experiences of successive lives therefore converge in the condition of *Citta*. The former provides underlying tendencies associated with the human constitution, while the latter continually shapes and reinforces the individual\'s particular dispositions. The Mind-Heart becomes the sphere through which these conditions are expressed, strengthened, and perpetuated.

**How Citta Can Dominate and Imprison Ātmā**

The central problem arises when *Citta* becomes dominated by accumulated defilements, attachments, and worldly tendencies. Its proper function is to serve the spiritual development of the individual and support the attainment of liberation from worldly bondage. When it becomes governed by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, however, it turns away from this purpose and binds the individual ever more deeply to manifested existence.

The Mind becomes drawn towards sensory objects, worldly pleasures, personal ambitions, and the pursuit of gratification. It repeatedly directs attention and intention towards what strengthens worldly attachment. Meanwhile, the deeper spiritual dimension of the Heart carries accumulated tendencies and impressions from previous lives, which can reinforce the same patterns of desire, aversion, and delusion. The inherited human constitution adds further weight to these tendencies, making the struggle against defilement particularly demanding.

In this condition, *Citta* effectively dominates *Ātmā*. The Soul-Spirit becomes entangled in the activities and demands of the Mind-Heart, while the Mind-Heart continues to pursue the objects of manifested existence. The individual consequently lives under the direction of accumulated conditioning rather than the aspiration for spiritual freedom. The very faculty that should serve the liberation of *Ātmā* becomes the instrument through which it remains bound.

This also explains why intellectual understanding alone cannot bring about liberation. An individual may understand spiritual teachings, recognize the limitations of worldly existence, and sincerely aspire towards freedom. Yet, if the Mind-Heart remains governed by deeply rooted worldly attachments and accumulated defilements, those conditions continue to direct the individual\'s conduct and experience. The Soul-Spirit remains entangled because the underlying condition of *Citta* has not been transformed.

The problem is therefore deeper than the temporary influence of particular thoughts or emotions. It concerns the accumulated condition of the entire Mind-Heart, including inherited tendencies, impressions from previous lives, and attachments continually reinforced through present experience. Unless these conditions are overcome, the individual remains subject to the same patterns of worldly bondage.

**The Necessity of Purifying Citta**

The purification of *Citta* is indispensable to the liberation of *Ātmā*. Since the Mind-Heart carries both the activities of the present life and the accumulated conditioning of previous lives, its purification must address the entire individual sphere of mental and spiritual existence.

The discipline of the present-life Mind begins with *Sīla*, through which conduct is restrained and the foundations of spiritual training are established. The development of *Samādhi* brings the Mind under control, allowing it to become settled, collected, and free from its habitual pursuit of sensory gratification. Through sustained mental discipline and sense restraint, the practitioner gradually weakens the influence of worldly attachments and develops the stability necessary for deeper spiritual purification.

The purification of the Heart concerns the deeper accumulated conditions that continue to bind the individual across successive lives. The eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* must reach these underlying tendencies and attachments. The practitioner must therefore progress beyond the temporary restraint of outward conduct and the calming of immediate mental activity, towards the complete removal of the conditions that sustain worldly bondage.

This undertaking is particularly significant for present-day humanity. If the human constitution carries an inherited spiritual burden from its hybridized history, the practitioner must overcome both this underlying condition and the defilements accumulated through individual existence. The burden is inherited, but its perpetuation can be overcome through Gautama's liberation path. It is not a reason for despair or resignation; it establishes the seriousness of the spiritual task.

The predominantly *Manussa* constitution of human beings remains the basis of this possibility. Despite the inherited burden, the Soul-Spirit retains its fundamental spiritual potential for purification. Through the complete discipline of the Mind-Heart, the individual can progressively overcome the conditions that separate it from its higher spiritual heritage.

In this process, *Citta* must be restored to its proper function: serving the liberation of *Ātmā*. The Mind must cease to be driven by sensory gratification, and the Heart must be purified of the accumulated defilements that perpetuate worldly existence. As these conditions are overcome, the Mind-Heart no longer exercises its dominating influence over the Soul-Spirit.

This is why the training of *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, and *Paññā* occupies such a central place in Gautama's liberation path. Through *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, and *Paññā*, the practitioner progressively develops *Nibbidā* (disenchantment with the worldly existence), *Virāga* (fading of worldly passion), and ultimately *Vimutti* (liberation from worldly bondage). The complete purification of *Citta* culminates in the eradication of the defilements that bind the individual to manifested existence.

The relationship between *Citta* and *Ātmā* is therefore central to the Arahanist understanding of liberation. The Soul-Spirit cannot attain complete freedom while the Mind-Heart remains governed by defilement. The entire *Citta* must be purified so that it ceases to imprison the individual and instead becomes fully aligned with the attainment of liberation from worldly bondage. Through this purification, the Soul-Spirit can become unbound from the accumulated knots of manifested existence, overcome worldly bondage, and ultimately restore its spiritual connection with the *Manussa* or Brahmā family.

### 3.5. Viññāṇa: Experiential Consciousness and Its Accumulation

**The Nature of Viññāṇa**

In Arahanism, *Viññāṇa* is understood as **sense-generated consciousness**: the conditioned consciousness arising through the relationship between a sense faculty and its corresponding external worldly object. It is the consciousness through which an individual becomes aware of and experiences the manifested world.

The Pāli suttas describe six forms of *Viññāṇa*, arising in dependence upon the six sense faculties and their corresponding objects:

- *Cakkhuviññāṇa* --- eye-consciousness, arising in relation to visible forms.

- *Sotaviññāṇa* --- ear-consciousness, arising in relation to sounds.

- *Ghānaviññāṇa* --- nose-consciousness, arising in relation to odours.

- *Jivhāviññāṇa* --- tongue-consciousness, arising in relation to tastes.

- *Kāyaviññāṇa* --- body-consciousness, arising in relation to tangible objects.

- *Manoviññāṇa* --- mind-consciousness, arising in relation to mental objects.

These six forms describe the different ways in which consciousness arises through the faculties of experience. *Viññāṇa* is therefore conditioned by the relationship between the individual and the manifested world. It enables the individual to experience sensory and mental objects, while its arising and functioning depend upon the relevant conditions.

This understanding is important because *Viññāṇa* has a specific function within the broader constitution of the individual. It represents consciousness associated with particular experiences, rather than the entirety of the Mind-Heart or the fundamental spiritual essence of *Ātmā*. It should consequently be distinguished from both *Citta* and *Ātmā*.

*Ātmā* is the individual Soul-Spirit. *Citta* is the broader Mind-Heart through which the individual experiences and carries its accumulated spiritual and mental conditions. *Viññāṇa* is the conditioned consciousness arising through experience within manifested existence. These three terms describe different dimensions of the individual and should not be treated as interchangeable.

**How Viññāṇa Arises Through Experience**

The arising of *Viññāṇa* is closely connected with the six sense faculties and their respective objects. When the eye encounters a visible form, eye-consciousness arises. When the ear encounters a sound, ear-consciousness arises. The same principle applies to the other sensory faculties and to the mind\'s engagement with mental objects.

The Pāli suttas repeatedly establish this dependent relationship. In the *Mahātaṇhāsaṅkhaya Sutta* (MN 38), Gautama explains that consciousness arises dependent upon conditions and is designated according to the conditions upon which it depends. Eye-consciousness, for example, arises dependent upon the eye and visible forms; ear-consciousness arises dependent upon the ear and sounds. The discourse emphasizes that consciousness does not arise independently of its supporting conditions.

Through these encounters, the individual experiences the manifested world. Sensory contact gives rise to experience, and experience may produce feelings of pleasure, displeasure, or neutrality. The individual then responds through attraction, aversion, recognition, intention, and other mental activities. These responses influence the condition of *Citta* and determine whether an experience strengthens attachment or contributes to spiritual understanding.

An experience is therefore more than a momentary encounter with an external object. It can leave an enduring impression upon the individual Mind-Heart. Repeated experiences can strengthen particular tendencies, establish habitual responses, and deepen attachments. The manner in which the individual receives and responds to experience consequently has profound implications for the spiritual condition of *Citta*.

**The Incorporation of Viññāṇa into Citta**

Within the Arahanist understanding, the experiential contents arising through *Viññāṇa* become incorporated into the broader sphere of *Citta*. The Mind-Heart receives and retains the impressions of experience, allowing them to influence the individual\'s subsequent thoughts, perceptions, inclinations, and conduct.

This distinction between *Viññāṇa* and *Citta* is essential. *Viññāṇa* arises through particular conditions of experience, whereas *Citta* encompasses the broader individual field in which the accumulated contents and consequences of those experiences become established.

For example, repeated encounters with an object may produce recurring feelings of pleasure and attraction. When the individual repeatedly pursues that experience, the associated desire can become strengthened. What initially arose as a particular sensory experience may develop into a habitual attachment. Similarly, experiences associated with fear, hostility, or suffering may strengthen aversion and defensive tendencies. Through repetition, these patterns become increasingly influential within the Mind-Heart.

The accumulation is not limited to conscious recollection of particular events. The deeper significance lies in the impressions, inclinations, attachments, and tendencies that experiences establish within the individual. Even when a particular experience is no longer consciously remembered, its influence may continue through the dispositions it has strengthened.

Thus, *Viññāṇa* contributes to the formation and conditioning of *Citta*. The Mind encounters the manifested world and engages with its objects, while the deeper Heart carries the accumulated spiritual and experiential contents of the individual. Through this continuing process, the Mind-Heart becomes shaped by the individual\'s experiences and responses.

**The Accumulation of Experiential Contents Across Successive Lives**

The Arahanist understanding of *Citta* extends this process beyond the duration of a single physical life. The deeper spiritual dimension of the Mind-Heart, the Heart, carries the continuity of the individual across successive existences. Consequently, the accumulated contents arising through experience are not confined to the present physical lifetime.

The experiences of successive lives contribute to the accumulated condition of the individual. Desires repeatedly pursued, aversions repeatedly strengthened, and attachments repeatedly maintained become part of the deeper conditioning carried by the Mind-Heart. These accumulated tendencies influence the individual\'s dispositions in subsequent existences and help explain why different individuals may exhibit deeply established inclinations from an early age.

Within this framework, the present-life Mind begins its activities under conditions that have already been shaped by previous existence. It does not begin as an entirely unconditioned faculty. Its inclinations and responses are influenced by the accumulated contents of the Heart, while new experiences continually add to and reinforce those conditions.

This process also operates alongside the inherited spiritual constitution of humanity discussed in the previous section. The human Mind-Heart carries both the deeper tendencies associated with the inherited condition of the human lineage and the experiential accumulation of successive lives. These influences can reinforce one another, contributing to the considerable strength of worldly attachments and defilements.

It is therefore important to understand that the present individual inherits a complex spiritual condition. The Mind encounters the world under the influence of accumulated tendencies, and its responses generate further experiences that can either strengthen bondage or contribute to purification. Unless this process is brought under control, the same patterns of attachment and aversion continue to reproduce themselves throughout successive existences.

**How Viññāṇa Contributes to the Defilement of Citta**

The accumulation of experiential contents becomes particularly consequential when experiences repeatedly reinforce *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

Through *rāga*, the individual becomes attached to pleasurable experiences and develops an increasingly strong desire to possess, repeat, and preserve them. Through *dosa*, unpleasant experiences can strengthen resentment, hostility, fear, and aversion. Through *moha*, the individual fails to understand the true nature of these experiences and remains attached to the conditions that perpetuate worldly existence.

These three fundamental defilements influence how the individual engages with the manifested world. They also shape the impressions retained within *Citta*. Experiences received through *Viññāṇa* can therefore become the basis for further attachment, reinforcing the very conditions that keep the Mind-Heart defiled.

This creates a continuing cycle. The individual encounters an object, experiences it, develops a response, and strengthens a particular tendency. That tendency then influences subsequent encounters and responses. Repeated over successive lives, this process contributes to the accumulation of deeply rooted defilements within the Heart.

The significance of *Viññāṇa* therefore extends beyond the arising of momentary sensory awareness. Its role within the Arahanist framework concerns the way experience contributes to the continuing spiritual condition of the individual. The Mind-Heart becomes burdened through accumulated attachments and tendencies, while the Soul-Spirit remains entangled in the resulting conditions of manifested existence.

This also explains why the purification of *Citta* requires more than simply avoiding particular sensory objects. The practitioner must overcome the underlying attachments and tendencies established through experience. Otherwise, even when external conditions change, the accumulated conditioning within the Mind-Heart can continue to generate the same desires, aversions, and delusions.

**The Purification of Viññāṇa and the Liberation of Ātmā**

The liberation path requires the practitioner to transform the relationship between sensory experience and the Mind-Heart. The six sense faculties continue to encounter their respective objects, but the individual must learn to prevent these encounters from generating further attachment and bondage.

Through *Sīla*, the practitioner establishes restraint in bodily and verbal conduct. Through *Samādhi*, the Mind becomes collected and progressively freed from its habitual pursuit of sensory gratification. Through *Paññā*, the practitioner develops the understanding necessary to overcome the underlying conditions of attachment and delusion.

The purpose of this training is not simply to reduce the number of experiences or suppress the functioning of the senses. It is to eradicate the defilements through which experience becomes a source of further bondage. When *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* are progressively abandoned, the individual ceases to reinforce the accumulated conditions that keep *Citta* defiled.

The deeper purification of the Heart must also be completed. The practitioner must overcome the accumulated tendencies carried from previous existences, together with the attachments strengthened through present experience. The entire Mind-Heart must reach a condition in which the old patterns of worldly bondage no longer govern the individual.

In this sense, the purification of *Citta* concerns the entire history of the individual within manifested existence. The inherited spiritual burden, the accumulated contents of successive lives, and the tendencies continually reinforced through present experience must all be overcome. Only through such complete purification can the Soul-Spirit become fully unbound.

The distinction between *Viññāṇa*, *Citta*, and *Ātmā* therefore reveals an important dimension of the Arahanist liberation path. *Viññāṇa* is the conditioned consciousness through which experience arises; *Citta* is the Mind-Heart in which the consequences of experience accumulate; and *Ātmā* is the individual Soul-Spirit whose liberation depends upon the purification of that Mind-Heart.

The practitioner must therefore understand the nature of experience, the accumulation of its consequences, and the defilements that sustain this process. Through the complete eradication of these conditions, *Citta* can cease perpetuating worldly bondage, allowing *Ātmā* to attain liberation and the Deathless.

### 3.6. Mano and Cetanā: The Directing Faculty and Volitional Activity

**The Nature of Mano: The Mind-Will**

Within the Arahanist understanding, *Mano* (Sanskrit: *Manas*) is understood as **Mind-Will**, the directing faculty operating within the broader sphere of *Citta*. It is through *Mano* that the individual directs attention, determines the orientation of thought, considers possibilities, forms intentions, and exercises volitional direction over mental activity.

While *Citta* encompasses the entire Mind-Heart, *Mano* performs a more specific function within it. The Mind receives and processes experiences, while *Mano* directs the Mind towards particular objects, thoughts, and courses of action. It can turn the Mind towards sensory gratification, worldly ambitions, and habitual attachments, or direct it towards sense restraint, concentration, wisdom, and liberation.

This distinction is fundamental to understanding the individual\'s capacity for spiritual development. The Mind may be influenced by accumulated tendencies, sensory impressions, inherited dispositions, and previous experiences. Nevertheless, the individual possesses the capacity to direct the Mind through *Mano*. The direction chosen determines which tendencies are strengthened and which are gradually overcome.

*Mano* therefore occupies a decisive position in the functioning of *Citta* and Gautama's liberation path. It is the faculty through which the individual can either perpetuate the existing condition of worldly bondage or deliberately undertake the discipline necessary for liberation from worldly bondage.

**Manopubbaṅgamā Dhammā: Mind-Will as the Forerunner**

The importance of *Mano* is already apparent in the very first verse of the *Dhammapada*:

*Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā,*\
*Manasā ce paduṭṭhena, bhāsati vā karoti vā,*\
*Tato naṃ dukkhamanveti, cakkaṃ\'va vahato padaṃ.*

An Arahanist rendering, emphasizing the directing function of *Mano*, is:

**Mind-Will is the forerunner of all dhamma. Mind-Will is their chief; they are all produced by mind-will. With impure mind-will, one speaks or acts, and suffering follows, just as the wheel follows the hoof of the ox.**

The opening declaration, *Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā*, establishes the primacy of *Mano*. The direction of the mind-will precedes the individual\'s expression and conduct. The following expressions, *manoseṭṭhā* and *manomayā*, further emphasize its governing and formative significance.

The verse then illustrates the consequences of an impure direction of mind-will. When the individual allows *Mano* to be governed by defilement, speech and action follow that direction, and suffering inevitably accompanies the resulting conduct.

The second verse of the *Dhammapada* presents the corresponding positive principle. When one speaks or acts with a purified mind, happiness follows, just as a shadow never departs from its source. Together, these opening verses establish the importance of the condition and direction of the mind in determining the consequences of human conduct.

For Arahanism, this teaching has a profound implication. The individual is not merely a passive recipient of sensory experiences and accumulated tendencies. There is an active directing faculty within *Citta* through which the individual can determine how to respond to those conditions. The direction of *Mano* influences the development of the entire Mind-Heart.

The first verse of the *Dhammapada* therefore provides an important foundation for understanding the role of Mind-Will in Gautama's liberation path. The direction of the will determines whether the individual continues to strengthen worldly bondage or begins to overcome it.

**Cetanā: Volition and the Formation of Kamma**

Closely related to *Mano* is *Cetanā*, which Arahanism renders as **Volition**. It refers to the volitional activity through which intention is formed and directed towards particular actions.

The distinction between these two faculties is important. *Mano* is the directing faculty of Mind-Will, while *Cetanā* is the volitional activity through which the individual intends and engages in action. They function in close relationship within the broader sphere of *Citta*.

The significance of *Cetanā* is explicitly established by Gautama in the *Nibbedhika Sutta* (AN 6.63):

*Cetanāhaṃ, bhikkhave, kammaṃ vadāmi.*

\"Bhikkhus, it is intention that I call kamma.\"

The discourse continues by explaining that, having intended, one performs actions through body, speech, and mind.

This statement is fundamental to understanding moral responsibility and the formation of kamma. Kamma is intimately connected with intention. The individual\'s actions are not understood solely through their outward appearance; the volitional direction underlying them is essential.

Within the Arahanist framework, *Mano* directs the orientation of the Mind, while *Cetanā* expresses that direction through volitional activity. When *Mano* is governed by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, volition becomes directed towards actions that reinforce attachment and bondage. When *Mano* is disciplined and increasingly purified, volitional activity can instead support ethical conduct, spiritual development, and liberation.

The relationship may therefore be understood as follows:

- **Citta --- Mind-Heart:** The broader individual sphere of mental and spiritual consciousness.

- **Mano --- Mind-Will:** The directing faculty that determines the orientation of mental activity.

- **Cetanā --- Volition:** The volitional activity through which intention is expressed and actions are undertaken.

- **Kamma --- Intentional action:** The morally significant action arising through intention and expressed through body, speech, or mind.

These functions are closely connected, but they should not be confused. Understanding their relationship reveals how the individual participates in the formation of kamma and how the same faculties can be trained towards liberation.

**Free Will and the Capacity for Spiritual Direction**

The existence of *Mano* also raises an important question: do human beings possess free will?

Arahanism affirms that the individual possesses a genuine capacity to direct the Mind through *Mano*. Human beings are influenced by inherited tendencies, accumulated experiences, sensory desires, and the existing condition of *Citta*. Yet these influences do not entirely eliminate the individual\'s capacity to choose a direction and exercise volitional control.

A person may experience desire without necessarily following it. Anger may arise without requiring an angry response. Fear may influence the Mind without determining every subsequent decision. The individual can recognize these conditions, restrain the immediate impulse, redirect attention, and deliberately cultivate a different course of conduct.

This capacity is fundamental to spiritual training. If the individual were entirely incapable of directing the Mind, the undertaking of renunciation, ethical discipline, sense restraint, meditation, and wisdom would have no meaningful foundation. The very possibility of training presupposes an ability to exercise direction over one\'s mental activities.

Arahanism therefore understands free will as the capacity of the individual to exercise direction through *Mano*, even while the Mind-Heart remains influenced by accumulated conditioning. This capacity can be weakened when *Mano* is dominated by defilements, or strengthened through sustained spiritual discipline.

Free will does not mean that every decision arises independently of all previous conditions. The individual begins from an existing condition shaped by previous experiences, accumulated tendencies, and inherited characteristics. Nevertheless, within that condition, the individual retains the capacity to direct the Mind and influence the course of subsequent development.

This distinction is especially important for understanding human responsibility. The inherited spiritual burden discussed in the previous section may make the struggle against *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* more demanding, but it does not remove the individual\'s capacity to undertake that struggle. Human beings are responsible for how they exercise their will in response to the conditions they inherit and encounter.

The capacity to direct *Mano* is therefore both a responsibility and an opportunity. It allows the individual to interrupt habitual patterns, resist the demands of sensory gratification, and gradually establish a new direction towards spiritual freedom.

**The Misuse and Restoration of Mano**

Although *Mano* provides the capacity for spiritual direction, it can also become an instrument through which *Citta* perpetuates its own defilement.

When dominated by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, *Mano* repeatedly directs attention towards objects of desire, aversion, and delusion. It encourages the pursuit of sensory gratification, strengthens habitual reactions, and reinforces the accumulated tendencies within the Heart.

In this condition, the directing faculty that could lead the individual towards liberation instead serves the continuation of worldly bondage. The Mind-Will becomes occupied with the preservation and gratification of the very attachments from which the Soul-Spirit must become free.

The restoration of *Mano* therefore requires deliberate training. Through *Sīla*, the individual establishes restraint over bodily, verbal, and mental conduct. Through *Samādhi*, the directing faculty becomes increasingly stable and capable of maintaining its chosen orientation without being continually distracted by sensory objects and habitual impulses. Through *Paññā*, the individual develops the understanding necessary to recognize and abandon the conditions that sustain defilement.

As this training develops, *Mano* increasingly directs the Mind towards inward purification rather than outward gratification. Instead of allowing every desire to determine conduct, the individual learns to examine, restrain, and ultimately abandon the underlying attachment. Instead of responding to aversion through anger or hostility, the individual develops the capacity to overcome its underlying causes.

The purpose of this discipline is to restore the proper function of Mind-Will within the Mind-Heart. *Mano* must become an instrument of spiritual development, directing the activities of the present-life Mind towards the purification of the deeper Heart and the liberation of *Ātmā*.

**Mano as an Instrument of Liberation**

The significance of *Mano* ultimately lies in its role in the liberation of the individual. The human condition is influenced by inherited tendencies, accumulated experiences, and the defilements carried within *Citta*. Yet the individual possesses the capacity to undertake the discipline necessary to overcome these conditions.

Through the proper direction of *Mano*, the practitioner can establish ethical restraint, develop concentration, cultivate wisdom, and progressively abandon the underlying tendencies that perpetuate worldly existence. The direction of the will changes from the pursuit of manifested objects towards the attainment of spiritual freedom.

This is why the training of the Mind is central to the Arahanist path. The practitioner must learn to direct the Mind deliberately, sustain that direction through concentration, and employ wisdom to eradicate the defilements that continue to influence the Mind-Heart.

The relationship between *Mano*, *Cetanā*, and *Citta* thus reveals the active dimension of spiritual liberation. *Citta* is the broader Mind-Heart; *Mano* provides the directing faculty; and *Cetanā* expresses the volitional activity through which intentions become actions. Together, they explain how the individual participates in the continuation of bondage and how the same individual can undertake the discipline leading to freedom.

Ultimately, the liberation of *Ātmā* requires the complete purification of the Mind-Heart. *Mano* must cease directing the individual towards worldly gratification and become fully aligned with the pursuit of liberation. When the defilements are eradicated, the will no longer serves the perpetuation of bondage. It has fulfilled its role in the path towards the Deathless.

### 3.7. The Bondage of Ātmā and the Purification of Citta

**The Fundamental Conflict Within the Individual**

The preceding sections have established the relationship between *Ātmā*, *Citta*, *Viññāṇa*, *Mano*, and *Cetanā*. Together, these concepts reveal the inner constitution of the individual and the conditions that determine whether the Soul-Spirit remains bound to manifested existence or progresses towards liberation from worldly bondage.

*Ātmā* is the individual Soul-Spirit, carrying its spiritual heritage and continuity through successive existences. *Citta* is the Mind-Heart through which the individual experiences manifested existence and accumulates the consequences of those experiences. *Viññāṇa* provides the sense-generated consciousness through which experiences arise, while *Mano* (Mind-Will) directs mental activity and *Cetanā* (Volition) expresses the individual\'s intentions through action.

These dimensions function in close relationship, yet their condition can become deeply corrupted by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. The inherited tendencies of the human constitution, together with the accumulated impressions and attachments of successive lives, can cause the Mind-Heart to become increasingly dominated by worldly concerns.

The fundamental spiritual conflict arises when *Citta*, which should serve the development and liberation of *Ātmā*, instead becomes the instrument of its bondage to the manifested world. The Mind-Will repeatedly directs attention towards sensory gratification, while accumulated worldly attachments within the Heart reinforce the same tendencies. Volitional activity then perpetuates the cycle through intentional thoughts, speech, and bodily actions.

In this condition, the individual becomes governed by the demands of the defiled Mind-Heart. The Soul-Spirit remains entangled in the accumulated conditions of manifested existence, while the very faculties through which it could pursue liberation continue to strengthen its worldly bondage.

The central task of the liberation path is therefore to reverse this relationship. The Mind-Heart must be disciplined and purified so that it ceases to dominate the Soul-Spirit and instead becomes fully directed towards its liberation from worldly bondage.

**The Treachery of Citta: The Teaching of Anūpama**

A particularly striking illustration of this inner conflict appears in the *Theragāthā*, in the verses attributed to the elder Anūpama (Thag 2.47):

*Nandamānāgataṁ cittaṁ, sūlamāropamānakaṁ;*\
*Tena teneva vajasi, yena sūlaṁ kaliṅgaraṁ.*

*Tāhaṁ cittakaliṁ brūmi, taṁ brūmi cittadubbhakaṁ;*\
*Satthā te dullabho laddho, mānatthe maṁ niyojayī.*

Literal translation:

"The *citta*, drawn toward delight (*nanda-mānāgataṁ*), is like one being hoisted onto a sharp execution stake (*sūlamāropamānakaṁ*); With that very \[*citta*\] you wander here and there (*tena teneva vajasi*), toward where the stake and the dry log lie (*yena sūlaṁ kaliṅgaraṁ*).

Therefore, I declare that *citta* a defeat (*tāhaṁ cittakaliṁ brūmi*), I declare that *citta* a treacherous betrayer (*taṁ brūmi cittadubbhakaṁ*); Though you have obtained the Teacher, who is rare to find (*satthā te dullabho laddho*), you yoke me \[into service\] for the sake of pride/vanity (*mānatthe maṁ niyojayī*)."

**Arahanist** **Commentary: An Internal Courtroom Scene**

The **attā** (the inner soul-spirit core, the true subject operating through the body) is directly confronting, exposing, and indicting the **citta** for treason.

**The Dynamics of the Accusation:**

1)  **The Attā as the Betrayed Sovereign:** The *attā* entered the dispensation of the rare Teacher (*satthā te dullabho laddho*) to achieve a permanent liberational exit from the *loka* into the *Nibbāna-dhātu*.

2)  **The Citta as the Hijacker:** Instead of serving that liberation, the *citta* acted as a rogue agent (*cittakali*, *cittadubbhakaṁ*). Driven by its addiction to sensory delight (*nandamāna*), it hijacked the physical vehicle and forced the *attā*---and the practitioner\'s entire existence (*maṁ*)---to labor for petty ego-gratification and social vanity (*mānatthe maṁ niyojayī*).

This internal courtroom scene highlights why the renunciant needs to tame the *citta.* It must be reined in through ***samādhi***, and starved of material fuel (**upadhi**) so that the *attā* can finally break free from the womb-matrix (*yoni*).

These verses present a powerful confrontation with the wandering and unreliable *Citta*. The Mind is portrayed as continually drawn towards objects of delight, carrying the individual towards conditions of danger and suffering. The speaker condemns this behaviour and recognizes the Mind as an adversary that obstructs the purpose for which the spiritual life was undertaken.

The final expression, *mānatthe maṁ niyojayī*, conveys the accusation that the Mind has directed the individual towards vanity, conceit, or self-exaltation, despite having encountered a Teacher who is exceedingly difficult to find.

The Arahanist interpretation understands this passage as illustrating a deeper conflict between the Soul-Spirit and its Mind-Heart. The individual has entered the dispensation of a rare and incomparable Teacher with the aspiration of attaining liberation from worldly entrapment. Yet the Mind continues to pursue worldly gratification and self-centred interests, diverting the individual from that purpose.

The accusation against *Citta* can therefore be understood as an accusation of spiritual betrayal. The Soul-Spirit seeks freedom, but the Mind-Heart repeatedly directs the individual towards the very conditions that perpetuate worldly bondage. Instead of serving the liberation of *Ātmā*, it becomes an unruly agent of worldly attachment.

This interpretation gives particular significance to the need to discipline *Citta*. The practitioner must recognize that the Mind-Heart cannot be allowed to determine the direction of spiritual life according to its accumulated desires and habitual tendencies. It must be brought under control through sustained training, until its activities are aligned with the aspiration for liberation.

The verse also illuminates the importance of *Mano*, the Mind-Will. The wandering Mind must be deliberately redirected. The individual cannot simply follow every inclination arising within *Citta*, expecting that liberation will somehow follow. The Mind-Will must be trained to resist the demands of defilement and maintain its direction towards the Deathless.

**Samādhi and the Discipline of the Mind-Heart**

The purification of *Citta* requires a systematic transformation of the entire Mind-Heart. *Sīla* alone cannot complete this undertaking, because the deeper attachments and tendencies may remain active even when outward conduct has become restrained. Intellectual understanding alone is equally insufficient, because accumulated conditioning can continue to influence the individual\'s thoughts, intentions, and responses.

*Samādhi* is therefore indispensable. Through sustained mental discipline, the practitioner develops the ability to collect, settle, and direct the Mind-Will. The Mind gradually becomes less vulnerable to sensory distractions and habitual impulses. Rather than being continually pulled towards objects of gratification, it becomes increasingly capable of maintaining a deliberate inward direction.

This discipline also concerns the relationship between *Mano* and *Cetanā*. The Mind-Will must learn to direct mental activity towards wholesome and liberating purposes, while Volition must cease generating actions that reinforce attachment and worldly bondage. The practitioner progressively gains mastery over the processes through which thoughts become intentions and intentions become actions.

As *Samādhi* develops, the Mind becomes increasingly stable. Its habitual restlessness diminishes, and its capacity for sustained attention and inward investigation increases. This stability provides the foundation for the deeper development of *Paññā*, through which the underlying causes of defilement can be understood and eradicated.

The objective is not merely to produce temporary tranquillity. The deeper purpose of mental discipline is to overcome the conditions that allow *Citta* to dominate *Ātmā*. The Mind-Heart must be purified at its roots, including the accumulated attachments and tendencies carried from previous existences.

**The Abandonment of Upadhi and the Release from Bondage**

Another important aspect of this purification is the abandonment of *upadhi*, the accumulated basis or conditions of attachment and continued worldly existence.

As long as the individual continues to appropriate and cling to the objects of manifested existence, the Mind-Heart remains bound to them. Desires, attachments, conceit, and deeply rooted tendencies provide the conditions through which worldly bondage continues. The individual repeatedly establishes connections with manifested existence and becomes subject to the consequences of those attachments.

The practitioner must therefore progress beyond the temporary calming of the Mind and overcome the underlying conditions that sustain appropriation and bondage. Through the development of *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, and *Paññā*, the individual progressively abandons the defilements and attachments that constitute the basis of continued worldly existence.

This process is especially significant within the Arahanist understanding of the human condition. The present human Soul-Spirit carries both the inherited burden associated with its hybridized history and the accumulated experiential conditioning of successive lives. These conditions contribute to the strength of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, making the purification of the Mind-Heart a demanding undertaking.

Nevertheless, the inherited condition does not determine the final spiritual destiny of the individual. The predominantly *Manussa* constitution of the human Soul-Spirit preserves the possibility of complete purification. Through the eradication of defilement and the abandonment of the conditions sustaining worldly existence, the individual can overcome the accumulated burden and attain liberation.

The abandonment of *upadhi* is therefore inseparable from the liberation of *Ātmā*. The Soul-Spirit must become free from the accumulated attachments and conditions through which it remains entangled in manifested existence. This requires the complete purification of the Mind-Heart, rather than the temporary suppression of particular desires or mental disturbances.

**The Liberation of Ātmā and the Attainment of the Deathless**

The entire Arahanist liberation path can now be understood through the relationship between *Ātmā* and *Citta*. The Soul-Spirit is the individual whose freedom is sought; the Mind-Heart is the sphere that must be purified; and the disciplined functioning of *Mano* and *Cetanā* provides an essential means through which that purification is undertaken.

The practitioner begins with *Sīla*, establishing sense restraint and purifying bodily and verbal conduct. Through *Samādhi*, the Mind-Will becomes disciplined and the Mind increasingly settled. Through *Paññā*, the underlying conditions of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* are progressively eradicated.

This development leads to *Nibbidā* (disenchantment with worldly existence), *Virāga* (fading of worldly passion), and ultimately *Vimutti* (liberation from worldly bondage). The practitioner no longer pursues manifested existence as a source of lasting satisfaction and gradually abandons the conditions that perpetuate worldly bondage.

The completion of this process is Arahantship: the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. The Mind-Heart has been purified, the underlying tendencies have been overcome, and the conditions sustaining worldly bondage have been abandoned. The Soul-Spirit is no longer held captive by the defilements and accumulated attachments of manifested existence.

Within the wider Arahanist cosmology, this attainment also signifies the restoration of the individual\'s spiritual relationship with the higher *Manussa* heritage and the Brahmā family. The inherited burden that once made the human condition particularly difficult has been overcome through complete purification.

The liberation of *Ātmā* therefore represents the culmination of the entire spiritual undertaking. The Mind-Heart has fulfilled its proper function, the Soul-Spirit has become unbound from the accumulated knots of manifested existence, and the individual has attained the Deathless.

**The purpose of the entire liberation path is the complete purification of the individual: the eradication of defilement, the release of the Soul-Spirit from worldly bondage, and the attainment of Arahantship.**

## IV. The Testimony of the Ancient Elders: Attā and the Inner Spiritual Individual

### 4. Attā and Ajjhatta: The Higher Inner Spiritual Individual

**Dhamma-Anusati** • *The Exalted Recollection of the Dhamma*

**Svākkhāto Bhagavatā Dhammo, Sandiṭṭhiko, Akāliko, Ehipassiko, Opanayiko, Paccattaṃ, Veditabbo Viññūhī\'ti.**

Well-explained *(Svākkhāto)* is the ***Dhamma*** by the Divinely Blessed One (*Bhagavatā*), Completely Seen *(Sandiṭṭhiko),* Timelsss *(Akāliko),* Come and See! (for yourself!) *(Ehipassiko),* Leading inward *(Opanayiko),* **To the Attā (Soul-Spirit)** *(Paccattaṃ),* To be Directly Realized *(Veditabbo),* For One Who Possesses Discernment *(Viññūhi).*

The preceding chapters established the principal concepts of the Arahanist liberation: *Attā*, the individual Soul-Spirit; *Citta*, the Mind-Heart; and *Viññāṇa*, sense-generated consciousness. We now turn to the testimonies of those who undertook Gautama\'s training and attained its ultimate goal.

The Theragāthā and Therīgāthā preserve the voices of elder bhikkhus and bhikkhunīs who describe their renunciation, spiritual discipline, meditative development, and liberation. Their words provide something essential to this investigation: the testimony of practitioners who completed the path. Their verses allow us to examine how the language of *Attā*, *Citta*, *Samādhi*, and inward spiritual development appears within the lived experience of the early renunciant community.

The question before us is direct. When the elders speak of *Attā*, what are they referring to? When they describe the inward direction of spiritual cultivation through *Ajjhatta*, what kind of inward development are they expressing? And how does this language relate to the liberation of the individual from worldly bondage?

Arahanism approaches these questions from the understanding that *Attā* refers, in its deeper spiritual usage, to the Soul-Spirit of the living individual. The five aggregates constitute the conditioned manifestations through which ordinary worldly existence is experienced. The practitioner must understand their nature and relinquish attachment to them. The deeper spiritual task concerns the purification and liberation of the individual Soul-Spirit from the accumulated bondage associated with worldly existence.

The verses of the elders will therefore be considered as testimony to the practical transformation of the individual. We will begin with a particularly important declaration in the Therīgāthā, proceed to the expression *Ajjhattarato* in the Dhammapada, and then examine the relationship between *Attā*, self-examination, refuge, and Samādhi. Finally, we will consider a parallel preserved in the Jain renunciant literature.

### 4.1 Attā in the Language of the Elders

One of the most direct passages for this investigation appears in the Therīgāthā, in the verses attributed to Sirimā.

Theragāthā 2.20 --- Sirimā

*Pare ca naṃ pasaṃsanti, attā ce asamāhito;*\
*Moghaṃ pare pasaṃsanti, attā hi asamāhito.*

*Pare ca naṃ garahanti, attā ce susamāhito;*\
*Moghaṃ pare garahanti, attā hi susamāhito.*

**Arahanist translation:**

Others praise a person when the Soul-Spirit (*Attā*) is not firmly established in Samādhi. Their praise is in vain, for the Soul-Spirit is not firmly established.

Others criticize a person when the Soul-Spirit (*Attā*) is excellently established in Samādhi. Their criticism is in vain, for the Soul-Spirit is excellently established.

This passage deserves particular attention because *Attā* appears directly in relation to *Samāhita*, the state of being collected, composed, and firmly established in *Samādhi*. The verse contrasts two conditions: *Attā ce asamāhito* and *Attā ce susamāhito*. The condition of the individual is presented as the decisive matter, while the opinions of other people are shown to be unreliable measures of spiritual accomplishment.

The Arahanist reading understands *Attā* here as the individual\'s inner Soul-Spirit. Its spiritual condition is determined by its establishment in *Samādhi*, not by public approval, criticism, reputation, or social standing. The verse directs attention away from the judgments of others and towards the actual condition of the spiritual individual.

This is an important distinction. A person may receive praise and recognition while remaining spiritually unestablished. Another may be criticized or misunderstood while possessing profound inner stability. External opinion cannot determine the attainment of liberation. The decisive question is whether the individual has cultivated the inner condition required for freedom from worldly bondage.

The expression *Attā ce susamāhito* is therefore central to the Arahanist investigation. It presents *Attā* in a direct relationship with the attainment of *Samādhi*. The Soul-Spirit must be brought into a condition of profound spiritual stability through the discipline of the liberation path.

A careful reader may observe that *attā* can also function as a reflexive expression meaning oneself or one\'s own person. That ordinary grammatical usage must be acknowledged. However, it should not automatically settle every occurrence of the term, especially where the subject is spiritual cultivation and liberation. Arahanism reads this passage within the wider context of ancient Indian spiritual language, the distinction between the aggregates and *Attā*, and the repeated association of inward cultivation with *Samādhi*.

The interpretation is not based on this stanza in isolation. Its significance becomes clearer when considered alongside the language of *Ajjhattarato*, *Attanā Codayattānaṃ*, and the wider renunciant vocabulary of *Appā* and *Ātmā*.

### 4.2 Ajjhattarato: Taking Delight in the Higher Inner Dimension

A particularly important expression appears in Dhammapada verse 362:

*Hatthasaṃyato pādasaṃyato,*\
*Vācāsaṃyato saṃyatuttamo;*\
*Ajjhattarato samāhito,*\
*Eko santusito tamāhu bhikkhuṃ.*

**Arahanist translation:**

Restrained in the hands, restrained in the feet, restrained in speech, and supremely disciplined; taking delight in the higher inner Soul-Spirit (*Ajjhatta-rato*), established in *Samādhi*, dwelling alone and contented --- such a person they call a bhikkhu.

The verse presents a distinctive picture of the renunciant. His discipline is expressed through bodily restraint, restraint of speech, inward orientation, *Samādhi*, solitude, and contentment. These qualities belong together. The bhikkhu is not merely someone who has adopted an external religious identity. He is one who has undertaken the transformation of his entire existence through renunciant discipline.

The expression *Ajjhatta-rato* is particularly significant. It combines *Ajjhatta* with *Rata*. The latter conveys delight, attachment, or taking pleasure in something. The expression therefore concerns the object or dimension in which the practitioner takes delight.

The Pāli term *Ajjhatta* carries the meaning of inwardness, interiority, or that which pertains to oneself. Its Sanskrit linguistic counterpart is *Adhyātma*. In ordinary usage, these expressions can refer to what is internal or personal. Arahanism gives the expression a more specific spiritual significance when it occurs within the context of renunciation and liberation.

The Arahanist interpretation understands *Ajjhatta* as referring to the deeper inward dimension of the individual associated with *Attā*. The word *higher* expresses this spiritual interpretation: the practitioner progressively turns away from identification with the external and conditioned manifestations of existence and directs attention towards the *higher Soul-Spirit*, or *higher self*.

Thus, *Ajjhattarato* is understood as taking delight in the higher inner *self* or *Soul-Spirit*. This interpretation must be read together with the remaining expressions in the verse. The person described is restrained in body and speech, established in *Samādhi*, solitary, and contented. The inward orientation is therefore inseparable from the practical discipline of renunciation. The practitioner withdraws from the attractions of worldly existence and cultivates a condition of inner stability.

The verse also provides an important connection between *Ajjhatta* and *Samādhi*. The practitioner who takes delight in the inward spiritual dimension is described as *Samāhito*. This association supports the Arahanist understanding that the inward development of the individual is cultivated through concentration and spiritual discipline.

The meaning of *Ajjhatta* should therefore not be reduced to a merely psychological interpretation. In the context of this verse, Arahanism understands the term as pointing towards the deeper spiritual direction of the renunciant life. The bhikkhu turns inward because the liberation path requires a transformation of identification. His attention and spiritual effort must no longer remain absorbed in the changing conditions of worldly existence. He must cultivate the inward stability through which the Soul-Spirit becomes free from worldly bondage.

### 4.3 Attanā Codayattānaṃ: The Soul-Spirit as the Object of Spiritual Examination

Another important passage appears in Dhammapada verse 379:

*Attanā codayattānaṃ,*\
*Paṭimāsetam attanā;*\
*So attagutto satimā,*\
*Sukhaṃ bhikkhu vihāhisi.*

**Arahanist translation:**
