With the Soul-Spirit, one should urge and encourage the Soul-Spirit; one should examine and investigate through the Soul-Spirit. Thus, the bhikkhu, guarded by the Soul-Spirit and endowed with mindfulness, will dwell happily.

The repeated occurrence of *Attanā* and *Attānaṃ* gives this verse particular importance for the Arahanist understanding of spiritual self-examination.

The practitioner is instructed to examine, encourage, and guard the individual through *Attā*. The verse places responsibility for spiritual cultivation within the individual. The practitioner must actively undertake the work of transformation.

The expression *Attagutto* is especially relevant. It describes one who is guarded or protected by *Attā*. In the Arahanist reading, the Soul-Spirit becomes the centre of spiritual protection and discipline. The practitioner must develop the capacity to examine the condition of the individual, recognize the forces that lead towards worldly bondage, and cultivate the qualities that lead towards liberation from worldly bondage.

This teaching is closely related to the distinction established in the preceding chapters between *Citta* and *Attā*. The Mind-Heart is the field in which thoughts, intentions, impressions, and accumulated experiences operate. It must be trained and purified. The Soul-Spirit is the deeper spiritual individual whose liberation is the ultimate concern of the path.

The verse therefore provides a useful foundation for understanding the relationship between spiritual examination and liberation. The practitioner must become capable of directing the inner life towards the goal of freedom. This requires mindfulness, restraint, concentration, and discernment.

The Arahanist rendering of *Attā* as Soul-Spirit is an interpretive choice that should be assessed through the wider textual context. The verse itself repeatedly uses the term, and its language of urging, examining, and guarding provides a significant basis for investigating the deeper spiritual meaning of *Attā*.

Read alongside the declaration *Attā ce susamāhito* and the expression *Ajjhattarato*, the verse contributes to a coherent pattern: the individual must turn inward, cultivate spiritual stability, and guard the inner life through disciplined practice.

### 4.4 Attadīpa and Attasaraṇa: Taking Attā as Island and Refuge

The importance of inward spiritual orientation is also reflected in Gautama\'s instruction preserved in the Mahāparinibbāna Sutta:

*Attadīpā viharatha, attasaraṇā anaññasaraṇā.*

The expression is commonly rendered along the lines of dwelling with oneself as an island and oneself as refuge, without another refuge. Arahanism gives particular attention to the deeper spiritual significance of *Attadīpa* and *Attasaraṇa*.

An island stands above the surrounding waters. It provides stable ground amid the movement of the flood. The image is especially powerful when considered in relation to the recurring descriptions of worldly existence as a flood that carries beings through birth, aging, and death.

Within the Arahanist interpretation, *Attā* is the inner spiritual ground towards which the practitioner must turn. The individual must cultivate the Soul-Spirit as the inward refuge through which worldly bondage is overcome.

This reading is connected with the preceding discussion of *Attā ce susamāhito*. The Soul-Spirit must be firmly established through *Samādhi*. It is this inward spiritual stability that enables the practitioner to remain unshaken by the changing conditions of worldly existence.

The expression *Attadīpa* also brings the discussion into contact with the wider liberation vocabulary of ancient India. The image of crossing a flood, reaching firm ground, guarding the individual, and establishing oneself in *Samādhi* appears in different forms across renunciant traditions.

Arahanism understands these expressions as related aspects of the same spiritual task: the practitioner must cease depending upon worldly conditions as the ultimate ground of security and cultivate the inward condition necessary for liberation from worldly bondage.

This does not mean that the ordinary personality, social identity, or changing psychological condition should be elevated into an ultimate refuge. The Arahanist interpretation concerns the deeper Soul-Spirit, which must be distinguished from the changing manifestations of the five aggregates.

The instruction therefore carries a practical demand. The practitioner must undertake the discipline through which the inward spiritual individual becomes established and guarded. This is achieved through the liberation path, culminating in the complete eradication of *rāga, dosa,* and *moha*.

### 4.5 The Wider Śramaṇa Parallel: Appā Samāhio

The relationship between the Soul-Spirit and Samādhi is also reflected in the Jain renunciant literature. One particularly relevant passage appears in the Uttarādhyayana Sūtra:

**Uttarādhyayana Sūtra 29.16**

*Appā samāhio; appā gutto, sati-rakkhito;*\
*Muni tarai oghaṃ duddaraṃ.*

**Arahanist translation:**

The Soul-Spirit (*Appā*) is established in *Samādhi*; the Soul-Spirit is guarded and protected by mindfulness. The *muni* crosses the difficult flood.

The linguistic relationship between Pāli *Attā* and Prakrit *Appā* has already been discussed in Chapter II. Here, the importance of the passage lies in the association between *Appā*, *Samādhi*, protection, mindfulness, and crossing the flood.

The expression *Appā Samāhio* is especially noteworthy when placed alongside the Pāli expression *Attā ce susamāhito*. Both passages bring the corresponding forms of the ancient Indian term for the spiritual individual into direct association with *Samādhi*.

The Jain passage also describes the *Appā* as *gutto* and *sati-rakkhito*: guarded and protected by mindfulness. The practitioner who cultivates this inward stability is described as crossing the difficult flood.

The parallel is significant for the wider historical and spiritual context of the liberation path. It shows that the relationship between the individual spiritual principle, concentration, mindfulness, and liberation belongs to a broader field of ancient Indian renunciant inquiry.

The Jain understanding of *Jīva*, together with its teachings on karma, spiritual purification, and liberation, reveals a profound alignment with the wider renunciant concern for the spiritual individual and the conditions of worldly bondage. *Jīva* is an ancient term belonging to the Jain spiritual heritage, preserved through its Tīrthaṅkara lineage for thousands of years.

Sanskrit *Ātman*, meanwhile, originates in the Vedic spiritual heritage transmitted by the Āriyan people from the west, and its derived linguistic forms, including Pāli *Attā* and Prakrit *Appā*, developed through the historical evolution of ancient Indo-Aryan languages. As Vedic spiritual knowledge spread and interacted with other ancient Indian traditions, particularly those rooted in yogic discipline, meditation, and renunciant cultivation, its terminology and spiritual understandings became part of a wider landscape of Indian spiritual practice.

The Jain use of *Appā* provides a further linguistic connection with this shared heritage. Different renunciant lineages have preserved their own terminology and modes of expression, yet their teachings can reveal closely aligned understandings of spiritual existence, concentration, purification, the overcoming of worldly bondage, and liberation. The significance of this comparison therefore lies both in the historical relationship of these terms and, more importantly, in the Dhamma and spiritual principles conveyed through their respective teachings.

The Jain passage therefore provides a valuable comparative witness. It demonstrates that the language of an inward spiritual individual established in *Samādhi* was not confined to one textual community.

### 4.6 Ajjhatta: The Establishment of Citta in the Higher Soul-Spirit

One of the most significant textual passages for understanding the relationship between Citta and Ātmā appears in the Aṅguttara Nikāya 4.170, the *Yuganaddha Sutta*. In this discourse, the Venerable Ānanda describes four approaches through which bhikkhus and bhikkhunīs attain Arahantship. The fourth approach is particularly important for the Arahanist understanding of Citta and the Higher Soul-Spirit.

**Aṅguttara Nikāya 4.170 --- Yuganaddha Sutta**

*Puna caparaṁ, āvuso, bhikkhuno dhammuddhaccaviggahitaṁ mānasaṁ hoti. Hoti so, āvuso, samayo yaṁ taṁ cittaṁ ajjhattameva santiṭṭhati sannisīdati ekodi hoti samādhiyati. Tassa maggo sañjāyati. So taṁ maggaṁ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti. Tassa taṁ maggaṁ āsevato bhāvayato bahulīkaroto saṁyojanāni pahīyanti, anusayā byantīhonti.*

**Translation:**

Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu\'s mind (mānas/mano) is shaken/overcome (vigghīta) by turbulence/agitation (uddhacca) concerning the Dhamma (dhamma-uddhacca-vigghītaṁ mānasaṁ hoti). There comes a time, however, when his citta fully establishes itself (santiṭṭhati) in High-Self (Ajjhatta) precisely (eva) (ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati), rests, becomes unified, and enters Samādhi (ekodi hoti samādhiyati). Then the path (maggo) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (saṁyojanāni) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (anusayā) are eliminated.

This is a particularly important passage because it describes a direct transformation in the establishment of Citta. The practitioner initially experiences agitation concerning the Dhamma. Yet a decisive moment arrives when *ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati*: Citta establishes itself precisely in Ajjhatta, the Higher Soul-Spirit.

The sequence of expressions that follows makes the significance of this establishment even clearer:

- *Ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati* --- Citta establishes itself precisely in the Higher Soul-Spirit.

- *Sannisīdati* --- Citta settles and becomes firmly composed.

- *Ekodi hoti* --- Citta becomes unified.

- *Samādhiyati* --- Citta enters Samādhi.

These expressions describe a progressive settling and unification of Citta. The passage does not stop at a momentary inward orientation. It describes Citta becoming established, settled, unified, and concentrated. Immediately thereafter, the discourse declares: *Tassa maggo sañjāyati* --- the path arises in him.

This sequence is central to the Arahanist understanding of Ajjhatta. Citta must change its establishment. Its habitual engagement with external worldly manifestations gives way to a firm establishment upon the Higher Soul-Spirit. This is the decisive reorientation through which the Mind-Heart becomes properly aligned with its innermost Soul-Spirit core.

The importance of *eva* in *ajjhattameva* should also be noted. It gives emphasis to the expression: precisely in Ajjhatta. The passage thus directs attention to the particular establishment of Citta. The Arahanist interpretation understands this as Citta becoming established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit, rather than continuing to be dispersed among external worldly manifestations.

Most importantly, the sutta connects this establishment with the actual development of the path to Arahantship. After the path arises, the practitioner pursues, develops, and cultivates it. The result is explicitly stated: *saṁyojanāni pahīyanti, anusayā byantīhonti* --- the fetters are abandoned and the underlying tendencies are eliminated.

The doctrinal significance is profound. Citta\'s establishment in Ajjhatta is presented within a path that culminates in the eradication of the very conditions that bind the practitioner to worldly existence. In the Arahanist framework, this is the practical relationship between the purification of Citta and the liberation of Ātmā.

This passage also clarifies why the purification of the entire Mind-Heart is indispensable. Citta cannot remain continually agitated by worldly and conceptual movements while attaining the firm spiritual establishment required for liberation. It must become unified and established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit. Through this establishment, Samādhi develops, the path arises, and the fetters and underlying tendencies are eliminated.

The fourth approach in AN 4.170 is therefore a particularly important foundation for the Arahanist teaching of Citta and Ātmā. It explicitly identifies the establishment of Citta in Ajjhatta, followed by unification, Samādhi, the arising of the path, and the abandonment of bondage.

A related expression appears in Dhammapada 362:

*Hatthasaṃyato pādasaṃyato, vācāsaṃyato saṃyatuttamo;*\
*Ajjhattarato samāhito, eko santusito tamāhu bhikkhuṃ.*

**Translation:**

Restrained in hands (hatthasaṃyato), restrained in feet (pādasaṃyato), restrained in speech (vācāsaṃyato), restrained supreme (saṃyatuttamo); taking delight in higher soul-spirit (ajjh-atta-rato), established in samādhi (samāhito), solitary (eko), contented (santusito)---him (taṃ) they call (āhu) a bhikkhu (bhikkhuṃ).

Dhammapada 362 complements the more explicit instruction in AN 4.170. It describes a bhikkhu who is disciplined in bodily conduct and speech, takes delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit, and is established in Samādhi.

The expression *ajjhattarato* identifies the practitioner\'s spiritual orientation: delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit. This is an important characteristic of the renunciant whose source of fulfilment has shifted away from worldly attractions and towards the higher spiritual dimension of existence.

The two passages therefore illuminate complementary aspects of the same Arahanist principle. Dhammapada 362 describes delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit together with Samādhi, while AN 4.170 describes Citta establishing itself precisely in Ajjhatta, becoming unified and entering Samādhi, after which the path to Arahantship arises.

AN 4.170 is particularly decisive because it explicitly identifies Citta as the subject of establishment and directly connects that establishment with the arising and cultivation of the liberating path. The Arahanist interpretation brings this together with the fundamental relationship between Citta and Ātmā: the entire Mind-Heart must become established upon its innermost Soul-Spirit core. This is the spiritual foundation upon which the purification of Citta and the liberation of Ātmā proceed.

### AN 4.170 Yuganaddha Sutta: In Conjunction - The Four Paths Leading to Arahantship 

Thus have I heard (*Evaṁ me sutaṁ*): On one occasion, the Venerable (*Āyasmā*) Ānanda was dwelling at Kosambī in Ghosita's monastery (*Ghositārāme*). There, Venerable Ānanda addressed the bhikkhus: \"Friends, bhikkhus!\" (*\"Āvuso bhikkhave!\"*).

\"Friend!\" (*\"Āvuso!\"*), those bhikkhus replied to Venerable Ānanda. Venerable Ānanda said this:

\"Whoever, friends---whether a bhikkhu or a bhikkhunī---declares the attainment of Arahantship (*arahattappattiṁ*) in my presence, all of them do so through four paths (*catuhi maggehi*), or one among them. What four?

**1. Samatha Preceded by Vipassanā (*Samathapubbaṅgamaṁ Vipassanaṁ*)**

Here, friends, a bhikkhu develops **Vipassanā** preceded by **Samatha** (*Samatha-pubbaṅgamaṁ vipassanaṁ bhāveti*). As he develops **Vipassanā** preceded by **Samatha**, the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**2. Vipassanā Preceded by Samatha (*Vipassanāpubbaṅgamaṁ Samathaṁ*)**

Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu develops **Samatha** preceded by **Vipassanā** (*vipassana-pubbaṅgamaṁ samathaṁ bhāveti*). As he develops **Samatha** preceded by **Vipassanā**, the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**3. Samatha and Vipassanā Yoked Together (*Samathavipassanaṁ Yuganaddhaṁ*)**

Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu develops **Samatha** and **Vipassanā** yoked in conjunction (*samathavipassanaṁ yuganaddhaṁ bhāveti*). As he develops **Samatha** and **Vipassanā** yoked in conjunction, the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**4. Mind Agitated by Dhamma-Restlessness (*Dhammaddhaccavigghītaṁ Mānasaṁ*)**

Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu\'s mind (*mānas/mano*) is shaken/overcome (*Vigghīta*) by turbulence/agitation (*Uddhacca*) concerning the Dhamma (*dhamma-uddhacca-vigghītaṁ mānasaṁ hoti*). There comes a time, however, when his *citta* fully establishes itself (*santiṭṭhati*) in High-Self (***Ajjhatta***) precisely (eva) (*ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati*), rests, becomes unified, and enters Samādhi (*ekodi hoti samādhiyati*). Then the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**Conclusion**

Whoever, friends---whether a bhikkhu or a bhikkhunī---declares the attainment of Arahantship (*arahattappattiṁ*) in my presence, all of them do so through these four paths, or through one among them.\"

Commentary: On the Fourth Path

**1. *Ajjhattameva* =** Ajjhatta + eva

- **Ajjhatta** (*adv. / adj.*): Derived from Sanskrit **adhyātma** (adhi- + ātman).

  - Adhi- = \"high\" / \"higher\" / \"over\" / \"Superior\".

  - -atta (attā / ātman) = \"the self\" / \"soul\" / "soul-spirit".

  - **Meaning:** *higher self*, *soul*, *soul-spirit*, *Internal (higher dimension or the inner domain)* (in direct opposition to *bahiddhā*, external worldly).

- **Eva** (*emphatic particle*): *Only, precisely, strictly, indeed.*

**2. *Cittaṁ***

- **Cittaṁ** (*noun, neuter nominative singular*): Derived from \$\\sqrt{cit}\$ (*to perceive, think, be conscious*).

- **Meaning:** *Mind-heart, consciousness.*

**3. *Santiṭṭhati***

Santiṭṭhati = Saṁ- + ṭhā (tiṭṭhati)

- **Saṁ-** (*prefix*): *Together, completely, fully, thoroughly.*

- **ṭhā / tiṭṭhati** (*root verb*): *To stand, remain, stay, endure.*

- **Santiṭṭhati** (*3rd person singular, present active*): *Stands firm, settles down completely, becomes steady, establishes itself.*

**Structural & Textual Meaning**

**\"\...ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati\...\"**

*\"...the Citta settles down / stands firm precisely in Higher Self/ Soul-spirit precisely\...\"*

**The Dynamics of the Inward Orientation**

**Reversal of Movement:** Prior to this moment, the mind (*manas/mano*) was shaken/overcome (*Vigghīta*) by turbulence/agitation (*Uddhacca*) concerning the Dhamma.

**Inward Establishment (*Ajjhattameva*):** The awareness ceases projecting outward (*bahiddhā*) or grasping at surface mental phenomena (*anattā* aggregates). It turns 180 degrees inward to anchor directly in its Higher Self / Soul-Spirit (*adhi-attā*).

The \"higher self\" or "Soul-Spirit" is the **innermost, higher dimensional, and elevated core of being/existence**---as opposed to external, worldly attachments. It captures the essential truth of AN 4.170: that true breakthrough occurs when awareness ceases wandering outward to worldly existence and establishes itself firmly in the elevated depth, higher dimensional core essence within.

**Firm Standing (*Santiṭṭhati*):** The prefix **saṁ-** emphasizes completeness. The *citta* does not merely pause momentarily; it becomes thoroughly rooted and unshakeable in its inner center, it Soul-Spirit, opening the threshold for the Liberation Path (*maggo*) to be born.

Viewing the higher self or soul/soul-spirit through a **multidimensional structure** provides a clear framework for understanding sutta AN 4.170, why *adhi-attā* (the elevated/higher aspect of the self) functions as a deeply internal realization. When existence is understood across multiple dimensions of density, subtle energy, and awareness, the concept of a \"higher self\" describes the **higher-dimensional part of one\'s own being/existence** that operates beyond the dense, lower three-dimensional plane of physical and mental aggregates.

**1). Multidimensional Self vs. External World**

In many cultures, anything \"higher\" is projected outward into the sky as something external. However, in our multidimensional structural framework:

- **Lower Dimensions (3D / Dense Realm):** The physical body, surface sensory impressions, and the flickering, restless thoughts (*dhammaddhacca*) that react to external conditions (*bahiddhā*).

- **Higher Dimensions (Subtle / Transcendent Realms):** The higher-dimensional core of the spirit/soul (*attā*)---the quiet, luminous, and unshakeable core of human existence.

- **The Integration:** Because these dimensions exist simultaneously, accessing the higher-dimensional self is not an outward journey. It is an **inward descent/ascent** (*adhi-attā* -\> *ajjhatta*) into the deeper, higher-vibrational layers of one\'s own existing consciousness.

**2). *Adhi-attā* in AN 4.170 as Dimensional Shift**

This dimensional perspective illuminates the exact process described in the fourth category of **AN 4.170 (*Yuganaddha Sutta*)**:

a)  **Surface Restlessness (*Dhammaddhaccavigghītaṁ Mānasaṁ*):** The practitioner\'s mind is trapped in lower-dimensional processing---conceptualizing, analyzing, and wrestling with surface Dhamma concepts at the mental/intellectual level.

b)  **Turning Within (*Ajjhattameva*):** The practitioner shifts awareness away from lower 3D external inputs and intellectual chatter, turning directly into the inner, higher-dimensional axis (*adhi-attā*).

c)  **Unification (*Ekodi Hoti Samādhiyati*):** Through ***Samādhi,*** The scattered lower mind aligns and merges with the steady frequency of the higher-dimensional self.

d)  **Birth of the Path (*Maggo Sañjāyati*):** From this higher-dimensional alignment, true vipassanā and liberation from worldly bondage manifest, cutting through lower-dimensional fetters (*saṁyojanāni*).

**3). Guarding of the Higher Self/ Soul-Spirit (*Rakkhitatto*)**

This multidimensional understanding aligns with the imperative in early texts to **guard and protect the self/ Soul-Spirit** (*rakkhitatto*):

- The lower-dimensional aggregates (physical body, fleeting feelings, surface perceptions) are transient, world-bound, and non-self (*anattā*). They belong to the laws of cause, effect, and decay in this physical world (*loka*).

- The **higher-dimensional spirit/soul**, however, must be guarded (*rakkhitatto*) against becoming enticed by or entangled in those lower physical and mental manifestations.

- By disenchanting the lower, world-bound aggregates, the *citta* transcends the lower dimensions entirely, passing through the threshold of Brahma Pure Abode realm into the deathless realm of **Nibbāna**.

***Adhi-* as Both \"Higher\" and \"Inward\"**

If one views the *attā* (the core self/spirit) as something that must be protected, guarded (*rakkhitatto*), and purified from external defilements (*rāga, dosa, moha*), then turning the awareness **inward** (*adhi-attā* -\> *ajjhatta*) is literally elevating the mind away from low, worldly, external conditions toward its highest, uncorrupted state.

Understanding the self as a multidimensional continuum resolves any false conflict between \"internal\" and \"higher self\" (*ajjhatta* vs. *adhi-attā*). The higher self is not a separate external entity; it is the **higher-frequency, higher-dimensional anchor of our own spirit**, reached precisely by turning completely inward beyond the lower, dense surface mind.

In deep meditative practice, turning inward (*ajjhatta*) is not merely looking at biological organs or mundane thoughts; it is going deeper than the superficial, scattered, and restless surface mind (*dhammaddhacca*).

When the *citta* settles **internally to the Higher Self / Soul-Spirit** (*ajjhattameva*):

- It withdraws from external distraction (*bahiddhā*).

- It moves inward to a unified, quiet, and *samādhi* state (*ekodi hoti samādhiyati*).

- In modern spiritual language, going \"deep within\" to find that quiet center of *samādhi* and awareness is precisely what people mean when they say contacting or realizing their **\"higher self.\"**

#### Bāhiya Sutta (Udāna 1.10) 

is the most famous example in the entire Pāḷi Canon of the **4th path described in the Yuganaddha Sutta (AN 4.170)**---where a practitioner whose mind is stirred/agitated suddenly turns completely inward (*ajjhattameva*), aligns with the higher self / soul-spirit (*adhi-attā*), and attains immediate Arahantship. Here is how the Bāhiya Sutta connects directly to this dimensional breakthrough and liberation:

**1. The Agitation and Immediate Turning Within**

Bāhiya Dārucīriya was desperately seeking true liberation. After traveling across India without rest, he rushed up to the Buddha in Savatthī while the Buddha was walking for alms. Bāhiya repeatedly begged for instruction on the spot.

His mind was intensely urgent---a prime example of **dhammaddhacca** (spiritual urgency/restlessness regarding the Dhamma).

The Buddha gave him the concise instruction on sense-perception: *\"Diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattaṁ bhavissati, sute sutamattaṁ bhavissati, mute mutamattaṁ bhavissati, viññāte viññātamattaṁ bhavissati\...\"*

*\"In what is seen, there will be merely the seen; in the heard, merely the heard; in the sensed, merely the sensed; in the cognized, merely the cognized\...\"*

**2. Disentangling the Higher-Dimensional Spirit from Lower Aggregates**

The core of the Buddha\'s teaching to Bāhiya is the absolute separation between lower-dimensional, worldly phenomena and the true, guarded soul-spirit: *\"Tato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tena. Yato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tena, tato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tattha. Yato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tattha, tato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, nevīdha na huraṁ na ubhayantare. Esevanto dukkhassā'ti.\"*

*\"When, Bāhiya, for you there is merely the seen in the seen, merely the heard in the heard\... then, Bāhiya, **you are not \'by that\' (na tena)**. When you are not \'by that\' (na tena), **you are not \'in that\' (na tattha)**. When you are not \'in that\' (na tattha), then **you are neither here, in this current life/realm (na idha), nor next,** **in the next existence or hereafter (na huraṁ), nor in between the two (na ubhayantare)**. Just this is the end (anto) of dukkha.\"*

**The Dimensional Shift:**

- **The Worldly Aggregates (*Anattā*):** The sights, sounds, thoughts, and sensory processes belong to this physical world (*loka*). They are transient, subject to cause and effect, and are not our soul-spirit, nor part of our soul-spirit, nor belonging to our soul-spirit.

- **Not \'In That\' *(na tattha)*/ Not \'By That\' *(na tena)*:** Bāhiya is instructed to immediately stop identifying his true spirit (*attā*) with these lower-dimensional sensory inputs.

- **Neither Here, Nor Next (*Nevīdha na huraṁ*):** The purified *Citta* drops all spatial/dimensional location within the physical or mental universe. It is no longer bound \"here\" (in this current life/realm) or \"next\" (in the next existence or hereafter, next reincarnation).

**3. Immediate Realization of Arahantship**

Upon hearing this brief instruction, Bāhiya's mind was instantly freed from all taints right on the spot without remaining clinging (*anupādāya āsavehi cittaṁ vimucci*). He attained complete Arahantship in a moment.

Because he completely severed attachment to the lower-dimensional aggregates, when he was killed shortly after by a cow with a young calf, his final physical body dissolved.

The Buddha then uttered the solemn solemnity (*udāna*) regarding Bāhiya\'s ultimate transcendence: *\"Yattha āpo ca pathavī, tejo vāyo na gādhati\... Tadaññāāya munino, monena brāhmaṇo; Rūpā rūpā ca muccati, dukkhā muccati sabbaso.\"*

*\"Where water (āpo), earth (pathavī), fire (tejo), and air (vāyo) find no footing (na gādhati)\... Having fully known this (tadaññāya) for himself, the muni, the true Brāhmaṇa through pure inner awareness (monena); From form (rūpa) and the formless (arūpa) he is completely freed (muccati); from dukkha he is freed in every way (sabbaso)."*

***mona*** is **ñāṇa**: *higher direct knowledge*, **paññā:** *wisdom*, and **vijjā:** *transcendent vision*.

**Munino:** For the *muni* who has quieted the restless lower mind.

**Monena:** Through **pure inner awareness / silent sagehood**---not merely because he stopped talking, but because his *Citta* turned completely inward (*ajjhattameva*), disengaged from the four elements (*āpo, pathavī, tejo, vāyo*), and stood firm in its unconditioned core.

*Rūpā Arūpā Ca Muccati* **\"Freed from Form and the Formless\"**: The Arahant\'s *Citta* drops all clinging to both physical/material planes (*rūpa-loka*) and subtle non-material planes (*arūpa-loka*).

This verse highlights the final threshold of an Arahant - **Exiting the Universe**:

1.  **Transcending the Four Elements:** Physical manifestations and material aggregates (*anattā*) are built entirely of the four elements (*pathavī, āpo, tejo, vāyo*). When the Buddha states that these elements *find no footing* (*na gādhati*), it signifies that the unconditioned core of *Citta* has completely disengaged from material density.

2.  **Beyond Form and Formless:** By transcending both *rūpa* (dense 3D material worlds) and *arūpa* (subtle higher worlds), the Arahant is no longer bound to any realm within the law of death and rebirth (*saṁsāra*).

#### Ajjhatta in Jain Tradition

In Jain literature, both the term **adhyātma** (Prakrit: *ajjhattha*) and the title **Arhat / Arihant** (Prakrit: *Arahanta*) occupy a central position. Examining how Jainism treats these concepts reveals profound structural parallels---as well as important doctrinal differences.

**1. *Adhyātma* in Jain Literature: The Internal Science of the Soul**

In Jainism, ***adhyātma*** (*adhi* + *ātman*) literally means **\"pertaining to the ultimate inner Soul/Self (*jīva* or *ātman*)\"**. It forms an entire branch of Jain philosophy and mysticism known as **Adhyātma-śāstra** (the discipline of inward realization of Higher Self/Soul).

**A. The Core Meaning: Turning Inward Away from *Bahir-ātman***

Jain texts explicitly divide the human experience into three progressive stages of the self (most famously in Ācārya Kundakunda\'s *Samayasāra* and Śubhacandra\'s *Jñānārṇava*), division of the soul into **Bahir-ātman**, **Antar-ātman**, and **Param-ātman** is systematically laid out in Acharya Pujyapada's *Samādhitantra* (5th century CE) and elaborated in Acharya Yogindu's *Paramātmaprakāśa* (6th century CE) and Śubhacandra's *Jñānārṇava* (11th century CE).

**Acharya Pujyapada, *Samādhitantra* (Verse 4)**: Bahir-antaḥ paraś-ca iti tridhā-ātmā sarva-dehiṣu, Bahir-ātmā śarīrādau jātātma-bhrāntir, Āntaraḥ citta-doṣātma-vibhrāntiḥ, Paramātmeti kīrtyate.

In all embodied beings (*sarva-dehiṣu*), the self (*ātmā*) is threefold (*tridhā*): external (*bahir*), internal (*antaḥ*), and supreme (*paraś-ca iti*). The external self (*bahir-ātmā*) is the misconception of selfhood (*jātātma-bhrāntir*) regarding the physical body and external objects (*śarīrādau*). The internal self (*āntaraḥ*) is \[the state free from\] the delusion of selfhood (*ātma-vibhrāntiḥ*) regarding mental defilements (*citta-doṣa*); \[the third\] is proclaimed as the supreme self (*paramātmeti kīrtyate*).

**Acharya Pujyapada, *Samādhitantra* (Verse 7)**: *Bahir-ātmā samūḍhātmā, Kalevaram ahetukam swam eva abhimanyeta, Swa-para-avedakaḥ.*

The external self (*bahir-ātmā*) is a deeply deluded self (*samūḍhātmā*). Through illusion (*bhrāntyā*), lacking knowledge (*avedakaḥ*) of what belong to oneself (*swa*) and external/foreign (*para*), it falsely considers (*abhimanyeta*) the transient (*Ahetukam*) physical body (*kalevaram*) to be verily (*eva*) its own self (*swam*).

**Ācārya Kundakunda, Samayasāra (Gāthā 186)**: *aham edam udo cedi ya ajjhavasāṇaṁ karei jo jīvo \| so parabhāvaṁ ṇecchai ṇiyattade so a aṇṇāṇā \|\|*

Which soul (*jo jīvo*) makes the mental delusion (*ajjhavasāṇaṁ karei*) that \'I am this, this is mine\' (*aham edam udo cedi ya*)\... That soul does not desire foreign modifications (*so parabhāvaṁ ṇecchai*), and that one turns away (*so a ṇiyattade*) from ignorance (*aṇṇāṇā*).

**Ācārya Yogindu Deva, *Paramātmaprakāśa* (Gāthā 11):** *ṇimmala-ṇāṇa-sahāu jiu kamma-malehiṁ vimukku \| so paramappā ṇicchaeṇa jo samāhihiṁ ṭhiu \|\|*

The soul (jiu) whose inherent nature is spotless, pure direct knowledge (ṇimmala-ṇāṇa-sahāu), completely freed from karmic stains (kamma-malehiṁ vimukku)\... Which stands firm in Samādhi (jo samāhihiṁ ṭhiu), that one, in absolute reality (so ṇicchaeṇa), is the supreme ***ātma*** (paramappā).

**Ācārya Yogindu Deva, *Yogasāra* (Verse 1):** *jiu ṇimmalu ṇāṇa-sahāu jiu kamma-malehiṁ vimukku \| appi-chahi jo maṇu dharai so bohiya paramappā \|\|*

The soul (*jiu*) whose inherent nature is spotless, pure direct knowledge (*ṇimmalu ṇāṇa-sahāu jiu*), and completely freed from karmic stains (*kamma-malehiṁ vimukku*)\... Whosoever holds their mind (*jo maṇu dharai*) in desireless contemplation of that Self / Soul ***ātma*** (*appi-chahi*), that awakened one knows the supreme ***ātma*** (*so bohiya paramappā*).

**B. Major Jain Texts Dedicated to *Adhyātma***

**Ācārya Haribhadra Sūri, Yogabindu (Verse 31):** *adhyātmaṁ bhāvanā dhyānaṁ samatā vṛttisaṁkṣayaḥ \| mokṣeṇa yojanādyogo eṣa śreṣṭho yathottaram \|\|*

The Higher Self / Soul (*adhyātmaṁ*), worldly manifestation / becoming (*bhāvanā*), Jhana meditation (*dhyānaṁ*), equanimity (*samatā*), and the complete destruction of mental fluctuations (*vṛtti-saṁkṣayaḥ*)--- From joining (*yojanāt*) with liberation (*mokṣeṇa*), this (*eṣa*) is yoga (*yogaḥ*); each successive stage is superior (*śreṣṭho yathottaram*) to the preceding one.

- **Upādhyāya Yaśovijaya (17th century CE):** Authored the famous **Adhyātmasāra** (\"The Essence of Adhyātma\"), where *adhyātma* is defined as the internal purification of the mind (*manaḥśuddhi*) and direct experience (*anubhava*) of the pure self.

**Mahāpadhyāya Yaśovijaya Gaṇi, *Adhyātmasāra* (1.1--4):** *ātmānam adhikṛtyaiṣā pravṛttir adhyātmam ucyate \| manaḥśuddhyādikā sā ca jñeyā samyag-vimuktaye \|\| ragādimatamattyāgaḥ śuddhir mana ity ucyate \| sā caiva svātmano bhāvaḥ śuddha-saṁvittilakṣaṇaḥ \|\| cittair viśuddhair manaso virago, virāgataḥ syād anubhūtir ātmanaḥ \| tato bhavet kevala-bodha-lakṣmiḥ, sa caiva mokṣaḥ sa sukhaṁ tad eva \|\| anubhavasi yadātvam ātmānaṁ svatantraṁ, parama-pada-samīsthaṁ śuddha-bodha-svarūpam \| tada iha sakala-duḥkhaiḥ mucyase saṁsārāt, labhase ca nija-mūlaṁ śāśvataṁ brahma-dhāma \|\|*

This (*eṣā*) directed activity (*pravṛttir*) having taken the Soul / *ātmā* (*ātmānam*) as its supreme subject (*adhikṛtya*) is declared as the Higher Self / Soul (*adhyātmam ucyate*). And (*ca*) that (*sā*), having mind-purification as its primary quality (*manaḥ-śuddhy-ādikā*), must be understood (*jñeyā*) for complete, right liberation (*samyag-vimuktaye*).

The complete abandonment of the intoxicated conviction of attachment and related defilements (*rāgādi-mata-māt-tyāgaḥ*) is declared as the purification of the mind (*śuddhir manasa iti ucyate*). And that \[purification\] is verily (*sā caiva*) the intrinsic state of one\'s own Self / Soul (*svātmano bhāvaḥ*), defined by pure, direct consciousness (*śuddha-saṁvitti-lakṣaṇaḥ*).

Through fully purified minds (*cittair viśuddhair*), detachment of the mind (*manaso virāgo*) \[occurs\]; From detachment (*virāgataḥ*), the direct experience (*anubhūtir*) of the Soul (*ātmanaḥ*) arises (*syād*). From that (*tato*), the splendor (*lakṣmiḥ*) of omniscience knowledge (*kevala-bodha*) arises (*bhavet*); and that verily is liberation (*sa caiva mokṣaḥ*), that precisely is ultimate bliss (*sa sukhaṁ tad eva*).

When (*yadā*) you (*tvam*) directly experience (*anubhavasi*) the Soul (*ātmānaṁ*) as self-dependent (*svatantraṁ*), situated at the supreme state (*parama-pada-samīsthaṁ*), whose intrinsic form is pure direct knowledge (*śuddha-bodha-svarūpam*)\... Then (*tadā*), here (*iha*), from all sufferings (*sakala-duḥkhaiḥ*) and from *saṁsāra* (*saṁsārāt*) you are released (*mucyase*), and (*ca*) you attain (*labhase*) one\'s own primordial root (*nija-mūlaṁ*), the eternal/deathless (*śāśvataṁ*) *brahma-dhāma*.

- **Acharya Pujyapada & Yogindu Dev:** Works like *Samādhitantra* and *Yogasāra* frame *adhyātma* as the direct contemplation of the uncorrupted soul, severing the bond with karmic particles.

**Ācārya Pūjyapāda, *Samādhitantra* (Verse 14):** *ātmānam ātmany evābhyasya samyag-jñāna-mayena hi \| prāpnoty ātmani samyag-jñānaṁ karmāṇi kṣapayaty api \|\|*

Having repeatedly contemplated (*abhyasya*) the Soul (*ātmānam*) in the Soul (*ātmani*) precisely (*eva*) through \[a mind\] consisting of right direct knowledge (*samyag-jñāna-mayena*), indeed (*hi*)\... One attains (*prāpnoti*) right direct knowledge (*samyag-jñānaṁ*) in the Soul (*ātmani*), and also (*api*) exhausts / severs (*kṣapayati*) the karmic particles (*karmāṇi*).

**(Verse 26-27)** Pūjyapāda details how contemplating the soul as uncorrupted and distinct from body/karma acts as the cutting tool that severs the karmic bond.

*dehāntara-gatenāpi dehe \'smin nātmanā saha \| ekatvaṁ bhāvyate \'yuktāiḥ kṣīrodakavat ātmanaḥ \|\| pṛthag-bhāvaṁ tu sañcintya deho \'yaṁ nātmanā saha \| dhyāyed ātmānam evāgre karma-bandha-vinirmokṣāya \|\|*

But (*tu*) having deeply contemplated (*sañcintya*) the distinct nature (*pṛthag-bhāvaṁ*)---that this physical body (*deho \'yaṁ*) is not (*na*) together with (*saha*) the Soul (*ātmanā*)\... One should principally meditate (*dhyāyed*) upon the Soul (*ātmānam*) alone (*eva*), for the complete severance (*vinirmokṣāya*) from the bond of karmic particles (*karma-bandha*).

**Ācārya Yogindu Deva, *Yogasāra* (Verse 12):** *kamma-malehiṁ vimukku jiu ṇaü saṁsāre hoi \| appa-jhaṇeṇaṁ jo ṭhiu kammaṁ ḍahai so i \|\|*

The soul (*jiu*) completely freed (*vimukku*) from karmic stains (*kamma-malehiṁ*) is not (*ṇaü*) in *saṁsāra* (*saṁsāre hoi*). Which \[soul\] stands firm (*jo ṭhiu*) through meditation on the *ātman* (*appa-jhaṇeṇaṁ*), that one verily (*so i*) burns up / severs (*ḍahai*) karmic matter (*kammaṁ*).

**2. Does *Adhyātma* Lead to \"Arahantship\" for Jains?**

**Yes, directly.** In Jainism, establishing oneself in *adhyātma* (turning inward away from material aggregates) is the exact path that leads to becoming an **Arihant** (Pāḷi/Prakrit: *Arahanta*; Sanskrit: *Arhat*).

However, the Jain definition of an **Arihant / Arahant** has its own precise technical framework:

**A. The Definition of a Jain Arihant**

In Jainism, an **Arihant** (literally translated traditionally as *\"destroyer of the inner enemies\"*---namely *krodha* \[anger\], *māna* \[pride\], *māyā* \[deceit\], and *lobha* \[greed\]) is a living human being who has attained **Kevala Jñāna** (omniscience/infinite direct awareness/knowledge).

- In the sacred Jain **Ṇamōkāra Mantra**, the very first bow is offered to the Arihants:

Ṇamō Arihantāṇaṁ \"I bow to the Liberated Worthy Ones\"

- An Arihant has destroyed the four destructive karmas (*ghātiyā karmas*) through total internal absorption in *ātma*.

**Ācārya Kundakunda, *Niyamasāra* (Gāthā 159):** *jo jhādi paramappāṇiṁ ghādi-kamma-kkhayaṁ kareṁti so hodi \| kevali-aṇaṁta-ṇāṇī aṇaṁta-suha-daṁsaṇo jīvo \|\|*

Which soul (*jo jīvo*) meditates upon (*jhādi*) the supreme Soul/*ātman* (*paramappāṇiṁ*), that one performs (*kareṁti*) the complete destruction of the destructive karmas (*ghādi-kamma-kkhayaṁ*)\... That soul becomes (*so hodi*) an omniscient one (*kevali*), possessing infinite direct knowledge (*aṇaṁta-ṇāṇī*), infinite bliss, and infinite direct perception (*aṇaṁta-suha-daṁsaṇo*).

**B. The Two Stages of Final Liberation in Jainism**

While an Arihant is fully liberated from inner defilements, Jainism distinguishes between two final states:

1.  **Arihant / Kevalin (Embodied Liberation):** The Arahant remains in a physical body, teaching the path until their remaining non-destructive physical karmas (*aghātiyā karmas*) exhaust naturally.

2.  **Siddha (Disembodied / Ultimate Transcendence):** When the physical body dissolves at death, the Arahant sheds the final physical frame and ascends to the **Siddhashila** (the top of the universe) as a disembodied, pure, eternal soul (*Siddha*).

### 4.7 What the Testimonies Reveal About Attā

#### Theragāthā 2.18. Kimbila (2nd)

**Pācīnavaṁsadāyamhi, sakyaputtā sahāyakā;\
Pahāyānappake bhoge, uñchāpattāgate ratā.**

**Āraddhavīriyā pahitattā, Niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā;\
Ramanti dhammaratiyā, Hitvāna lokiyaṁ ratin.**

*In the East Bamboo Park (Pācīnavaṁsadāya), the Sakyan sons, spiritual companions (sakyaputtā sahāyakā), Having abandoned no small amount of wealth and possessions (pahāya anappake bhoge), take delight in food gathered bit by bit into the bowl (uñchā-pattāgate ratā). Aroused in spiritual energy (āraddha-vīriyā), intent toward the attā (soul-spirit) (pahita-attā), constantly possessing unshakeable, relentless striving (niccaṁ daḷha-parakkamā); They delight in the spiritual joy of the Dhamma (ramanti dhamma-ratiyā), having discarded all worldly delight (hitvāna lokiyaṁ ratin).*

The Sakyan companions are characterized by three compounding terms of intense physical and mental exertion:

a)  **Āraddha-vīriyā**: Spiritual energy aroused to the highest pitch, leaving no room for sloth or complacency.

b)  **Pahit-attā**: Intent entirely set on the Soul-Spirit **attā**.

c)  **Niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā**: Unceasing, firm, unshakeable striving.

This level of effort is required because the goal of the renunciant is not peaceful worldly co-existence or psychological adjustment, but the complete termination of physical reincarnation.

#### Theragāthā 1.5. Dabba

**Yo duddamiyo damena danto, Dabbo santusito vitiṇṇakaṅkho;\
Vijitāvī apetabheravo hi, Dabbo so parinibbuto ṭhitātto.**

*He who (yo) was difficult to tame (duddamiyo), disciplined (danto) through self-control (damena); Dabba \[the Capable One\] (dabbo), fully contented (santusito), having crossed over (vitiṇṇa) doubt (kaṅkho); A conqueror (vijitāvī), free from fear (apetabheravo) indeed (hi); That (so) Capable One (dabbo), completely unbound (parinibbuto), is steadfast (ṭhita) in the Soul-Spirit (attā).*

**Completely Unbound from the World, Established in the Soul-Spirit (*Attā / Ātmā*) (*Parinibbuto Ṭhitatto*):**

**(1). Parinibbuto: Completely Unbound from the world / Cessation of Worldly Becoming**

**Pari- (Complete / Fully):** Denotes total completion with no remaining trace. **Nibbuto** literally means **unbound**, **unfettered**, or **disentangled**.

**Stop Arising from the World / Worldly Becoming:** *Parinibbuto* means that the process of worldly becoming (*bhava*) has been completely halted. Because the roots of worldly craving (*taṇhā*), *rāga (worldly carving and attachment)*, *dosa (survival instincts manifested as fear, anger, aversion, violence and hatred)*, and *moha (delusion and ignorance, not knowing the liberation from worldly bondage)* have been starved through fading off worldly passion (*virāga*), no new fuel is added. The momentum of birth in the world, worldly rising, comes to a total stop.

**Completely Unbound from the World:** The soul-spirit (*attā*) is thoroughly unbound from the cords of worldly existence. It is free from worldly bondage and yoke, completely liberated from the universe, is freed from worldly trap.

**(2). Ṭhitatto: Established in the Soul-Spirit (*Attā / Ātmā*)**

**Ṭhita (Sanskrit: *Sthita*)** literally means *\"to stand, to place, to be established, to abide.\"*

**Ṭhitatto: Internal Centering**

**External Centering:** The average person anchors their life, attention, and identity externally in worldly things. This is the cause of *dukkha*. Rather than chasing external, worldly phenomena that belong to the world (*loka*), the practitioner disenchants them, leaves them behind, and grounds their life completely on the soul-spirit (*attā*).

**Internal Centering (Ṭhitatto):** **Ṭhitatto** means one shifts the entire locus of life away from the external world and centers it internally upon the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*). To cultivate and develop the mind-heart *citta* which envelops the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*) using Gautama's teachings (*dhamma*).

**(3). The Unified Reality: Parinibbuto Ṭhitatto**

**The Negative Operation (*Parinibbuto*):** The total unbinding from the world (*loka*) and the complete stopping of the arising of birth in this world, no more another becoming/life in this world.

**The Positive Realization (*Ṭhitatto*):** The state of being permanently established, rooted, and stationary in the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

Together, **Parinibbuto Ṭhitatto** describes the Arahant who has ceased all worldly becoming, completely unbound the soul-spirit from the matrix of worldly *saṃsāra*, and stood firm in ultimate, world-transcending freedom.

**Two Sides of One Transcendent Realization:** *Parinibbuto* describes what has been cast off and extinguished (worldly pollution of the soul-spirit and suffering-aging and death with the world), while *ṭhitatto* describes the resulting internal immovable abide with the soul-spirit, the true self (*attā/ātmā*). A practitioner who is **parinibbuto ṭhitatto** has fulfilled the *Brahmacariya*. Upon the dissolution of their final body, the purified *citta* enters the **Pure Abodes Brahma heavens and the** **Nibbāna-dhātu**.

#### Theragāthā 1.6. Sītavaniya

**Yo sītavanaṁ upagā bhikkhu, Eko santusito** **samāhitatto;\
Vijitāvī apetalomahaṁso, Rakkhaṁ kāyagatāsatiṁ dhitimā ti.**

*That bhikkhu who has gone to the Cool/* *peaceful Forest (Sītavana), Solitary/* *alone (Eko), completely content (Santusito), centered internally in Samādhi upon the soul-spirit (attā) (samāhitatto); A conqueror (Vijitāvī), fearless (Apetaloma-haṁso), Guarding (Rakkha) mindfulness directed to the body (Kāyagatāsati), resolute/* *fortitude (Dhitimā).*

**Commentary**: **Qualities of Bhikkhu with Internal Mastery**

**Sītavanaṁ:** *Sīta* (cool / peaceful) + *vana* (forest). Specifically refers to the Sītavana (Cool Grove/Charnel Ground near Rājagaha).

**Eko:** *Solitary, alone, single-minded.*

**Santusito:** *Sṁ-* (completely) + *tuṣ* (content/satisfied) -\> *thoroughly content, needing no external worldly stimulation.*

**Samāhitatto:** One who centers life not on external worldly phenomena, but internally, collected in Concentration (Samādhi) upon the *attā/ātmā*.

- *Samāhita:* Collected, centered in Samādhi (Concentration).

- *Atto / Attā:* Soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

**Vijitāvī:** a conqueror who has overcome all worldly attachments.

**Apetaloma-haṁso:** One from whom hair-raising terror and fear have completely departed; utterly fearless in the desolate charnel grounds. *Apeta:* Gone away, departed, eradicated. *Loma:* Body hair. *Haṁsa:* standing on end (goosebumps from fear or dread).

**Rakkhaṁ:** *Guarding, protecting, sustaining*.

**Kāyagatāsatiṁ:** *Kāya* (body) + *gatā* (directed to / gone into) + *sati* (mindfulness) -\> *mindfulness directed to the body, leads to **Samādhi**.*

**Dhitimā:** *Dhiti* (firmness, fortitude, resolve) + *-mant* (possessing) -\> *steadfastly resolute, endowed with unshakeable spiritual strength.*

#### Theragāthā 1.7. Bhalliya

**Yopānudī maccurājassa senaṁ, Naḷasetuṁva sudubbalaṁ mahogho;\
Vijitāvī apetabheravo hi, Danto so parinibbuto ṭhitatto ti.**

*He who has dispelled the army of the King of Death (Maccurājassa), like a mighty flood sweeps away a fragile bridge of reeds; A conqueror (Vijitāvī), free from fear and terror (Apetabheravo) indeed (hi),* *Tamed (Danto), he is completely unbound (Parinibbuto), centering life internally on the soul-spirit (attā) (Ṭhitatto).*

**Commentary**: The Kinetic, Uncompromising Force of the Noble Path

The army of Death (*Māra*) controls beings only as long as they are attached to the worldly (*loka*). By becoming **danto** (tamed through internal discipline and concentration), one is like a mighty flood (*mahogho*), **Death\'s army is flimsy like dried reeds.** It relies entirely on the delusion (*moha*) of the unpurified mind. The moment the disciple centers life internally on the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*), Death loses its hold completely.

**The practitioner is the mighty flood.** The momentum of the noble path---fueled by **Appamattā** (zeal) and **Samādhi**---sweeps over the weak structures of *saṃsāra* without being stopped.

Having stopped the arising of future becoming, the Arahant is **parinibbuto** (completely unbound from the world) and **ṭhitatto**---living centered firmly and permanently on the true self, the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

***Apānudī**:* *Apa-* (away) + *nud* (to push, strike, drive off, dispel) -\> *pushed away, drove off, destroyed.*

**Maccurājassa:** *Maccu* (death / King Māra) + *rāja* (king) in the genitive case -\> *of the King of Death.*

**Senaṁ:** *Army, host, forces* (accusative singular). The army of Death consists of the defilements (*kilesa*): craving, aversion, delusion, fear, and attachment to the worldly (*loka*).

**Mahogho (Maha + ogha):** *Maha* (great/mighty) + *ogha* (flood, torrent) -\> *a mighty, overwhelming flood. Metaphor:* Just as a massive flash flood effortlessly smashes and sweeps away a flimsy bridge made of dried reeds, the practitioner\'s power of wisdom and concentration completely obliterates the forces of Māra/Death.

**Vijitāvī:** *Vi-* (intense) + *ji* (to conquer) -\> *a conqueror over all worldly attachments and defilements.*

**Apetabheravo:** *Apeta* (departed, eradicated) + *bherava* (terror, fear, dread) -\> *one from whom all fear and terror have completely departed.* Fear (*dosa*) vanishes because the mind no longer clings to external worldly phenomena that are subject to decay and death.

**Danto:** Past participle of *dam -\>* *tamed, subdued, disciplined through internal restraint.*

**Parinibbuto:** *Pari-* (completely) + **nibbuto** -\> *completely unbound from the world, stopping the arising and becoming of worldly birth.*

**Ṭhitatto (Ṭhita + Attā/Ātmā):** One who centers life not on external worldly phenomena, but internally on the soul-spirit (*attā/ātmā*).

- *Ṭhita:* Established, firmly rooted, no longer swayed.

- *Attā / Ātmā:* Soul-spirit.

#### Theragāthā 1.8. Vīra

**Yo duddamiyo damena danto, Vīro santusito vitiṇṇakaṅkho;\
Vijitāvī apetalomahaṁso, Vīro so parinibbuto ṭhitatto ti.**

*He who (yo) was difficult to tame (duddamiyo), disciplined (**danto**) through self-control (**damena**); A hero (**vīro**), fully content (**santusito**), having crossed over doubt (**vitiṇṇa-kaṅkho**); A conqueror (**vijitāvī**), **fearless** (apetalomahaṁso); That (so) hero (vīro) \[is\] completely unbound (**parinibbuto**), centering life internally on the soul-spirit (**Attā / Ātmā**) (ṭhitatto).*

#### Theragāthā 1.19. Kula

**Udakañhi nayanti nettikā, Usukārā namayanti tejanaṁ;\
Dāruṁ namayanti tacchakā, Attānaṁ damayanti subbatā ti.**

*Irrigators/ canal-builders (nettikā) indeed (hi) lead/guide (nayanti) water (udakaṁ); arrow-makers (usukārā) bend/ straighten/ shape (namayanti) the arrow (tejanaṁ); Carpenters (tacchakā) bend / straighten/ shape (namayanti) the wood (dāruṁ); Those of good vows / precepts (Sīla and Damana) (subbatā) tame / discipline / train (damayanti) the **soul-spirit ātmā/attā** (attānaṁ).*

**Commentary**: Taming the mind-heart **Citta**, purifying the **soul-spirit *ātmā/attā***

The verse contrasts three external crafts with internal spiritual training of the **soul-spirit *ātmā/attā***. Irrigators channel water. Arrow-makers heat and bend crooked shafts to make straight arrows. Carpenters carve and shape rigid timber. These three craftsmen take physical material/form and shape them for certain purpose through skill and effort.

**Subbatā** (Pali), Sanskrit **subrata** (**suvrata**).

- **Su-** (Prefix): Derived from Proto-Indo-European *\*su-:* \"good\", \"well\", \"virtuous,\" \"proper,\" or \"thorough.\"

- **-vrata** (Noun): Derived from the Sanskrit root **vṛ** (Sanskrit: *वृ*), meaning \"to choose,\" \"to select,\" \"to decide,\" or \"to cover/restrain.\"

  In classical Sanskrit and Pali, *vrata* (*vata*) evolved to mean a religious vow, a holy practice, a self-imposed observance, or a moral duty.

- **-ā** (Inflection): Nominative plural masculine termination (*subvataiḥ* / *subvatā*), meaning \"those who possess good vows\" or \"those who are dedicated to virtuous observances.\"

**suvrata** denotes one who is well-disciplined, docile to spiritual training, steadfast in sacred vows, or devoted to an ordered, holy lifestyle (*Brahmacariya*).

**suvrata** (सुव्रत) is ancient, appearing directly in the earliest **Ṛgveda**. Its usage in the Vedic context illuminates how the concept evolved from divine order to spiritual self-mastery.

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Vedic Text**      **Context**                  **Vedic Meaning & Application**
  ------------------- ---------------------------- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Ṛgveda 1.83.1**   *Suvratásya* (Genitive)      Associated with the divine order (*Ṛta*). Refers to divine beings (like Indra or Varuṇa) or sacrificers who strictly adhere to the established order and laws.

  **Ṛgveda 3.60.3**   *Suvratāsaḥ* (Nom. Plural)   Applied to the *Ṛbhus* (divine artisans/deities) who achieved immortality through their flawless, devoted craftsmanship and righteous conduct (*sv-apatyā/su-vrata*).

  **Ṛgveda 8.25.2**   *Suvratā* (Dual/Plural)      Applied to Mitra and Varuṇa as the guardians of law (*dharmamātarā*), who are inherently \"bound to good vows\" and uphold truth against chaos (*Anṛta*).

  **Atharvaveda**     *Suvrata* (Adjective)        Extends to human initiates (sacrificers and trainees) who have taken sacred vows (*vrata*) during rites of passage, marking them as bound to truth and spiritual discipline.
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

**Semantic Evolution from Vedic to Early Buddhist Thought**

1.  **Vedic Foundation (*Ṛta* and *Vrata*):** In the Ṛgveda, a *vrata* was an inviolable command, rule, or sacred domain governed by a deity (especially Varuṇa). A person who was *suvrata* was one whose conduct aligned harmoniously with *Ṛta* (the divine order instead of chaos) by executing their sacred vows and sacrificial duties perfectly.

2.  **Buddhist Internalization (*Sīla* and *Damana*):** Early Buddhism adopted the term **subvata** (**subbatā**) but shifted its focus to internal discipline according to Gautama's ***Sīla* and *Damana***, such as self-taming in this verse (*attānaṁ damayanti*).

In this light, the *subbatā* are those who use disciplined action (*Sīla* and *Samādhi*) to align the *citta* toward wholesomeness (*Sugato*), turning the mind away from the chaotic tendencies and steering it toward ultimate liberation. The verse illustrates that if human effort can shape, redirect, and control physical matter, a yogi practitioner can certainly shape, direct, and tame their own **soul-spirit *ātmā/attā***.

**Taming Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā* through the Holy Life *Brahmacariya***

In the practice of *Brahmacariya*, taming **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā*** refers to subduing the ***Citta***\'s (as envelop of **Soul-Spirit *Ātmā/Attā***) untamed tendencies through *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, and *Paññā*. Left to its habitual inclinations, the mind flows toward sensual attachment, much like unchanneled water. Through deliberate effort directed toward wholesomeness and virtuousness (*Sugato*), the *bhikkhu* disenchants the mind-heart from the world (*nibbida*), purify the mind-heart of worldly passion (*viraga*), this leads to the liberation of the mind-heart from the world (*vimutti*). This process of self-taming leads directly along the ancient path to the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*.

#### Theragāthā 1.29. Hārita (1st)

**Samunnamayamattānaṁ, usukārova tejanaṁ;\
Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna, avijjaṁ bhinda hāritā ti.**

*Straightening (samunnamayaṁ) your soul-spirit attā (attānaṁ), just as an arrow maker (usukāro) \[straightens\] an arrow (tejanaṁ); having made (karitvāna) the citta straight (ujuṁ), pierce through/shatter (bhinda) ignorance (avijjaṁ), O Hāritā (hāritā)!*

**Commentary**: The practice of **straightening the soul-spirit (*samunnamayaṁ attānaṁ*)**, making the **mind-heart straight (*cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna*)**, and **shattering ignorance (*avijjaṁ bhinda*)** is the central discipline of the ancient path. Ignorance (*avijjā*) is dense, crooked, and multi-layered; it cannot be pierced by a mind that is warped by impulse, hostility, or worldly distortion.

To shatter *avijjā*, the *citta* must be straightened like a fletcher's arrow (*usukāro va tejanaṁ*). This straightening is accomplished through a clear, progressive training (*sikkhā*) anchored in value, self-restraint, deep concentration, and penetrating wisdom. The process of aligning and straightening the *citta* follows a definite, sequential discipline:

**Foundation of Values & Self-Restraint (*Brahmavihāra* & *Brahmacariya*)**

- **Purifying the Base with Brahmavihāra:** Before the mind can achieve razor-sharp straightness, its emotional base must be cleansed of gross distortions. *Mettā* (kindness) unbends the mind by removing ill-will (*byāpāda*). *Karuṇā* (compassion) removes cruelty (*vihesā*). *Muditā* (Brahmic joy) removes envy and inner contraction (*arati*). *Upekkhā* (equanimity/transcendental elevation) removes bias, reactivity, and emotional volatility (*paṭigha* and *anurodha*).

- **Establishing Brahmacariya & Sīla:** *Brahmacariya* (the noble, pure life of restraint) acts as the outer casing that keeps the energy of the *citta* from leaking into low, power-based, or impulse-driven channels. Without strict *sīla*, any attempt to straighten the mind fails because new crookedness is continuously introduced through unskillful action.

The motif of \"straightening\" the mind like an arrow or reed, and using that straightness to cut through defilement, appears across the early discourses:

#### Theragāthā 1.88. Ajjuna

**Asakkhiṁ vata attānaṁ, uddhātuṁ udakā thalaṁ,\
Vuyhamāno mahogh eva, saccāni paṭivijjhahan ti.**

*I was truly able (asakkhiṁ vata) to rescue my soul-spirit (attānaṁ), to raise (uddhātuṁ) from the water / the flood/ocean of saṃsāra (udakā) onto dry land / the firm, unshakeable ground, a direct epithet for Nibbāna-dhātu) (thalaṁ), as if (eva) being swept away (vuyhamāno) by a great flood (mahogheva); by fully realizing (paṭivijjhahan) the Truths (saccāni).*

The passages examined in this chapter establish a significant pattern in the language of ancient Indian spiritual cultivation.

In the Therīgāthā, *Attā* appears in direct association with Samādhi:

*Attā ce susamāhito.*

In the Dhammapada, the renunciant is described as:

*Ajjhattarato samāhito.*

He takes delight in the inward spiritual dimension and is established in Samādhi.

The Dhammapada also instructs the practitioner:

*Attanā codayattānaṃ.*

The individual must undertake spiritual examination and cultivation through *Attā*.

The Mahāparinibbāna Sutta preserves the instruction:

*Attadīpā viharatha, attasaraṇā anaññasaraṇā.*

The practitioner is directed towards *Attā* as island and refuge.

The Jain Uttarādhyayana Sūtra provides a related expression:

*Appā samāhio; appā gutto, sati-rakkhito.*

The *Appā* is established in Samādhi, guarded, and protected by mindfulness.

Finally, the Yuganaddha Sutta describes the establishment of *Citta* on *Ajjhatta* (high self or soul-spirit) as part of a path leading to Arahantship:

*Cittaṃ ajjhattameva santiṭṭhati sannisīdati ekodi hoti samādhiyati.*

Taken together, these passages provide a substantial basis for the Arahanist investigation of *Attā* and *Ajjhatta*. They repeatedly associate spiritual cultivation of the *attā* (soul-spirit) with Samādhi, mindfulness, self-examination, sense restraint, and the abandonment of the conditions that perpetuate worldly existence.

The central Arahanist interpretation is that the deeper spiritual meaning of *Attā* must be distinguished from ordinary worldly identity and from the five aggregates. The practitioner must understand the conditioned nature of bodily and mental manifestations, relinquish attachment to them, and cultivate the spiritual dimension of *attā* (soul-spirit) through which liberation becomes possible.

Although these verses may employ different terminology and must each be understood within their respective textual and spiritual traditions, their underlying ideas reveal significant convergence. The Jain understanding of *Appā*, in particular, offers an important point of connection with the spiritual vocabulary inherited from the wider Vedic heritage. These convergent expressions provide valuable evidence for examining the shared renunciant understanding of the soul-spirit, cultivation of *Ajjhatta* (high self or soul-spirit), and the attainment of liberation within ancient Indian spiritual traditions.

These passages also contribute to the foundation for the wider Arahanist understanding developed in Part III. Their immediate significance lies in the recognition of the soul-spirit, the orientation of spiritual cultivation of the *Ajjhatta* (high self or soul-spirit), and the role of *Samādhi* in the practitioner\'s progress towards liberation. Next chapter will develop these foundations further, examining their implications within the broader Arahanist understanding of *Attā*, *Citta*, and *Viññāṇa*.

The question that follows is therefore unavoidable. If *Attā* is the deeper spiritual individual, the soul-spirit, and if *Citta* must become established on the *Ajjhatta* (high self or soul-spirit) and purified, what is the precise relationship between them? Why does the Mind-Heart repeatedly become disturbed, drawn towards worldly gratification, and diverted from the purpose of liberation? And how did the elder bhikkhus and bhikkhunīs overcome these inner obstacles?

These questions bring us to the next stage of the investigation: the nature of *Citta*, its relationship with *Attā*, and the purification of the Mind-Heart.

The testimony of the elders will now guide us into that examination.

## V. The Mind-Heart: Citta, Its Bondage, And Its Purification

### 5. The Nature of Citta: The Mind-Heart of the Renunciant

Throughout the Theragāthā and Therīgāthā, the liberated elders repeatedly describe the training, purification, and liberation of their Citta. Their declarations reveal the transformation of Citta from its worldly establishment upon external manifestations towards its proper spiritual establishment upon Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta). This relationship is central to understanding their attainment. Citta encompasses the entire sphere of Mind-Heart, while Attā represents its innermost Soul-Spirit. The renunciant\'s task is to establish Citta upon this higher spiritual foundation, cultivate it through Samādhi, and purify the Soul-Spirit through the power of Tapas. Through this transformation, worldly disenchantment (Nibbidā), the fading of worldly passion (Virāga), and complete liberation from worldly bondage (Vimutti) become the successive stages of the path to Arahantship.

In the Arahanist understanding, Citta is the Mind-Heart whose proper spiritual foundation is Attā, the Soul-Spirit. The relationship between them is fundamental to the liberation process. Citta must establish itself upon the Higher Soul-Spirit, expressed through the Pāli concept of *Ajjhatta*. Arahanism understands *Ajjhatta* in relation to *Attā*, with the element *ajjh-* conveying the higher dimension of the Soul-Spirit. Thus, *Ajjhatta* signifies the Higher Self or Higher Soul-Spirit, the proper spiritual foundation upon which Citta must become established.

Citta has two fundamental orientations. It may become established upon the external manifestive world, engaging continually with worldly objects, sensory experiences, attractions, aversions, and attachments. Alternatively, it may establish itself upon the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta), directing its function towards the spiritual reality of Attā. These orientations have fundamentally different consequences.

When Citta is established upon the external world, it remains exposed to worldly influences and the conditions that generate defilements. Through this worldly engagement, new defilements continually arise and enter the Soul-Spirit, sustaining its attachment to manifestive existence. Citta consequently becomes the channel through which worldly bondage is perpetuated. The problem is therefore fundamental to the spiritual direction of the individual: as long as Citta continues to establish itself upon the external world, the Soul-Spirit remains subject to the continued accumulation of worldly defilements.

The renunciant path requires the transformation of this relationship. Citta must withdraw its establishment from the external world and become established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit. This transformation gives rise to **Nibbidā**, disenchantment with worldly existence. Through discernment, renunciation, and spiritual cultivation, Citta becomes disenchanted with worldly attractions and ceases to seek its foundation in external phenomena. As Citta becomes established upon Ajjhatta, the continual influx of new worldly defilements into Attā comes to an end.

Nevertheless, the cessation of further defilements does not complete the purification of the Soul-Spirit. The defilements already established within Attā must also be removed. This is the work of **Samādhi and Tapas**. Through Samādhi, Citta becomes deeply concentrated, collected, and stable in its establishment upon the Higher Soul-Spirit, which naturally induces Tapas, understood in the Arahanist framework as the primordial spiritual force for the purification of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, the existing defilements are progressively consumed and removed.

Tapas occupies an important place in the wider renunciant heritage of ancient India. Its significance can be examined through the spiritual disciplines associated with Gautama and the Jain Tīrthaṅkaras, where ascetic exertion and purification occupy an important place in the pursuit of liberation. Arahanism recognises Tapas as a fundamental principle of spiritual purification, through which the defilements already established within the Soul-Spirit are overcome.

The purification of Attā culminates in **Virāga**, the fading and complete eradication of worldly passion. Nibbidā and Virāga therefore describe two essential developments in the liberation process. Nibbidā marks the cessation of the continued influx of new defilements through the transformation of Citta\'s establishment. Virāga marks the progressive purification and eradication of the defilements already present within the Soul-Spirit. When both developments are complete, Attā is no longer subject to the defilements that sustain worldly bondage.

The culmination is **Vimutti**, liberation. With the complete eradication of defilements, the Soul-Spirit is released from worldly bondage. The attainment of Arahantship represents the completion of this process: Citta has become properly established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit, the influx of new defilements has ceased, and existing defilements have been completely purified. Attā has attained complete freedom from manifestive existence and is ready for its final release from the worldly domain.

The testimonies of the elders provide important textual foundations for examining these developments. Subhūti declares that his Citta is well concentrated and liberated. Godhika and Subāhu likewise describe the collected condition of their Citta. Vimala speaks of the settling of mental activity as his Citta becomes well concentrated. Abhirūpanandā instructs Nandā to cultivate Citta through contemplation of the unattractive, developing it as one-pointed and well collected. Anūpama gives a particularly striking testimony concerning Citta\'s attraction towards worldly delight and the danger this presents to the renunciant\'s spiritual purpose. Mettikā, in turn, declares that her Citta was liberated, alongside her attainment of the Three Knowledges.

These declarations reveal that the training and transformation of Citta are central to the elders\' attainment. Their testimonies concerning concentration, collectedness, discipline, and liberation provide the textual foundation for examining the relationship between Citta and the Soul-Spirit. Arahanism brings these elements together through the principle that Citta must establish itself upon the Higher Soul-Spirit rather than upon the external manifestive world. This proper establishment makes possible the cessation of further defilements, the purification of existing defilements, and the ultimate liberation of Attā.

This chapter therefore examines Citta as the central instrument through which the Soul-Spirit undergoes its liberation process. It investigates the nature of Citta, the consequences of its establishment upon the external world, its proper establishment upon Ajjhatta, and its progressive training through Samādhi and Tapas. Through the testimonies of the Theragāthā and Therīgāthā, we will examine the transformation of Citta, the development of concentration, the fading of worldly passion, and the attainment of Vimutti.

The ultimate purpose of this training is the complete liberation of the Soul-Spirit (Attā) from worldly bondage.

**Mainstream Academia's Wrong Accusation on Tapas**

Mainstream academia claims that "Before the Buddha, contemporary movements like Jainism and various ascetic sects used *tapas* to mean severe physical self-mortification---such as starvation, holding painful postures, or exposing oneself to extreme weather---to burn up bad karma. The Buddha tried these extreme practices for six years and nearly died. Consequently, throughout the Pali Canon, he **redefines Tapa from external physical torture to internal mental discipline**."

This is completely ignorant and wrong accusation.

The textual evidence across the Pali sutta demonstrates that Gautama Buddha never rejected **tapas**. Rather, he rejected physical torture (*attāntāpana* or *dukkarakārikā*), which is precisely **NOT tapas**.

He explicitly used the term *tapas* (and its verbal/adjectival forms *tapati*, *tāpaso*, *ātāpī*) to describe the radiant, primordial, concentrated spiritual energy of the citta that incinerates mental defilements (*kilesa*) and powers the attainment of **Arahantship** and **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

**(1). Direct Affirmation: Gautama Buddha Declares His Own Path as Tapas**

In the **Bhaya-bherava Sutta (MN 4)**, Gautama Buddha directly refutes the notion that he is an opponent of *tapas*. He affirms that he lives possessing genuine *tapas* (*tapasī*): *So kho ahaṁ, brāhmaṇa, tapassī ceva homi paramo ca tapassīnāṁ \|*

*\"Indeed, brāhmaṇa, I am one who has **tapas** (tapassī), and I am the foremost among those who possess **tapas**.\"*

He does not abandon the word or distance himself from it---he claims the supreme mastery of it.

**(2). True Tāpasa*:* One who Practise *Tapas***

In the **Sutta Nipāta (verse 492, Māgha Sutta)**, Gautama Buddha described *tāpaso* (one who practices *tapas*): *Ye vedha pāpāni nudanti hitvā, pariggahaṁ amamā cerayanti \|Te tādisā tāpasā sādhurūpā, etehi havyāny anumodayetha \|\|*

*\"Those here who drive away evil (pāpāni), who have abandoned worldly possessions (amamā)---such ones are true tāpasā of good nature.\"*

Here, one who practices *tapas* is to have good nature by abandoning evil*,* unwholesome states (*pāpāni*) of the mind, one abandons worldly possessions, ownership (*amamā*), and becomes spiritual oriented, such person is one could induce the primordial spiritual force *tapas*, that can purify the citta, lead to liberation from worldly bondage.

**(3). Manifestation of** ***Tapas*: Patient Endurance (*khantī*)**

In the **Dhammapada (verse 184, Ovāda-Pātimokkha)**, Gautama Buddha defines the supreme manifestation of *tapas*: *Khantī paramaṁ tapo titikkhā, nibbānaṁ paramaṁ vadanti buddhā \|*

*\"Patient endurance (khantī) is the supreme **tapas** (paramaṁ tapo); \'Nibbāna is supreme,\' say the Buddhas.\"*

*Tapas* as the inner spiritual power of the mind-heart, manifest externally as **patient endurance** (*khantī*) for any worldly sufferings, for such a person with *Tapas* already sets their eyes on the supreme *Nibbāna.*

**(4). *Tapas* Ignited Through Jhāna, *Tapas* as Spiritual Fire/Power (*tejas*)**

In the **Dhammapada (verse 387)**, Gautama Buddha explicitly ties the verbal root of *tapas* (*tapati* = to shine forth/ radiate) to the practice of **jhāna**: *Divā tapati ādicco, rattim ābhāti candimā \| Sannaddho khattiyo tapati, jhāyī tapati brāhmaṇo \| Atha sabbam ahorattiṁ, buddho tapati tejasā \|\|*

*\"The sun shines (tapati) by day; the moon glows by night. The warrior shines (tapati) in armor; the brāhmaṇa shines (tapati) by absorption in* **Jhāna** *(jhāyī). But day and night, all through, the Buddha shines (tapati) by his spiritual fire/ power (tejas).\"*

**(5) Tapas** as the internal ignition of primordial consciousness innate to the soul-spirit (*ātmā*) can burn away defilements and purify the Citta.

**SN 12.70 (Susima Sutta): Tapas as Internal Ignition for Liberation**

When Susīma asks about the spiritual life, the Buddha explicitly affirms *tapas* as: *\"Susīma, **tapas** is for the purpose of direct knowledge, for the purpose of full understanding, for the purpose of the complete destruction of the taints (āsava).\"*

In the *Satipaṭṭhāna Sutta* (MN 10), the formula for establishing mindfulness opens with: *\"ātāpī sampajāno satimā\"* (\"ardent, fully aware, mindful\").

**Mainstream Academia's Wrong Accusation and Attack on *Tapas***

Academic Indology and Buddhist Studies routinely translate *tapas* as \"austerity,\" \"penance,\" or \"self-mortification,\" equating it with physical self-torture. This translation is a fundamental distortion of the early Pali Suttas. It misreads the critique of bodily self-infliction, missing how Gautama Buddha distinguished external physical torment from internal spiritual exertion.

**The Fundamental Flaw: Confusing *Paritāpana* with *Tapas***

Mainstream scholarship accuses early Buddhism of either maintaining a contradiction---rejecting *tapas* in principle while demanding \"asceticism\" in practice---or presenting the Buddha as a reformer who simply softened harsh self-mortification into a milder form of physical restraint.

Both assertions rest on a category error. Across the early Suttas (e.g., *Kandaraka Sutta* \[MN 51\], *Māgaṇḍiya Sutta* \[MN 75\], *Devadaha Sutta* \[MN 101\]), the Buddha does **not** reject *tapas*. He rejects **paritāpana** (the deliberate infliction of affliction, torment, and physical pain upon the body), which has nothing to do with ***tapas***.

When the Buddha critiques those who starve their stomachs, bake under the summer sun, or sleep on thorns, he labels them **attāntapo attāparitāpanānuyogamanuyutto** (\"self-tormentors devoted to the pursuit of afflicting themselves\"). The critique targets the **paritāpana**---the useless physical torment that leaves *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* completely untouched within the citta.

**Root of the Academic Misinterpretation**

Mainstream academia\'s erroneous translation and accusation on *tapas* stems from three methodological failures:

**(1). Indiscriminate Application of Hinduism Frameworks**

Mainstream academic Indology has fundamentally misunderstood the ancient meaning of *tapas*, reducing a diverse field of spiritual disciplines to physical mortification. Ancient practitioners employed numerous methods to cultivate spiritual power, including profound meditative states such as *jhāna* and *samādhi*, as well as demanding bodily austerities such as prolonged fasting. Jesus\' forty-day fast in the wilderness provides another familiar example. Such practices were understood as means of inducing profound spiritual transformations and extraordinary capacities. Accounts of near-death experiences, in which individuals sometimes report unusual perceptions or psychic powers, further illustrate the complexity of this subject. By ignoring this broader spiritual context and indiscriminately equating *tapas* with bodily self-mortification, mainstream academics expose the limitations of their historical understanding and their reductionism methodology. Their sweeping judgments reveal not scholarly precision, but a fundamental ignorance of the very spiritual traditions they presume to explain.

**(2). Victorian Theological Framing**

Nineteenth-century colonial translators (such as T.W. Rhys Davids and V. Trenckner) viewed Indian spiritual practices through the lens of medieval Christian monasticism. They mapped the Latinate concepts of **\"penance\"** and **\"mortification of the flesh\"** directly onto *tapas*. This linguistic choice loaded the term with connotations of self-punishment for sin, a concept entirely alien to the early Pali framework of mind-purification.

### 5.1. Citta as the Centre of Spiritual Training

The renunciant\'s spiritual training centres upon the transformation of Citta, the Mind-Heart. Its establishment, concentration, and purification determine whether the Soul-Spirit (Attā) remains subject to worldly bondage or progresses towards complete liberation. The Theragāthā preserves several concise but significant declarations in which the elders describe their Citta as *susamāhita*---well and firmly established in *samādhi*. Among these, the verses of Subhūti, Godhika, and Subāhu are particularly instructive. Their expressions reveal different dimensions of Citta\'s collected condition and provide an important foundation for understanding its role in the liberation of Attā.

In the Arahanist understanding, Citta encompasses the total sphere of Mind-Heart, extending from its external worldly engagements to its innermost spiritual depth, whose essential core is Attā. The central task of renunciant training is to bring Citta into its proper spiritual establishment upon this Soul-Spirit.
