When Citta remains established upon external worldly manifestations, it continues to engage with the conditions that generate attachment and defilement. When it becomes established upon Attā, its orientation is directed towards the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta), providing the foundation for Samādhi and the purification that culminates in liberation.

The significance of this training becomes especially clear when we examine the declarations of the three elders.

**Subhūti: Citta Established in Samādhi and Liberated**

Subhūti declares:

*Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā,*\
*Vassa deva yathāsukhaṁ;*\
*Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ,*\
*Ātāpī viharāmi vassa devā ti.*

"My hut is covered, comfortable, and sheltered from the wind. Rain, O Deva, as you wish. My Citta is well established in Samādhi and liberated; I live possessing Tapas, O Deva."

The verse presents a carefully constructed relationship between physical shelter and spiritual attainment. Subhūti first describes his hut as *channā*, covered; *sukhā*, comfortable; and *nivātā*, sheltered from the wind. He then declares the condition of his Citta:

*Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ.*

The expression contains two important descriptions. *Susamāhitaṁ* indicates that Citta is thoroughly collected and firmly established in Samādhi. *Vimuttaṁ* declares its liberated condition. The two expressions occur together, showing that the elder describes both the established condition of Citta and its freedom from worldly bondage.

The grammatical construction is significant. *Cittaṁ* is the subject of the description, while *me* indicates possession: this is his Citta. The two predicates, *susamāhitaṁ* and *vimuttaṁ*, describe its condition. Subhūti therefore makes a direct declaration of spiritual attainment. His Citta is firmly established and liberated.

The final expression, *ātāpī viharāmi*, adds another important dimension. Subhūti describes himself as living with *ātāpī*, a term in the Arahanist interpretation, connects with *Tapas*, the primordial spiritual force of ascetic purification. The elder\'s declaration consequently brings together three essential elements: the established condition of Citta, liberation from worldly bondage, and the spiritual force associated with Tapas.

The imagery of the sheltered hut reinforces the declaration. Rain, wind, and the surrounding natural conditions cannot disturb the security of his dwelling. Correspondingly, his Citta has attained a condition in which worldly influences no longer determine its spiritual state. The physical shelter provides the setting for a declaration of spiritual freedom.

The significance of Subhūti\'s testimony becomes clearer through the Arahanist understanding of Citta and Attā. Citta is the total field of Mind-Heart, while Attā is its essential Soul-Spirit core. The liberation of Attā requires Citta to be properly established upon this spiritual foundation. Subhūti\'s declaration of a well-established and liberated Citta therefore provides a direct expression of the attainment towards which this training is directed.

**Godhika: The Citta Firmly Established**

Godhika uses an almost identical setting:

*Vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ,*\
*Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā;*\
*Cittaṁ susamāhitañca mayhaṁ,*\
*Atha ce patthayasi pavassa deva ti.*

"The Deva rains like a beautiful melody. My hut is covered, comfortable, and sheltered from the wind. My Citta is also well established in Samādhi. If you wish, rain, O Deva."

Godhika\'s central declaration is:

*Cittaṁ susamāhitañca mayhaṁ.*

The expression *susamāhitaṁ* again describes Citta as thoroughly collected and established in concentration. The word *mayhaṁ* expresses possession: it is his Citta that has attained this condition. The particle *ca* adds the sense of "also" or "and," connecting the declaration with the preceding description.

Godhika\'s verse does not include the word *vimuttaṁ*, which appears in Subhūti\'s declaration. We should preserve this distinction. Subhūti explicitly declares Citta both concentrated and liberated, whereas Godhika emphasises its firmly established condition.

Nevertheless, the shared setting is significant. Both elders describe a covered and comfortable hut, sheltered from the wind, while rain falls outside. Both then declare the well-established condition of Citta. Their verses place the external environment alongside the spiritual condition of the renunciant. The weather may change, and the rain may fall as it wishes, but the elder\'s spiritual establishment remains secure.

Through the Arahanist framework, Godhika\'s declaration points towards the central requirement of spiritual training: Citta must attain a stable establishment upon Attā. Concentration is essential because Citta must cease being governed by the fluctuations and attractions of worldly manifestations. Its proper establishment upon the Higher Soul-Spirit provides the basis for the deeper purification of Attā.

Godhika\'s testimony therefore complements Subhūti\'s. Subhūti describes the concentrated and liberated Citta; Godhika emphasises the condition of firm concentration itself.

**Subāhu --- Kāyagatāsati and the Collectedness of Citta**

Subāhu\'s declaration in the Theragāthā (1.52) reads:

*Vassati devo yathā sugītaṁ,*\
*Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā;*\
*Cittaṁ susamāhitañca kāye,*\
*Atha ce patthayasi pavassa deva ti.*

The central expression is:

*Cittaṁ susamāhitañca kāye.*

A direct rendering is:

"And Citta is well collected in the body."

The expression *kāye* is the locative form of *kāya*, meaning "in the body." Its significance becomes clearer when understood in connection with **kāyagatāsati**, mindfulness directed towards the body.

Kāyagatāsati is an important contemplative discipline through which the practitioner establishes mindfulness upon the body. Rather than allowing Citta to remain dispersed among worldly objects, concerns, and sensory engagements, the practitioner directs mindfulness towards the bodily field of contemplation. Through sustained practice, mindfulness becomes stable, and Citta develops increasing collectedness, leading to Samādhi.

Subāhu\'s declaration therefore expresses the relationship between bodily mindfulness and the collected condition of Citta. His words place particular emphasis on the body as the field upon which mindfulness is established, and upon the resulting stability of Citta.

Within the Arahanist understanding, this practice forms part of the progressive training through which Citta withdraws its establishment from worldly manifestations and becomes disciplined through contemplative practice. Kāyagatāsati provides a practical foundation for the development of Samādhi. As Citta becomes increasingly collected, the practitioner advances towards the deeper spiritual establishment required for the purification and liberation of Attā.

Subāhu\'s testimony thus adds an important practical dimension to the other two declarations. It shows how the disciplined direction of mindfulness supports the collectedness of Citta, which is essential to the renunciant\'s progress.

**Comparing the Three Elders**

The three declarations share the expression *susamāhita*, placing the collectedness of Citta at the centre of their testimonies. Nevertheless, each elder expresses a different aspect of spiritual training.

  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Elder**   **Pāli expression**                **Principal emphasis**
  ----------- ---------------------------------- ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Subhūti     *Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ*   Citta is well collected and liberated.

  Godhika     *Cittaṁ susamāhitañca mayhaṁ*      The well-collected condition of Citta.

  Subāhu      *Cittaṁ susamāhitañca kāye*        Citta\'s collectedness in relation to the body, understood through kāyagatāsati.
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Subāhu\'s declaration highlights a contemplative discipline through which mindfulness is directed towards the body and Citta develops collectedness. Godhika emphasizes the resulting condition of a well-collected Citta. Subhūti goes further by explicitly declaring Citta both well collected and liberated.

Their verses therefore illuminate complementary dimensions of the renunciant\'s training: the practical discipline of mindfulness, the development of Samādhi, and the attainment of liberation.

The Arahanist understanding places these declarations within a broader account of the relationship between Citta and Attā. Citta is the Mind-Heart through which the human being engages with the manifestive world and undertakes spiritual training. At its deepest spiritual dimension lies Attā, the Soul-Spirit. The proper establishment of Citta upon this innermost Soul-Spirit core is fundamental to liberation.

The three elders do not explicitly use the word *Attā* in these particular declarations. Their testimonies describe the collectedness and liberation of Citta, while the Arahanist interpretation understands these achievements within the larger Citta--Attā relationship. The practice of mindfulness and the cultivation of Samādhi are essential stages in this training, progressively preparing Citta for its proper establishment upon Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta).

This distinction is important for understanding the entire path. The collectedness of Citta is not an isolated meditative accomplishment. It serves the deeper purpose of spiritual purification. Through Nibbidā, Citta relinquishes its continued establishment upon worldly manifestations and becomes established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit. This prevents the continued influx of new worldly defilements into Attā. Through Samādhi and Tapas, existing defilements are progressively purified. As Virāga develops, worldly passion fades, culminating in Vimutti---the complete liberation of Attā from worldly bondage.

The testimonies of Subhūti, Godhika, and Subāhu consequently establish an important foundation for the Arahanist understanding of spiritual training: **the cultivation and collectedness of Citta serve the purification and ultimate liberation of the Soul-Spirit (Attā).**

### 5.2. The Untrained and Wandering Citta

Before Citta can become firmly established upon Attā, its untrained condition must be understood. The ordinary Citta is continually drawn towards the attractions of the manifestive world. It follows what appears desirable, pursues sensual gratification, reacts to agreeable and disagreeable experiences, and moves from one object to another according to its inclinations. Without discipline, Citta remains subject to this continuous movement, unable to maintain a stable spiritual establishment.

This wandering tendency is a fundamental source of worldly bondage. When Citta repeatedly follows external attractions, *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* continue to influence its direction. Sensual desire draws it towards pleasurable objects; anger and fear arise in response to unpleasant experiences; and ignorance and delusion prevents it from recognizing the consequences of remaining bound to worldly existence. Thus, the untrained Citta continually renews its engagement with the very conditions from which the renunciant seeks liberation.

**The Citta That Wanders Wherever It Wishes**

The Theragāthā preserves a particularly direct declaration by the elder Hatthārohaputta:

*Idaṁ pure cittamacāri cārikaṁ,*\
*Yenicchakaṁ yatthakāmaṁ yathāsukhaṁ;*\
*Tadajjahaṁ niggahessāmi yoniso,*\
*Hatthippabhinnaṁ viya aṅkusaggaho ti.*

(Theragāthā 1.77)

"Previously, this Citta wandered about, wherever it wished, wherever it desired, and however it pleased. Today, I shall restrain it through wise attention (touching the source/*yoni*), just as a mahout restrains a wild elephant in rut with an elephant goad."

The opening expression, *idaṁ pure cittamacāri cārikaṁ*, describes the former wandering of Citta. The elder speaks of a Citta that moved about freely, without proper restraint or direction. The following expressions intensify this description:

- *Yenicchakaṁ* --- wherever it wished.

- *Yatthakāmaṁ* --- wherever it desired.

- *Yathāsukhaṁ* --- however it pleased.

Together, these expressions portray a Citta governed by its own inclinations. It follows attraction and desire without regard for the consequences. Its direction is determined by whatever captures its interest, and its movement remains uncontrolled.

The elder then makes a decisive declaration: *Tadajjahaṁ niggahessāmi yoniso.*

"Today, I shall restrain it through wise attention (touching the source/*yoni*)."

The verb *niggahessāmi* expresses the determination to restrain, control, and bring Citta under discipline.

The word *yoniso* traditional rendered as wise and systematic attention. The elder recognizes that the wandering Citta must be actively brought under control through deliberate training.

**Yoniso** is not a superficial catchword or a mild synonym for "Wise" or \"sensible\" or \"careful\" as we see in the standard mainstream academia's interpretation. It actually cuts to the absolute ontological root of existence.

Tracing the etymology back through the earliest strata of the *Ṛg Veda* reveals why this term carries the explosive force required to shatter the wheel of reincarnation.

**1. Etymological Root: *Yoni* in the *Ṛg Veda***

- **The Root:** Derived from the Indo-Iranian and Proto-Indo-European root **\*yónh₂-** (connected to generation, begetting, and birth).

- **Primary Vedic Meaning:** In the *Ṛg Veda*, **yoni** (योनि) literally and fundamentally means **the womb, the place of origin, the matrix, the nest, the fountainhead, or the generative source** from which anything is born, manifested, or brought forth.

- **Application:** In the oldest Vedic hymns, *yoni* denotes the primal root source or womb---the literal source from which forms, worlds, and lives emerge into *bhava* (worldly becoming or manifestation).

*Ṛg Veda* 3.29.2: ***yoniḥ ta āśayo kṛto, yathā vaso ratna-dhānam:** **etām ā-vaha: yoniḥ eṣā, yaḥ ta āśayo kṛto, yataḥ jāto arocathāḥ.***

This (eṣā) is your (te) true place of origin /matrix (yoniḥ). A place of rest and dwelling (āśayo) has been prepared (kṛto) for you, like (yathā) a container of precious treasures (ratna-dhānam), O dwelling light (vaso, *dwelling one, radiant lord, an epithet for **Agni,** the Divine Fire, while also functioning in the plural (**Vasavaḥ**) as a major class of elemental deities*). Bring her forth (etām ā-vaha)! This (eṣā) is the original womb-source (yoniḥ), which (yaḥ) was made (kṛto) your (te) resting place (āśayo), from which (yataḥ) having been born (jāto), you shone forth (arocathāḥ).

In the *Ṛg Veda*, **yoni** is not an abstract metaphor; it is the concrete, generative origin from which fire (*Agni*) or divine force is drawn forth into manifestation.

The text describes the ritual extraction of Agni from the wooden matrix (*araṇī*). The lower wood block is explicitly named the **yoni**---the physical womb and generative source. The verse asserts that Agni cannot shine (*arocathāḥ*) until he emerges directly from his true **yoni** (*yataḥ jāto*).

This establishes the foundational Vedic definition: **yoni** is the hidden, underlying source-matrix where an entity resides before it emerges into manifest existence.

When this Vedic framework was inherited by Śramaṇa practitioners, the adverbial form **yoniso** (*yoni* + suffix *-śas*, meaning \"by way of the source\" or \"from the root matrix\") retained this exact structural logic: to trace a phenomenon **yoniso** is to strip away its surface manifestation and track it directly back into its generative womb (*yoni*).

By applying **yoniso manasikāra** (root-level, causal insight into the womb-matrix of all physical phenomena), the practitioner sees that *all* worldly forms of birth---whether human (*manussa*), animal, or divine---are impermanent, manifest within the world (*loka*), and governed by the universal law of decay and death.

By penetrating the *yoni* down to its root, the ascetic completely extinguishes the accumulation of *upadhi*, ensuring that the current physical body is their **final body** (*antima-sarīra*), and extracts the *citta* entirely out of the universe into the unconditioned and deathless **Nibbāna-dhātu**.

Therefore, when the texts speak of **yoniso manasikāra**, they do not mean \"thinking positively, wisely or appropriately\" or \"using common sense.\" They mean:

**\"Directing the mind straight to the womb-source, tracking every phenomenon back to its generative root and structural cause.\"**

**Let's look at Early Jain Śramaṇa Parallel (*Ācārāṅga Sūtra*):**

*Ācārāṅga Sūtra* 1.1.5 (Sanskritized): *yāṃ ca yonim anupariyatamānaḥ, karma-samudaya-mūlaṃ yonikam: yoni-parijñāṁ parijānāti.*

And whichever (yām) original womb-source (*yonim*) \[one\] continuously wanders and revolves around (*anupariyatamānaḥ*), that very womb-matrix (*yonikam*) is the root (*mūlam*) of the accumulation of karma (*karma-samudaya*). \[He/the ascetic\] fully comprehends (*parijānāti*) the thorough direct knowledge of the womb-source (*yoni-parijñām*).

In early Jain Śramaṇa literature---composed primarily in Ardhamāgadhī and Śaurasenī Prakrit---the term *yoni* (Prakrit: *joṇi*) and its diagnostic applications (*joṇi-pariṇṇā* / *yoni-parijñā*, mirroring Pāli *yoniso*) serve as the foundational mechanical entry point for ending reincarnation.

In the Jain matrix, a **yoni** is not merely an anatomical organ; it is a specific **physico-karmic seedbed or nucleus of material manifestation** (traditionally categorized into 8.4 million *joṇi-ppamāṇa* across the universe) where a sentient entity (*jīva*) is bound by karmic matter (*kamma-puggala*) to take physical form.

To achieve liberation (*mokṣa*), the ascetic must execute **Yoni-Parijñā** (the thorough, radical penetration and dismantling of the womb-matrix).

Here are more verses from the oldest strata of Jain Āgamas:

**1. *Ācārāṅga Sūtra* (*Āyāraṁga Sutta*) 1.1.2 (Sanskritized):** *yo yoniṁ sarvaśo jñāna-parijñayā parijānāti, sa bhavati viṣṭhā-vinaya-paravasa-laṅghana-parāṅmukhaḥ.*

This verse establishes the direct link between ignorance of the womb-matrix (*yoni*) and continuous physical destruction.

Whoever (yo) thoroughly comprehends (parijānāti) the womb-source (yoniṁ) in all its aspects (sarvaśo) through direct discerning knowledge (jñāna-parijñayā), he (sa) becomes averse (parāṅmukhaḥ) to remaining subject to foreign force (paravasa), suffering physical degradation (viṣṭhā), and being bound down (vinaya).

**2. *Sūtraktāṅga Sūtra* (*Sūyagaḍaṁga*) 1.1.1.5:** *yoniṃ-anupraviṣṭānām, na vigacched a-paśyakaḥ; karmaṇām-upasaṅgāt, mucyate bandhanād uta.*

This text addresses the mechanics of how the soul (*jīva*) becomes drawn into a *yoni* through ignorance, generating the material fuel for rebirth.

For those who have entered into (anupraviṣṭānām) the womb-matrix (yoniṃ), the blind one who does not see (a-paśyakaḥ) cannot escape (na vigacchet). Only by dissolving the attachment to (upasaṅgāt) karma-particles (karmaṇām) is one released (mucyate) from the prison of bondage (bandhanāt).

**3. *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (*Uttarajjhayaṇa*) 29.11:** *yoni-parijñayā nūnaṁ jīvaḥ, yoni-saṁsṛtiṃ chindati; yoni-saṁsṛti-chinnānām, tathā-pākaṁ niyacchati.*

This explicit verse details the direct causal chain connecting **Yoni-Parijñā** (the thorough knowledge of the womb-matrix) to the complete severance of reincarnation (*bhava*).

By means of the radical discernment of the womb-source (yoni-parijñayā), the soul (jīvaḥ) certainly (nūnaṁ) cuts off (chindati) the continuous cycle of entering womb-matrices (yoni-saṁsṛtiṃ). For those who have severed (chinnānām) the cycle of entering womb-matrices (yoni-saṁsṛti), \[the soul\] attains (niyacchati) the ultimate state of mature liberation (tathā-pākaṁ).

**The Contrast: *Ayoniso* vs. *Yoniso***

The profound opposition between *ayoniso* and *yoniso* maps directly onto the mechanics of bondage and liberation:

**Ayoniso (Not from the source):**

- Observes the physical body as an end in itself.

- Leads directly to bodily ornamentation (*maṇḍanaṁ*), attachment to sexual and sensual craving (*kāma-rāga*), and the accumulation of *upadhi* (the womb-fuel for rebirth).

**Yoniso (Tracing back to the womb-source):**

- Penetrates beneath the surface of physical phenomena.

- Recognizes that physical bodies are temporary worldly manifestations born of cause, effect, and decay---belonging entirely to the world (*loka*).

- Uproots the *citta* from worldly becoming (*bhava*) to secure the final body (*antima-sarīra*).

**Why Yoniso Leads Directly to Liberation**

When a practitioner applies yoniso manasikāra, they refuse to look at things superficially. They look into the \"womb\" (yoni) of dukkha and see that every physical form, every material desire, and every worldly honor is rooted in the conditioned matrix of saṃsāra.

By tracing phenomena back to this root source with radical clarity, the citta undergoes complete disenchantment (nibbindati). It realizes that physical phenomena are just manifestations of the Citta/Soul-Spirit---they are worldly, they belong to the world (loka), and the citta must leave them behind and transcend them.

Through this root-level vision (yoniso), the practitioner violently extracts the citta from bhava (bhave cittaṁ udabbahiṁ), ensuring that they never again enter a womb and then to lie down with a broken head again, exiting the universe entirely into the Nibbāna-dhātu.

Now, Let's continue. He compares this task to a mahout controlling a wild elephant in rut. Such an elephant possesses enormous strength, but without proper direction its strength becomes difficult to govern. The mahout uses an elephant goad (*aṅkusa*) to restrain and guide it. Similarly, the renunciant must exercise firm discipline over Citta rather than allow it to follow every worldly attraction.

This verse reveals an important principle: the untrained Citta does not naturally remain established upon the spiritual objective of liberation. It must be restrained, trained, and directed through sustained practice.

**Worldly Attractions and the Movement of Desire**

The wandering of Citta is closely connected with its attraction towards sensual objects and worldly experiences. Sight, sound, smell, taste, bodily sensation, possessions, recognition, relationships, and the pleasures of worldly existence continually provide objects towards which Citta may become directed.

When Citta delights in these objects, attachment develops. When it encounters something disagreeable, aversion may arise. When it fails to understand the nature and consequences of these movements, ignorance continues to govern its direction.

The problem therefore extends beyond occasional distraction. The untrained Citta repeatedly establishes itself upon worldly objects, becoming attached to their attractions and disturbed by their absence or loss. Its attention is continually captured by changing circumstances, and its stability remains dependent upon conditions that cannot provide lasting security.

This is why sensual desire occupies such an important place in the renunciant\'s discipline. The pursuit of sensual gratification continually draws Citta towards worldly existence. Even when one object has been obtained, another attraction may arise. Satisfaction remains temporary, and the movement of desire continues.

The renunciant must therefore recognize the direction in which Citta is moving. As long as it remains governed by worldly attractions, its training cannot reach completion.

**Agitation, Distraction, and the Unstable Mind-Heart**

The wandering Citta is also characterized by agitation and distraction. Because it continually follows changing objects, it cannot maintain sustained collectedness. Its attention is pulled towards whatever appears attractive, threatening, desirable, or disturbing.

This instability is closely related to the operation of the defilements:

- **Rāga** draws Citta towards sensual attraction, pleasure, and attachment.

- **Dosa** produces aversion, fear, anger, resentment, hostility and violence.

- **Moha** obscures understanding and allows these movements to continue without proper recognition.

Restlessness and distraction further weaken the stability required for Samādhi. Citta becomes occupied with worldly concerns, repeatedly moving between attraction and aversion. Even when a practitioner sits in meditation, these tendencies may continue to influence the Mind-Heart unless they are properly restrained.

The difficulty is therefore not simply a lack of concentration during meditation. It concerns the fundamental orientation of Citta. A Citta continually established upon worldly attractions cannot become firmly established upon Attā without a decisive transformation in its direction.

**The Necessity of Discipline**

The declaration of Hatthārohaputta establishes the necessity of deliberate restraint. His words, *niggahessāmi yoniso*, express a determination to bring Citta under control through wise attention. This is an essential principle of renunciant training.

Discipline begins with Sīla, through which the practitioner restrains bodily and verbal conduct and establishes clear boundaries against unwholesome activity. Sense restraint further prevents Citta from becoming carelessly absorbed in the attractions of the sensory world. Mindfulness and wise attention enable the practitioner to recognize the movement of desire and prevent unwholesome thoughts from developing unchecked.

Through sustained practice, Citta gradually becomes less dependent upon external attractions. Its habitual movements are restrained, its agitation is reduced, and its capacity for collectedness develops. This provides the necessary foundation for Samādhi.

Within the Arahanist framework, however, restraint and concentration serve a deeper objective. The ultimate task is to change the establishment of Citta itself. Through Nibbidā, Citta becomes disenchanted with worldly existence and relinquishes its continued establishment upon external manifestations. It becomes established upon Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta). This transformation prevents the continued influx of new worldly defilements into Attā and prepares the way for its purification through Samādhi and Tapas.

The untrained Citta wanders wherever desire directs it. The trained Citta is restrained, collected, and progressively established upon its essential Soul-Spirit foundation. This is why discipline is indispensable to the renunciant\'s path: without mastery of Citta, the deeper work of spiritual purification and liberation cannot be accomplished.

### 5.3. The Betrayal of Citta: Anūpama\'s Testimony

The relationship between Citta and Attā is not always harmonious. Although Citta is the Mind-Heart through which the individual engages with existence, it can become dominated by worldly attractions and pursue purposes contrary to the liberation of the Soul-Spirit. In such a condition, Citta effectively takes control of the individual\'s direction. Instead of serving the spiritual purpose of Attā, it compels the Soul-Spirit to participate in activities that sustain worldly attachment, pride, and vanity.

The Theragāthā preserves a particularly striking testimony of this conflict in the verses attributed to the elder Anūpama.

**Theragāthā 2.47 --- Anūpama**

*Nandamānāgataṁ cittaṁ, sūlamāropamānakaṁ;*\
*Tena teneva vajasi, yena sūlaṁ kaliṅgaraṁ.*

*Tāhaṁ cittakaliṁ brūmi, taṁ brūmi cittadubbhakaṁ;*\
*Satthā te dullabho laddho, mānatthe maṁ niyojayī.*

**Translation:**

The Citta, driven towards delight (*nandamānāgataṁ*), is like one being raised upon a sharp execution stake (*sūlamāropamānakaṁ*). With that very Citta, you wander here and there (*tena teneva vajasi*), towards the place where the execution stake and the dry log lie (*yena sūlaṁ kaliṅgaraṁ*).

I declare that Citta a source of defeat (*cittakaliṁ*); I declare that Citta a treacherous betrayer (*cittadubbhakaṁ*). You have obtained a Teacher who is exceedingly difficult to find (*satthā te dullabho laddho*), yet you have compelled me---the Soul-Spirit---to serve the purposes of your pride and vanity (*mānatthe maṁ niyojayī*).

**Commentary: An Internal Courtroom Scene**

This verse presents an extraordinary internal confrontation. It can be understood as a courtroom scene in which Attā, the Soul-Spirit, brings a direct accusation against Citta, the Mind-Heart.

The speaker is not simply expressing frustration with an uncontrolled mind. In the Arahanist interpretation, the first-person *maṁ*---"me"---in the final line identifies the Soul-Spirit as the one who has been compelled into service. Attā is the injured party. Citta is the accused.

The distinction is essential. The verse exposes a conflict between the spiritual purpose of the Soul-Spirit and the worldly direction pursued by Citta.

**1. The Accused: Citta Driven by Delight**

*Nandamānāgataṁ cittaṁ*

The accusation begins by identifying the condition of Citta: it is drawn towards delight.

Citta pursues what attracts it. It moves towards pleasurable experiences, sensory gratification, worldly recognition, and the satisfaction of desire. Its attention is repeatedly captured by whatever promises enjoyment or personal advantage.

This tendency becomes dangerous when Citta allows worldly delight to determine the direction of the entire individual. The Mind-Heart begins to pursue gratification as though gratification were its principal purpose. It becomes preoccupied with obtaining, possessing, experiencing, defending, and repeating worldly pleasures.

The problem therefore extends beyond occasional distraction. Citta has become governed by the very attractions from which the renunciant has undertaken to become free.

**2. The Consequence: Wandering Towards Destruction**

*Sūlamāropamānakaṁ; Tena teneva vajasi, yena sūlaṁ kaliṅgaraṁ.*

The imagery is severe. Citta is compared to someone being raised upon an execution stake, while continuing to wander towards the place associated with that stake and the dry log.

The image exposes the contradiction in worldly attachment. Citta pursues delight while leading the individual towards suffering, decay, and death. It repeatedly directs the individual towards the very conditions that perpetuate bondage.

The Soul-Spirit seeks liberation from worldly existence, yet Citta continues to pursue worldly gratification. It therefore leads the individual in a direction contrary to the purpose of renunciation.

The danger is especially serious for a renunciant. Having recognized the limitations of worldly existence and entered the dispensation of Gautama the Supreme Arahant, the practitioner has already undertaken a life directed towards liberation. Yet Citta can continue to behave as though the old worldly objectives remain its highest priorities.

The body may have entered the Saṅgha, while Citta continues to serve the attractions of the world.

**3. The Indictment: Cittakali and Cittadubbhaka**

*Tāhaṁ cittakaliṁ brūmi, Taṁ brūmi cittadubbhakaṁ.*

The accusation now becomes explicit.

The speaker declares Citta to be *cittakali* and *cittadubbhaka*. These are forceful expressions of condemnation. Citta is identified as the source of defeat and as a treacherous betrayer.

Why does the speaker condemn Citta so severely?

Because Citta has betrayed its proper spiritual responsibility. The Soul-Spirit has entered the path of renunciation for liberation, but Citta continues to pursue worldly gratification. Rather than supporting the liberation of Attā, it diverts the individual\'s existence towards purposes that strengthen worldly bondage.

The betrayal is therefore not simply that Citta becomes distracted. It is that Citta directs the individual\'s life against the very objective for which the renunciant has undertaken the holy life.

In the Arahanist understanding, this is the fundamental conflict between Citta and Attā: the Mind-Heart has the capacity to pursue worldly purposes, even when those purposes contradict the liberation of the Soul-Spirit.

**4. The Victim: Attā Compelled into the Service of Pride and Vanity**

*Satthā te dullabho laddho, Mānatthe maṁ niyojayī.*

The final line delivers the central accusation.

The speaker first recalls that a rare Teacher has been obtained. Gautama\'s dispensation is an extraordinary opportunity to undertake the path leading beyond worldly bondage. The individual has encountered the teaching and entered the renunciant life with the possibility of complete liberation.

Yet Citta has continued to pursue *māna*---pride, conceit, and vanity.

The expression *mānatthe maṁ niyojayī* is particularly important. The first-person *maṁ*, "me," is the object of the accusation. In the Arahanist courtroom interpretation, this "me" is Attā, the Soul-Spirit.

The meaning of the accusation can therefore be stated directly:

**The Soul-Spirit is complaining that Citta has compelled it to serve Citta\'s own pride and vanity.**

Citta pursues recognition, superiority, admiration, status, and personal gratification. In doing so, it makes the Soul-Spirit participate in worldly ambitions that have nothing to do with liberation. The Soul-Spirit, which should be moving towards freedom from worldly bondage, is instead made to serve the desires and conceit sustained by Citta.

This is the betrayal.

The individual may have renounced household life, received the rare teaching, and adopted the external discipline of a bhikkhu. Nevertheless, if Citta remains governed by pride and worldly gratification, the deeper spiritual objective remains obstructed.

A person can therefore wear the robes of renunciation while Citta continues to pursue the very world that renunciation is intended to leave behind.

**The Internal Courtroom: Attā Against Citta**

The Arahanist interpretation of this verse reveals four dimensions of the conflict:

**First: Attā has a definite spiritual objective.**\
The Soul-Spirit seeks liberation from worldly bondage through the teaching of Gautama the Supreme Arahant. The attainment of the rare Teacher provides the opportunity to fulfil this purpose.

**Second: Citta pursues worldly gratification.**\
Instead of directing its activities towards liberation, the Mind-Heart continues to follow attraction, pleasure, recognition, and conceit. Its attention remains occupied by worldly manifestations.

**Third: Citta commandeers the individual\'s existence.**\
Because Citta governs the individual\'s engagement with the world, its worldly orientation affects the entire course of life. The Soul-Spirit is compelled to participate in the pursuits generated by Citta. This is the force of the accusation expressed through *maṁ niyojayī*: Citta makes the Soul-Spirit serve purposes that perpetuate worldly bondage.

**Fourth: Attā brings the betrayal into judgment.**\
The expressions *cittakali* and *cittadubbhaka* expose the gravity of Citta\'s conduct. The Mind-Heart has failed in its proper responsibility and has become an obstacle to the liberation of the Soul-Spirit.

The courtroom scene is therefore an account of the conflict within the individual between the liberation objective of Attā and the worldly pursuits of Citta.

**Reclaiming Citta for the Liberation of Attā**

Anūpama\'s testimony also reveals why the training of Citta is indispensable.

Citta must be disciplined so that it no longer directs the individual\'s existence towards worldly gratification. Its activities must be brought under the direction of the liberation objective of Attā. The Mind-Heart must become established upon its innermost Soul-Spirit core rather than remain occupied with external worldly manifestations.

This transformation unfolds through the progressive training of the renunciant.

**Sīla** establishes restraint over bodily and verbal conduct and prevents the continual reinforcement of worldly desires.

**Nibbidā** brings disenchantment with worldly existence. Citta recognizes the limitations of worldly attractions and changes its establishment from external worldly manifestations towards Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta). This change prevents the continued influx of new worldly defilements into Attā.

**Samādhi** brings Citta into firm collection and establishment upon Attā. The Mind-Heart becomes stable and ceases its restless pursuit of external gratification.

**Tapas** supplies the primordial spiritual force of ascetic purification. Through sustained spiritual absorption and purification, existing defilements are progressively removed.

**Virāga** marks the fading of worldly passion. As the influence of rāga, dosa, and moha is eradicated, the Soul-Spirit is progressively purified from the conditions of worldly bondage.

**Vimutti** is the completion of this process: the liberation of Attā from worldly bondage, culminating in Arahantship.

The proper relationship between Citta and Attā is thus restored. Citta no longer commandeers the individual\'s existence for the pursuit of pride, vanity, and sensory gratification. It becomes firmly established upon Attā and fulfils its proper role in the liberation of the Soul-Spirit.

**Conclusion: The Mind-Heart Must Serve Liberation**

Anūpama\'s verse exposes a profound danger within the renunciant life. Even after obtaining the rare Teacher and entering the dispensation of Gautama, Citta can continue to pursue worldly gratification and compel the Soul-Spirit to serve its pride and vanity.

The accusation is therefore directed at the very centre of spiritual training. The renunciant must recognize whether Citta is serving the liberation of Attā or continuing to serve the world.

The purpose of spiritual discipline is to reclaim the direction of Citta. Through Sīla, Nibbidā, Samādhi, and Tapas, the Mind-Heart must be brought into proper establishment upon Attā. Through Virāga, the remaining worldly passion and defilements are eradicated. Through Vimutti, the Soul-Spirit is completely liberated from worldly bondage.

The courtroom scene ends with a decisive demand: **Citta must cease betraying Attā. The Mind-Heart must fulfil its proper responsibility and become established upon the Soul-Spirit, serving the attainment of complete liberation.**

### 5.4. Citta and the Five Aggregates

The elders of the early Arahanist Saṅgha repeatedly speak about the physical body, the five aggregates, and the necessity of relinquishing attachment to conditioned worldly existence. Their verses reveal an essential aspect of the liberation training: the renunciant must understand the nature of the physical and mental manifestations through which worldly existence is experienced, and must cease treating these manifestations as the true Soul-Spirit.

The five aggregates (*pañcakkhandhā*) are rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, and viññāṇa. They constitute the conditioned framework of worldly existence. The physical body belongs to rūpa, while the remaining aggregates concern feeling, perception, formations, and sense-generated consciousness.

These aggregates arise and operate within the worldly order. They undergo change, remain subject to conditions, and are bound by the laws governing worldly manifestation. They cannot provide an enduring foundation for the Soul-Spirit.

The central task of the renunciant is therefore to understand the aggregates according to their actual nature, relinquish attachment to them, and prevent Citta from remaining established upon these conditioned manifestations.

**1. The Physical Body and the Soul-Spirit**

The physical body is the most immediate manifestation through which the individual encounters the world. It experiences pleasure and pain, hunger and thirst, attraction and aversion, youth and old age, sickness and death. It is subject to the conditions of the physical universe and cannot escape its laws.

Yet the physical body must be distinguished from Attā, the Soul-Spirit.

In the Arahanist understanding, Attā is the essential spiritual core of the individual, while the physical body is a worldly manifestation through which the Soul-Spirit operates. The body is therefore not the entirety of the being. Its appearance, condition, and eventual dissolution do not define the full reality of Attā.

This distinction is important because worldly attachment frequently begins with the physical body. Citta becomes fascinated by appearance, beauty, strength, sexual attraction, physical comfort, and the preservation of bodily identity. It then directs the individual\'s activities towards maintaining and gratifying the body, treating its temporary condition as though it were the ultimate object of concern.

The elders repeatedly confront this tendency through direct contemplation of the body\'s actual constitution.

**Theragāthā 1.18 --- Siṅgālapitā**

*Ahu buddhassa dāyādo, Bhikkhu bhesakaḷāvane;*\
*Kevalaṁ aṭṭhisaññāya, Aphari pathaviṁ imaṁ;*\
*Maññehaṁ kāmarāgaṁ so, Khippameva pahissatī ti.*

**Translation:**

There was an heir of the Buddha, a bhikkhu in the Bhesakaḷā forest. Through the contemplation of the skeleton (*aṭṭhisaññā*), he pervaded this entire earth. I believe that he will very quickly abandon sensual passion (*kāmarāgaṁ*).

This elder describes a renunciant who employs contemplation of the skeletal structure of the body with a fruit of overcoming sensual passion. The significance of this practice is direct: the body that Citta finds attractive must be examined according to its actual physical constitution.

The contemplation of bones, flesh, organs, and the other bodily components dismantles the fascination that arises from external appearance. Beauty and sexual attraction lose their power when the body is understood beyond its outward presentation.

The purpose is not to develop hostility towards the body. The purpose is to remove the delusion and attachment through which Citta becomes captivated by physical form.

The body remains a necessary vehicle for undertaking the renunciant training, but its temporary condition must never become the foundation of spiritual identity.

Skeleton Contemplation (*aṭṭhisaññā*) is a core meditation for ancient renunciants known as Piṇḍastha Dhyāna (Meditation on the Physical Body/Frame) that lead directly into **Rūpātīta Dhyāna** (Formless / Arūpa Meditation). In this framework, the **skeleton** serves as the crucial **bridge or threshold**.

This system progresses through five distinct visualizations (*dhāraṇā*s) that lead directly into **Rūpātīta Dhyāna** (Formless / Arūpa Meditation):

1.  **Pārthivī Dhāraṇā (Earth Visualization):** The practitioner visualizes a vast ocean of milk, a thousand-petaled golden lotus blooms, topped with a pristine white throne (*Siddhāsana*). The practitioner sitting calmly on this lotus throne at the center of the cosmos, fully embodied, pure, and unshakeable.

2.  **Āgneyī Dhāraṇā (Fire Visualization):**

    The focus shifts from the outer surroundings (the cosmic ocean and lotus) to the **internal transformation of that seated body**:

    **The Ignition:** While sitting on that same lotus, a subtle seed-syllable (typically the fire mantra *Raṃ*) is visualized in the navel center (*nābhi-kamala*).

    **The Consumption:** A smokeless yogic fire ignites from this navel seed-syllable within the practitioner who was seated there all along. The flame spreads outward from the inside of the body, burning away the fleshy, physical elements---the skin, blood, organs, and flesh---from that very frame, until only the bare, white skeletal structure (*asthi-pañjara*) remains sitting on the lotus.

3.  **Mārutī Dhāraṇā (Air / Wind Visualization):**

    The Movement: Sitting as the bare, white skeletal frame (asthi-pañjara) atop the lotus, a powerful wind (ignited by the seed-syllable Yaṃ) arises at the throat or heart center.

    The Action: This cosmic gale blows violently against the burnt remains and ashes of the physical body. It sweeps away all residual ash and incinerated material from around the skeleton, scattering them into the infinite cosmic expanse until no trace of burnt flesh remains anywhere in space. The shining, purified skeleton stands entirely clear.

4.  **Vāruṇī Dhāraṇā (Water Visualization):**

    The Movement: As the wind subsides, vast rain clouds (associated with the seed-syllable Vaṃ and the cosmic moon/water element) gather above in the sky.

    The Action: A torrential shower of divine nectar and pure water falls across the entire cosmos. It completely washes the skeletal frame, dissolving any microscopic particles or lingering burnt residue into nothingness. The entire space---and the frame sitting at its center---is rendered pristine, cool, and thoroughly purified.

5.  **Tattvarūpatā / Svastha Dhāraṇā (Self-Abidance):**

    The Transformation: Having served its purpose as the threshold for dismantling physical attachment, the mental image of the skeletal frame itself fully dissolves.

    The Final Realization (Rūpātīta / Arūpa): What remains seated on that golden lotus is no longer a physical body or a bone structure, but the Jīva (the radiant, formless soul). The soul shines with infinite knowledge (Kevala-jñāna), completely unbound by rūpa (form), flesh, or asthi (bones), pervading the space (Lokākāśa) in absolute purity and detachment.

By concentrating on the skeletal structure (*asthi-pañjara*) during the fire and wind stages, the meditator strips away all transient, fleshy attributes. Once the visualization burns and dissolves the skeletal frame itself, the practitioner transcends all physical forms (*rūpa*) to enter **Rūpātīta (Arūpa) Dhyāna**---where consciousness (*citta/jīva*) pervades the entire universe (*Lokākāśa*) without any physical shape, bones, or boundary.

**Verse References from Jain Texts**

Below are 5 examples from classical Jain texts detailing how the meditation moves from the physical frame/skeleton, through cosmic pervasion, to the formless (*arūpa*) realization:

**1. *Jñānārṇava* of Ācārya Śubhacandra (Chapter 27, Verses 12--15)**

*Explains the fire visualization reducing the body to bare bones, followed by their total dissolution.*

*dagdhvā tadakhilaṃ dehaṃ paśyet kevalamasthikam \| tato vātena vidhūmāgner bhasma tat pravilīyate \|\| tadrūpaṃ tu parityajya svarūpaṃ paramātmanaḥ \| amūrtaṃ cinmayaṃ dhyāyet sarva-loka-vyāpakaṃ vibhum \|\|*

Having burned (*dagdhvā*) that (*tat*) entire (*akhilaṃ*) body (*dehaṃ*), one should see (*paśyet*) only (*kevalam*) the bone-structure (*asthikam*). Then (*tatas*), by the wind (*vātena*), that (*tat*) ash (*bhasma*) of the smokeless fire (*vidhūm-āgneḥ*) is dissolved (*pravilīyate*). Abandoning (*parityajya*) indeed (*tu*) that form (*tad-rūpaṃ*), one should meditate (*dhyāyet*) \[upon\] the own-nature/inherent form (*sva-rūpaṃ*) of the Supreme *ātmana* (*paramātmanaḥ*)---formless (*amūrtaṃ*), made of awareness / consisting of consciousness (*cin-mayaṃ*), pervading the whole universe (*sarva-loka-vyāpakaṃ*), \[and\] all-pervasive (*vibhum*).

**2. *Yogāśāstra* of Ācārya Hemacandra (Prakāśa 4, Verses 68--71)**

*Details the transition from Pārthivī (Earth) and Asthi (Skeleton) visualization to the formless expanse.*

*asthi-pañjara-mātraṃ tu saṃhṛtya kramaśo dhyātā \| ātmānaṃ kevalaṃ dhyāyet siddha-rūpaṃ nirañjanam \|\| arūpamasparśamaśabdamagrahītuṃ na śakyate \| sarvagāmī yathākāśaṃ tathātmā dhyāyate muniḥ \|\|*

Withdrawing (*saṃhṛtya*) step-by-step (*kramaśas*) indeed (*tu*) the bare (*mātraṃ*) bone-cage (*asthi-pañjara*), the meditator (*dhyātā*) should meditate (*dhyāyet*) \[upon\] the *ātmāna* alone (*kevalaṃ*)---\[having the\] perfected-form (*siddha-rūpaṃ*), unstained (*nirañjanam*). Formless (*arūpam*), touchless (*asparśam*), soundless (*aśabdam*), to be grasped (*agrahītuṃ*) \[it is\] not (*na*) possible (*śakyate*). Everywhere-going (*sarvagāmī*) just as (*yathā*) space (*ākāśaṃ*), so (*tathā*) the *ātmā* is meditated upon (*dhyāyate*) \[by\] the *muni*.

**3. *Dravyasagraha* of Ācārya Nemicandra (Verse 49)**

*Focuses on transcending physical meditation elements (like bones and earth) into pure consciousness.*

*ṇavi sadda-rūa-gandho ṇavi rasa-phāsā ya jassa ṇiccamhi \| arūvamaceyaṇaṃ vā ceyaṇa-bhāvaṃ tu taṃ jāṇe \|\|*

Not indeed (*ṇavi*) sound (*sadda*) - form (*rūa*) - smell (*gandho*), not indeed (*ṇavi*) taste (*rasa*) - touch (*phāsā*) and (*ya*) of which (*jassa*) eternally (*ṇiccamhi*), formless (*arūvam*), un-conscious (*aceyaṇaṃ*) \[in physical matter\], or (*vā*) \[having\] the consciousness-nature (*ceyaṇa-bhāvaṃ*) indeed (*tu*), know (*jāṇe*) that (*taṃ*) \[ātmāna / jīva\].

**4. *Samayasāra* of Ācārya Kundakunda (Verse 50)**

*Contemplating the soul as formless and all-pervading, completely distinct from bones and elemental matter.*

*aruvimamuttiṃ cedaṃ aṭṭhi-maṃsādi-vippamukkaṃ ya \| jo passai appāṇaṃ so lahai ṇivāṇa-parama-sukham \|\|*

Formless (*aruvim*), non-corporeal (*amuttiṃ*), and (*cedaṃ*) completely freed from (*vippamukkaṃ*) bone (*aṭṭhi*), flesh (*maṃsa*), etc. (*ādi*), who (*jo*) beholds (*passai*) the appāṇaṃ (*appāṇaṃ*), he (*so*) obtains (*lahai*) the supreme bliss of Nibbāna (*ṇivāṇa-parama-sukham*).

**5. *Pañcāstikāyasāra* of Ācārya Kundakunda (Verse 27)**

*Pervasion of consciousness (citta/jīva) across space once physical objects (bones/body) are dissolved.*

*logāgāse savvattha jīva-davvaṃ aṇāi-ṇihaṇaṃ ya \| arūva-sattā-sahidaṃ dhijjai ṇāṇīhiṃ ṇicca-kālaṃ \|\|*

In cosmic space (*logāgāse*), everywhere (*savvattha*), the jīva-substance (*jīva-davvaṃ*), beginningless and endless (*aṇāi-ṇihaṇaṃ*), and (*ya*), endowed with formless-existence (*arūva-sattā-sahidaṃ*), is meditated upon (*dhijjai*) by the wise (*ṇāṇīhiṃ*) eternally (*ṇicca-kālaṃ*).

**2. The Five Aggregates as Conditioned Worldly Manifestations**

The distinction between Attā and the physical body must be extended to the entire framework of the five aggregates.

The body is only one aggregate, rūpa. The other four---vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, and viññāṇa---also belong to the conditioned structure of worldly existence.

Feeling arises in dependence upon conditions. Perception operates through contact with phenomena. Formations arise and change according to conditions. Sense-generated consciousness occurs in relation to the corresponding sensory conditions.

Citta engages with these manifestations and can become attached to them. It can pursue pleasant feelings, become disturbed by unpleasant feelings, construct perceptions around worldly identity, follow formations, and remain occupied with sense-generated consciousness.

When Citta becomes established upon these conditioned manifestations, the individual continues to participate in the processes of worldly attachment and bondage.

The five aggregates must therefore be understood in their proper place. They are conditioned manifestations to be comprehended and relinquished. They must not be mistaken for the entirety of Attā, the Soul-Spirit.

The Arahanist understanding of *Anattā* is central to this examination. Gautama repeatedly challenges the practitioner to examine whether any of the five aggregates can rightly be regarded as one\'s possession, one\'s identity, or one\'s true Soul-Spirit. The mistaken identification is expressed through the formula:

*Etaṁ mama, eso\'hamasmi, eso me attā.*

"This is mine; this I am; this is my Soul-Spirit."

This formula expresses the attachment that sustains worldly bondage. When Citta regards the aggregates in this manner, it becomes bound to conditioned phenomena and remains subject to their arising, alteration, and dissolution.

The corresponding formula of relinquishment is:

*Netaṁ mama, nesohamasmi, na meso attā.*

"This is not mine; this I am not; this is not my Soul-Spirit."

The practitioner applies this contemplation to each of the five aggregates, recognizing that none of these conditioned manifestations constitutes the true Soul-Spirit. This is the proper function of *Anattā* in the Arahanist understanding: to remove mistaken identification with the aggregates and free Citta from its attachment to worldly manifestations.

Through this comprehension, disenchantment with worldly existence develops. Citta relinquishes its dependence upon conditioned phenomena and becomes established upon Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta). This marks an essential movement in the path towards Samādhi, Virāga, and final liberation.

**Rejection of Both \"No Soul-Spirit\" and \"Soul-Spirit is the Aggregates\" (*Sabbāsava Sutta*, MN 2):** In MN 2, the Buddha explicitly classifies the dogmatic assertion \"I have no soul-spirit\" (*natthi me attā*) as a thicket of views (*diṭṭhigahana*) and a fetter (*diṭṭhibandhana*) that locks a person into worldly bondage: *\"Tassa evaṁ ayoniso manasikaroto channaṁ diṭṭhīnaṁ aññatarā diṭṭhi uppajjati: \'Atthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati; \'Natthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati\... Ayaṁ vuccatābhikkhave, diṭṭhigahaṇaṁ diṭṭhikantāraṁ\... diṭṭhisaṁyojana saṁyutto.\"*

*"When one attends unwisely in this way, one of six views arises as true and established: The view \'I have a soul-spirit\' arises\... or the view \'I have no soul-spirit\' arises\... This is called a thicket of views, a wilderness of views, a contortion of views\... bound by the fetter of views."*

**Analysis:** The canonical text explicitly rejects the annihilationist/ materialist stance (\"I have no soul-spirit / there is no soul\") as an unwise, speculative trap. Denying spiritual reality entirely keeps the mind trapped within the worldly framework.

To uncover why the statement **\"I have a soul-spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)** is also classified as an unwisely (not touching the source/yoni) attended view (*ayoniso manasikāra*). It boils down to **who the \"I\" is** and **the direction of ownership**.

**The Trap of \"I Have a Soul-Spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)**

When someone says **\"I have a soul-spirit,\"** the sentence grammatically and conceptually sets up a false subject-object relationship: **The False \"I\":** The \"I\" claiming ownership in this statement is actually the **ego/personality rooted in the physical reality and five aggregates** (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*). **The False \"Object\":** It treats the true spiritual essence (*attā*) as a secondary possession---a \"thing\" that this worldly body-mind complex *owns* or *possesses* (like saying \"I have a car\" or \"I have a house\").

This is an inverted, upside-down understanding. It places the worldly manifestation in the driver\'s seat and treats the transcendent soul-spirit as an attribute or accessory belonging to the physical person.

**Correct Understanding: The Citta / Soul-Spirit as the True Subject**

To correct this inversion we need to place the true subject back where it belongs: **The True Subject:** **I am the soul-spirit (the purified Citta). The Manifestation / Entrapment:** **I have a temporary manifestation / vehicle in this physical reality (the five aggregates).**

Under this understanding: **The Physical/Mental Equipment is the Burden:** The physical body and cognitive machinery are physical/mental phenomena manifested in this physical world (*loka*). They are subject to cause, effect, decay, and death (*dukkha*). **The Identification Error:** Delusion occurs when the true subject (the Citta) misidentifies with its temporary manifestation, thinking: *\"This physical body/mind is me, this is mine, this is my self.\"* **The Trap:** Thinking \"I (this person) have a soul-spirit\" keeps you grounded in the physical identity. Recognizing \"I am the soul-spirit trapped in/manifesting through this temporary world-bound equipment\" restores the true scale of spiritual reality.

**How the Pāḷi Canon Frames This Correction**

This exact distinction is illustrated in the **Bhāra Sutta (SN 22.22)** through the analogy of the **Burden and the Bearer**: **The Burden (*Bhāra*):** The five aggregates---the physical body, feelings, perceptions, choices, and sensory consciousness---are the worldly burden. **The Bearer (*Bhārahāra*):** The individual spiritual entity (the soul-spirit / Citta) is the bearer carrying the burden.

The error of saying *\"I (the body/mind) have a soul-spirit\"* is equivalent to the **burden claiming it owns the bearer**. The correct realization is recognizing that the bearer (the Citta / soul-spirit) is currently carrying an external, heavy, worldly burden. Through sense restraint, disenchantment, Samadhi, and Paññā, the bearer simply **drops the burden** (*bhāranikkhepanaṁ*), severs all worldly attachment, and realizes ultimate liberation in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**The Analogy of Carrying the Worldly Load (*Bhāra Sutta*, SN 22.22)**

In SN 22.22, the Buddha explicitly separates the five aggregates from the true bearer (*bhārahāra*) who carries and eventually drops them: *\"Bhārā ha pañcakkhandhā, bhārahāro ca puggalo; Bhārādānaṁ dukkhaṁ loke, bhāranikkhepanaṁ sukhaṁ.\"*

*"The five aggregates are indeed the burden (bhāra); the individual bearer (bhārahāra) is the carrier of the burden. Taking up the burden is suffering in the world; laying down the burden is supreme bliss."*

**Analysis:** The five aggregates constitute the worldly burden (*bhāra*). The spiritual essence (the Citta / person) is the bearer (*bhārahāra*). Equating the bearer directly with the burden---or claiming the burden is all that exists---is the precise reductionist mistake that prevents the Citta from dropping the load, achieving dispassion (*virāga*), and realizing ultimate liberation (*Vimutti*) in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**3. Seeing the Aggregates According to Reality**

The elder Paviṭṭha expresses the completion of this understanding in a concise declaration.

**Theragāthā 1.87 --- Paviṭṭha**

*Khandhā diṭṭhā yathābhūtaṁ, Bhavā sabbe padālitā;*\
*Vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro, Natthi dāni punabbhavo ti.*

**Translation:** The aggregates have been seen according to their true nature (*khandhā diṭṭhā yathābhūtaṁ*); all worldly existences have been shattered (*bhavā sabbe padālitā*); the cycle of birth and worldly wandering has been exhausted (*vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro*); there is now no further worldly becoming (*natthi dāni punabbhavo*).

The opening declaration is particularly important: *khandhā diṭṭhā yathābhūtaṁ*---the aggregates have been seen according to their true nature.

This is the direct comprehension required of the renunciant. The five aggregates must be understood as conditioned worldly manifestations, subject to arising, alteration, and dissolution. Their apparent stability and their capacity to provide temporary gratification must not obscure their actual nature.

The elder then declares that all worldly existences have been shattered. This is a decisive statement. The comprehension of the aggregates is connected with the destruction of the conditions sustaining further worldly manifestation.

The verse therefore presents a complete movement: seeing the aggregates according to reality, bringing worldly existence to an end, exhausting the cycle of birth, and eliminating further worldly becoming.

In the Arahanist framework, this is the result of Citta no longer remaining established upon worldly manifestations. Through disenchantment and relinquishment, Citta ceases to sustain the attachment that binds Attā to continued worldly existence.

**4. The Five Aggregates Remain, but Their Roots Are Cut**

An especially significant declaration appears in the verse of the elder Sāmidatta.

**Theragāthā 1.90 --- Sāmidatta**

*Pañcakkhandhā pariññātā, Tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā;*\
*Vikkhīṇo jātisaṁsāro, Natthi dāni punabbhavo ti.*

**Translation:**

The five aggregates have been completely comprehended (*pañcakkhandhā pariññātā*); they remain, but their roots have been cut (*tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā*); the cycle of birth and worldly wandering has been exhausted; there is now no further worldly becoming.

The expression *tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā* deserves particular attention.

The five aggregates remain, but their roots have been cut.

This declaration distinguishes the continued presence of the present conditioned manifestations from the causes that would sustain further worldly becoming. The Arahant does not need to deny the existence of the present body or the remaining aggregates. They continue according to their existing conditions, but the roots sustaining further bondage have been severed.

This is an important feature of liberation. The present physical body can continue until the completion of its remaining lifespan, while the causes that would generate another worldly manifestation have been eradicated.

The verse therefore prevents a misunderstanding of the liberation process. The destruction of worldly bondage does not require the immediate disappearance of the present aggregates. What must be destroyed are the roots sustaining further worldly becoming.

In the Arahanist interpretation, these roots are associated with the defilements and attachments that keep Citta established upon worldly manifestations and bind Attā to continued material existence.

The elder\'s declaration is therefore a testimony to completed liberation: the aggregates remain, but their power to generate further worldly existence has been cut off.

**5. The Physical Body as the Final Manifestation**

The distinction between the Soul-Spirit and its physical manifestation becomes particularly clear in the declaration attributed to Kassapa the Prince.

**Theragāthā 2.41 --- Kassapa the Prince**

*Aho buddhā aho dhammā, Aho no satthu sampadā;*\
*Yattha etādisaṁ dhammaṁ, Sāvako sacchikāhiti.*

*Asaṅkheyyesu kappesu, Sakkāyādhigatā ahū;*\
*Tesamayaṁ pacchimako, Carimoyaṁ samussayo;*\
*Jātimaraṇasaṁsāro, Natthi dāni punabbhavo.*

**Translation:**

Wonderful are the Awakened Ones! Wonderful is the Dhamma! Wonderful is the magnificent attainment of our Teacher, through whose teaching a disciple can directly realize such a profound Dhamma.

Through incalculable eons, the acquisition of worldly bodily existence continued. Of those, this is the final one; this is the last physical aggregate. The cycle of birth, ageing, and death has ended. There is now no further worldly becoming.

The second stanza is particularly significant for understanding the relationship between Attā and the physical body.

The expression *carimoyaṁ samussayo* identifies the present bodily aggregate as the final one. The physical body is a manifestation of the Attā within worldly existence, and the Arahant declares that no further bodily manifestation will follow.

The body is therefore not the final measure of the Soul-Spirit. Its present existence has a definite termination, but the cessation of further bodily manifestation does not mean the annihilation of Attā.

Rather, the causes that would bring about another worldly manifestation have been eradicated.

The elder\'s declaration connects the completion of the liberation training with the termination of further birth and death. The present body is the final physical manifestation, and the Soul-Spirit is no longer bound to another cycle of worldly existence.

This is why the renunciant must distinguish between the temporary physical manifestation and the deeper spiritual reality of Attā. Attachment to the body and its continued worldly existence perpetuates bondage. The eradication of the causes of further manifestation brings that bondage to an end.

**6. Citta Must Relinquish the Five Aggregates**

The preceding verses reveal a consistent direction in the training of the elders.

Siṅgālapitā contemplates the body\'s skeletal nature to overcome sensual passion. Paviṭṭha declares that the aggregates have been seen according to reality and that worldly existence has been shattered. Sāmidatta declares that the five aggregates remain, but their roots have been cut. Kassapa the Prince identifies the present bodily manifestation as the final one.

Together, these declarations illuminate the relationship between Citta, the aggregates, and Attā.

Citta is the Mind-Heart through which the individual engages with the conditioned world. When it becomes attached to the aggregates, it continually pursues their pleasures, identifies with their conditions, and remains occupied with their changing manifestations.

This attachment must be relinquished.

The renunciant undertakes a systematic transformation of Citta\'s establishment.

Through Sīla, worldly conduct is restrained, preventing the continual reinforcement of attachment and defilement.

Through Nibbidā, Citta becomes disenchanted with worldly existence. It recognizes the conditioned nature of the aggregates and changes its establishment from external worldly manifestations towards Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta). This prevents the continued influx of new worldly defilements into Attā.

Through Samādhi, Citta becomes firmly collected and established upon Attā. Its restless pursuit of worldly manifestations is brought under control.

Through Tapas, the existing defilements are progressively purified and eradicated. The Soul-Spirit is no longer continually burdened by the influx of new worldly defilements, while the defilements already present are subjected to the force of spiritual purification.

Through Virāga, worldly passion fades and the remaining defilements are eradicated.

Through Vimutti, Attā is completely liberated from worldly bondage.

The five aggregates are therefore comprehended and relinquished as the conditioned framework of worldly existence. Citta ceases to make them the foundation of its activities and becomes established upon its essential Soul-Spirit core.

**Conclusion: Beyond the Conditioned Manifestations**

The elders do not regard the physical body and the five aggregates as the ultimate destination of spiritual life. Their verses repeatedly direct the renunciant towards the comprehension and relinquishment of conditioned worldly existence.

The body is subject to ageing and death. Feelings change. Perceptions arise and disappear. Formations are conditioned. Sense-generated consciousness operates through its corresponding conditions. None of these manifestations provides an enduring foundation for the Soul-Spirit.

The renunciant must therefore cease treating the aggregates as the true identity of the being. Through the understanding expressed in *Anattā*, Citta relinquishes identification with these conditioned phenomena and becomes established upon Attā.

This is the deeper significance of the elders\' declarations. The aggregates can remain while their roots have been cut. The present body can reach the end of its lifespan without generating another worldly manifestation. The Soul-Spirit can attain liberation from the cycle of birth and death.

**The purpose of understanding the five aggregates is ultimately to free Attā from attachment to worldly manifestation. Citta must recognize the conditioned nature of the aggregates, relinquish its dependence upon them, and become firmly established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit.**

### 5.5. The Purification of Citta

The purification of Citta is the central work of the renunciant path. Having recognized the wandering nature of the untrained Mind-Heart, understood its attachment to the five aggregates, and begun to relinquish its dependence upon worldly manifestations, the practitioner must cultivate Citta until it becomes concentrated, unified, purified, and ultimately liberated.

The elders of the Theragāthā repeatedly describe this transformation through expressions that reveal the condition of the Mind-Heart at different depths of spiritual attainment. Four particularly important terms are *susamāhita*, *ekagga*, *parisuddha*, and *vimutta*. These expressions illuminate the movement from a scattered and worldly Citta towards a collected, purified, and liberated Mind-Heart.

**Susamāhita: The Mind-Heart Firmly Established in** *samādhi*

*Susamāhita* describes a Citta that is thoroughly collected, well-concentrated, and firmly established in Samādhi. The prefix *su-* intensifies the meaning of *samāhita*, expressing a condition of thorough and accomplished concentration.

An important testimony appears in the first verse of the Theragāthā, spoken by Elder Subhūti:

*Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā, Vassa deva yathāsukhaṁ;*\
*Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ, Ātāpī viharāmi vassa devā ti.*

\"My hut is covered, comfortable, and sheltered from the wind. Rain, O heavenly one, fall as you please. My Mind-Heart is thoroughly concentrated and liberated; I dwell possessing the primordial spiritual force of Tapas.\"

Subhūti\'s declaration is especially significant because he places *susamāhitaṁ* and *vimuttaṁ* together in the same description of Citta. His Mind-Heart is not merely calm or temporarily undisturbed. It has become firmly established in concentration and has reached liberation.

The image of the sheltered hut provides a further dimension to his declaration. Just as the physical dwelling is protected against external conditions, the well-concentrated Citta is no longer scattered by worldly disturbances. Its stability arises from the accomplishment of spiritual training.

The concluding expression *ātāpī viharāmi* also deserves attention. Subhūti describes himself as dwelling with ardour and the force of Tapas. In the Arahanist understanding, Tapas is the primordial spiritual force of ascetic purification through which existing defilements are progressively burned away. The purified condition of Citta is therefore associated with sustained spiritual purification, rather than concentration alone.

**Ekagga: The One-Pointed Mind-Heart**

*Ekagga* expresses the condition of one-pointedness. The Mind-Heart is no longer dispersed among competing objects, attractions, and impulses. Its activity has become unified and directed towards a single spiritual establishment.

An important parallel appears in the early Jain renunciant tradition, in the discussion of *Cittassa Ovahāṇa*. It explains that withdrawal of mental activity produces *ekagga-mmaṇa*, a one-pointed mind. When the mind becomes one-pointed, it gains the capacity to restrain its engagement with worldly phenomena.

This parallel is relevant to the wider Indian renunciant heritage. The cultivation of a unified Mind-Heart was an important feature of ascetic purification among the renunciant traditions of ancient India.

Within the Arahanist path, one-pointedness has a definite direction. Citta must cease scattering itself among external worldly manifestations and become steadily established upon Attā, the innermost Soul-Spirit core. Its concentration is therefore connected with the transformation of its fundamental establishment.

A scattered Citta remains vulnerable to rāga, dosa, and moha. It follows one attraction after another, repeatedly becoming involved in worldly conditions. An *ekagga* Citta possesses the collectedness necessary to resist this movement and sustain its spiritual orientation.

One-pointedness is consequently an essential foundation for the deeper purification of the Mind-Heart.

**Parisuddha: The Purified Mind-Heart**

*Parisuddha* means purified, thoroughly cleansed, or made pure. It describes a Citta from which the hindrances and defiling conditions have been progressively removed.

A particularly important canonical expression appears in the description of the fourth jhāna and the subsequent development of higher knowledge. The *Sāmaññaphala Sutta* (DN 2) describes the condition of the Mind-Heart as:

*Evaṁ samāhite citte parisuddhe pariyodāte anaṅgaṇe vigatūpakkilese mudubhūte kammaniye ṭhite āneñjappatte.*

\"When the Mind-Heart has become thus concentrated, purified, bright, free from blemishes, rid of defilements, pliable, workable, steady, and attained to imperturbability.\"

This description is particularly valuable because it presents several qualities together. The Citta is *samāhita*, thoroughly concentrated; *parisuddha*, purified; *pariyodāta*, bright and radiant; *anaṅgaṇa*, free from blemishes; and *vigatūpakkilesa*, rid of defilements.

These expressions describe a profound transformation of the Mind-Heart. Its former disturbances have been overcome, its condition has become refined, and it has acquired the stability necessary for higher spiritual knowledge.

The passage also shows that purification and concentration are closely connected. The collected Mind-Heart becomes capable of further purification, while the removal of defilements strengthens its stability and clarity.

In the Arahanist understanding, this purification must be considered in relation to the Citta--Attā relationship. When Citta becomes established upon Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta), it ceases continually drawing new worldly defilements into its essential spiritual depth. Through Samādhi and Tapas, the defilements already present are progressively purified.

Thus, *parisuddha* describes the refinement of Citta through the removal of the conditions that sustain worldly bondage.

**Vimutta: The Liberated Mind-Heart**

*Vimutta* means released, unbound, or liberated. It describes the condition of Citta when it has been freed from the worldly fetters and defilements that previously held it in bondage.

Subhūti\'s verse, already examined above, declares:

*Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ.*

\"My Mind-Heart is thoroughly concentrated and liberated.\"

The significance of this declaration lies in the direct relationship between concentration and liberation. Subhūti speaks of a Mind-Heart that has attained both qualities. Its collectedness is no longer merely a preparatory condition; it belongs to the accomplished state of liberation.

**The Testimony of the Elders: The Completion of Purification**

The elders also describe purification through the severance of the roots sustaining worldly existence.

Elder Isidattā declares in Theragāthā 1.120:

*Pañcakkhandhā pariññātā, Tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā;*\
*Dukkhakkhayo anuppatto, Patto me āsavakkhayo ti.*

\"The five aggregates have been completely comprehended. They remain, but their roots have been severed. The destruction of suffering has been reached; the destruction of the defilements has been attained by me.\"

This declaration gives us an important perspective on the completion of spiritual purification. The aggregates may continue to remain as the present conditioned manifestations, but their roots have been severed. The conditions that would sustain further worldly becoming have been eliminated.

The verse therefore connects the comprehension of the aggregates with the destruction of the defilements and the completion of the path.

Considered together, these testimonies reveal the transformation of Citta through the renunciant training:

- *Susamāhita* --- the Mind-Heart becomes thoroughly collected and firmly established.

- *Ekagga* --- the Mind-Heart becomes unified and one-pointed, no longer dispersed among worldly attractions.

- *Parisuddha* --- the Mind-Heart is purified through the removal of defilements and the progressive refinement of its spiritual condition.

- *Vimutta* --- the Mind-Heart is liberated from the worldly fetters that sustained bondage.

These expressions describe interconnected qualities of the accomplished Mind-Heart. They show how the elders understood the purpose of concentration and purification: the complete liberation of Citta from worldly bondage.

**The Citta--Attā Relationship in the Purification of the Mind-Heart**

The Arahanist understanding brings these qualities together through the relationship between Citta and Attā.

Citta encompasses the entire sphere of the Mind-Heart, from its engagement with external worldly manifestations to its deepest spiritual dimension. Attā is its innermost Soul-Spirit core. The central work of purification is therefore the transformation of Citta\'s establishment.

When Citta remains established upon worldly manifestations, it continues to participate in the processes of attachment, desire, and bondage. Through Nibbidā, it becomes disenchanted with worldly existence and changes its establishment towards Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta).

Samādhi then enables Citta to become firmly collected and established upon Attā. Through the force of Tapas, existing defilements are progressively purified. As this purification advances, worldly passion fades through Virāga, and the remaining bonds are eradicated.

The final condition is Vimutti: the liberation of Attā from worldly bondage and the completion of Arahantship.

The testimonies of Subhūti, Bhagu, and Isidattā show that the elders understood the transformation of the Mind-Heart through direct experience. They speak of a Citta that has become thoroughly concentrated, joined to tranquillity, freed from attachment, and liberated.

The purpose of the renunciant training is precisely this transformation. Citta must be mastered, collected, purified, and established upon its essential Soul-Spirit core. When its worldly bondage has been completely overcome, the work of liberation is accomplished.

### 5.6. Citta-vimutti: The Liberation of the Mind-Heart

Throughout the preceding sections, we have examined Citta as the centre of spiritual training: its wandering and susceptibility to worldly attractions, its betrayal to the Soul-Spirit through attachment to pride and vanity, its relationship with the five aggregates, and the process through which it is purified. The testimonies of the elder renunciants now bring this training to its fulfilment. Their declarations reveal what happens when Citta has been thoroughly trained and liberation has been attained.

Among the most direct declarations are those of the elder nuns Mettikā and Dhammā. Both conclude their testimonies with the same expression:

*atha cittaṁ vimucci me* --- "thereupon my citta was fully liberated."

These words are particularly significant. The elders do not merely describe an improvement in their mental condition. They declare the liberation of Citta as the culmination of their renunciant training.

**Mettikā: The Liberation of Citta**

In the Therīgāthā 2.6, Mettikā recounts her experience:

Mettikā --- Therīgāthā 2.6

*Kiñcāpi khomhi dukkhitā, dubbalā gatayobbanā;*\
*Daṇḍamolubbha gacchāmi, pabbataṁ abhirūhiya.*

*Nikkhipitvāna saṅghāṭiṁ, pattakañca nikujjiya;*\
*Nisinnā camhi selamhi, atha cittaṁ vimucci me;*\
*Tisso vijjā anuppattā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan.*

**Translation:**

Even though I am afflicted with dukkha (kiñcāpi khomhi dukkhitā), feeble (dubbalā), my youth long gone (gatayobbanā); Leaning upon a walking stick (daṇḍamolubbha), I go (gacchāmi), ascending up the mountain (pabbataṁ abhirūhiya).
