Having laid down (nikkhipitvāna) my outer monastic robe saṅghāṭi, and having turned my alms-bowl upside down (pattakañca nikujjiya); I sat down upon the mountain rock (nisinnā camhi selamhi), and thereupon my citta was fully liberated (atha cittaṁ vimucci me).

The Three Knowledges have been attained (tisso vijjā anuppattā), the dispensation of the Awakened One has been fulfilled (kataṁ buddhassa sāsanaṁ).

Mettikā\'s testimony is striking because it describes the attainment of liberation in the midst of physical weakness. Her body is afflicted, her strength has diminished, and her youth has passed. She climbs the mountain leaning upon a walking stick. Yet these physical conditions do not prevent the fulfilment of her spiritual training.

Having laid down her outer monastic robe and turned her alms-bowl upside down, she sits upon the mountain rock. It is at this point that she declares: *atha cittaṁ vimucci me* --- thereupon my Citta was fully liberated.

The significance of this declaration lies in the relationship between the training of Citta and the liberation that follows. Mettikā\'s testimony presents liberation as the culmination of renunciant cultivation. Her physical weakness does not determine the condition of her Citta. The decisive achievement is its liberation.

The following declaration confirms the completeness of this attainment: *Tisso vijjā anuppattā, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanaṁ* --- the Three Knowledges have been attained, and the dispensation of the Awakened One has been fulfilled. Her testimony therefore joins the liberation of Citta with the completion of the Buddha\'s dispensation.

**Dhammā: Seeing the Danger in the Body**

A closely related testimony appears in the Therīgāthā 1.17, where the elder nun Dhammā describes her own experience:

Dhammā --- Therīgāthā 1.17

*Piṇḍapātaṁ caritvāna, daṇḍamolubbha dubbalā;*\
*Vedhamānehi gattehi, tattheva nipatiṁ chamā;*\
*Disvā ādīnavaṁ kāye, atha cittaṁ vimucci me ti.*

**Translation:**

Having gone about for alms (piṇḍapātaṁ caritvāna), feeble (dubbalā) and leaning on a staff (daṇḍamolubbha); with trembling limbs (vedhamānehi gattehi), right there I fell down to the ground (tattheva nipatiṁ chamā); having seen the danger (disvā ādīnavaṁ) in the body (kāye), then my citta was liberated (atha cittaṁ vimucci me).

Dhammā\'s testimony parallels Mettikā\'s in several important respects. Both elders describe physical weakness, both rely upon a walking stick, and both conclude with the same declaration of Citta\'s liberation.

Dhammā, however, identifies a particular moment of recognition: *disvā ādīnavaṁ kāye* --- having seen the danger in the body. Her experience of physical vulnerability brings the danger associated with bodily existence into clear recognition. She then declares: *atha cittaṁ vimucci me*.

The body is subject to weakness, trembling, illness, and eventual disintegration. A renunciant who clearly comprehends its danger no longer regards bodily existence as a secure foundation for lasting happiness. This recognition contributes to ***Nibbidā***, the disenchantment through which Citta relinquishes its establishment upon worldly manifestations.

The testimonies of Dhammā and Mettikā reveal a significant common direction in the process of liberation: the recognition of the body\'s unattractive, vulnerable, and deteriorating nature. Dhammā explicitly declares that, having seen the danger in the body (*disvā ādīnavaṁ kāye*), her Citta was liberated. Mettikā describes herself as afflicted, physically feeble, and having passed beyond youth. Her difficult ascent of the mountain vividly depicts the limitations and suffering of bodily existence. These experiences point towards a common spiritual turning: when the practitioner clearly recognizes the true nature of the physical body, the infatuation that sustains worldly attachment to it gives way to disenchantment and aversion.

This aversion is an important movement in the liberation process. Through discernment (*paññā*), the practitioner recognizes the body\'s unattractive nature and becomes disenchanted with bodily existence. This recognition extends beyond the physical form, weakening Citta\'s attachment to worldly manifestations and giving rise to *Nibbidā*. As worldly passion fades through *Virāga*, the defilements binding Attā to worldly existence are progressively eradicated. Through the purification and firm establishment of Citta in *Samādhi*, this process reaches its fulfilment in *Vimutti*. The declarations of both elders, *atha cittaṁ vimucci me*, therefore point towards the liberation that follows this profound recognition of the body\'s true nature. The examination of bodily unattractiveness, the arising of aversion, and their role in the progression towards liberation will be developed in the following chapter.

**Subhūti: Citta Well-Concentrated and Liberated**

The Theragāthā also provides a direct declaration connecting the concentration and liberation of Citta. The elder Subhūti states:

Subhūti --- Theragāthā 1.1

*Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā, Vassa deva yathāsukhaṁ;*\
*Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ, Ātāpī viharāmi vassa devā ti.*

**Translation:**

Covered is my kuti (little hut), comfortable, Wind-sheltered; O rain deva, do as you like. My Citta (mind-heart) is well-concentrated (susamāhitaṁ) and liberated (vimuttaṁ); I live (viharāmi) with the primordial spiritual force: tapas (ātāpī), O rain deva.

Subhūti\'s declaration is particularly valuable because it places two essential qualities of the trained Citta together: *susamāhitaṁ* and *vimuttaṁ*.

*Susamāhitaṁ* describes Citta as thoroughly collected and well-established in Samādhi. *Vimuttaṁ* declares its liberated condition. These expressions are not unrelated descriptions. They reveal the relationship between the purification and concentration of Citta and the attainment of liberation.

Subhūti also declares: *Ātāpī viharāmi* --- he lives with the primordial spiritual force: Tapas. This is important within the Arahanist understanding of renunciant purification. Tapas is the primordial spiritual force through which existing defilements are subjected to purification. It belongs to the disciplined spiritual life that brings Citta towards complete liberation.

Subhūti\'s testimony therefore brings together Samādhi, Tapas, and Vimutti. His Citta is well-concentrated and liberated, while his life remains established in the primordial spiritual force of Tapas.

**Other Declarations of the Completion of Liberation**

The Theragāthā and Therīgāthā contain further declarations that illuminate the completion of renunciant training. Although not every testimony uses the expression *cittaṁ vimucci*, these declarations describe the destruction of defilements and the attainment of the supreme peace.

The elder Isidattā states:

Isidattā --- Theragāthā 1.120

*Pañcakkhandhā pariññātā, tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā;*\
*Dukkhakkhayo anuppatto, patto me āsavakkhayo ti.*

**Translation:**

The five aggregates are fully comprehended (pañcakkhandhā pariññātā), they stand with their roots severed (tiṭṭhanti chinnamūlakā); the destruction of dukkha has been attained (dukkhakkhayo anuppatto), the destruction of defilements has been attained by me (patto me āsavakkhayo).

Isidattā\'s declaration establishes the relationship between the complete comprehension of the five aggregates and the destruction of defilements. The aggregates stand with their roots severed, and the destruction of dukkha has been attained. This is a declaration of completed spiritual training.

The elder Pārāpariya likewise describes the removal of the root of misery:

Pārāpariya --- Theragāthā 1.116

*Chaphassāyatane hitvā, guttadvāro susaṁvuto;*\
*Aghamūlaṁ vamitvāna, patto me āsavakkhayo ti.*

**Translation:**

Having abandoned the six fields of contact (chaphassāyatane hitvā), with guarded doors and thoroughly restrained (guttadvāro susaṁvuto); having vomited out the root of misery (aghamūlaṁ vamitvāna), the destruction of defilements has been attained by me (patto me āsavakkhayo).

The expression *aghamūlaṁ vamitvāna* --- having vomited out the root of misery --- conveys the decisive removal of the source of suffering. The elder\'s declaration of *āsavakkhaya* confirms that the destruction of defilements has been attained.

The elder nun Saṅghā gives another declaration of completed renunciation:

Saṅghā --- Therīgāthā 1.18

*Hitvā ghare pabbajitvā, hitvā puttaṁ pasuṁ piyaṁ;*\
*Hitvā rāgañca dosañca, avijjañca virājiya;*\
*Samūlaṁ taṇhamabbuyha, upasantāmhi nibbutā ti.*

**Translation:**

Having given up household life and renounced (hitvā ghare pabbajitvā), having given up beloved son/ children and livestock (hitvā puttaṁ pasuṁ piyaṁ); having completely faded away rāga, dosa, and also avijja-ignorance (hitvā rāgañca dosañca, avijjañca virājiya); having uprooted craving completely with its root (samūlaṁ taṇha-mabbuyha), I am in the supreme peace \[Nibbāna\] (upasantāmhi) and unbound/ attained Nibbāna (nibbutā).

Saṅghā\'s declaration brings the renunciant journey to its completion. Household attachments have been relinquished, rāga and dosa have faded away, avijjā has been overcome, and craving has been uprooted together with its root. She declares herself to be in the supreme peace and attained Nibbāna.

Together, these testimonies reveal several dimensions of the same final attainment: the liberation of Citta, the severing of the roots of the aggregates, the destruction of defilements, the uprooting of craving, and the attainment of Nibbāna.

**What Does It Mean for Citta to Be Liberated?**

The repeated declaration *cittaṁ vimucci* raises an important question: what exactly has been liberated?

Within the Arahanist understanding, Citta is the Mind-Heart through which the Soul-Spirit engages with the world and undergoes spiritual training. Its worldly establishment exposes it to the continual influence of external manifestations and the arising of rāga, dosa, and moha. When Citta remains established upon worldly objects, the Soul-Spirit continues to be burdened by worldly bondage.

The training of Citta therefore requires a fundamental change in its establishment. Through Nibbidā, Citta becomes disenchanted with worldly existence and establishes itself upon Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta). This marks a decisive change in the direction of spiritual life: Citta ceases to seek its foundation in external worldly manifestations and becomes established upon its own innermost Soul-Spirit core.

Through Samādhi and Tapas, Citta becomes firmly collected and the existing defilements are progressively purified. As Virāga develops, worldly passion fades away. When the defilements have been completely eradicated, Vimutti is attained: the Soul-Spirit is liberated from worldly bondage, and Arahantship is fulfilled.

The declarations of Mettikā and Dhammā, *atha cittaṁ vimucci me*, must be understood within this complete process. Their words identify the liberation of Citta as the decisive culmination of their spiritual training. Subhūti\'s *cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ* further demonstrates the connection between a Citta established in Samādhi and its liberated condition.

The expression *Citta-vimutti* thus carries a central significance in Arahanism. The Mind-Heart has undergone its transformation: from wandering among worldly attractions to becoming established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit; from agitation and susceptibility to defilement to firm concentration and purification; and finally, from worldly bondage to liberation.

Citta is the centre of this spiritual transformation, while Attā is the Soul-Spirit whose liberation constitutes the ultimate purpose of the renunciant path. The liberation of Citta and the liberation of Attā are therefore understood through their proper relationship within the complete training of the human spiritual constitution.

The elders\' declarations are not merely retrospective accounts of spiritual experiences. They are testimonies that the work of renunciation has been accomplished. The Three Knowledges have been attained, the defilements have been destroyed, craving has been uprooted, and the supreme peace of Nibbāna has been realised.

This is the fulfilment of the Buddha\'s dispensation: Citta purified and liberated, and Attā freed from worldly bondage.

### 5.7. Citta and Ātmā: The Arahanist Interpretation

This chapter has examined Citta as the centre of spiritual training: its engagement with worldly manifestations, its susceptibility to defilement, its relationship with the five aggregates, and the process through which it becomes purified, concentrated, and liberated. We must now bring these investigations together by returning to the fundamental relationship between Citta and Ātmā established earlier in this work.

In the Arahanist understanding, Citta and Ātmā refer to two closely related dimensions of human spiritual existence. Citta is the entire Mind-Heart, encompassing the spectrum of conscious experience from its external worldly engagements to its deepest spiritual dimension. Ātmā, or Attā in Pāli, is the innermost Soul-Spirit core of Citta.

This distinction is essential for understanding the practical purpose of Citta\'s purification. The entire Mind-Heart must undergo transformation because its worldly engagements and attachments affect the condition of the Soul-Spirit at its innermost depth. The purification of Citta is therefore indispensable to the liberation of Ātmā.

The verses preserved in the Theragāthā, Therīgāthā, and Dhammapada provide several important points of textual support for this relationship.

**Subhūti: Citta Must Become Established in Samādhi and Liberated**

We may begin with the declaration of the elder Subhūti, already examined in this chapter:

Subhūti --- Theragāthā 1.1

*Channā me kuṭikā sukhā nivātā, Vassa deva yathāsukhaṁ;*\
*Cittaṁ me susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ, Ātāpī viharāmi vassa devā ti.*

**Translation:**

Covered is my kuti (little hut), comfortable, Wind-sheltered; O rain deva, do as you like. My Citta (mind-heart) is well-concentrated (susamāhitaṁ) and liberated (vimuttaṁ); I live (viharāmi) with the primordial spiritual force: tapas (ātāpī), O rain deva.

The significance of this verse extends beyond the simple declaration that the elder\'s Citta has attained concentration. Subhūti places *susamāhitaṁ* and *vimuttaṁ* together. His Citta is thoroughly established in Samādhi and liberated from worldly bondage.

The accompanying expression *ātāpī viharāmi* further connects this attainment with the primordial spiritual force: Tapas. Within the Arahanist framework, Tapas participates in the purification of existing defilements, while Samādhi establishes Citta firmly upon its essential spiritual foundation.

The verse explicitly identifies Citta as well-concentrated and liberated. Its relationship to Ātmā becomes clear through the broader Citta--Ātmā understanding developed in this work: Ātmā is the innermost Soul-Spirit core of Citta, and the purification of the Mind-Heart provides the necessary condition for its liberation.

**Sirima: Attā Excellently Established in Samādhi**

An especially important testimony appears in the Therīgāthā 2.20, where the elder nun Sirima repeatedly identifies the condition of Attā:

Sirima --- Therīgāthā 2.20

*Pare ca naṁ pasaṁsanti, attā ce asamāhito;*\
*Moghaṁ pare pasaṁsanti, attā hi asamāhito.*

*Pare ca naṁ garahanti, attā ce susamāhito;*\
*Moghaṁ pare garahanti, attā hi susamāhito.*

**Translation:**

And others praise him (pare ca naṁ pasaṁsanti), if his attā (soul-spirit) is not firmly established in Samadhi (attā ce asamāhito); In vain do others praise him (moghaṁ pare pasaṁsanti), for his attā (soul-spirit) is not firmly established in Samadhi (attā hi asamāhito).

And others criticize him (pare ca naṁ garahanti), if his attā (soul-spirit) is excellently and firmly established in Samadhi (attā ce susamāhito); In vain do others criticize him (moghaṁ pare garahanti), for his attā (soul-spirit) is excellently and firmly established in Samadhi (attā hi susamāhito).

This verse is particularly significant for the present investigation because it explicitly names *Attā* and directly associates it with Samādhi.

The contrast is unmistakable. The verse first describes Attā as *asamāhito*, not firmly established in Samādhi, and then describes Attā as *susamāhito*, excellently and firmly established in Samādhi. The opinions of other people cannot determine the spiritual condition of the practitioner. What matters is the actual establishment of Attā.

This testimony provides an important textual foundation for the Arahanist understanding that the Soul-Spirit itself must attain a state of firm spiritual establishment. The ultimate concern of renunciant training is therefore not simply the temporary composure of ordinary mental activity. It is the establishment of the Soul-Spirit in Samādhi, free from the disturbances and bondage of worldly existence.

Read alongside Subhūti\'s declaration, Sirima\'s verse reveals two complementary dimensions of the same spiritual accomplishment. Subhūti explicitly describes Citta as *susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ*, while Sirima explicitly describes Attā as *susamāhito*. The former identifies the condition of the Mind-Heart; the latter identifies the condition of the Soul-Spirit.

The Arahanist interpretation brings these two declarations into a coherent relationship: Citta must undergo complete purification and become firmly established upon its innermost Soul-Spirit core, so that Attā may attain liberation from worldly bondage.

**Ajjhatta: Delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit**

AN 4.170 --- Citta, Attā, and the Establishment in Ajjhatta

The *Yuganaddha Sutta* (AN 4.170) provides a particularly significant foundation for understanding the relationship between Citta and Attā within the Arahanist framework. Its fourth approach to Arahantship describes a decisive transformation: when the practitioner\'s mind is agitated by concerns relating to the Dhamma, a moment arrives when *ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati* --- Citta establishes itself precisely in Ajjhatta, the Higher Soul-Spirit.

This statement identifies the essential direction of spiritual transformation. Citta, understood as the entire Mind-Heart, must become firmly established upon its innermost Soul-Spirit core, Attā. Through this establishment, Citta settles (*sannisīdati*), becomes unified (*ekodi hoti*), and enters Samādhi (*samādhiyati*). The sutta then declares that the path arises (*maggo sañjāyati*), and through its continued development, the fetters are abandoned and the underlying tendencies eliminated.

The Arahanist interpretation understands this sequence as the practical relationship between the purification of Citta and the liberation of Attā. When Citta remains occupied with external worldly manifestations, Attā remains exposed to the continued influence of worldly defilements. Through Nibbidā, Citta relinquishes its attachment to worldly existence and becomes established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit. Samādhi and Tapas then provide the foundation for the purification and eradication of existing defilements. As Virāga develops, worldly passion fades, and the process reaches its fulfilment in Vimutti --- the complete liberation of Attā from worldly bondage.

The significance of AN 4.170 lies in its explicit sequence: the establishment of Citta in Ajjhatta, its unification in Samādhi, the arising of the path, and the eradication of fetters and underlying tendencies. Although the sutta does not present the complete Citta--Attā framework in these precise doctrinal terms, its fourth approach provides the textual foundation upon which the Arahanist interpretation develops this relationship.

The central Arahanist principle is therefore clear: **the liberation of Attā requires the complete purification of Citta, and the purification of Citta requires its firm establishment upon the Higher Soul-Spirit.** This is the spiritual orientation through which the practitioner progresses from Nibbidā to Samādhi, from Samādhi to Virāga, and ultimately to Vimutti and Arahantship.

A further connection appears in Dhammapada 362, which describes the practitioner as *ajjhattarato samāhito* --- taking delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit and established in Samādhi --- AN 4.170 establishes the central importance of Ajjhatta in the renunciant path.

Dhammapada 362

*Hatthasaṃyato pādasaṃyato, vācāsaṃyato saṃyatuttamo;*\
*Ajjhattarato samāhito, eko santusito tamāhu bhikkhuṃ.*

**Translation:**

Restrained in hands (hatthasaṃyato), restrained in feet (pādasaṃyato), restrained in speech (vācāsaṃyato), restrained supreme (saṃyatuttamo); taking delight in higher soul-spirit (ajjh-atta-rato), established in samādhi (samāhito), solitary (eko), contented (santusito)---him (taṃ) they call (āhu) a bhikkhu (bhikkhuṃ).

This verse connects restraint, delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit, and Samādhi within the description of a true bhikkhu.

The expression *ajjhattarato* is particularly important. In the Arahanist interpretation, *Ajjhatta* refers to the higher dimension of Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit. The expression *ajjhattarato* therefore describes taking delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit.

The verse places this spiritual orientation alongside the disciplined restraint of the hands, feet, and speech, followed by the description *samāhito*. It thus presents a renunciant whose conduct is restrained, whose delight is established in the Higher Soul-Spirit, and whose spiritual condition is characterized by Samādhi.

The practical significance is considerable. The renunciant\'s source of fulfilment must change. Citta must cease seeking satisfaction through external worldly manifestations and become established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit. This is the transformation of spiritual orientation that the Arahanist framework identifies with Nibbidā and the development of Ajjhatta.

The verse explicitly connects *ajjhattarato* with Samādhi. The further explanation that this represents Citta becoming established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit is the Arahanist interpretation developed from the relationship between these terms and the wider teaching examined in this work.

**Attā as the Subject of Spiritual Examination**

Dhammapada 379 provides another important perspective:

Dhammapada 379

*Attanā codayattānaṃ, paṭimāsetam attanā;*\
*So attagutto satimā, sukhaṃ bhikkhu vihāhisi.*

With the soul-spirit (attanā), motivate your soul-spirit (codayattānaṃ \[codayā + attānaṃ\]); examine, and investigate your soul-spirit (paṭimāsetam, often read as paṭimāseta attanā or paṭimāse attānaṃ\]) by the soul-spirit (attanā). Thus (so), O bhikkhu, you will dwell (vihāhisi) with the soul-spirit guarded (attagutto), mindful (satimā), happily (sukhaṃ).

This verse repeatedly employs the word *Attā* in connection with spiritual examination and cultivation. The Soul-Spirit is to be motivated, examined, and investigated through the Soul-Spirit itself. The practitioner is then described as *attagutto*, one whose Soul-Spirit is guarded, and *satimā*, mindful.

The verse is important because it gives Attā an active place in the spiritual training of the renunciant. The Soul-Spirit is not merely a concept to be discussed philosophically. It is directly involved in the work of spiritual cultivation.

Within the Arahanist understanding, this examination must be considered together with the training of Citta. Citta is the entire Mind-Heart through which the practitioner engages in discernment, restraint, concentration, and purification. Attā is its innermost Soul-Spirit core. The training therefore requires the whole Mind-Heart to be properly directed and purified, while the Soul-Spirit becomes guarded and established in its higher spiritual condition.

**Abhirūpanandā: The Practical Training of Citta**

The elder nun Abhirūpanandā provides an especially useful example of how Citta is to be cultivated:

Abhirūpanandā --- Therīgāthā 2.1

*Āturaṁ asuciṁ pūtiṁ, passa nande samussayaṁ;*\
*Asubhāya cittaṁ bhāvehi, ekaggaṁ susamāhitaṁ.*

*Animittañca bhāvehi, mānānusayamujjaha;*\
*Tato mānābhisamayā, upasantā carissasī.*

**Translation:**

Look (passa) upon this mass piled up together / physical body (samussayaṁ), O Nandā---afflicted (āturaṁ), impure (asuciṁ), and foul-smelling (pūtiṁ); Develop (bhāvehi) the citta on the foul \[body\] (asubhāya), unifying it (ekaggaṁ) and firmly established in Samādhi (susamāhitaṁ).

Cultivate the signless (the un-manifestive) (animittañca bhāvehi), and completely cast out the underlying tendency to conceit (māna-anusayam-ujjaha); Then, from that eradication of conceit (tato māna-abhisamayā), you will live (carissasī) in supreme peace (upasantā).

This instruction demonstrates the practical importance of Citta\'s complete training. The practitioner is instructed to cultivate Citta, unify it, and establish it firmly in Samādhi. The training also includes cultivation of the signless and the eradication of the underlying tendency to conceit.

The verse explicitly identifies Citta as the object of cultivation and describes the qualities it must develop: *ekaggaṁ* and *susamāhitaṁ*. Its final movement points towards the eradication of conceit and the attainment of supreme peace.

The Arahanist interpretation places this instruction within the larger Citta--Ātmā relationship. The physical body and its manifestations are not to remain the foundation upon which Citta establishes its identity. Through discernment and disenchantment, Citta relinquishes its attachment to worldly manifestations and becomes established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit. Its unification and concentration then contribute to the progressive purification of the Soul-Spirit and the eventual attainment of liberation.

Abhirūpanandā\'s testimony therefore provides a practical illustration of the transformation examined throughout this chapter: Citta must be deliberately cultivated, unified, and purified.

**What the Verses Establish, and What Arahanism Interprets**

At this point, it is important to distinguish the explicit content of these verses from the complete doctrinal relationship developed through the Arahanist framework.

The verses explicitly establish several important points:

- Subhūti declares that Citta is *susamāhitaṁ vimuttaṁ*: well-concentrated and liberated.

- Sirima describes Attā as *susamāhito*: excellently and firmly established in Samādhi.

- Dhammapada 362 associates *ajjhattarato* with Samādhi and the disciplined life of a bhikkhu.

- Dhammapada 379 presents Attā as the subject of motivation, examination, investigation, guarding, and mindfulness.

- Abhirūpanandā instructs the practitioner to cultivate Citta, unify it, establish it in Samādhi, and eradicate the underlying tendency to conceit.

These are significant textual foundations. They demonstrate that Citta is cultivated and liberated, that Attā is explicitly associated with Samādhi, and that the higher dimension of Attā has an important place in the renunciant\'s spiritual orientation.

The verses do not, individually, present a complete systematic explanation of Citta as the total sphere of Mind-Heart with Ātmā as its innermost Soul-Spirit core. Nor does any single verse explicitly formulate the entire sequence through which Citta changes its establishment, the Soul-Spirit is progressively purified, and final liberation is attained.

That complete relationship is the Arahanist interpretation developed by bringing these testimonies together with the distinction between Attā and the five aggregates established earlier in this work.

Accordingly, the Arahanist understanding is as follows.

Citta encompasses the entire Mind-Heart, including its engagement with external worldly manifestations and its deepest spiritual dimension. Ātmā is the innermost Soul-Spirit core of Citta. When Citta remains established upon worldly manifestations, it continues to sustain attachment, identification, and the arising of rāga, dosa, and moha. These defilements perpetuate worldly bondage.

The purpose of spiritual training is therefore to transform the entire Mind-Heart. Through Nibbidā, Citta becomes disenchanted with worldly existence and changes its establishment from external manifestations to Attā, the Higher Soul-Spirit (Ajjhatta). This change prevents the continued accumulation of new worldly defilements through its former attachments.

Through Samādhi and Tapas, Citta becomes firmly established upon Attā, while existing defilements are progressively purified. Through Virāga, worldly passion fades away and the remaining defilements are eradicated. Finally, through Vimutti, Attā is liberated from worldly bondage. This is the attainment of Arahantship.

The distinction between Citta and Ātmā therefore does not divide spiritual training into two unrelated processes. It explains why the purification of the Mind-Heart is indispensable to the liberation of the Soul-Spirit. Citta is the entire field in which the transformation takes place; Ātmā is the innermost Soul-Spirit whose liberation is the ultimate purpose of that transformation.

**The Completion of Citta\'s Training**

The central conclusion of this chapter is that the liberation path requires the purification of the entire Mind-Heart.

A practitioner cannot reach the final goal while Citta remains governed by worldly attachment, unstable concentration, and the continual influence of rāga, dosa, and moha. The Mind-Heart must be trained through renunciation, Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā. It must become disenchanted with worldly manifestations, establish itself upon the Higher Soul-Spirit, and undergo the complete eradication of defilements.

The testimonies of the elders provide different but complementary expressions of this accomplishment. Subhūti declares Citta well-concentrated and liberated. Sirima describes Attā excellently established in Samādhi. The Dhammapada verses direct the practitioner towards delight in the Higher Soul-Spirit and the examination and guarding of Attā. Abhirūpanandā demonstrates the deliberate cultivation and unification of Citta.

Read together, these testimonies illuminate the practical significance of the Citta--Ātmā relationship.

The Mind-Heart must be purified in its entirety because the Soul-Spirit cannot attain liberation while Citta continues to sustain worldly bondage. The transformation of Citta is therefore not a secondary aspect of the path. It is indispensable to the liberation of Attā.

This completes our examination of Citta: its nature, its bondage, its relationship with the five aggregates, its purification, and its liberation. We now turn to the next stage of the investigation: the physical body and its unattractive nature, and how the recognition of bodily danger gives rise to disenchantment and aversion, opening the way towards liberation.

## VI. Viññāṇa, the Five Aggregates, and Anattā

### 6. The Five Aggregates: What the Elders Renounced

The question of *Anattā* lies at the centre of the present investigation. What, precisely, did Gautama teach his renunciants to relinquish? When the elders examined the five aggregates (*pañcakkhandhā*), what did they seek to overcome? Did they cultivate a practice directed towards denying the existence of Soul-Spirit (*Attā*), or did they recognize the conditioned nature of worldly existence and progressively abandon their attachment to its manifestations?

These questions require close attention to the actual words and spiritual practices recorded in the early Buddhist discourses. The interpretation of *Anattā* cannot be separated from the path the elders themselves followed. Their verses repeatedly describe contemplation of the body, recognition of its deterioration, restraint of the senses, purification of Citta, the abandonment of craving, and the attainment of liberation. These testimonies provide essential evidence for understanding what the five aggregates meant within the practical discipline of renunciation.

The preceding chapters have established the Arahanist understanding of *Attā*, *Citta*, and *Viññāṇa*. *Attā* refers to Soul-Spirit, the essential spiritual core of being. *Citta* encompasses the entire sphere of Mind-Heart, extending from its worldly engagements to its deepest spiritual dimension. *Viññāṇa*, meanwhile, refers to sense-generated consciousness, through which the world is experienced through the sense faculties. These distinctions are essential when examining the five aggregates, because the aggregate of sense-derived consciousness must not automatically be equated with the entirety of Citta or with Attā itself.

The five aggregates---*Rūpa, Vedanā, Saññā, Saṅkhārā,* and *Viññāṇa*---describe the principal constituents of conditioned human experience. The body, feelings, perceptions, mental formations, and sense-generated consciousness are subject to conditions and change. The renunciant\'s task is to understand their nature, recognize the danger of attachment, and relinquish the identification that binds spiritual existence to worldly manifestations.

The elders\' testimonies repeatedly direct attention towards this process. Abhirūpanandā instructs Nandā to contemplate the body as *āturaṁ, asuciṁ,* and *pūtiṁ*---afflicted, impure, and foul-smelling---and to cultivate Citta through the contemplation of unattractiveness, concentration, and the signless. Other elders describe the abandonment of the six fields of contact, the comprehension of the aggregates, the severing of their roots, and the destruction of the defilements. Their declarations reveal a practical discipline concerned with the transformation of the practitioner\'s relationship with conditioned existence.

This chapter therefore examines the five aggregates from the perspective of renunciant practice. It begins with the physical body, whose vulnerability, impurity, and deterioration provide a direct basis for disenchantment. It then considers the body as the final physical vessel and examines the expression *antimaṁ dehaṁ* in the context of liberation. From there, the discussion turns to *Viññāṇa*, investigating its relationship with sensory experience and distinguishing it from the broader nature of Citta. The examination then returns to *SN 22.59*, the *Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta*, and the language through which the aggregates are contemplated and relinquished. Finally, it considers *Animitta*, the signless, and the practices through which worldly identification is abandoned.

A central distinction must guide this investigation: **the relinquishment of the five aggregates as objects of attachment and mistaken identification is not, by itself, a declaration that Soul-Spirit does not exist.** The affirmative formula, *Etaṁ mama, eso\'hamasmi, eso me attā*---"This is mine, this I am, this is my Soul-Spirit"---expresses the identification that the practitioner must overcome. Its negative counterpart, *Netaṁ mama, nesohamasmi, na meso attā*---"This is not mine; this I am not; this is not my Soul-Spirit"---expresses the relinquishment of that identification. The precise doctrinal significance of these formulas must be considered in their textual and practical contexts.

The Arahanist interpretation approaches these teachings by distinguishing conditioned manifestations from the Soul-Spirit that must attain liberation from worldly bondage. The five aggregates are examined, comprehended, and relinquished as the foundations of worldly identification and attachment. Through this process, Citta is progressively purified and established upon the Higher Soul-Spirit (*Ajjhatta*), continuing the spiritual progression examined in the preceding chapter.

The purpose of this investigation is not to impose a conclusion upon the elders\' verses before examining them. It is to allow their own testimonies to establish what their practices involved. Did they describe a distinct discipline of denying Soul-Spirit, or do their accounts consistently emphasize contemplation, restraint, disenchantment, concentration, the abandonment of craving, and liberation? What do their actual words reveal about the relationship between *Anattā* and the five aggregates?

These are the questions that Chapter 6 seeks to answer. By returning to the language and practices of the elders, we can examine whether the conventional interpretation of *Anattā* adequately accounts for the full direction of their renunciant path---and whether the distinction between relinquishing the aggregates and denying Soul-Spirit is essential to recovering Gautama\'s original liberation teaching.

### 6.1. The Body as a Conditioned Manifestation

The physical body (*rūpa*) occupies a central place in the renunciant\'s examination of worldly existence. It is the most immediate and visible manifestation of human existence, through which the individual encounters the material world and experiences physical pleasure, discomfort, illness, ageing, and death. Yet the body is subject to continuous change. Its condition depends upon numerous causes and supporting conditions, and it cannot maintain its present form indefinitely. The elders\' contemplation of the body therefore provides an essential starting point for understanding the renunciation of conditioned existence.

A particularly direct instruction appears in the verses of Abhirūpanandā, preserved in the *Therīgāthā* (2.1). The elder addresses Nandā and directs her attention towards the actual nature of the physical body:

Abhirūpanandā --- Therīgāthā 2.1

*Āturaṁ asuciṁ pūtiṁ, passa nande samussayaṁ;*\
*Asubhāya cittaṁ bhāvehi, ekaggaṁ susamāhitaṁ.*

*Animittañca bhāvehi, mānānusayamujjaha;*\
*Tato mānābhisamayā, upasantā carissasī.*

**Translation:**

Look (passa) upon this mass piled up together / physical body (samussayaṁ), O Nandā---afflicted (āturaṁ), impure (asuciṁ), and foul-smelling (pūtiṁ); Develop (bhāvehi) the citta on the foul \[body\] (asubhāya), unifying it (ekaggaṁ) and firmly established in Samādhi (susamāhitaṁ).

Cultivate the signless (animittañca bhāvehi), and completely cast out the underlying tendency to conceit (māna-anusayam-ujjaha); Then, from that eradication of conceit (tato māna-abhisamayā), you will live (carissasī) in supreme peace (upasantā).

The opening line establishes the subject of contemplation through three striking descriptions: *āturaṁ, asuciṁ,* and *pūtiṁ*. Each directs attention towards a different aspect of the body\'s actual condition.

**Āturaṁ --- Afflicted**

The word *āturaṁ* presents the body as afflicted and vulnerable to suffering. Physical existence is exposed to illness, weakness, injury, and deterioration. Regardless of outward appearance, strength, or youth, the body remains susceptible to conditions that can undermine its health and stability. Its present condition cannot be secured permanently, and its apparent strength offers no guarantee against future affliction.

This recognition challenges the assumption that bodily well-being can provide a lasting foundation for happiness or security. A person may take pride in physical strength, beauty, and vitality, yet these qualities remain dependent upon conditions that cannot be controlled indefinitely. The body that appears healthy today may become weak or diseased tomorrow. The elder\'s description therefore directs Nandā away from fascination with the body\'s outward appearance and towards recognition of its inherent vulnerability.

**Asuciṁ --- Impure**

The description *asuciṁ* identifies the body\'s impure nature. Beneath its outward appearance, the physical body consists of substances and processes that do not possess the purity or permanence commonly attributed to bodily beauty. Its internal constitution involves organs, fluids, waste, and other material components. Its continued maintenance requires nourishment, cleansing, and constant physical care.

The distinction between outward appearance and actual constitution is important. Human beings frequently become attached to the body\'s appearance, treating beauty and physical attractiveness as qualities worthy of admiration and possession. Yet these appearances conceal the body\'s underlying material condition. Through contemplation of *asuciṁ*, the practitioner examines the body beyond its attractive external presentation.

**Pūtiṁ --- Foul-smelling**

The term *pūtiṁ* further emphasizes the body\'s foul and decaying nature. The physical body produces unpleasant odours and, following deterioration and death, undergoes decomposition. Its material constituents do not retain their original arrangement or appearance. The body that once appeared attractive eventually loses its vitality, becomes subject to decay, and returns to the processes of material dissolution.

This description brings the contemplation of bodily impurity into direct contact with deterioration. The body is not an enduring object of beauty. Its appearance is temporary, and the conditions that sustain it are continually changing. What is admired as attractive during youth cannot preserve that appearance against ageing, illness, and death.

Together, *āturaṁ, asuciṁ,* and *pūtiṁ* present a comprehensive challenge to bodily attachment. The body is afflicted, impure, and subject to decay. These descriptions direct the practitioner towards the physical reality beneath outward appearance and expose the instability of worldly identification based upon bodily form.

**The Body and the Reality of Deterioration**

Abhirūpanandā\'s instruction is particularly significant because it does not approach bodily existence through abstract speculation. She directs Nandā to look at the body itself: *passa nande samussayaṁ*---look upon this accumulated bodily structure. The object of contemplation is immediately present and can be examined through direct observation.

The expression *samussayaṁ* is important in this context. The body is a mass assembled from material constituents, maintained through supporting conditions, and subject to continual physical processes. Its apparent unity and solidity can encourage the assumption that it constitutes a stable foundation of personal identity. Yet its material constitution reveals a different reality: the body is dependent, vulnerable, and continually changing.

The body undergoes deterioration throughout its existence. From birth, it proceeds through growth, maturity, ageing, weakness, and ultimately death. Even during periods of apparent health, its constituent processes remain subject to change. Its strength, appearance, and physical capacities cannot be preserved permanently. The body therefore cannot provide an enduring foundation upon which the practitioner can establish freedom from suffering.

This recognition has direct implications for renunciation. A practitioner who remains fascinated by bodily appearance and physical gratification continues to regard a conditioned manifestation as a source of lasting satisfaction. Such attachment sustains *rāga* and strengthens identification with worldly existence. The contemplation of bodily affliction, impurity, and decay exposes the instability of that attachment.

Within the Arahanist framework, the physical body is a conditioned manifestation of worldly existence. It belongs to the aggregate of *Rūpa* and is subject to the material conditions through which bodily existence is maintained. It is an instrument through which the practitioner experiences the world, but its existence and appearance cannot be equated with the essential nature of Attā, Soul-Spirit.

This distinction is fundamental to the present investigation. The recognition that the physical body is afflicted, impure, and subject to deterioration establishes the necessity of relinquishing bodily identification. The practitioner must understand the body according to its actual nature rather than continue to regard it as an enduring possession or ultimate identity.

Abhirūpanandā\'s instruction therefore reveals an essential direction of renunciant practice: the body must be seen clearly, its conditioned nature understood, and the attachment sustained by its outward appearance relinquished. This contemplation provides the basis for *Nibbidā*, as fascination with bodily existence gives way to disenchantment with its unstable and deteriorating nature.

The significance of this teaching extends beyond the contemplation of physical appearance. It raises a further question: how should the renunciant understand the body within the larger course of spiritual existence? If the physical body is conditioned, vulnerable, and ultimately subject to death, what is its place in the journey towards liberation? The following section examines the body as the final physical vessel through the expression *antimaṁ dehaṁ* and considers its significance in the context of the renunciant\'s attainment of freedom from worldly bondage.

### 6.2. The Renunciant\'s Relationship with the Body

The renunciant\'s relationship with the physical body must be understood in the context of the final goal of liberation. The body is the physical vessel through which one experiences the conditions of worldly existence. It is subject to ageing, illness, deterioration, and death. Even after the attainment of Arahantship, the physical body remains until the end of its natural lifespan. What has ended is the cycle of renewed worldly existence: the liberated renunciant bears the final body, without generating another.

The elders repeatedly describe this condition through the expression *antimaṁ dehaṁ*---the final body. Their declarations are especially important because they speak from the standpoint of accomplished renunciation. They do not merely describe the body as impermanent or subject to decay; they declare that the present body is their last.

**Vīrā: Bearing the Final Body**

In *Therīgāthā* 1.7, the elder bhikkhunī Vīrā declares:

*Vīrā vīrehi dhammehi, bhikkhunī bhāvita-indriyā;*\
*Dhāreti antimaṁ dehaṁ, jetvā māraṁ savāhinin ti.*

Vīrā, by heroic (*vīrehi*) Dhammas (*dhammehi*), the bhikkhunī, whose sensory faculties (*indriya*) are completely cultivated and restrained (*bhāvita*), bears (*dhāreti*) her final body (*antimaṁ dehaṁ*), having conquered (*jetvā*) Māra with his army (*māraṁ savāhinin*).

The declaration connects the final body with the cultivation of the sensory faculties and the conquest of Māra. The expression *bhāvita-indriyā* indicates that the renunciant\'s faculties have been thoroughly cultivated. Sensory experience no longer serves as fuel for worldly craving and attachment. Having conquered Māra and his army, the elder continues to bear the physical body, but this body is no longer the foundation of another worldly existence.

The verb *dhāreti* is particularly significant. Vīrā does not declare that the body has already disappeared. She bears it. The body remains as the final physical manifestation of one who has overcome the conditions that produce renewed existence.

This distinction must be understood clearly: **Arahantship does not require the immediate disappearance of the physical body. It requires the complete termination of the causes that would produce another worldly existence.**

**Upasamā: Crossing Beyond the Realm of Death**

Another declaration appears in *Therīgāthā* 1.10, where the elder Upasamā is instructed:

*Upasame tare oghaṁ, maccudheyyaṁ suduttaraṁ;*\
*Dhārehi antimaṁ dehaṁ, jetvā māraṁ savāhinin ti.*

O Upasamā, cross over (*tare*) the flood (*oghaṁ*), the realm of Death (*maccudheyyaṁ*), very difficult to cross (*suduttaraṁ*); bear (*dhārehi*) your final body (*antimaṁ dehaṁ*), having conquered (*jetvā*) Māra with his army (*māraṁ savāhinin*).

Here, the instruction places the final body in direct relation to crossing beyond the realm of Death. The renunciant must cross the flood of worldly bondage and conquer Māra. The physical body is still borne, but its continued existence does not mean that the renunciant remains bound to the cycle of birth and death.

The verse therefore distinguishes between the continued existence of the present body and the continuation of worldly becoming. The former remains; the latter is brought to an end.

**Abhayā's Mother: Casting Aside the Fragile Body**

The testimony of Abhayā\'s Mother in *Therīgāthā* 2.9 provides another perspective:

*Abhaye bhiduro kāyo, yattha satā puthujjanā;*\
*Nikkhipissāmimaṁ dehaṁ, sampajānā satīmatī.*

*Bahūhi dukkhadhammehi, appamādaratāya me;*\
*Taṇhakkhayo anuppatto, kataṁ buddhassa sāsanan.*

This physical body is fragile (*bhiduro kāyo*), wherein ordinary worldlings abide (*yattha satā puthujjanā*); fearlessly (*abhaye*), I shall cast aside this physical frame (*nikkhipissāmimaṁ dehaṁ*), with mindfulness and clear comprehension (*sampajānā satīmatī*). Through manifold painful Dhammas (*bahūhi dukkhadhammehi*), by my adherence to spiritual zealousness (*appamāda-ratāya me*), the destruction of craving has been attained (*taṇhakkhayo anuppatto*), the dispensation of the Awakened One has been fulfilled (*kataṁ buddhassa sāsanaṁ*).

This verse is especially revealing because the elder speaks of the body\'s fragility and her readiness to cast it aside. Yet her declaration of attainment is not centred on bodily destruction. It is centred on *taṇhakkhaya*---the destruction of craving---and the fulfilment of Gautama\'s dispensation.

The physical body is to be relinquished at the appropriate time. The actual accomplishment of the renunciant, however, is the eradication of craving. The elder has fulfilled the training; the body has no further role in generating renewed worldly bondage.

The verse also places importance on *appamāda*, the spiritual vigilance and earnestness through which the renunciant completes the training. The body is fragile, but the path does not consist merely of waiting for its death. Liberation is attained through the eradication of the causes of bondage while the practitioner is still alive.

**Abhayā: The Body Contemplated, Craving Uprooted**

The testimony of Abhayā in *Therīgāthā* 2.8, discussed in the preceding section, brings this relationship into particularly sharp focus:

*Uddhaṁ pādatalā amma, adho ve kesamatthakā;*\
*Paccavekkhassumaṁ kāyaṁ, asuciṁ pūtigandhikaṁ.*

*Evaṁ viharamānāya, sabbo rāgo samūhato;*\
*Pariḷāho samucchinno, sītibhūtāmhi nibbutā.*

From the soles of the feet upward, mother (*uddhaṁ pādatalā amma*), and downward from the crown of the head (*adho ve kesamatthakā*); examine this physical body (*paccavekkhassumaṁ kāyaṁ*), which is foul and stinking of putrid odour (*asuciṁ pūtigandhikaṁ*).

For one dwelling thus (*evaṁ viharamānāya*), all craving and attachment (*sabbo rāgo*) is uprooted (*samūhato*); feverish burning (*pariḷāho*) is completely severed at the root (*samucchinno*), and I have become cool (*sītibhūtāmhi*), unbound (*nibbutā*).

Abhayā\'s testimony connects the contemplation of the physical body directly with the eradication of *rāga* and the ending of spiritual fever. The body is examined from the soles of the feet to the crown of the head. Through this contemplation, the practitioner overcomes infatuation with physical form and uproots craving and attachment.

The significance of this verse extends beyond the contemplation of bodily impurity. It demonstrates how the renunciant\'s relationship with the physical body is transformed through the path of liberation. The body is no longer an object of infatuation, and its physical existence no longer serves as the foundation for worldly passion.

Abhayā\'s declaration, *sītibhūtāmhi nibbutā*, expresses the culmination of this transformation: she has become cool and unbound. The fever of worldly passion has been extinguished.

**Antimaṁ Dehaṁ: The Final Physical Vessel**

Across these declarations, a consistent picture emerges. Vīrā bears the final body after cultivating her faculties and conquering Māra. Upasamā is instructed to bear the final body after crossing the flood of Death. Abhayā\'s Mother declares the destruction of craving and her readiness to cast aside the fragile body. Abhayā describes how contemplation of the physical body leads to the uprooting of all craving and the extinguishing of spiritual fever.

These verses establish the importance of distinguishing between the physical body and the liberated condition of the renunciant.

The expression *antimaṁ dehaṁ* does not describe a body that has already disappeared. It identifies the body that remains after the causes of renewed worldly existence have been overcome. The Arahant continues to live in the present physical vessel, but no further body will arise through renewed worldly becoming.

This is the significance of the final body: **the physical manifestation continues, while the cycle that would produce another manifestation has ended.**

The renunciant must therefore understand bodily existence in relation to the entire path. The body is contemplated, its unattractive and fragile nature is recognised, and attachment to it is overcome. Through the cultivation of the faculties, Samādhi, and the eradication of craving, the practitioner brings worldly bondage to an end. The eventual dissolution of the final body marks the end of that physical manifestation; it is not the event through which Arahantship is first attained.

The elders\' testimony thus presents a clear distinction between the body that is still borne and the worldly bondage that has already been overcome. Their declarations of *antimaṁ dehaṁ* preserve this distinction and provide an essential foundation for understanding the renunciant\'s relationship with the five aggregates.

### 6.3. Anattā and the Five Aggregates

The examination of the physical body and the final body of the liberated renunciant brings us to one of the most consequential questions in the interpretation of Gautama\'s teaching: what exactly is meant by *Anattā*? Does the declaration that the five aggregates are *Anattā* establish that no Soul-Spirit (*Attā*) exists, or does it establish that the aggregates themselves are not the Soul-Spirit?

These are two different propositions. The first makes a universal claim about the non-existence of *Attā*. The second identifies the five aggregates as something other than *Attā*. The distinction is fundamental to the Arahanist understanding of liberation.

**SN 22.59: The Five Aggregates Are Not Attā**

In the *Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta* (SN 22.59), Gautama addresses the five aggregates, beginning with form:

*Rūpaṁ, bhikkhave, anattā.*

\"Bhikkhus, form is not Attā.\"

The discourse proceeds through feeling (*vedanā*), perception (*saññā*), formations (*saṅkhārā*), and consciousness (*viññāṇa*), declaring each to be *Anattā*.

Gautama then explains the reasoning. If form were *Attā*, it would not lead to affliction, and one should be able to command it: \"Let my form be thus; let my form not be thus.\" Yet form is subject to affliction and does not remain under one\'s command. The same reasoning is applied to the remaining aggregates.

The immediate subject of this discourse is therefore the relationship between the five aggregates and *Attā*. Form is not *Attā* because it is afflicted, impermanent, and not subject to one\'s command. The other aggregates are examined according to the same principle.

Arahanism accepts the central distinction: the five aggregates are not the Soul-Spirit. They are conditioned manifestations associated with embodied existence. Their arising, alteration, deterioration, and dissolution do not establish that they constitute the true spiritual identity of the being.

However, a further conclusion must not be inserted into the passage without examination. The declaration that form is not *Attā* does not, by itself, establish the universal proposition that no *Attā* exists beyond form. Likewise, declaring feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness to be *Anattā* does not automatically establish that nothing exists beyond these five aggregates.

The sutta identifies what is not *Attā*. It does not explicitly identify the five aggregates as an exhaustive inventory of everything that can exist.

This distinction is especially important when considering the relationship between the embodied being and the Soul-Spirit. If the physical body is not *Attā*, its inability to remain under command is not evidence that *Attā* itself does not exist. It demonstrates that the physical manifestation is not the true spiritual principle.

**MN 44: The Four Modes of Relating Attā to Form**

The *Cūḷavedalla Sutta* (MN 44) provides a more elaborate formulation. In this discourse, Dhammadinnā explains the twenty forms of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* through four ways of regarding each of the five aggregates in relation to *Attā*.

Taking form (*rūpa*) as the example, the four formulations are:

1.  One regards form (*rūpaṁ*) as Attā (*attato*).

2.  One regards Attā (*attānaṁ*) as possessing form (*rūpavantaṁ*).

3.  One regards form (*rūpaṁ*) as in Attā (*attani*).

4.  One regards Attā (*attānaṁ*) as in form (*rūpasmiṁ*).

These four formulations are applied to each of the five aggregates, producing twenty modes of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi*.

The passage is important because it identifies four distinct relationships between the assumed self and the aggregates. Yet each formulation requires careful examination. They should not simply be accepted as interchangeable descriptions of an abstract psychological sense of identity.

**First: Regarding form as Attā --- rūpaṁ attato samanupassati**

This formulation regards the physical form itself as *Attā*. Arahanism rejects this identification. The body is a physical manifestation, subject to ageing, illness, deterioration, and death. It cannot be equated with the Soul-Spirit.

The same principle applies to the other aggregates. Feeling is not *Attā*; perception is not *Attā*; formations are not *Attā*; and sense-generated consciousness is not *Attā*. To identify any of these conditioned manifestations as the Soul-Spirit is to mistake the manifestation for that which manifests through it.

**Second: Regarding Attā as possessing form --- rūpavantaṁ attānaṁ samanupassati**

The second formulation regards *Attā* as possessing form. The expression *rūpavantaṁ* conveys having or possessing form. But what does this relationship mean when considered in the context of the actual condition of the physical body?

The body does not remain under the command of the being. It ages without permission, becomes ill without permission, and eventually disintegrates. One cannot simply command the body to remain young, healthy, or alive. In this sense, the physical body is not an object over which the Soul-Spirit can exercises control or is its owner.

Arahanism understands the body as a worldly manifestation associated with *Attā*, rather than an independently owned possession that *Attā* can freely manipulate. The manifestation is subject to the conditions governing physical existence. Its limitations cannot be used to define the nature or existence of the Soul-Spirit.

The distinction between manifestation and possession is therefore essential. The fact that a being manifests through a physical body does not mean that the body is an independently controlled possession of the Soul-Spirit. The Soul-Spirit is not the owner of the body.

**Third: Regarding form as being in Attā --- rūpaṁ attani samanupassati**

The third formulation regards form as being within *Attā*. Arahanism does not accept the physical body as an interior component contained within the Soul-Spirit in the manner of one physical object placed inside another.

The relationship between *Attā* and the physical body cannot be adequately explained through an ordinary three-dimensional spatial model. The physical body belongs to the material dimension. The Soul-Spirit, according to the Arahanist understanding, belongs to a higher multi-dimensional existence.

Consequently, the relationship between them should not be imagined as though the physical body were literally enclosed inside a larger physical container called *Attā*. Their relationship concerns different dimensions of manifestation and existence.

**Fourth: Regarding Attā as being in form --- rūpasmiṁ attānaṁ samanupassati**

The fourth formulation places *Attā* within form. This is particularly interesting because it raises a question about the relationship between the Soul-Spirit and the physical body.

The Arahanist understanding locates the seat of *Attā* in the heart. This does not mean that the Soul-Spirit is simply another physical organ, nor does it mean that it can be identified with the anatomical heart. The heart is the bodily location associated with its presence, while the Soul-Spirit itself belongs to a higher dimension of existence.

A three-dimensional physical body cannot be expected to contain or represent a higher-dimensional spiritual reality in the same way that it contains its ordinary physical organs. The relationship between the two must therefore be understood beyond the limitations of physical spatial measurement.

The fourth formulation consequently raises a genuine question: does the presence of *Attā* in relation to the physical body necessarily mean that *Attā* is reducible to physical form? Arahanism answers no. The presence or seat of the Soul-Spirit in the embodied being does not make it identical with the body or one of its material components.

These four formulations should therefore be examined individually. The first identifies form as *Attā*; the second describes *Attā* as possessing form; the third places form within *Attā*; and the fourth places *Attā* within form. Arahanism rejects the identification of the Soul-Spirit with the five aggregates, while understanding the relationship between *Attā* and the embodied being through the distinction between the higher-dimensional Soul-Spirit and its conditioned physical and mental manifestations.

**The Expansion of Sakkāya-diṭṭhi into an Abstract Theory of Identity**

The interpretation of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* in much of modern Buddhist scholarship and popular Buddhist literature requires particular scrutiny.

The term is frequently translated as \"identity view,\" \"personality view,\" or \"self-identity view.\" These translations are then developed into explanations involving ego, personal identity, the psychological construction of self, and the mistaken belief that a person possesses an enduring individual identity.

Such explanations can move considerably beyond the actual terminology and structure of the discourse.

In MN 44, the subject is not introduced through a general discussion of personality formation or the psychological construction of identity. Dhammadinnā identifies the five clinging aggregates and then explains twenty ways in which they may be regarded in relation to *Attā*. The discourse presents specific propositions concerning form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. It does not simply define *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* as the ordinary human sense of being somebody.

The translation \"identity view\" can therefore create an interpretive shift. Once the discussion is framed primarily in terms of identity, the physical body and the other aggregates can become secondary to an abstract psychological theory. The reader is encouraged to understand the problem as the construction of an ego or the belief in a personal self, rather than examining the actual relationship between the five aggregates and *Attā*.

This shift has **significantly negative** consequences. A discourse that distinguishes the aggregates from *Attā* can be presented as though it were denying the existence of *Attā* altogether. The rejection of mistaken relationships between the aggregates and *Attā* then becomes a rejection of Soul-Spirit itself.

That conclusion requires an additional argument. It cannot simply be assumed from the twenty formulations.

Arahanism therefore questions the tendency to **over-stretch, and thus wrongly-interpretate** *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* into a comprehensive theory of psychological identity and then use that theory to support a universal denial of Soul-Spirit. The terminology and the actual propositions in MN 44 must remain central to the interpretation.

There is also a distinction between rejecting the body as *Attā* and rejecting the existence of *Attā* because the body is not *Attā*. The first follows the reasoning of SN 22.59. The second is an additional metaphysical conclusion. These two positions should not be conflated.

**What the Five Aggregates Reveal**

The five aggregates describe the conditioned dimensions of embodied existence. They provide the framework through which physical form, feeling, perception, mental formations, and sense-generated consciousness are examined.

Their impermanence, vulnerability, and lack of complete controllability establish why they cannot be regarded as the Soul-Spirit. The renunciant must understand their nature and relinquish attachment to them. The body is not to be mistaken for the true spiritual identity; feelings are not to be clung to as the true spiritual identity; perceptions, formations, and consciousness are likewise not to be mistaken for it.

This understanding is consistent with the liberation practices described by the elders: contemplation of the body\'s foul and deteriorating nature, cultivation of the faculties, eradication of craving, and relinquishment of worldly attachment. Their testimony repeatedly emphasizes the abandonment of what binds the being to conditioned existence.

The question is therefore not whether the five aggregates are *Attā*. They are not. The question is whether their rejection as *Attā* must also entail the denial of a Soul-Spirit beyond them.

Arahanism maintains that these are separate questions. The five aggregates are conditioned manifestations; *Attā* is the Soul-Spirit. Confusing the two leads to a fundamental misunderstanding of the relationship between embodied existence and liberation.

The renunciant\'s task is not to establish the aggregates as an eternal identity. It is to understand their conditioned nature, abandon attachment to them, purify the Mind-Heart, and attain liberation from worldly bondage.

### 6.4. The Signless and the Abandonment of Worldly Identification

In Abhirūpanandā\'s instruction, the cultivation of the signless (*Animitta*) follows the contemplation of the physical body\'s afflicted and impure nature and the establishment of the Citta in Samādhi:

*Animittañca bhāvehi, mānānusayamujjaha;*\
*Tato mānābhisamayā, upasantā carissasī.*

Cultivate the signless (*Animitta*), and completely cast out the underlying tendency to conceit (*mānānusaya*); Then, through the eradication of conceit, you will live in supreme peace.

Here, *Nimitta* refers to a sign or manifestation through which something becomes perceptible and attracts attention. In worldly existence, physical appearance and sensory manifestations become objects of attraction, identification, and attachment. The practitioner repeatedly becomes absorbed in these manifestations, allowing the Citta to remain entangled with conditioned existence.

The cultivation of *Animitta* points towards withdrawing from this fixation upon manifested signs. Rather than continually attending to the appearances of the body and the world, the practitioner develops the Citta through Samādhi and relinquishes the conceit and attachment sustained by worldly manifestations.

The verse places particular emphasis on abandoning *mānānusaya*, the underlying tendency to conceit. This is significant because merely recognizing the body\'s impurity does not complete the work of liberation. The deeper tendency to identify with worldly existence must also be eradicated.

The instruction concludes with *upasantā*, supreme peace. The movement is therefore from contemplation of the conditioned body, through the development of concentration and cultivation of the signless, towards the abandonment of conceit and worldly attachment.

Arahanism understands this practice within the wider direction of liberation: the practitioner gradually withdraws the Citta from conditioned manifestations and ceases to remain bound to worldly existence. The ultimate goal is Nibbāna, the Unconditioned and Deathless.

**Conclusion**

The testimonies examined in this chapter reveal the practical orientation of the early renunciants. Their training involved contemplating the physical body, recognizing the conditioned nature of the five aggregates, abandoning attachment to worldly manifestations, cultivating Samādhi, and overcoming the defilements that sustain continued worldly existence. Their declared goal was liberation from bondage and the attainment of the Deathless.

The five aggregates are not Attā. They are conditioned manifestations, subject to affliction, deterioration, and the inability to remain under one\'s command. Recognizing this distinction is essential to understanding why the renunciants relinquished attachment to the body and the other aggregates. Yet the rejection of these manifestations as Attā does not, by itself, establish the non-existence of Soul-Spirit.

The elders\' language also directs our attention towards the inward dimension of spiritual training: the cultivation of *Citta*, its establishment in *Samādhi*, and the significance of *Attā* and *Ajjhatta* in the liberation process. These expressions open a wider question concerning the spiritual reality towards which the renunciant\'s training is directed.

The relationship between *Attā*, *Ajjhatta*, *Citta*, and *Anattā* therefore requires a more comprehensive examination. In the following chapter, we shall return to the testimonies of the elders, investigate their repeated language of *Attā*, and reconsider the canonical teachings that have come to be interpreted as a universal denial of Soul-Spirit.

The central question remains: **Did Gautama teach the liberation of the practitioner from worldly bondage, or the denial of the very Soul-Spirit towards which the renunciant\'s spiritual cultivation is directed?**

## VII. The Forgotten Attā: Recovering the Soul-Spirit in the Testimonies of the Elders

*An Examination of the Theragāthā, Therīgāthā, and the Degeneration of Gautama\'s Liberation Teaching*

### 7.1. The Remarkable Absence of Anattā

One of the most striking features of the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* is the complete absence of the term *Anattā*. These two collections preserve the spiritual testimonies of the early elder Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunis---men and women who undertook renunciation, disciplined their minds, endured the hardships of spiritual training, overcame worldly attachments, and declared their attainment of liberation. Their verses offer direct insight into the language, aspirations, and spiritual orientation of those who pursued the liberation taught by Gautama.

Yet throughout these two collections, the word *Anattā* does not occur.

This observation is based on a search of the complete searchable Pāli texts of both collections in the GRETIL plain-text transcriptions derived from the Pali Text Society editions of the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā*. The search included the textual root *anatt-*, thereby covering the ordinary spelling *anattā* as well as possible inflected or compound forms containing that sequence. No occurrence was returned in either collection. The digital texts identify their editorial basis as the PTS editions prepared by Hermann Oldenberg and Richard Pischel, with the later second-edition appendices of K. R. Norman and A. K. Warder/Alsdorf as specified in the respective records. The transcriptions themselves advise that they should be checked against the printed editions where exact textual readings are required.

The significance of this finding becomes clearer when we consider the nature of these works. The *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* are not abstract philosophical treatises. They are collections of verses attributed to renunciants who describe their own spiritual cultivation, disciplines, realizations, and liberation. Their language repeatedly returns to the practical realities of the renunciant life: the cultivation of Citta, the restraint of the faculties, the abandonment of sensual attachment, the overcoming of Māra, the destruction of craving, the attainment of Samādhi, and the realization of Nibbāna.

In these accounts, the elders speak repeatedly of *Attā*. The term appears in expressions concerning spiritual discipline, steadfastness, spiritual cultivation, and the attainment of liberation. Among the examples are *pahitattā*, *samāhitatto*, *ṭhitatto*, *attānaṁ damayanti*, and *samunnamayam attānaṁ*. These expressions occur within descriptions of serious spiritual training and the transformation of the practitioner. They are part of the vocabulary through which the elders communicate their renunciant undertaking.

The textual contrast is therefore remarkable: the collections contain repeated references to *Attā*, while the term *Anattā* is entirely absent from their verses.

This observation raises a fundamental question about the place standard mainstream academia subsequently mis-assigned to *Anattā* in the interpretation of Gautama\'s teaching. In much of modern Buddhist presentation, *Anattā* is elevated as the defining philosophical principle of Buddhism, frequently understood as the denial of any Soul-Spirit. It is often presented as the central insight that distinguishes Gautama\'s teaching from the wider Indian spiritual traditions. Yet the very collections that preserve the voices of his early elder disciples do not employ this term at all.

The absence cannot simply be dismissed as insignificant. These verses record the language through which renunciants expressed their spiritual practice and realization. If the universal denial of Soul-Spirit had been the central doctrinal principle governing their understanding of liberation, its complete absence from both collections would require a substantial explanation. Instead, their recorded testimonies repeatedly employ the language of *Attā* in contexts of spiritual cultivation and attainment.

Arahanism therefore begins its examination with the testimony preserved in these collections. The elders describe a demanding path of renunciation, purification, discipline, and liberation. Their language directs attention towards the cultivation and establishment of *Attā*, the training and purification of Citta, and the abandonment of worldly bondage. The absence of *Anattā* from their verses must be considered alongside this positive and repeated language.

This finding does not, by itself, settle every question concerning the historical development or interpretation of the term *Anattā*. It does, however, establish an important textual fact that cannot responsibly be overlooked: **the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* contain no occurrence of *Anattā*, whereas they repeatedly preserve the language of *Attā* in the spiritual testimonies of the elders.**

The next task is to examine those occurrences individually, understand the contexts in which the elders employ *Attā*, and determine what their language reveals about the spiritual reality towards which their training was directed.

### 7.2. The Repeated Language of Attā

The absence of *Anattā* from the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* becomes especially significant when considered alongside the repeated appearance of *Attā* throughout these collections. The elders employ this term in numerous expressions describing the renunciant\'s spiritual discipline, inner steadfastness, spiritual cultivation, and attainment. These are not incidental references. They occur in verses concerned with the central undertaking of renunciation and the realization of liberation.

Arahanism understands *Attā* in these spiritual contexts as Soul-Spirit, corresponding to the Sanskrit *Ātmā*. The elders\' language must therefore be examined in relation to the spiritual training they describe. Their verses repeatedly direct attention towards the cultivation, discipline, elevation, and establishment of Attā.

**Attā and Spiritual Steadfastness**

The opening verses of the *Theragāthā* already introduce the language of spiritual cultivation. The collection begins:

*Suṇātha bhāvitattānaṁ, gāthā attupanāyikā.*

Listen to the verses of those whose Attā has been cultivated and developed.

The expression *bhāvita-attānaṁ* appears in the introductory description of the elders whose testimonies follow. Arahanism reads this expression as pointing towards the cultivation of Soul-Spirit. The opening therefore places the verses within the context of accomplished spiritual development.

The first group of elders repeatedly employs expressions ending in *-tatto*:

**Dabba --- Theragāthā 1.5**

*Vijitāvī apetabheravo hi, Dabbo so parinibbuto ṭhitatto.*

The capable one has conquered; fear has departed. Dabba is completely unbound and steadfast in Attā.

**Sītavaniya --- Theragāthā 1.6**

*Eko santusito samāhitatto.*

Solitary and content, with Attā firmly established in Samādhi.

**Bhalliya --- Theragāthā 1.7**

*Danto so parinibbuto ṭhitatto.*

Having mastered himself, he is completely unbound and steadfast in Attā.

**Vīra --- Theragāthā 1.8**

*Vīro so parinibbuto ṭhitatto.*

The spiritual hero is completely unbound and steadfast in Attā.

The repeated expression *ṭhitatto* is particularly important. These elders use it in descriptions of accomplished renunciants who have overcome fear, conquered Māra, and reached the state of liberation. Arahanism understands this expression as indicating steadfast establishment in Soul-Spirit. The state of being *parinibbuto* is thus presented alongside the positive description *ṭhitatto*: the renunciant is unbound from worldly bondage and established in Attā.

The expression *samāhitatto* in Sītavaniya\'s verse further connects Attā with Samādhi. The elder is described as solitary, content, and spiritually concentrated in the soul-spirit. The verse therefore places the language of Attā directly within the context of meditative cultivation.

These descriptions are difficult to reconcile with an interpretation that treats the entire spiritual undertaking as the realization that Soul-Spirit does not exist. The elders repeatedly use Attā-related expressions to describe the qualities and attainment of accomplished renunciants.

**The Discipline and Training of Attā**

The elders also employ Attā in verses that describe the deliberate discipline of spiritual life.

**Kula --- Theragāthā 1.19**

*Udakañhi nayanti nettikā, Usukārā namayanti tejanaṁ;*\
*Dāruṁ namayanti tacchakā, Attānaṁ damayanti subbatā.*

Irrigators direct the water; arrow-makers straighten the arrow; carpenters shape the wood. In the same way, those of good conduct discipline and train the Attā.

The verse presents three practical occupations as comparisons for spiritual training. Irrigators direct water according to their purpose. Arrow-makers straighten their arrows. Carpenters shape wood. The good practitioners similarly undertake the discipline of Attā.
