The final line is decisive for understanding the verse\'s spiritual vocabulary: *attānaṁ damayanti*. Arahanism reads this as the discipline and training of Soul-Spirit. The verse describes spiritual cultivation as a deliberate undertaking requiring direction, correction, and mastery.

This understanding is reinforced by Hārita\'s verse.

**Hārita --- Theragāthā 1.29**

*Samunnamayam attānaṁ, usukārova tejanaṁ;*\
*Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna, avijjaṁ bhinda Hāritā.*

Elevate your Attā, just as an arrow-maker straightens an arrow. Having made the Citta straight, break through ignorance, Hārita!

Here the relationship between Attā and Citta becomes especially important. The verse gives two related instructions:

1.  *Samunnamayam attānaṁ* --- elevate and raise Attā.

2.  *Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna* --- having made Citta straight, break through ignorance.

The two expressions occur together in a single spiritual instruction. Attā is to be elevated, while Citta is to be made straight. Arahanism understands this as a description of the coordinated cultivation of Soul-Spirit and Mind-Heart. The training of Citta is not the whole of the spiritual undertaking; the verse also explicitly directs attention towards Attā.

This distinction is central to the Arahanist understanding of spiritual cultivation. Citta requires discipline and purification, while Attā is the higher spiritual reality towards which the training is directed.

**Attā as the Object of Spiritual Striving**

The language of Attā also appears in expressions of determination and sustained spiritual effort.

**Kimbila --- Theragāthā 2.18**

*Āraddhavīriyā pahitattā, Niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā;*\
*Ramanti dhammaratiyā, Hitvāna lokiyaṁ ratiṁ.*

Aroused in spiritual energy, intent upon Attā, constantly possessing firm and unshakeable striving, they delight in the joy of Dhamma, having abandoned worldly pleasures.

The expression *pahitattā* is especially relevant to the Arahanist interpretation. It occurs alongside *āraddhavīriyā* and *niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā*, describing practitioners who have aroused their energy and maintain relentless spiritual effort. Arahanism understands *pahitattā* here as expressing their orientation towards Attā.

The verse then identifies the consequence of this commitment: they delight in the Dhamma after abandoning worldly pleasure. Their renunciation is directed towards a higher spiritual objective. It is not a search for improved worldly circumstances or a more comfortable relationship with existence.

The language of Attā therefore appears in connection with the renunciant\'s determination, spiritual energy, and abandonment of worldly gratification.

**Attā and the Rescue from Saṁsāra**

Another striking testimony occurs in the verse of Ajjuna.

**Ajjuna --- Theragāthā 1.88**

*Asakkhiṁ vata attānaṁ, Uddhātuṁ udakā thalaṁ;*\
*Vuyhamāno mahogheva, Saccāni paṭivijjhahaṁ.*

I was indeed able to rescue Attā from the water onto dry land, while being swept away as if by a great flood, having penetrated the truths.

The imagery is unmistakably spiritual. The practitioner describes himself as being swept away by a great flood, yet succeeding in bringing Attā from the water to dry land. Arahanism understands the flood as the overwhelming force of worldly existence and bondage, while dry land represents firm spiritual ground.

The expression *attānaṁ uddhātuṁ* is central to the verse. The elder speaks of rescuing Attā. His testimony is therefore expressed through the language of spiritual deliverance, with Attā as the object of that rescue.

This image accords with the wider renunciant undertaking described throughout the two collections: overcoming the forces that sustain worldly existence and attaining liberation.

**Attā as Something to Be Protected and Guarded**

The elders also speak of protecting Attā.

In Theragāthā 1.142, the elder advises the practitioner to live in secluded dwellings, pursue freedom from bondage, and remain:

*Rakkhitatto satīmā.*

Guarding Attā, possessing mindfulness.

A similar instruction appears in Theragāthā 14.653:

*Nagaraṁ yathā paccantaṁ guttaṁ santarabāhiraṁ,*\
*Evaṁ gopetha attānaṁ, khaṇo ve mā upaccagā.*

As a frontier city is guarded within and without, so guard Attā. Do not let this opportunity pass.

The comparison with a fortified city conveys the seriousness of spiritual protection. The practitioner must guard Attā with vigilance, just as a city must be protected against dangers from within and without.

These verses reinforce the picture of a spiritual undertaking in which Attā is the focus of discipline, protection, and cultivation.

**The Testimony of the Elder Nuns**

The *Therīgāthā* also preserves the language of Attā in the testimonies of the elder nuns.

**Cittā --- Therīgāthā 2.8**

*Saṅghāṭiṁ nikkhipitvāna, Pattakaṁ ca nikujjiya;*\
*Sele khambhesiṁ attānaṁ, Tamokkhandhaṁ padāliya.*

Having put aside my robe and overturned my bowl, I established Attā firmly upon the mountain, having shattered the mass of darkness.

The expression *sele khambhesiṁ attānaṁ* is particularly significant. Cittā describes establishing Attā firmly upon the mountain. Arahanism reads this as an expression of spiritual steadfastness: Attā is established upon firm ground while the mass of darkness is shattered.

Her testimony is a declaration of spiritual accomplishment. The language of Attā appears in the account of her own renunciant practice and realization.

Another example appears in the verses attributed to Ubbirī.

**Ubbirī --- Therīgāthā 3.5**

*Amma Jīvā ti vanamhi kandasi, Attānaṁ adhigaccha Ubbiri.*

Why do you cry in the forest, saying, "Jīvā, my daughter"? Ubbirī, attain Attā!

The instruction *attānaṁ adhigaccha* directs Ubbirī towards spiritual attainment. In the Arahanist reading, the verse turns her attention away from grief and towards the attainment of Soul-Spirit.

The significance lies in the direction of the instruction. The elder is urged to attain Attā rather than remain consumed by worldly grief.

**The Cumulative Testimony**

The examples examined above reveal a consistent pattern across the two collections. Attā appears in descriptions of:

- spiritual cultivation and development;

- steadfastness and establishment;

- concentration and Samādhi;

- discipline and mastery;

- elevation and spiritual striving;

- rescue from the flood of worldly existence;

- protection and guarding;

- the attainment of spiritual realization.

These expressions occur in the actual testimonies of the elders. They are embedded in accounts of renunciation, meditative discipline, the abandonment of worldly attachment, and the attainment of liberation.

The pattern is especially significant when considered alongside the absence of *Anattā* established in the preceding section. The two collections repeatedly preserve the language of Attā in spiritually consequential contexts, while the term *Anattā* does not occur in their verses.

Arahanism therefore gives these testimonies their full doctrinal significance. The elders speak of cultivating, disciplining, elevating, establishing, guarding, and rescuing Attā. Their language presents Soul-Spirit as a central concern of the renunciant\'s spiritual undertaking.

The next question is what this Attā signifies. Is it merely a conventional expression for the ordinary personality, or does it refer to the higher spiritual reality recognized in the wider Indian heritage as Ātmā? Arahanism identifies Attā with Soul-Spirit and examines this relationship in the following section.

### 7.3. Attā as Ātmā: Recovering the Original Spiritual Meaning

The repeated appearance of *Attā* throughout the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā* raises a fundamental question concerning its original spiritual meaning. Why did the elders repeatedly speak of cultivating, disciplining, elevating, establishing, guarding, and attaining Attā? What spiritual reality did this term signify within their renunciant undertaking?

Arahanism understands *Attā* as Soul-Spirit, corresponding to Sanskrit *Ātmā*. This interpretation must be considered within the wider historical development of Indian spiritual knowledge, beginning with the ancient Vedic heritage of the Āryan civilization.

The meaning of *Attā* cannot be adequately understood by isolating the Pāli word from its linguistic ancestry and the spiritual traditions of the Indian subcontinent. Its historical counterpart, Sanskrit *Ātmā*, belongs to an ancient body of spiritual knowledge that preceded the emergence of Pāli literature. The transmission and development of this knowledge across different Indian linguistic and religious communities provide an essential background for understanding the terminology employed by Gautama and the early renunciants.

**The Āryan Origins of Ātmā and Brahman**

The earliest foundation for examining this spiritual heritage is the Vedic civilization of the Āryan people. The four Vedas preserve an ancient body of sacred knowledge, religious practices, cosmological understanding, and spiritual inquiry. Among the oldest surviving records of this civilization is the Ṛgveda, whose hymns preserve the early vocabulary and religious understanding of the Vedic tradition.

The knowledge associated with *Ātmā* and *Brahman* belongs to this wider Vedic heritage. Importantly, the word *Ātman* is already found in the Ṛgveda. Its early occurrences demonstrate that the vocabulary existed long before the composition of the principal Upaniṣads. The Upaniṣads subsequently developed profound inquiries into Ātmā, Brahman, and the ultimate nature of spiritual existence, drawing upon an older Vedic inheritance.

The historical sequence is therefore important. The Upaniṣads did not originate the entire knowledge of Ātmā. They represent a later stage in the development of an older spiritual tradition.

The Vedic understanding of Ātmā concerns the life principle and the innermost spiritual reality of a being. The wider inquiry into Brahman concerns the ultimate spiritual principle and the nature of humanity and its progenitor *manussa*. These became central subjects of Indian spiritual foundation, although their precise meanings and relationship developed differently across the various Vedic schools and texts.

For Arahanism, this ancient heritage provides the historical starting point for understanding the spiritual vocabulary subsequently encountered in Pāli and other Indian languages.

**The Transmission of Vedic Spiritual Knowledge Across India**

As the Āryan civilization established itself across northern India and interacted with the diverse peoples and cultures of the subcontinent, its spiritual knowledge entered a much wider cultural environment. This historical process involved the transmission of religious vocabulary, sacred knowledge, ritual practices, cosmological conceptions, and spiritual teachings.

The knowledge of Ātmā and Brahman consequently became part of the broader Indian spiritual inheritance. Different communities encountered this heritage and incorporated elements of it into their own religious and renunciant developments. The resulting traditions did not necessarily preserve identical doctrines. They received, interpreted, and developed spiritual concepts according to their own teachings and practices.

This process is important for understanding the emergence of the wider renunciant spirituality in which Gautama taught. The renunciant traditions of ancient India did not develop in complete isolation from the older spiritual heritage of the subcontinent. They existed within a cultural and intellectual environment shaped by centuries and even millenniums of religious interaction, spiritual inquiry, and the transmission of inherited knowledge.

The use of Attā and its corresponding forms across different Indian languages must be understood within this historical setting.

**From Sanskrit Ātmā to Pāli Attā and Prakrit Appā**

The linguistic relationship between Sanskrit *Ātmā* and Pāli *Attā* provides an important connection to this older heritage.

Sanskrit *ātman* and Pāli *attan* are historically related forms. The Pāli nominative singular is *attā*, while other grammatical forms include *attānaṁ*, *attanā*, *attano*, and *attani*. Prakrit traditions likewise preserve corresponding forms, including *appā*.

These differences reflect the historical development of Indo-Aryan languages. Sanskrit represents an Old Indo-Aryan literary language, while Pāli and the Prakrit languages belong to the Middle Indo-Aryan linguistic stage. They developed through related but distinct linguistic histories, involving changes in pronunciation, consonant clusters, grammatical forms, and vocabulary.

Pāli should therefore not simply be described as simplified Sanskrit. It is a distinct Middle Indo-Aryan language. Nevertheless, the relationship between Sanskrit *ātman*, Pāli *attan*, and Prakrit *appa* is part of the established historical linguistics of the Indo-Aryan languages.

The significance of this relationship extends beyond phonetic development. These languages transmitted and preserved vocabulary belonging to the wider Indian religious and spiritual environment. The word *Attā* in Pāli must therefore be examined in relation to the older Sanskrit *Ātmā*, rather than interpreted as though it had emerged independently of that heritage.

For Arahanism, *Attā* and *Ātmā* designate the same fundamental spiritual reality: Soul-Spirit. Their different linguistic forms do not require us to assign them unrelated spiritual meanings.

**The Incorporation of Ātmā into Different Spiritual Traditions**

As the knowledge of Ātmā circulated within the wider Indian spiritual environment, different traditions incorporated and developed it in their own ways.

The Vedic tradition preserved and elaborated the inquiry into Ātmā and Brahman. The Upaniṣads developed teachings concerning the innermost spiritual reality and its relationship to ultimate reality. Other Indian renunciant traditions employed corresponding terminology within their own systems of spiritual discipline and liberation.

The Jain tradition, for example, through its unbroken tradition of millenniums years already developed an extensive teaching concerning *jīva*, the living soul, and its bondage through karma. Its understanding of the soul belongs to its own distinctive doctrinal system, even though it developed within the same wider Indian spiritual environment.

The significance of these traditions lies in the fact that the spiritual question concerning the innermost reality of a being was not foreign to ancient India. It was a major subject of inquiry across different religious and renunciant communities.

Arahanism understands Gautama\'s teaching within this wider historical and spiritual continuum. His teaching emerged in an environment where the vocabulary of Ātmā and its corresponding forms already had an established history. The Pāli term *Attā* belongs to that inherited linguistic environment.

This historical background does not mean that every tradition taught precisely the same doctrine of Soul-Spirit. It means that the vocabulary and the spiritual questions associated with it must be understood in their proper historical context. Gautama\'s use of *Attā* should not be detached from this heritage and assigned a meaning solely through later mis-interpretations.

**Attā in the Spiritual Testimonies of the Elders**

The linguistic and historical background becomes particularly significant when we return to the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā*.

As established in the preceding section, the elders repeatedly employ Attā-related expressions when describing their spiritual practices and accomplishments. Their language includes:

- *Bhāvitattānaṁ* --- those whose Attā has been cultivated and developed.

- *Pahitattā* --- spiritually directed towards Attā.

- *Samāhitatto* --- Attā established in Samādhi.

- *Ṭhitatto* --- steadfastly established in Attā.

- *Attānaṁ damayanti* --- disciplining and training Attā.

- *Samunnamayam attānaṁ* --- elevating Attā.

- *Attānaṁ uddhātuṁ* --- rescuing Attā.

- *Rakkhitatto* --- guarding Attā.

- *Attānaṁ adhigaccha* --- attaining Attā.

These expressions occur in the context of renunciation, spiritual discipline, meditative cultivation, the destruction of ignorance, and liberation from worldly bondage.

Hārita\'s instruction is particularly revealing:

*Samunnamayam attānaṁ, usukārova tejanaṁ;*\
*Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna, avijjaṁ bhinda Hāritā.*

Elevate your Attā, just as an arrow-maker straightens an arrow. Having made the Citta straight, break through ignorance, Hārita!

Here, Attā and Citta appear together in a single spiritual instruction. Attā is to be elevated, while Citta is to be made straight. Arahanism understands this as an expression of two related dimensions of spiritual cultivation: the elevation of Soul-Spirit and the training and purification of Mind-Heart.

Likewise, the repeated expression *ṭhitatto* describes elders who have conquered fear and attained liberation. Their spiritual accomplishment is expressed through steadfastness in Attā alongside their unbinding from worldly existence.

These testimonies acquire a coherent spiritual meaning when Attā is understood as Soul-Spirit. The elders are describing a path of renunciation in which the practitioner disciplines Citta, abandons worldly attachments, overcomes ignorance, and directs spiritual cultivation towards the higher reality of Attā.

**The Distortion of Attā Through Its Translation**

The historical and spiritual relationship between *Ātmā* and *Attā* also raises an important question concerning translation.

The widespread practice of translating *Attā* simply as \"self\" has obscured its connection with the older Indian spiritual vocabulary. In modern English, \"self\" can refer to personal identity, ego, personality, individuality, or merely the grammatical subject of a reflexive expression. These meanings do not adequately communicate the spiritual significance that Arahanism recognizes in the elders\' use of Attā.

When *attānaṁ damayanti* is translated merely as \"they discipline themselves,\" or *samunnamayam attānaṁ* as \"elevate oneself,\" the reader may understand these instructions as ordinary exercises in personal discipline. The deeper spiritual significance of Attā is thereby obscured.

Arahanism rejects the reduction of Attā to a psychological personality or an ordinary sense of personal identity. In its spiritual interpretation, Attā means Soul-Spirit, corresponding to Sanskrit Ātmā.

The grammatical forms of the Pāli word must, of course, be understood accurately in each passage. Yet grammatical analysis alone cannot determine the complete spiritual significance of a term. Its historical relationship, textual context, and place within the wider spiritual vocabulary must also be considered.

The elders\' repeated language of cultivation, elevation, steadfastness, protection, and attainment provides the basis for the Arahanist interpretation. Their verses must be allowed to communicate the spiritual reality expressed through their own vocabulary.

**Recovering the Original Spiritual Meaning**

The relationship between *Attā* and *Ātmā* reconnects the spiritual testimonies of the early renunciants with the ancient Indian heritage from which their language emerged.

The Vedic tradition preserved the early knowledge and vocabulary of Ātmā and Brahman. This spiritual heritage entered a wider Indian cultural environment, where different linguistic communities and religious traditions incorporated and developed its concepts. Sanskrit Ātmā, Pāli Attā, and Prakrit Appā belong to the linguistic history through which this vocabulary was transmitted and preserved.

Within this historical continuum, the repeated appearance of Attā in the testimonies of the elders assumes considerable significance. They speak of cultivating it, disciplining it, elevating it, establishing it, guarding it, and attaining it. Their language describes an active spiritual undertaking directed towards liberation.

Arahanism therefore recognizes Attā as Soul-Spirit and understands its cultivation as an essential dimension of Gautama\'s liberation teaching. This interpretation also requires us to reconsider the relationship between Attā and Citta, particularly the significance of inward spiritual cultivation.

The next subject, *Ajjhatta*, provides an important key to this relationship. It directs our attention towards the inward spiritual dimension of the renunciant\'s training and the establishment of Citta. By examining Ajjhatta alongside Attā, we can further clarify the spiritual orientation of the elders and the nature of the liberation they sought.

### 7.4. The Elders\' Spiritual Practice: Cultivating and Establishing Attā

The testimonies of the elders reveal that Attā was repeatedly associated with the practical discipline of renunciant life. Their verses speak of directing spiritual effort towards Attā, establishing it in Samādhi, making it steadfast, disciplining it, elevating it, and rescuing it from the dangers of worldly existence.

These expressions deserve particular attention because they occur in descriptions of actual spiritual practices and accomplishments. The elders do not speak of Attā merely as an abstract subject of philosophical speculation. They employ the term while explaining what a renunciant must cultivate and what is accomplished through spiritual training.

Arahanism understands these expressions as references to Soul-Spirit and regards them as important evidence for recovering the spiritual orientation of Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

**Pahitattā: Directing Spiritual Effort Towards Attā**

Consider the testimony of Kimbila:

**Kimbila --- Theragāthā 2.18**

*Āraddhavīriyā pahitattā, Niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā;*\
*Ramanti dhammaratiyā, Hitvāna lokiyaṁ ratiṁ.*

Aroused in spiritual energy, intent towards Attā, constantly possessing firm and unshakeable striving; they delight in the joy of Dhamma, having abandoned worldly pleasures.

The expression *pahitattā* appears alongside two other descriptions of the practitioners:

- *Āraddha-vīriyā* --- possessing aroused and established spiritual energy.

- *Pahitattā* --- directed towards Attā.

- *Niccaṁ daḷhaparakkamā* --- constantly possessing firm and relentless striving.

These three expressions describe the determination of practitioners who have abandoned worldly gratification and devoted themselves to spiritual cultivation.

Arahanism understands *pahitattā* as expressing the orientation of the practitioner towards Soul-Spirit. The significance of the expression becomes apparent from its immediate context: these practitioners have aroused their spiritual energy, directed themselves towards Attā, and maintained unshakeable striving.

Their delight is now found in the Dhamma rather than worldly pleasure. Their undertaking is directed towards liberation from worldly bondage.

The expression therefore contributes to the wider pattern in which Attā appears as a central concern of renunciant spiritual practice.

**Samāhitatto: Attā Established in Samādhi**

Another important expression occurs in the testimony of Sītavaniya.

**Sītavaniya --- Theragāthā 1.6**

*Yo sītavanaṁ upagā bhikkhu, Eko santusito samāhitatto;*\
*Vijitāvī apetalomahaṁso, Rakkhaṁ kāyagatāsatiṁ dhitimā ti.*

The expression *samāhitatto* is particularly significant because it connects Attā with Samādhi.

The elder describes a monk who has entered the forest, dwells alone, is content, and is *samāhitatto*. Arahanism understands this expression as indicating that Attā is firmly established in Samādhi.

This interpretation places Attā within the actual practice of meditative cultivation. Samādhi is not merely a temporary condition of mental calm. It is an essential discipline through which the practitioner develops spiritual stability and overcomes the disturbances associated with worldly existence.

The relationship between Attā and Citta must also be considered here. In the Arahanist understanding, Citta is the faculty that undergoes training, concentration, and purification. Through Samādhi, Citta is disciplined and brought into spiritual alignment. The establishment of Attā in Samādhi therefore belongs to a larger process of spiritual cultivation involving both Soul-Spirit and Mind-Heart.

This relationship will become particularly important when we examine *Ajjhatta* in the following section.

**Ṭhitatto: Steadfast Establishment in Attā**

The expression *ṭhitatto* appears repeatedly in the early verses of the *Theragāthā*. Three examples illustrate its significance.

**Dabba --- Theragāthā 1.5**

*Vijitāvī apetabheravo hi, Dabbo so parinibbuto ṭhitatto.*

Dabba has conquered and fear has departed. He is completely unbound and steadfast in Attā.

**Bhalliya --- Theragāthā 1.7**

*Yopānudī maccurājassa senaṁ, Naḷasetuṁva sudubbalaṁ mahogho;*\
*Vijitāvī apetabheravo hi, Danto so parinibbuto ṭhitatto ti.*

Having overcome the army of the King of Death, as a great flood overwhelms a fragile bridge, he has conquered and is free from fear. Having mastered himself, he is completely unbound and steadfast in Attā.

**Vīra --- Theragāthā 1.8**

*Yo duddamiyo damena danto, Vīro santusito vitiṇṇakaṅkho;*\
*Vijitāvī apetalomahaṁso, Vīro so parinibbuto ṭhitatto ti.*

The spiritual hero, having mastered what is difficult to master, is content and free from doubt. Having conquered and overcome fear, he is completely unbound and steadfast in Attā.

The repeated appearance of *ṭhitatto* in these verses is noteworthy. The elders associate this expression with accomplished practitioners who have conquered fear, overcome Māra, and attained liberation.

Arahanism understands *ṭhitatto* as steadfast establishment in Soul-Spirit. The expression communicates a positive spiritual condition: Attā is established and remains steadfast.

The accompanying expression *parinibbuto* describes the practitioner\'s complete unbinding. These two descriptions belong together. The renunciant has overcome worldly bondage and attained a state of spiritual steadfastness.

This language deserves serious consideration. The elders describe their liberation through expressions that include the positive establishment of Attā. Their testimonies therefore provide evidence for examining liberation in relation to both the abandonment of worldly bondage and the spiritual establishment of Soul-Spirit.

**Attānaṁ Damayanti: Disciplining and Training Attā**

One of the clearest expressions of practical Attā cultivation appears in Kula\'s verse.

**Kula --- Theragāthā 1.19**

*Udakañhi nayanti nettikā, Usukārā namayanti tejanaṁ;*\
*Dāruṁ namayanti tacchakā, Attānaṁ damayanti subbatā ti.*

Irrigators direct the water; arrow-makers straighten the arrow; carpenters shape the wood. In the same way, those of good conduct discipline and train Attā.

The verse presents three familiar occupations as illustrations of spiritual training.

Irrigators direct water towards the intended fields. Arrow-makers carefully straighten their arrows. Carpenters shape and refine wood according to their purpose. In the same way, practitioners of good conduct undertake the discipline of Attā.

The expression *attānaṁ damayanti* is central to the verse. Arahanism understands it as the deliberate disciplining and training of Soul-Spirit.

The comparison is important because each occupation involves direction, correction, and the removal of irregularities. Spiritual training likewise requires deliberate effort. The practitioner must overcome the conditions that obstruct spiritual development and cultivate the qualities necessary for liberation.

This verse also challenges interpretations that reduce the elders\' language of Attā to ordinary personality or psychological identity. The expression occurs within an explicit comparison of practical training and spiritual discipline. Its significance must be considered in that context.

**Samunnamayam Attānaṁ: Elevating Attā**

The relationship between Attā and Citta becomes particularly clear in Hārita\'s instruction.

**Hārita --- Theragāthā 1.29**

*Samunnamayam attānaṁ, usukārova tejanaṁ;*\
*Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna, avijjaṁ bhinda Hāritā ti.*

Elevate your Attā, just as an arrow-maker straightens an arrow. Having made the Citta straight, break through ignorance, Hārita!

This verse contains two closely connected instructions:

1.  *Samunnamayam attānaṁ* --- elevate Attā.

2.  *Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna* --- having made Citta straight, break through ignorance.

The comparison with the arrow-maker again emphasizes deliberate spiritual cultivation. Just as an arrow-maker straightens an arrow, the practitioner must undertake the elevation of Attā.

The second instruction concerns Citta. It must be made straight so that ignorance can be penetrated and destroyed.

Arahanism considers the conjunction of these two instructions especially important. Attā and Citta are both present in the same verse, yet they are addressed through different expressions. Attā is to be elevated; Citta is to be straightened.

This distinction is central to the Arahanist understanding of spiritual training. Citta undergoes discipline and purification, while Attā represents the higher Soul-Spirit towards which the spiritual undertaking is directed.

The verse therefore provides an important foundation for understanding the relationship between Soul-Spirit and Mind-Heart. It also prepares the way for examining the significance of *Ajjhatta*, particularly the inward establishment and orientation of Citta.

**Attānaṁ Uddhātuṁ: Rescuing Attā from the Flood**

The language of Attā also appears in a striking testimony of spiritual rescue.

**Ajjuna --- Theragāthā 1.88**

*Asakkhiṁ vata attānaṁ, Uddhātuṁ udakā thalaṁ;*\
*Vuyhamāno mahoghev(a), Saccāni paṭivijjhahan ti.*

I was indeed able to rescue Attā from the water onto dry land, while being swept away as if by a great flood, having penetrated the truths.

The imagery of this verse is powerful. The elder describes the condition of one being carried away by a great flood, yet succeeding in rescuing Attā from the water and bringing it onto dry land.

The expression *attānaṁ uddhātuṁ* is particularly significant. The elder speaks explicitly of rescuing Attā.

Arahanism understands the flood as representing the overwhelming forces of worldly existence and bondage. The dry land represents firm spiritual ground, associated with freedom from the conditions that sustain continued worldly existence.

The verse presents liberation through the language of rescue. Attā is brought out of the flood and established upon secure ground. The elder\'s realization of the truths is associated with this successful spiritual undertaking.

This testimony is consistent with the wider pattern of the elders\' verses. They speak of overcoming Māra, abandoning worldly attachment, destroying ignorance, and attaining liberation. Here, the same undertaking is expressed through the image of rescuing Attā from a great flood.

**The Coherent Pattern of Spiritual Cultivation**

The expressions examined in this section reveal a consistent pattern in the elders\' descriptions of spiritual practice.

  ------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Pāli expression**      **Arahanist understanding**
  ------------------------ -----------------------------------------
  *Pahitattā*              Directing spiritual effort towards Attā

  *Samāhitatto*            Attā established in Samādhi

  *Ṭhitatto*               Steadfast establishment in Attā

  *Attānaṁ damayanti*      Disciplining and training Attā

  *Samunnamayam attānaṁ*   Elevating Attā

  *Attānaṁ uddhātuṁ*       Rescuing Attā from the flood
  ------------------------------------------------------------------

Taken together, these expressions describe a spiritual undertaking involving direction, concentration, discipline, elevation, steadfastness, and rescue.

They also reveal that the elders\' language of Attā is closely connected with the practical realities of renunciant life. Their concern is the attainment of liberation through sustained spiritual effort, the overcoming of ignorance, and the abandonment of worldly bondage.

For Arahanism, these testimonies provide important evidence that Attā, understood as Soul-Spirit, occupies a central place in the spiritual vocabulary of the early renunciants.

**From Attā to Ajjhatta**

The examination of these verses brings us to another important dimension of spiritual cultivation.

The elders speak of directing effort towards Attā, establishing it in Samādhi, disciplining it, elevating it, and rescuing it. These expressions raise a further question: where is this spiritual cultivation directed, and how is Citta established in relation to the higher spiritual reality?

The answer requires examining the Pāli term *Ajjhatta*.

In Arahanist understanding, *Ajjhatta* points towards the inward Higher Soul-Spirit and the higher spiritual reality towards which Citta must be directed. Its significance cannot be adequately understood if it is reduced to ordinary physical inwardness or psychological introspection.

The relationship between Attā, Ajjhatta, and Citta is therefore essential to understanding the elders\' spiritual undertaking. The next section examines Ajjhatta in greater detail, including its canonical usage and its significance for the inward orientation of Citta and the attainment of liberation.

### 7.5. Ajjhatta: The Higher Soul-Spirit and the Inward Direction of Liberation

The preceding sections have established the repeated presence of *Attā* in the spiritual vocabulary of the elders. Their testimonies describe the cultivation, discipline, elevation, protection, and steadfast establishment of Attā. Their language also repeatedly refers to the training of *Citta*, its unification, and its establishment in *Samādhi*. These expressions direct our attention towards an important dimension of Gautama\'s liberation teaching: *Ajjhatta*, the inward spiritual reality towards which the renunciant\'s cultivation is directed.

The Pāli expression *Ajjhatta* is commonly translated as \"internal,\" \"inward,\" or \"within oneself.\" Such translations convey its general directional sense, but they do not, by themselves, explain its deeper significance in the Arahanist understanding of liberation. Arahanism understands *Ajjhatta* in relation to the Higher Soul-Spirit, the inward spiritual reality of the being. It is this higher spiritual dimension that gives meaning to the cultivation of Attā and the purification and establishment of Citta.

The distinction between the physical body, the five aggregates, Citta, and Attā must therefore remain clear. The body belongs to the manifested physical existence. The five aggregates constitute the conditioned manifestations through which worldly experience takes place. Citta is the Mind-Heart that must undergo spiritual training and purification. Attā is the Soul-Spirit, the higher spiritual reality towards which this training is directed. *Ajjhatta* expresses the inward orientation of this spiritual process.

**Citta Becoming Established Inwardly**

An important canonical passage appears in the *Aṅguttara Nikāya* 4.170, the *Yuganaddha Sutta*. Ānanda describes four approaches through which practitioners attain Arahantship. In the fourth approach, he describes a practitioner whose mind has become disturbed by agitation concerning the Dhamma:

*Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno dhammuddhaccaviggahitaṃ mānasaṃ hoti. Hoti so, āvuso, samayo yaṃ taṃ cittaṃ ajjhattameva santiṭṭhati sannisīdati ekodi hoti samādhiyati. Tassa maggo sañjāyati.*

A translation of the passage is:

\"Furthermore, friends, a monk\'s mind becomes seized by agitation concerning the Dhamma. There comes a time when that Citta becomes established inwardly (*ajjhattameva santiṭṭhati*), settles down (*sannisīdati*), becomes unified (*ekodi hoti*), and enters Samādhi (*samādhiyati*). From this, the Path arises.\"

The sequence of expressions is significant. The Citta becomes established inwardly, settles, becomes unified, and enters Samādhi. The passage then states that the Path arises. The practitioner cultivates and repeatedly develops that Path, through which the fetters are abandoned and the underlying tendencies are destroyed. Ānanda concludes that practitioners who declare their attainment of Arahantship do so through one or another of these four approaches.

This passage establishes a direct relationship between the inward establishment of Citta, Samādhi, and the arising of the Path leading to Arahantship. The inward direction is therefore integral to the process of liberation.

Arahanism understands this inward establishment in relation to Attā. When Citta is scattered among worldly objects and conditioned manifestations, it remains entangled in worldly existence. Through renunciant discipline, Samādhi, and spiritual purification, Citta becomes unified and established inwardly. In the Arahanist understanding, this inward establishment is the orientation of Citta towards Higher Soul-Spirit.

The passage does not explicitly define *Ajjhatta* as Attā. Its importance lies in the relationship it establishes between inward establishment of Citta and the arising of the liberating Path. Arahanism interprets this relationship alongside the elders\' repeated use of Attā and their descriptions of spiritual cultivation. Taken together, these expressions reveal a coherent direction of practice: Citta must be purified and established upon the higher inward spiritual reality.

**The Dhamma Leads Inward**

The same inward direction appears in the well-known formula of *Dhamma-Anusati*, the Exalted Recollection of the Dhamma:

*Svākkhāto Bhagavatā Dhammo, Sandiṭṭhiko, Akāliko, Ehipassiko, Opanayiko, Paccattaṃ Veditabbo Viññūhī\'ti.*

The formula describes the Dhamma as:

- *Svākkhāto* --- Well-explained by the Divinely Blessed One.

- *Sandiṭṭhiko* --- Completely seen.

- *Akāliko* --- Timeless.

- *Ehipassiko* --- Come and see for yourself.

- *Opanayiko* --- Leading inward.

- *Paccattaṃ Veditabbo Viññūhi* --- To be directly realized by the discerning, in relation to the Attā (Soul-Spirit).

The expression *Opanayiko* is particularly significant. The Dhamma leads inward. Its realization requires the practitioner to turn away from dependence upon worldly manifestations and undertake the inward spiritual cultivation through which liberation becomes possible.

The expression *Paccattaṃ Veditabbo Viññūhi* further emphasizes direct realization. Arahanism understands *Paccattaṃ* in relation to *Attā*, reading the formula as directing realization towards the Soul-Spirit. The Dhamma must be realized through direct spiritual knowledge, rather than merely accepted as an external teaching or intellectual proposition. This is consistent with the inward direction expressed by *Opanayiko*.

The formula thus presents a significant relationship between the Dhamma, inward spiritual cultivation, and direct realization. The Dhamma leads inward, and its truth must be realized through the practitioner\'s own spiritual attainment. In the Arahanist interpretation, this inward realization concerns the Higher Soul-Spirit.

**Ajjhatta and the Liberation of Attā**

The significance of *Ajjhatta* becomes clearer when considered alongside the spiritual progression established in the preceding chapters.

The practitioner begins with Sīla, restraining bodily and verbal conduct and abandoning the actions that sustain worldly bondage. Through Samādhi, Citta becomes unified and purified. Through Paññā, the practitioner recognizes the conditioned nature of worldly manifestations and develops Nibbidā towards them. Citta consequently relinquishes its attachment to worldly establishment and turns towards the higher inward spiritual reality.

This transition is central to the Arahanist understanding of liberation. Citta must cease its dependence upon the five aggregates and become established upon Higher Soul-Spirit. Through continued cultivation, worldly passion fades through Virāga, and the defilements sustaining bondage are eradicated. Vimutti is the liberation of Attā from worldly bondage, culminating in Arahantship.

Ajjhatta therefore provides an essential dimension of the relationship between Attā and Citta. Attā is the Soul-Spirit; Citta is the Mind-Heart undergoing purification and spiritual establishment; Ajjhatta expresses the inward spiritual direction of this cultivation. These concepts must be understood together when examining the liberation teaching preserved in the testimonies of the elders.

The repeated language of Attā in the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā*, the inward establishment of Citta in AN 4.170, and the inward direction expressed by *Opanayiko* in the Dhamma-Anusati formula all contribute to the Arahanist interpretation of this spiritual path.

The next question concerns the canonical teaching most frequently invoked to deny the existence of Soul-Spirit: *Anattā*. If the elders repeatedly speak of Attā, cultivate it, discipline it, and describe their spiritual attainment in relation to it, what exactly did Gautama reject when he declared the five aggregates to be *Anattā*? Did he reject the aggregates as Attā, or did he teach the universal non-existence of Soul-Spirit? This question must now be examined directly through SN 22.59 and MN 44.

### 7.6. Anattā and Sakkāya-diṭṭhi: What Did Gautama Actually Reject?

The preceding sections have examined the repeated language of *Attā* in the testimonies of the elders, the inward direction of spiritual cultivation expressed through *Ajjhatta*, and the relationship between the purification of *Citta* and the attainment of liberation. We must now address the canonical passages most frequently invoked to establish the doctrine that no Soul-Spirit exists: SN 22.59 and MN 44.

These two discourses are central to the interpretation of *Anattā* and the five aggregates. They must be examined according to their actual wording, their immediate arguments, and the specific propositions they establish. The question is whether Gautama\'s rejection of the five aggregates as Attā necessarily establishes the universal non-existence of Soul-Spirit.

Arahanism maintains that the five aggregates are not Attā. The physical body, feeling, perception, formations, and sense-generated consciousness are conditioned manifestations. They arise, change, become afflicted, and cannot remain under the complete command of the practitioner. The renunciant must understand their nature, relinquish attachment to them, and overcome the worldly bondage sustained through identification with them.

However, the rejection of these five aggregates as Attā must be distinguished from the proposition that Soul-Spirit does not exist. The distinction is fundamental to the Arahanist interpretation of Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

#### 7.6.1. Anattā in SN 22.59: The Five Aggregates Are Not Attā

In SN 22.59, commonly known as the *Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta*, Gautama addresses the group of five monks at Isipatana. He examines each of the five aggregates, beginning with *Rūpa*:

*Rūpaṃ, bhikkhave, anattā.*

\"Form, monks, is Anattā.\"

He then explains:

*Rūpañca hidaṃ, bhikkhave, attā abhavissa, nayidaṃ rūpaṃ ābādhāya saṃvatteyya, labbhetha ca rūpe: \'evaṃ me rūpaṃ hotu, evaṃ me rūpaṃ mā ahosī\'ti.*

\"If form, monks, were Attā, this form would not lead to affliction, and it would be possible to have it of form: \'Let my form be thus; let my form not be thus.\'\"

The same reasoning is then applied to feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. Each is declared *Anattā* because it is subject to affliction and cannot be maintained according to one\'s wishes.

The argument is direct. If form were Attā, it would not be subject to affliction in this manner, and one could determine its condition according to one\'s wishes. Yet the physical body deteriorates, becomes ill, ages, and eventually dies. The practitioner cannot command it to remain young, healthy, or unchanged.

The same limitation applies to the other aggregates. Feelings arise and disappear; perceptions change; formations are conditioned; and *Viññāṇa*, as sense-generated consciousness, arises and changes according to conditions. None can be maintained under complete personal command.

Arahanism accepts this teaching. The five aggregates are not Attā. They are conditioned manifestations and cannot serve as the Soul-Spirit or the ultimate spiritual foundation of liberation.

Gautama then develops the examination through another question:

*Yaṃ panāniccaṃ dukkhaṃ vipariṇāmadhammaṃ, kallaṃ nu taṃ samanupassituṃ: \'etaṃ mama, esohamasmi, eso me attā\'?*

\"That which is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change: is it proper to regard it as \'This is mine, this I am, this is my Attā\'?\"

The monks answer: *No hetaṃ, bhante.* \"No, Venerable Sir.\"

This formula is repeated for all five aggregates. It establishes three forms of mistaken appropriation:

- *Etaṃ mama* --- This is mine.

- *Esohamasmi* --- This is what I am.

- *Eso me attā* --- This is my Attā.

These three expressions expose the different ways in which a practitioner becomes bound to the aggregates: through ownership, identification, and the mistaken recognition of a conditioned manifestation as Soul-Spirit.

The discourse then extends the examination across every form of each aggregate, whether past, future, or present; inward or outward; gross or subtle; inferior or superior; far or near. All such manifestations must be understood through right wisdom:

*Netaṃ mama, nesohamasmi, na meso attā.*

\"This is not mine, this I am not, this is not my Attā.\"

The repeated declaration is decisive concerning the five aggregates. No form, feeling, perception, formation, or sense-generated consciousness should be appropriated as Attā. The practitioner must develop disenchantment towards them, relinquish passion, and attain liberation.

The conclusion of the discourse describes precisely this process:

*Evaṃ passaṃ, bhikkhave, sutavā ariyasāvako rūpasmimpi nibbindati, vedanāyapi nibbindati, saññāyapi nibbindati, saṅkhāresupi nibbindati, viññāṇasmimpi nibbindati. Nibbindaṃ virajjati; virāgā vimuccati.*

\"Seeing thus, monks, the instructed noble disciple becomes disenchanted with form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. Through disenchantment, passion fades; through the fading of passion, liberation is attained.\"

The direction of the discourse is therefore unmistakable: the practitioner must abandon attachment to the five aggregates, overcome passion towards them, and attain liberation.

Arahanism does not dispute this teaching. The question concerns the scope of its conclusion.

SN 22.59 examines the five aggregates and repeatedly declares them *Anattā*. It does not provide a separate examination of a Soul-Spirit existing beyond these aggregates, nor does it explicitly formulate the proposition that no Soul-Spirit exists anywhere or in any dimension of spiritual reality. Its immediate subject is the five aggregates and the mistaken appropriation of their manifestations.

The statement that the five aggregates are not Attā cannot simply be transformed into the statement that Attā does not exist. These are different propositions. The first rejects the identification of Soul-Spirit with conditioned manifestations. The second denies Soul-Spirit altogether.

The distinction must be preserved.

#### 7.6.2. Sakkāya-diṭṭhi in MN 44: The Four Relationships

A further and particularly important passage appears in MN 44, the *Cūḷavedalla Sutta*. Here the lay follower Visākha questions the bhikkhunī Dhammadinnā concerning *Sakkāya*, its arising, cessation, and the path leading to its cessation. He also asks how *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* arises and how it is abandoned.

Dhammadinnā first identifies *Sakkāya*:

*Pañca kho ime, āvuso Visākha, upādānakkhandhā sakkāyo vutto bhagavatā.*

\"Friend Visākha, these five aggregates subject to clinging are what the Blessed One has declared to be Sakkāya.\"

She identifies them as:

1.  *Rūpupādānakkhandha*: the aggregate of form subject to clinging.

2.  *Vedanupādānakkhandha*: the aggregate of feeling subject to clinging.

3.  *Saññupādānakkhandha*: the aggregate of perception subject to clinging.

4.  *Saṅkhārupādānakkhandha*: the aggregate of formations subject to clinging.

5.  *Viññāṇupādānakkhandha*: the aggregate of consciousness subject to clinging.

This identification is important. The discourse explicitly identifies the five aggregates subject to clinging as *Sakkāya*. It then explains that craving is their arising, the cessation of craving is their cessation, and the Noble Eightfold Path is the way leading to their cessation.

Visākha subsequently asks how *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* arises. Dhammadinnā answers by presenting four relationships between each of the five aggregates and Attā.

The first formulation is: *Rūpaṃ attato samanupassati.*

One regards form as Attā.

The second is: *Rūpavantaṃ vā attānaṃ.*

One regards Attā as possessing form.

The third is: *Attani vā rūpaṃ.*

One regards form as existing within Attā.

The fourth is: *Rūpasmiṃ vā attānaṃ.*

One regards Attā as existing within form.

The same four formulations are then applied to feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. The resulting twenty formulations constitute the definition of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* given in this discourse.

Each formulation must be considered individually.

**First: Rūpaṃ attato samanupassati --- Regarding form as Attā**

This formulation identifies the physical body directly with Soul-Spirit. It treats the material body as the ultimate spiritual identity of the being.

Arahanism rejects this identification. The body is a conditioned physical manifestation. It is subject to illness, deterioration, ageing, and death. It cannot be the Higher Soul-Spirit towards which renunciant cultivation is directed.

The same reasoning applies to the other aggregates. Feeling is not Attā; perception is not Attā; formations are not Attā; and sense-generated consciousness is not Attā.

This first formulation is therefore consistent with the central teaching of SN 22.59.

**Second: Rūpavantaṃ vā attānaṃ --- Regarding Attā as possessing form**

This formulation places form in a relationship of possession with Attā. The question is whether the Soul-Spirit is understood as an entity that possesses the physical body or the other aggregates as its attributes, constituents, or possessions.

The difficulty with this formulation becomes apparent when possession is understood as implying mastery over what is possessed. If Attā is conceived as possessing the body in the sense of being able to determine and command its condition, the formulation conflicts with the observable reality of bodily affliction and deterioration.

Arahanism distinguishes this conception of possession from the relationship between Attā and the physical body. The body is a conditioned manifestation associated with the being, but it is not a possession that the Soul-Spirit can command according to worldly desire. Its deterioration does not establish the non-existence of Attā; it establishes the conditioned nature of the body.

**Third: Attani vā rūpaṃ --- Regarding form as existing within Attā**

This formulation places form within Attā. It presents the body or another aggregate as something contained within the Soul-Spirit.

Arahanism does not identify the physical body as something literally contained within Attā. The physical body belongs to the manifested material dimension, whereas Attā belongs to a higher spiritual dimension. Their relationship cannot be adequately explained through ordinary physical containment.

The same distinction applies to feeling, perception, formations, and sense-generated consciousness. Their occurrence in association with a being does not establish that they are components contained within Soul-Spirit.

**Fourth: Rūpasmiṃ vā attānaṃ --- Regarding Attā as existing within form**

This formulation reverses the preceding relationship. Instead of placing form within Attā, it places Attā within form.

Here the Arahanist interpretation requires particular attention. Arahanism understands Attā as the Higher Soul-Spirit associated with the being and identifies the heart as its seat. This does not mean that Attā is an anatomical organ, a physical substance, or a component of the material body. Nor does it mean that Soul-Spirit is physically contained within the body in the ordinary spatial sense.

The relationship between the higher spiritual dimension and the manifested physical body must be understood according to their different modes of existence. The presence of Attā in association with embodied existence does not make Attā identical with the body or establish that it is a material constituent of the body.

These four formulations are then repeated for the remaining aggregates. The resulting twenty forms of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* are rejected in the discourse. Dhammadinnā explains that the instructed noble disciple does not regard any of the five aggregates through these four relationships.

The scope of the passage is therefore clear: the practitioner abandons these twenty forms of identification and association between Attā and the five aggregates.

#### 7.6.3. What the Four Relationships Establish

MN 44 must be taken seriously. It explicitly identifies twenty forms of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi*, and it cannot be dismissed or treated as though it merely repeats the statement that the body is not Attā. Its four formulations examine different ways in which the aggregates may be related to Soul-Spirit.

Nevertheless, the discourse does not state that Soul-Spirit does not exist. It identifies the mistaken relationships through which an uninstructed person regards the five aggregates in relation to Attā. It then explains that an instructed noble disciple abandons these ways of regarding them.

This distinction is central.

The discourse does not present the four formulations as an exhaustive examination of every possible understanding of Soul-Spirit. It examines the five aggregates and the mistaken views associated with them. The rejection of these twenty formulations therefore establishes that the aggregates should not be regarded through these relationships. It does not, by itself, establish that no Attā exists beyond the aggregates.

The fourth formulation, *rūpasmiṃ vā attānaṃ*, is particularly important for the Arahanist discussion. It demonstrates that the discourse considers the possibility of regarding Attā as existing in relation to form. However, the fact that a particular relationship is included among the twenty forms of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* does not settle every question concerning the nature, location, or mode of existence of Soul-Spirit. Those questions require further examination.

Arahanism therefore distinguishes the canonical definition of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* from the wider doctrinal conclusions subsequently drawn from it.

The five aggregates are *Sakkāya*. Craving and attachment sustain their appropriation. The four relationships generate twenty forms of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi*. The Noble Eightfold Path leads to the cessation of *Sakkāya* through the cessation of craving. These are the explicit subjects addressed in MN 44.

A universal denial of Soul-Spirit is a further proposition. It cannot simply be inserted into the discourse without demonstrating how its actual statements establish that conclusion.

#### 7.6.4. The Expansion of Anattā into a Universal Denial of Soul-Spirit

The interpretation of *Anattā* has often been extended beyond the direct rejection of the five aggregates. In many contemporary translations and explanations, the teaching is presented as establishing that there is no self, no enduring spiritual identity, and no Soul-Spirit whatsoever.

This interpretation requires a distinction between the canonical statements and the philosophical conclusion being drawn from them.

SN 22.59 declares the five aggregates *Anattā*. MN 44 identifies twenty forms of *Sakkāya-diṭṭhi* arising from four relationships between those aggregates and Attā. Both discourses establish the necessity of abandoning attachment and mistaken identification with conditioned manifestations.

Yet neither passage, in its immediate argument, provides a comprehensive examination of every possible understanding of Attā beyond the five aggregates. Neither presents a separate analysis of the Higher Soul-Spirit as understood in the Arahanist teaching.

The problem arises when the rejection of the aggregates as Attā is expanded into the assertion that Attā itself does not exist. Such an expansion moves from a statement concerning the nature of conditioned manifestations to a universal metaphysical conclusion concerning the non-existence of Soul-Spirit.

These are not identical claims.

Arahanism maintains that the five aggregates are not Attā precisely because they are conditioned, afflicted, and incapable of remaining under complete personal command. The renunciant must overcome attachment to them and purify Citta through spiritual cultivation. The goal is liberation from worldly bondage and the attainment of Arahantship.

This understanding also brings the two canonical discourses into relationship with the elders\' testimonies examined earlier in this chapter. Their repeated use of *Attā*, their descriptions of disciplining and elevating Attā, their language of steadfast establishment, and their cultivation of Citta all form part of the textual evidence that must be considered when interpreting Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

The question cannot be settled by isolating the word *Anattā* from the wider body of canonical language. Nor can the repeated references to Attā in the elders\' verses be dismissed without examination. Both bodies of evidence must be considered together.

#### 7.6.5. The Arahanist Conclusion

The teachings of SN 22.59 and MN 44 establish an essential principle of renunciant training: the five aggregates are not Attā, and the practitioner must abandon every form of attachment and mistaken identification associated with them.

The body is not Soul-Spirit. Feeling is not Soul-Spirit. Perception is not Soul-Spirit. Formations are not Soul-Spirit. Sense-generated consciousness is not Soul-Spirit.

The four relationships examined in MN 44 further demonstrate that the aggregates must not be regarded as Attā, as possessions of Attā, as existing within Attā, or as containing Attā in the ordinary relationships described by those formulations.

Arahanism accepts the necessity of relinquishing these mistaken relationships. Its disagreement concerns the expansion of these teachings into a universal denial of Soul-Spirit.

The distinction between *Anattā* and the non-existence of Attā must therefore be maintained. The former is the canonical declaration concerning the five aggregates. The latter is a broader metaphysical proposition that requires evidence beyond the mere rejection of those aggregates as Soul-Spirit.

The liberation teaching must also be understood in relation to the spiritual training of the elders. They cultivated Attā, disciplined it, elevated it, and described their attainment through expressions concerning its steadfastness. Their testimonies, together with the inward direction of *Ajjhatta* and the purification of Citta, provide essential context for understanding the purpose of renunciant cultivation.

The central Arahanist position is therefore clear: the five aggregates are to be relinquished as objects of attachment and mistaken identification; Citta is to be purified and established inwardly; and Attā is to be liberated from worldly bondage through the attainment of Arahantship.

The question that remains is how the repeated language of Attā in the early renunciant verses came to be interpreted as though it were incompatible with Gautama\'s teaching. This requires an examination of the translation and interpretation of *Attā*, the treatment of its linguistic heritage, and the doctrinal developments through which the Soul-Spirit came to be displaced from the received understanding of liberation.

### 7.7. The Translation and Displacement of Attā: How the Soul-Spirit Was Lost in Buddhist Interpretation

A further question now demands our attention: How did the Soul-Spirit, repeatedly expressed through the word *Attā* in the testimonies of the early renunciants, become displaced from the received understanding of Buddhist liberation? How did Gautama\'s true Dhamma concerning the Soul-Spirit become lost, obscured, and ultimately transformed in the course of Buddhist history? How did a liberation teaching that speaks repeatedly of cultivating, disciplining, elevating, and establishing Attā come to be interpreted as a doctrine that denies its very existence?

These questions strike at the heart of the historical transformation and degeneration of Gautama\'s true teaching. They require us to examine how the meaning of *Attā* was progressively reinterpreted, how *Anattā* came to be understood as a universal denial of Soul-Spirit, and how the original spiritual direction of renunciant cultivation was displaced from the received understanding of liberation.

The preceding sections have established an important body of textual evidence. The Theragāthā and Therīgāthā repeatedly preserve expressions concerning *Attā*: the elders speak of disciplining it, elevating it, protecting it, rescuing it, and establishing it in a steadfast spiritual condition. Their language is closely connected with the cultivation of Citta, the development of Samādhi, and the inward direction of liberation expressed through *Ajjhatta*.

These testimonies raise a fundamental problem for the received interpretation of Buddhist doctrine. If *Attā* simply means an illusory personal identity that must be abandoned, why do the elders repeatedly speak of cultivating and elevating it? If Gautama\'s teaching categorically denies the existence of Soul-Spirit, how should we understand the spiritual language preserved in these renunciant testimonies?

The answer requires an examination of the linguistic heritage of *Attā*, the translation practices through which its meaning was conveyed, and the historical development of Buddhist interpretations of *Anattā*.

#### 7.7.1. Attā and Its Linguistic Heritage

The word *Attā* belongs to a much older Indian linguistic and spiritual heritage. Its Sanskrit counterpart is *Ātmā*, conventionally written *Ātman* in its grammatical stem form. The relationship between Sanskrit *ātman* and Pāli *attan/attā* is recognized in the historical dictionaries of Pāli. The *Pali-English Dictionary* of the Pali Text Society explicitly identifies Pāli *attan* with Vedic Sanskrit *ātman*. The *Critical Pāli Dictionary* likewise records this linguistic relationship.

This connection is significant because *Ātmā* was already an established expression within the Indian spiritual world before the emergence of the Buddhist community. Its meanings were not confined to an ordinary reflexive pronoun. It was associated with the life principle, the innermost spiritual reality of a being, and the question of what constitutes one\'s true spiritual existence.

The Vedic understanding of Ātmā concerns the life principle and the innermost spiritual reality of a being. The wider inquiry into Brahman concerns the ultimate spiritual principle and the nature of humanity and its progenitor *manussa*. These became central subjects of Indian spiritual foundation, although their precise meanings and relationship developed differently across the various Vedic schools and texts.

As the Indian spiritual traditions developed, the terminology and associated questions entered different linguistic and philosophical environments. Sanskrit *Ātmā*, Pāli *Attā*, and Prakrit *Appā* belong to this wider linguistic relationship. Different communities inherited, adapted, and developed the terminology according to their own languages and spiritual understandings.

The Jain tradition, for example, developed its own extensive teaching concerning *jīva*, while the various Vedic traditions elaborated different understandings of Ātmā and Brahman. These traditions should not be treated as doctrinally identical. Nevertheless, their shared intellectual environment demonstrates that the question of the spiritual principle of a being was a central concern in ancient India.

Gautama\'s teaching emerged within this wider Indian spiritual world. The word *Attā* was therefore not an isolated expression without an established linguistic or philosophical background. Its use in the early Buddhist discourses must be examined with that heritage in view.

The important question is whether the meaning of *Attā* in Gautama\'s teaching was preserved in subsequent Buddhist interpretation. Arahanism maintains that the repeated spiritual use of this term in the testimonies of the elders has not received the attention it warrants. Its reduction to a generic notion of personal identity has obscured a substantial dimension of the renunciant vocabulary.

#### 7.7.2. From Attā to \"Self\": The Degeneration of Meaning Through Mis-Translation

One of the most consequential distortions in the transmission of Gautama\'s liberation teaching has been the translation of *Attā* as \"self.\" This translation has profoundly influenced how generations of readers have understood the nature of the human being, the purpose of renunciant cultivation, and the ultimate goal of liberation.

Arahanism regards this development as a serious mis-translation that has contributed to the degeneration of the original spiritual meaning of Gautama\'s teaching.

The word \"self\" in modern English commonly refers to personal identity, individuality, personality, ego, or one\'s psychological sense of existence. These meanings are not equivalent to the spiritual reality that Arahanism understands through *Attā*. By repeatedly rendering *Attā* as \"self\" without preserving its deeper spiritual significance, translators have encouraged readers to approach the word through an increasingly narrow psychological and personal framework.

The consequences extend far beyond vocabulary. Once *Attā* is understood as merely the personal self, the elders\' instructions to discipline, cultivate, elevate, protect, and establish *Attā* are easily interpreted as instructions concerning ordinary self-development. Their spiritual significance is thereby diminished, and the Soul-Spirit that Arahanism recognizes in these expressions disappears from the received interpretation.

Consider the verse:

*Udakañhi nayanti nettikā, Usukārā namayanti tejanaṁ;\
Dāruṁ namayanti tacchakā, Attānaṁ damayanti subbatā ti.*

The conventional translation commonly renders the final line as \"the well-disciplined tame themselves.\" Arahanism reads the expression as referring to the discipline of Attā---the Soul-Spirit. This reading places the verse within the wider context of renunciant spiritual training, in which the practitioner must overcome worldly bondage and attain liberation.

Likewise, Hārita\'s testimony states:

*Samunnamayamattānaṁ, usukārova tejanaṁ;\
Cittaṁ ujuṁ karitvāna, avijjaṁ bhinda hāritā ti.*

Here, the language of elevating *Attā* appears alongside the instruction to straighten *Citta* and destroy ignorance. The two expressions belong to the same spiritual undertaking. Yet when *Attā* is reduced to \"self\" in the sense of personal identity, the deeper relationship between Soul-Spirit and Mind-Heart becomes obscured.

This problem is compounded when the translation \"self\" is subsequently used to justify the doctrine that Buddhism denies the existence of any Soul-Spirit. The reasoning becomes circular: *Attā* is first translated through a narrow conception of personal identity; that conception is then rejected through the doctrine of *Anattā*; and the resulting interpretation is presented as proof that Gautama denied Soul-Spirit altogether.

In this way, a questionable translation becomes the foundation for an entire doctrinal interpretation.

The historical dictionaries themselves provide reason to question this narrowing of meaning. The *Pali-English Dictionary* of the Pali Text Society records \"soul\" among the meanings associated with *attan* and identifies its relationship with Vedic Sanskrit *ātman*. The *Critical Pāli Dictionary* likewise documents this linguistic heritage. These references do not independently establish every aspect of the Arahanist interpretation, but they demonstrate that the spiritual meaning of the term cannot simply be dismissed as linguistically illegitimate.

A translation must convey the meaning of the original expression in its actual context. When a translation systematically removes a central spiritual dimension from the original terminology, it ceases to be an adequate representation of the teaching. The repeated substitution of \"self\" for *Attā*, without adequate recognition of its Soul-Spirit significance, has helped transform how Gautama\'s liberation teaching is understood.

Arahanism therefore regards the displacement of *Attā* through mis-translation as one of the significant contributors to the historical degeneration of Buddhist interpretation. The problem is not merely that an imperfect English equivalent was selected. It is that the resulting conceptual framework has obscured the spiritual subject of renunciant cultivation and helped establish an interpretation in which the very Soul-Spirit repeatedly expressed in the elders\' testimonies is denied.

Recovering the meaning of *Attā* is therefore essential to recovering the original spiritual direction of Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

#### 7.7.3. From Anattā to the Universal Denial of Soul-Spirit

The second major degeneration concerns the interpretation of *Anattā*. What began as the recognition that the five aggregates are not *Attā* came to be interpreted as a universal denial of Soul-Spirit. This expansion represents a fundamental departure from the distinction preserved in the early renunciant testimonies and has profoundly distorted the understanding of Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

As examined in Section 7.6, the *Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta* (SN 22.59) declares that form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are *Anattā*. The discourse explains that these aggregates are subject to affliction and cannot be commanded to remain in a desired condition. It instructs the practitioner to regard them as:

*Netaṁ mama, nesohamasmi, na meso attā.*

\"This is not mine; I am not this; this is not my Attā.\"

The immediate subject of this teaching is the five aggregates. The practitioner must recognize that these conditioned manifestations are not the true spiritual subject and must relinquish attachment to them.

The distinction is fundamental:

1.  The five aggregates are not *Attā*.

2.  No Soul-Spirit exists.

The first proposition is explicitly established in the discourse. The second is a much broader ontological conclusion that cannot simply be derived from the first. Yet, in the received interpretation of Buddhist doctrine, these two propositions have increasingly been treated as though they were identical.

This is where the doctrinal degeneration becomes apparent. The rejection of the aggregates as *Attā* has been extended into the denial of Soul-Spirit itself. A teaching directed towards recognizing the conditioned and afflicted nature of worldly manifestations has thus been made to support a universal doctrine of non-existence.

The historical development of Buddhist thought reveals that this interpretation was progressively systematized. After Gautama\'s passing, Buddhist communities developed different approaches to the nature of the person, continuity, consciousness, and liberation. Disagreements emerged over how the teachings concerning the aggregates and *Anattā* should be understood.

The existence of the Pudgalavāda traditions is particularly significant. These Buddhist schools developed a distinctive account of the person, demonstrating that the interpretation of personal existence remained a subject of substantial debate within Buddhist intellectual history. Other Buddhist scholastic traditions developed increasingly elaborate arguments concerning the absence of an enduring self or person.

Over time, the denial of a permanent self became a defining formulation of mainstream Buddhist philosophical identity. These interpretations were developed through generations of scholastic reasoning, defended against competing Indian philosophical positions, and incorporated into subsequent doctrinal presentations.

Arahanism identifies a fundamental error in this development: the rejection of the five aggregates as *Attā* was elevated into a comprehensive denial of Soul-Spirit. The distinction between rejecting conditioned manifestations as *Attā* and denying every possible spiritual reality was progressively obscured.

The consequences are profound. The positive spiritual language preserved in the Theragāthā and Therīgāthā becomes difficult to accommodate within a framework that has already excluded Soul-Spirit. The elders\' repeated instructions to discipline, elevate, protect, and establish *Attā* are consequently interpreted through assumptions that diminish or remove their spiritual significance.

This is not a minor difference in terminology. It concerns the very subject and purpose of liberation. When *Anattā* is interpreted as the universal non-existence of Soul-Spirit, the original spiritual direction of renunciant cultivation is displaced. The practitioner is no longer understood as cultivating and liberating Attā from worldly bondage; instead, liberation is presented through a framework in which the existence of Attā itself must be denied.

Arahanism regards this development as a serious doctrinal degeneration. The teaching concerning the rejection of the five aggregates as *Attā* has been expanded beyond its immediate subject and made to carry a universal conclusion that the discourse does not explicitly formulate.

The central question must therefore be confronted: did Gautama teach that the five aggregates are not Soul-Spirit, or did he teach that Soul-Spirit does not exist at all? These are fundamentally different propositions. Their conflation has been one of the most consequential sources of misunderstanding in the received interpretation of Buddhist liberation.

#### 7.7.4. The Doctrinal Degeneration and Displacement of Attā in Buddhist Interpretation

The exclusion of Soul-Spirit from mainstream Buddhist doctrine cannot be attributed to a single mistranslation or one isolated interpretation. It is the consequence of successive doctrinal developments through which the original spiritual meaning of *Attā* was progressively obscured, reinterpreted, and ultimately displaced. What began with the rejection of the five aggregates as *Attā* came to be established as a comprehensive doctrine denying the existence of Soul-Spirit.

Arahanism regards this development as one of the most consequential degenerations in the history of Buddhist interpretation. It has affected not merely the understanding of a particular Pāli term, but the very foundation upon which Gautama\'s liberation teaching is understood.

Several developments contributed to this degeneration.

**First: the elevation of analytical interpretations above the original spiritual teachings.**

The early discourses repeatedly direct the renunciant towards the purification and liberation of Citta, the abandonment of worldly attachment, and the attainment of the Deathless. The elders\' testimonies also preserve repeated references to *Attā* in the context of spiritual discipline and attainment.

However, subsequent Buddhist intellectual traditions increasingly approached liberation through systematic analyses of conditioned phenomena. The aggregates, their characteristics, and their relationship to personal existence became subjects of increasingly elaborate philosophical classification.

The Abhidhamma traditions played a significant role in this development. They produced extensive analytical systems concerning mental and physical phenomena and became influential in the subsequent presentation of Theravāda doctrine. Arahanism does not recognize the Abhidhamma as part of Gautama\'s original textual authority. Its historical influence must nevertheless be acknowledged.

The danger arises when later analytical frameworks become the controlling authority through which the Sutta is interpreted. Instead of allowing the early discourses and the elders\' testimonies to establish the spiritual direction of the teaching, later categories are imposed upon them. Expressions concerning the cultivation and establishment of *Attā* are then reinterpreted to fit a framework in which Soul-Spirit has already been excluded.

**Second: the systematic establishment of the doctrine of no-self.**

The debates among Buddhist schools concerning the person, consciousness, continuity, and liberation contributed to the increasingly systematic rejection of an enduring self.

The Pudgalavāda traditions demonstrate that Buddhist communities did not always hold one uniform position concerning the person. Their distinctive account of the *pudgala* became the subject of extensive debate with other Buddhist schools. These historical disagreements show that the interpretation of personal and spiritual existence was a contested subject within Buddhist intellectual history.

Nevertheless, the rejection of an enduring person or self became increasingly central to the doctrinal identity of influential Buddhist traditions. Arguments concerning the five aggregates and the absence of a permanent personal entity were developed into comprehensive philosophical positions.

Arahanism challenges the assumption that these later systematic formulations can simply be identified with Gautama\'s original teaching. The existence of an elaborate philosophical argument does not establish that its conclusion was explicitly taught in the early discourses. Each claim must be tested against the actual words and spiritual context of the Sutta.

The fundamental error, from the Arahanist perspective, was allowing the rejection of conditioned aggregates as *Attā* to become a universal denial of Soul-Spirit. This went beyond the immediate teaching of SN 22.59 and obscured the distinction that the elders\' positive use of *Attā* requires us to confront.

**Third: the authority of commentarial interpretations over the early testimonies.**

The commentarial traditions further consolidated particular interpretations of the Buddha\'s teachings. Their explanations became influential in determining how later generations understood *Attā*, *Anattā*, the aggregates, and liberation.
