Within mainstream Theravāda, the doctrine of non-self became firmly established as a defining principle. Commentarial explanations consequently shaped the way canonical passages were read and understood.

Arahanism rejects the elevation of these later explanations above the Sutta and Vinaya. Commentarial authority cannot be treated as a substitute for the original discourses, particularly when its conclusions are used to dismiss or reinterpret the explicit spiritual language preserved in the early renunciant verses.

The elders\' testimonies must be allowed to speak for themselves. Their repeated references to disciplining, elevating, protecting, and establishing *Attā* cannot be dismissed merely because later doctrinal systems have adopted a different understanding of the term.

Where later interpretations contradict or obscure the spiritual direction evident in the early texts, those interpretations must themselves be subjected to criticism.

**Fourth: the institutionalization of mis-translation and doctrinal distortion.**

The translation of *Attā* as \"self\" and *Anattā* as \"no-self\" has helped institutionalize the interpretation that Soul-Spirit does not exist.

As these translations entered dictionaries, textbooks, academic publications, religious instruction, and modern Buddhist literature, they became familiar formulations through which Buddhism was presented to successive generations.

The distinction between \"the aggregates are not Attā\" and \"there is no Soul-Spirit\" consequently became increasingly difficult for ordinary readers to recognize. The latter proposition came to be presented as though it were the straightforward and unquestionable meaning of the former.

Modern scholarship has further reinforced this framework by frequently describing early Buddhism as a doctrine that rejects the existence of a permanent self or soul. Although scholars differ in their explanations and philosophical formulations, the general denial of an enduring spiritual subject has become a dominant feature of modern presentations of Buddhist thought.

Arahanism challenges this intellectual consensus. Scholarly acceptance does not establish textual truth. A proposition does not become Gautama\'s teaching simply because generations of translators, commentators, academics, and religious authorities have repeated it.

The responsibility of scholarship is to examine the original texts, not to protect inherited doctrinal conclusions from scrutiny.

**Fifth: the suppression of the spiritual significance of the elders\' testimonies.**

Perhaps the clearest evidence of this degeneration is the way the positive language of *Attā* in the Theragāthā and Therīgāthā has been marginalized.

The elders repeatedly speak of *Attā* in connection with spiritual discipline, elevation, protection, steadfastness, and attainment. These expressions are not isolated references without connection to the wider liberation process. They occur alongside instructions concerning Citta, Samādhi, the abandonment of worldly attachment, and the overcoming of ignorance.

Yet the dominant no-self framework has encouraged these passages to be interpreted through the assumption that *Attā* cannot refer to a genuine Soul-Spirit. Their language is consequently reduced to ordinary self-discipline, personal composure, or psychological development.

This interpretive approach places the conclusion before the evidence. The doctrine of no-self is first accepted as an unquestionable premise; the positive references to *Attā* are then reinterpreted so that they cannot challenge that premise.

Arahanism rejects this method. The elders\' words must be examined without first imposing a later doctrinal conclusion upon them. Their repeated references to *Attā* are substantial textual evidence and must be given their proper place in understanding the liberation teaching.

The consequences of this historical degeneration are profound. The Soul-Spirit has been removed from the centre of Buddhist liberation, the inward direction of *Ajjhatta* has been obscured, and the relationship between *Attā* and Citta has been deprived of its original spiritual significance.

The practitioner is consequently presented with a fundamentally different account of liberation: one in which the existence of Soul-Spirit is denied, rather than one in which the Soul-Spirit is liberated from worldly bondage through the complete eradication of *Rāga*, *Dosa*, and *Moha*.

Arahanism considers this a serious departure from Gautama\'s true Dhamma. The cumulative effect of mis-translation, doctrinal expansion, commentarial authority, and inherited scholarly assumptions has obscured the spiritual teaching preserved in the early renunciant testimonies.

These developments must no longer be accepted merely because they have become established in Buddhist tradition. They must be examined, challenged, and measured against the original teachings.

The question is no longer simply how later generations came to understand *Attā* differently. It is whether the dominant interpretation of Buddhist doctrine has preserved Gautama\'s liberation teaching or has progressively displaced its original spiritual foundation.

For Arahanism, the answer demands a fundamental reassessment of the received doctrine of *Anattā* and the recovery of the Soul-Spirit at the heart of renunciant cultivation.

### 7.8. The Restoration of Saddhamma: Recovering Gautama, the Supreme Arahant\'s True Liberation Teaching

The examination undertaken throughout this chapter brings us to a matter of the greatest significance: the restoration of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant\'s true liberation teaching concerning the Soul-Spirit.

We have examined the repeated language of *Attā* in the testimonies of the early renunciants, its linguistic heritage in the wider Indian spiritual tradition, the relationship between *Attā*, *Citta*, and *Ajjhatta*, and the teachings concerning the five aggregates and *Anattā*. We have also confronted the historical developments through which the spiritual meaning of *Attā* became obscured by mis-translation, doctrinal expansion, and subsequent interpretations.

The evidence presented throughout this chapter demands serious reconsideration of the dominant understanding of Buddhist liberation. The elders repeatedly speak of disciplining, elevating, protecting, and establishing *Attā*. Their spiritual vocabulary connects the cultivation of *Citta* with Samādhi, inward establishment, the abandonment of worldly attachment, and the attainment of liberation. These testimonies cannot responsibly be dismissed or reduced to ordinary psychological self-development simply because later interpretations have come to deny the existence of Soul-Spirit.

For Arahanism, the central issue is unmistakable: the five aggregates are not *Attā*, but the recognition of this truth does not establish the non-existence of Soul-Spirit. The renunciant\'s task is to abandon attachment to conditioned worldly manifestations, purify Citta, overcome *Rāga*, *Dosa*, and *Moha*, and attain liberation from worldly bondage. This is the spiritual direction that Arahanism recognizes in Gautama\'s original liberation teaching.

#### 7.8.1. The Significance of What Has Been Lost

The displacement of Soul-Spirit from the understanding of Buddhist liberation has consequences extending far beyond the translation of a single Pāli word.

When *Attā* is reduced to an ordinary personal identity, the spiritual significance of the elders\' testimonies is diminished. When *Anattā* is expanded into a universal denial of Soul-Spirit, the distinction between conditioned manifestations and the spiritual reality towards which renunciant cultivation is directed becomes obscured. When *Citta* is understood without its relationship to *Attā* and *Ajjhatta*, the inward direction of spiritual training loses its proper place.

The entire purpose of liberation is consequently presented through a different doctrinal framework.

This is a matter of profound concern. The path of renunciation is undertaken to transcend worldly bondage, overcome the defilements, and attain the Deathless. It involves a complete transformation of one\'s spiritual condition through Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā, culminating in *Nibbidā*, *Virāga*, and *Vimutti*.

If the Soul-Spirit is excluded from this understanding, how are the elders\' repeated references to its cultivation and elevation to be understood? If the inward spiritual direction of *Ajjhatta* is overlooked, how can the full significance of the renunciant\'s training be recognized?

These questions cannot be avoided. They strike at the foundation of how Gautama\'s liberation teaching is understood and transmitted.

For nearly two millennia, Buddhist traditions have developed, preserved, and transmitted interpretations of *Anattā* that have frequently been presented as the definitive denial of Soul-Spirit. Generations of practitioners have inherited these interpretations, often without being encouraged to reconsider the wider textual evidence or the spiritual significance of the elders\' language.

The tragedy, from the Arahanist perspective, is that the words themselves have survived. The testimonies of the elders remain in the Theragāthā and Therīgāthā. Their references to *Attā* have not disappeared. Yet their deeper spiritual significance has been obscured by the very interpretive framework through which these texts have been transmitted.

A teaching can remain physically preserved in scripture while its meaning becomes lost to successive generations. This is the danger that Arahanism seeks to confront.

#### 7.8.2. The Urgency of Restoring Saddhamma

The restoration of the *Saddhamma* cannot be postponed indefinitely.

The passage of centuries does not turn an interpretation into truth. The repetition of a doctrinal position by religious institutions, commentators, translators, and scholars does not establish that it faithfully represents the original teaching. Nor should the authority of an established tradition prevent sincere practitioners from examining the words of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, directly.

Future generations deserve more than inherited conclusions. They deserve access to the original textual evidence and the opportunity to examine the teachings for themselves. They must be able to distinguish what Gautama actually taught from interpretations developed in subsequent generations.

They must be able to ask:

What did the early renunciants mean when they spoke of *Attā*? Why did they repeatedly describe its discipline, elevation, protection, and establishment? What is the relationship between *Attā*, *Citta*, and *Ajjhatta*? What exactly did Gautama reject through his teaching of *Anattā*? And how did the denial of the five aggregates as *Attā* become an established doctrine denying Soul-Spirit altogether?

These are not peripheral questions. They concern the nature of the human being, the purpose of renunciation, and the ultimate goal of spiritual cultivation.

Arahanism therefore considers the restoration of the Soul-Spirit to be an urgent responsibility. The early texts must be examined again, inherited assumptions must be challenged, and later interpretations must be measured against the Sutta and Vinaya.

The purpose is to distinguish truth from error, the original liberation teaching from subsequent doctrinal constructions, and the spiritual testimony of the elders from interpretations that have obscured their meaning.

How much longer should generations inherit a doctrine without being given the opportunity to examine the evidence that may challenge it?

The *Saddhamma* must be brought back into the centre of this inquiry. Its preservation requires more than the physical survival of scriptures. It requires the preservation of their true spiritual meaning.

#### 7.8.3. A Bhikkhu\'s Responsibility as a Son and Heir of the Dhamma

This undertaking also arises from a deeply personal responsibility.

I am a bhikkhu, a son of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, born from his Dhamma and heir to his Dhamma. My life has been dedicated to renunciation, spiritual cultivation, and the pursuit of liberation through the teaching of the Supreme Teacher. The Dhamma is not merely a subject of intellectual study to me. It is my spiritual inheritance, the foundation of my renunciant life, and the path to the Deathless.

How, then, can I remain silent when I see what I understand to be the true Dhamma concerning Soul-Spirit obscured, misrepresented, and displaced by interpretations that deny its very existence?

How can I simply accept that a doctrine I regard as contrary to Gautama\'s original liberation teaching has assumed such a dominant position in the Buddhist world? How can I bear to see generations of practitioners inherit this understanding without being given the opportunity to examine the evidence preserved in the words of the early renunciants?

I cannot bear it.

A son must not abandon his inheritance when it is being obscured. An heir must take responsibility for what has been entrusted to him. For a bhikkhu who has dedicated his life to the Dhamma of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the preservation of its truth is a profound spiritual responsibility.

I cannot simply inherit an interpretation because it has been repeated for centuries. I cannot regard institutional acceptance as proof of truth. And I cannot allow the passage of nearly two millennia to become a reason for leaving fundamental questions unexamined.

This is not a matter of seeking recognition, establishing personal authority, or gaining acceptance from existing Buddhist institutions. It is a matter of fidelity to Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, and to the Dhamma that I have undertaken to preserve.

If the Dhamma has been misunderstood, it must be examined. If its meaning has been obscured, it must be brought back into the light. If the words of the elders have been misinterpreted, they must be read again. If generations have inherited an understanding that contradicts the original spiritual direction of liberation, that understanding must be challenged.

I cannot accept silence as an appropriate response to what I regard as such a profound loss.

#### 7.8.4. The Mission of Arahanism: Preserving the Dhamma for Generations to Come

Arahanism undertakes this work as a commitment to the preservation, restoration, and manifestation of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant\'s original liberation teaching.

Its foundation is the Sutta and Vinaya. Its concern is the recovery of the spiritual meaning preserved in the early discourses and the testimonies of the renunciants. Its purpose is to restore the proper understanding of *Attā*, *Citta*, *Ajjhatta*, and *Anattā* within the wider process of liberation.

This undertaking requires courage to question established interpretations, intellectual discipline to examine the original texts, and spiritual commitment to uphold the truth even when it challenges prevailing religious conventions.

Arahanism does not seek to preserve a doctrine merely because it belongs to an established tradition. Nor does it seek to replace one inherited dogma with another. Its commitment is to examine what is true through the original teachings and to restore what has been obscured.

The generations to come must know that the interpretation of *Anattā* as a universal denial of Soul-Spirit is not beyond examination. They must encounter the elders\' testimonies, understand the significance of their language, and have the opportunity to consider the Arahanist interpretation of Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

They must be able to distinguish the words of the Supreme Arahant from the interpretations of later generations. They must be able to recognize the difference between the five conditioned aggregates and the Soul-Spirit, between sense-generated consciousness and Citta, and between worldly identification and the inward spiritual direction of liberation.

Above all, they must have the opportunity to understand the purpose of renunciation: the eradication of *Rāga*, *Dosa*, and *Moha*, the purification of Citta, and the liberation of Attā from worldly bondage through Arahantship.

The loss of a teaching does not necessarily mean that its words have disappeared. Sometimes the words remain in the scriptures, while their meaning becomes obscured by the interpretations imposed upon them. The testimonies of the elders remain. Their verses have survived. Their references to *Attā* have not vanished from the canonical record. What must now be recovered is their true spiritual significance.

Nearly two millennia of obscuration are enough. The generations to come must have the opportunity to know what is true and what is false, what belongs to Gautama\'s original teaching and what arose through subsequent interpretation.

The *Saddhamma* must be preserved. The inheritance of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, must not be surrendered to misunderstanding.

I undertake this work as a son and heir of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant\'s Dhamma: to preserve what is true, to expose what is false, and to restore the original liberation teaching for the generations yet to come.

**This is the responsibility of Arahanism. This is the purpose of my renunciant life. And this is the Dhamma that I undertake to preserve for the future.**

## About the Author

**Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera** is a Canadian Theravāda bhikkhu, meditation teacher, religious scholar, and author. He is the **Founder and Spiritual Head of the Arahanist Order of Canada** and the **Founding Ācariya of Arahanism --- The Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**. His life\'s work is dedicated to preserving, recovering, articulating, and transmitting Gautama\'s original liberation teaching, with particular emphasis on renunciation, spiritual cultivation, and the attainment of Arahantship. As Founding Ācariya, he undertakes the contemporary teaching and development of Arahanism as a living spiritual movement grounded in the ancient liberation path.

Ordained as a bhikkhu in 2013 at Pa-Auk Forest Monastery and Meditation Center in Myanmar, Bhante Mudita undertook intensive training in *samatha--vipassanā* meditation, Pāḷi Canon study, and monastic discipline. He subsequently spent five years practising within the Thai forest tradition, deepening his training through solitude, simplicity, renunciation, and sustained contemplative practice. Returning to Canada in November 2024, he devoted himself to the systematic teaching and development of Arahanism.

His scholarship encompasses early Buddhist literature, Pāḷi canonical studies, meditation, renunciant spirituality, and comparative religious research. Through translations, extensive commentaries, and doctrinal studies, he examines the teachings and spiritual testimonies preserved in ancient religious texts. His research gives particular attention to the *Theragāthā* and *Therīgāthā*, whose verses preserve the first-person accounts of early elder Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs who undertook the renunciant life and pursued liberation. He approaches these testimonies not merely as historical or literary compositions, but as records of spiritual training, discipline, and realization that illuminate the practical path towards freedom from worldly bondage.

His wider publications explore Gautama\'s liberation teaching, the *Dhammapada*, the *Suttanipāta*, meditation and Samādhi, the *Bhagavad-Gītā*, early Christianity, and the broader Indian spiritual heritage. Across these subjects, his work investigates the foundations of renunciation, the nature of spiritual existence, and the ultimate purpose of religious practice.

His contemplative practice and doctrinal investigations give particular attention to the relationship between **Attā, Citta, and Viññāṇa**. Within the Arahanist understanding, *Attā* refers to Soul-Spirit, *Citta* to the Mind-Heart requiring purification and spiritual establishment, and *Viññāṇa* to sense-generated consciousness. His work seeks to recover the distinctions between these dimensions of human existence and reconsider their significance within Gautama\'s liberation teaching. He places particular importance on the relationship between *Attā* and *Ajjhatta*, the inward spiritual direction of cultivation, and the teaching of *Anattā* in relation to the five conditioned aggregates.

At the heart of his teaching is the progressive cultivation of **Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā**, leading towards **Nibbidā, Virāga, and Vimutti**. Through ethical withdrawal, restraint of the senses, concentration, and liberating wisdom, the practitioner works towards the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---worldly craving and attachment, survival instincts of fear, anger, aversion, violence, and hostility, and ignorance and delusion. This training culminates in the purification of Citta, the liberation of Attā from worldly bondage, and the realization of *Nibbāna-dhātu*, the Deathless realm.

As Founder and Spiritual Head of the **Arahanist Order of Canada**, Bhante Mudita seeks to establish a living Canadian Theravāda lineage dedicated to canonical study, monastic discipline, meditation, progressive renunciation, and the training of future generations of renunciants. His work also encompasses religious education, scholarly publication, and the preservation of Arahanist literature through international digital archives and open research platforms.

As the Founding Ācariya of Arahanism, his enduring commitment is to preserve, restore, and manifest **the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant**, so that future generations may encounter the testimonies of the ancient renunciants, examine the foundations of spiritual cultivation, and undertake the path towards its ultimate goal: liberation from worldly bondage and the realization of the Deathless.

Looking towards the future, Bhante Mudita envisions Arahanism developing into a living spiritual tradition through which Gautama\'s original liberation teaching can be studied, practised, and transmitted across generations. His long-term aspiration is to establish a sustainable foundation for monastic training, spiritual education, contemplative practice, and the progressive cultivation of renunciants, while making the teachings and literature of Arahanism accessible to sincere seekers throughout the world. He hopes that future generations will carry forward this work with the same commitment to truth, renunciation, and spiritual realization, preserving the *Saddhamma* and ensuring that the path towards Arahantship remains a living possibility for humanity.
